The algebraic modular functor conjecture in type A n A_{n} quantum Teichmüller theory
Gus Schrader, Alexander Shapiro

Abstract
. Fock and Goncharov introduced a quantization of higher Teichmüller theory using cluster Poisson varieties and their noncommutative deformations, associating to a complex semisimple Lie group G G and a marked surface S S a quantum algebra 𝕃 G , S \mathbb{L}_{G,S} equipped with an action of the surface mapping class group. They conjectured that these quantizations form an algebraic analog of a modular functor: cutting a surface along a simple closed curve should correspond to a canonical gluing isomorphism for the associated algebras. In this paper we prove this conjecture for G = PGL n + 1 G=\mathrm{PGL}_{n+1} . Our approach requires two extensions of the Fock–Goncharov framework: (1) enhanced moduli spaces incorporating additional boundary data, providing algebro-geometric analogs of Fenchel–Nielsen twist coordinates; and (2) the residue universal Laurent ring, a refinement of the quantum universal Laurent ring obtained by localizing and imposing residue conditions. Using these tools, we construct canonical cutting isomorphisms that are equivariant under mapping class group actions and suffice to reconstruct the entire algebra 𝕃 G , S \mathbb{L}_{G,S} from data associated to the cut surface.



1.
Introduction

In a series of papers roughly two decades ago, Fock and Goncharov described a precise sense in which higher rank Teichmüller theory admits a
quantization
. Their approach to constructing this quantization involved two key insights. The first of these is that if
S
is a surface with a nonempty collection of interior marked points and
G=\mathrm{PGL}_{n}(\mathbb{C})
, the
moduli space of decorated
G
-local systems on
S

provides a natural setting for an algebro-geometric approach to rank
n
Teichmüller theory. Indeed, in
[
FG06b
]
it is shown that these moduli spaces are
cluster Poisson varieties
: they admit ‘cluster atlases’ whose charts are split algebraic tori with distinguished coordinate frames, and whose gluing maps are composites of certain special subtraction-free birational maps known as cluster mutations. Moreover, the natural action of the surface mapping class group
\Gamma_{S}
on the moduli space is realized in these coordinates as a composite of cluster transformations. Any cluster Poisson variety has a well-defined totally positive locus, and for the moduli spaces above with
G=\mathrm{PGL}_{2}
, Fock and Goncharov show that a certain symplectic leaf in this locus is canonically identified with the classical Teichmüller space for the surface obtained by regarding the interior marked points as punctures. This identification is compatible with the mapping class group actions on both sides.

The second key insight, developed in
[
FG09a
,
FG09b
]
, is that cluster Poisson varieties admit ‘chartwise’ non-commutative deformations, in which the combinatorics of the cluster atlas is used to construct something like a ‘non-commutative scheme.’ In more detail, one assigns to each chart the atlas a non-commutative ‘quantum torus algebra’
\mathcal{T}^{q}
defined over
\mathbb{Z}[q^{\pm 1}]
, whose
q=1
specialization recovers the coordinate ring of the chart in question. And for a pair of charts related by a single mutation, it was explained in
[
FG09a
]
that there exists a non-commutative analog of the classical gluing map, defined using the quantum dilogarithm function. Crucially, they show that any relation between classical mutations lifts to a relation between the corresponding quantum ones, ensuring the consistency of the gluing at the quantum level. Taking ‘global sections’ of the resulting object, we get a single non-commutative algebra
\mathbb{L}
known as the
quantum universal Laurent ring
associated to the cluster Poisson variety, which consists of elements that are skew Laurent polynomials in every quantum torus in the cluster atlas. Moreover, any symmetry of the classical variety which can be realized via cluster transformations automatically extends to an automorphism of
\mathbb{L}
.

In this way, given a pair
(G,S)
as above Fock and Goncharov construct in
[
FG09a
]
a non-commutative algebra
\mathbb{L}_{G,S}
carrying an action of the mapping class group
\Gamma_{S}
by quantum cluster transformations. Yet several natural structures and properties of these quantizations have remained conjectural since their definition. First, associated to each simple closed curve
c
on S and finite dimensional representation
V
of
G
, there is a canonical function
\chi_{V}(c)
on the classical moduli space computing the trace of the monodromy of the local system along
c
in the representation
V
. In
[
FG09a
]
, it was conjectured that there should exist corresponding elements
\chi^{q}_{V}(c)
of the quantum universal Laurent ring
\mathbb{L}_{G,S}
, and that for a fixed curve
c
these elements should generate a commutative subalgebra
R_{G}(c)
in
\mathbb{L}_{G,S}
isomorphic to a copy of the representation ring of
G
.

In the same paper, Fock and Goncharov also made a deeper conjecture: based on the physical interpretation of the moduli space of flat connections on a surface as parametrizing solutions of the equations of motion in Chern-Simons theory, they suggested that the moduli spaces of decorated local systems and their quantizations should provide an algebraic avatar of a topological quantum field theory. A little more precisely, they conjectured that for fixed
G
, the assignment
S\rightsquigarrow\mathbb{L}_{G,S}
should behave like an algebraic analog of a modular functor in 2D conformal field theory in the following sense.

It is immediate from the construction of
\mathbb{L}_{G,S}
that each interior ‘puncture’ point
p
on
S
gives rise to a central (and thus mutation-invariant) subalgebra
R_{T}(p)\subset\mathbb{L}_{G,S}
isomorphic to the representation ring of the maximal torus
T
of
G
. Less obviously, whenever
p
is not the only special point on its connected component, there is also an action of the Weyl group
W_{G}
on
\mathbb{L}_{G,S}
by cluster transformations, which restricts to the reflection representation on the central subalgebra
R_{T}(p)
. Hence the subalgebra of invariants
R_{G}(p)=R_{T}(p)^{W_{p}}
gives a canonical copy of the representation ring of
G
associated to each such puncture.

Now suppose that
c
is a simple closed curve on
S
, and
S^{\prime}
the surface obtained by cutting
S
open along
c
, and
S^{\circ}
the surface obtained by shrinking the corresponding boundary circles
c_{\pm}
on
S^{\prime}
to punctures
p_{\pm}
. Then there is a central ideal
\mathcal{I}_{c}\subset\mathbb{L}_{G,S^{\prime}}
generated by the relations
\chi_{p_{+}}=\chi^{*}_{p_{-}}
identifying the central element in
R_{T}(p_{+})
corresponding to a character
\chi
with the dual character in
R_{T}(p_{-})
. Assuming that neither of
p_{\pm}
is the only special point on its connected component in
S^{\prime}
, we have an action of the product of Weyl groups
W_{p_{+}}\times W_{p_{-}}
, and the ideal
\mathcal{I}_{c}
is preserved by the diagonal subgroup
W(c)
.
The centralizer
\Gamma_{S;c}
of the Dehn twist along
c
in the mapping class group
\Gamma_{S}
can be identified with the quotient of the mapping class group
\Gamma_{S^{\prime}}
by the central subgroup generated by the product
\tau_{c_{+}}\tau_{c_{-}}
of Dehn twists along
c_{\pm}
.

Conjecture 1.1
.

[Algebraic modular functor conjecture for
G=\mathrm{PGL}_{n+1}
; see
[
FG09a
]
Section 6.2,
[
GS19
]
Conjectures 2.27 and 2.29.]

For any essential simple closed curve
c
as above, there is a canonical subalgebra
R_{G}(c)\subset\mathbb{L}_{G,S}
, and an isomorphism of algebras

(1.1)

\displaystyle\eta_{c}\colon\mathbb{L}_{G,S}^{R_{G}(c)}\simeq\left(\mathbb{L}_{G,S^{\prime}}/\mathcal{I}_{c}\right)^{W(c)}

where
\mathbb{L}_{G,S}^{R_{G}(c)}
denotes the centralizer of
R_{G}(c)
in
\mathbb{L}_{G,S}
. The map (
1.1
) is equivariant with respect to the action of
\Gamma_{S;c}
by cluster transformations on both sides, and restricts to an isomorphism
R_{G}(c)\simeq R_{G}(p)
.

The main result of this paper is a proof this conjecture. Our proof involves developing extensions of both of the original key insights of Fock and Goncharov: the classical limit of our story is most naturally formulated in terms of a new version of the moduli space of decorated local systems incorporating extra data associated to the boundary components
c_{\pm}
created when cutting
S
along
c
, while to construct its quantization we need to go beyond the standard theory of cluster algebras and work with a new object we call the
residue universal Laurent ring.

The need to work with an enhanced version of the moduli spaces can be understood by analogy with classical Teichm̈uller theory for bordered Riemann surfaces with parametrized geodesic boundaries of non-fixed length, as considered by Mirzakhani
[
Mir07
]
and Alekseev–Meinrenken
[
AM24
]
. Indeed, for each pants decomposition of a surface
S
with
s
parametrized geodesic boundaries (i.e. a presentation of
S
as the collection of 3-holed spheres glued along a collection of pairs of boundary curves
c_{i}
) there is a corresponding system of Fenchel–Nielsen length/twist coordinates
(l_{i},\tau_{i})_{i=1}^{3g-3+2s}
on
\mathrm{Teich}_{\mathrm{bordered}}(S)
which are canonically conjugate with respect to the Weil–Petersson form. The coordinates
l_{i}
parametrizes the geodesic length of the boundary curve
c_{i}
, while the dual coordinates
\tau_{i}
are the twist parameters used to glue the pieces together. In this context, the gluing map amounts to a
symplectic reduction
: in order to glue the pants along a pair of geodesic boundaries
c_{\pm}
we require
l_{c_{+}}=l_{c_{-}}
, while simultaneously twisting both boundaries
c_{\pm}
by the same small angle results in the same hyperbolic structure after gluing.

In the original algebro-geometric approach of Fock–Goncharov, the length coordinates
l_{i}
associated to a boundary component
c_{+}
has a direct interpretation in terms of the Poisson-central subalgebra
R_{T}(p)
for the surface where we shrink
c_{+}
to a puncture, but the dual twist coordinate is necessarily absent, since it does not Poisson commute with
l_{i}
. In Definition
2.20
we introduce a new kind of algebro-geometric datum associated to boundary components which will allows us to incorporate analogs of the twist coordinates, and therefore construct well-behaved gluing maps. The components to which we associate such data we call
tacked circles
. We construct a cluster Poisson structure on this new moduli space
\mathcal{P}^{\diamond}_{G,S}
, allowing us to quantize it following the procedure of Fock–Goncharov. And in parallel to the classical gluing construction sketched above, given a surface
S^{\prime}
with pair of tacked circles
c_{\pm}
and a diffeomorphism
\phi\colon c_{+}\simeq c_{-}
we show there is a canonical quantum Hamiltonian reduction
\mathbb{L}^{c}_{G,S^{\prime}}
of the corresponding quantum universal Laurent ring which carries an action of the Weyl group
W(c_{\pm})
as in Conjecture
1.1
.

However it turns out that the symplectically reduced universal Laurent ring
\mathbb{L}^{c}_{G,S^{\prime}}
is still not sufficient to reconstruct the universal Laurent ring
\mathbb{L}_{G,S}
associated to the glued surface. To achieve this, we define the residue universal Laurent ring
\mathbb{L}_{G,S^{\prime};\phi}
associated to the gluing datum
(S^{\prime},\phi)
by localizing
\mathbb{L}_{G,S^{\prime}}
at a canonical mutation-invariant collection of divisors
Ø(c_{\pm})
, taking invariants for
W(c_{\pm})
, and then passing to the subalgebra consisting of elements having only simple poles at each such divisor with residues satisfying a certain condition — see Definition
8.6
for details. With these definitions, we in fact prove the following stronger result allowing us to completely reconstruct
\mathbb{L}_{G,S}
in terms of data associated to the cut surface
S^{\prime}
:

Main Theorem
.

Let
(G,S,c)
be as in Conjecture
1.1
. Then there is a
\Gamma_{S;c}
equivariant algebra isomorphism

(1.2)

\displaystyle\eta_{c}\colon\mathbb{L}_{G,S}\simeq\mathbb{L}_{G,S^{\prime};\phi}.

Let us now give a sketch of how we prove Theorem
Main Theorem
. In the Fock–Goncharov theory, the most important charts in the cluster atlas for moduli spaces of decorated local systems on
S
are those associated to
ideal triangulations
of
S
. Our construction of the gluing isomorphism (
1.2
) when
\mathrm{rank}(G)>1
involves different charts specially adapted to a simple closed curve
c
of the form described in Conjecture
1.1
, where each component of
S^{\prime}
has at least one boundary component or special point apart from the tacked circles
c_{\pm}
. Any such curve on S can be isolated in an ideal cylinder formed by two ideal triangles. Given the choice of such a cylinder, we construct a special cluster chart in which the action of the Dehn twist by cluster transformations localizes: for
G=PGL_{n+1}
, it is given by a composite of just
n
commuting mutations occuring within a quantum sub-torus of rank
2n
. In fact, the quantum universal Laurent ring associated to this subquiver is precisely the quantized coordinate ring of the phase space for the
GL_{n+1}
open Toda integrable system, whose commuting quantum Hamiltonians provide a candidate for the commutative subalgebra
R_{G}(c)
associated to
c
. In Theorem
8.34
we prove that the
\chi^{q}_{V}(c)
defined this way do not depend on the choice of isolating ideal cylinder, and are thus indeed canonically associated to the curve
c
.

In Section
6
we develop an algebraic version of the Whittaker transform for the
GL_{n+1}

q
-difference open Toda chain, identifying the corresponding local universal Laurent ring with a variant of the spherical part of the nil double affine Hecke algebra for
GL_{n+1}
. Then using a variant of the residue characterization of Hecke algebras developed by Ginzburg, Kapranov, and Vasserot in
[
GKV97
]
, we construct in Theorem
8.31
a cutting isomorphism
\eta_{c}(\underline{\Delta})\colon\mathbb{L}_{G,S}\simeq\mathbb{L}_{G,S;\phi}
associated to each isolating ideal cylinder for
c
. Finally, we show in Theorem
8.34
that these isomorphisms do not depend on the auxiliary choice of isolating cylinder, and in Theorem
8.36
that the resulting canonically defined isomorphism (
1.2
) is equivariant with respect to the action of
\Gamma_{S;c}
.

Working with the residue universal Laurent rings opens the door to extending Conjecture
1.1
and Theorem
Main Theorem
to the exceptional case that the surface
S^{\prime}
obtained by cutting
S
along
c
has a connected component containing a single boundary circle
c_{-}
. Although there is no action of the Weyl group
W(c_{\pm})
on
\mathbb{L}^{c}_{S^{\prime}}
by cluster transformations in this case, we can use the trick of first cutting
S
along an auxiliary cycle
a
, cutting along
c
, and gluing back along
a
in order to define (see Definition
8.42
) an analog of the residue Laurent ring in this setting. Note that this would not be possible at the level of the cutting map in the original conjecture, which is defined only on a centralizer subalgebra in
\mathbb{L}_{S}
. In Theorem
8.43
we show that when
g(S_{-})>1
the resulting isomorphism does not depend on the choice of auxiliary curve
a
, and establish its
\Gamma_{S;c}
equivariance in Theorem
8.44
.

The corresponding results in the remaining corner case
g(S_{-})=1
require different techniques, and will be treated in the companion paper
[
SS25
]
, where given a simple closed curve
b
on
S
which crosses the cutting curve
c
, we derive explicit formulas for the image of the elements
\chi^{q}_{V}(b)
under the cutting map
\eta_{c}
.

2.
Decorated surfaces and Ptolemy groupoids

2.1.
Moduli spaces of decorated local systems

In this section we recall the setup of stratified surfaces and decorated local systems following
[
JLSS21
]
. The moduli spaces of decorated local systems considered in
[
FG06b
]
and
[
GS19
]
can then be recovered as particular cases of this construction.

Definition 2.1
.

A
stratified surface
is an oriented surface
S
together with a finite collection of non-intersecting simple curves embedded into
S
in such a way, that for any curve
c
in the collection we have
\partial c\subset\partial S
and
c\smallsetminus\partial c\subset S\smallsetminus\partial S
. We refer to the curves as
walls
and denote their union by
C\subset S
. Connected components of
S\smallsetminus C
are called
regions.

Convention 2.2
.

We will always follow the convention that the orientation of
S
induces an orientation of its boundary
\partial S
via the following recipe: if
\vec{n}
is an inwards pointing tangent vector at a point
x\in\partial S
, and
\vec{v}
a positively oriented tangent vector in the tangent space to
\partial S
at x, then
(\vec{v},\vec{n})
forms a positively-oriented frame in
T_{x}S
. When drawing surfaces on the page, we will use the convention that the ordered pair consisting of a right-horizontal and an up-vertical vector forms a positive frame.

Fix a connected reductive group
G
, an embedding
B\hookrightarrow G
of a Borel subgroup, and a projection
B\twoheadrightarrow T
onto the quotient
T=B/N
, where
N
is the unipotent radical of
B
. A
(G,T)
-coloring
of a stratified surface
S
is an assignment of a “color”
G
or
T
to every region of
S
in such a way that no two regions sharing a wall have the same color. Note that a coloring is defined by the color of any of the regions, and thus there are either two
(G,T)
-colorings of
S
or none.

Definition 2.3
.

A
decorated surface

\mathbb{S}
is a stratified surface
S
together with a
(G,T)
-coloring. We denote the union of all regions colored
G
by
\mathbb{S}_{G}
and that of all regions colored
T
by
\mathbb{S}_{T}
.

Definition 2.4
.

A stratified surface
S
is
marked
if it is endowed with a possibly empty collection
M\subset S\smallsetminus C
of
marked
points. A decorated surface
\mathbb{S}
is
marked
1
1

1

Marked points on a decorated surface play the same role as gates in
[
JLSS21
]
.
if the underlying stratified one is. Furthermore, we set
M_{G}=M\cap\mathbb{S}_{G}
and
M_{T}=M\cap\mathbb{S}_{T}
.

Example 2.5
.

On Figure
1
we show examples of decorated surfaces, where
\mathbb{S}_{G}
is shown in purple and
\mathbb{S}_{T}
in yellow.

Figure 1.
Examples of decorated surfaces.

Definition 2.6
.

Let
\mathbb{S}_{1}
and
\mathbb{S}_{2}
be decorated surfaces. A
decorated embedding

\iota\colon\mathbb{S}_{1}\hookrightarrow\mathbb{S}_{2}
is an oriented embedding of surfaces which respects walls, regions, colors, and marked points. A
decorated isotopy
is a continuous path through the space of decorated embeddings.

In what follows, we shall only consider decorated surfaces up to decorated isotopies.

Recall that a data of a (Betti)
G
-local system is equivalent to that of a principal
G
-bundle with a flat connection. A reduction of a local system to a subgroup
H
amounts to the specification of an
H
-subbundle preserved by the parallel transport. We will use this notion for the Borel
B
considered as a subgroup of
G\times T
-via the map
B\hookrightarrow G\times T
obtained as the composition of the diagonal embedding of
B
into
B\times B
with the product of the embedding
B\hookrightarrow G
and the projection
B\twoheadrightarrow T
.

Definition 2.7
.

Let
\mathbb{S}
be a decorated surface. A
decorated local system

\mathcal{L}
on
\mathbb{S}
is a triple
(\mathcal{L}_{G},\mathcal{L}_{T},\mathcal{L}_{B})
, consisting of a
G
-local system
\mathcal{L}_{G}
on
\mathbb{S}_{G}
, a
T
-local system
\mathcal{L}_{T}
on
\mathbb{S}_{T}
, and a reduction
\mathcal{L}_{B}
to
B
of the
G\times T
-local system
\mathcal{L}_{G}\times\mathcal{L}_{T}
over
C
. A decorated local system
\mathcal{L}
is
framed
if it is endowed with a trivialization of
\mathcal{L}_{G}
and
\mathcal{L}_{T}
at each point in
M_{G}
and
M_{T}
respectively. A morphism of framed, decorated local systems is a pair of morphisms between the corresponding
G,T
-local systems on
\mathbb{S}_{G},\mathbb{S}_{T}
respecting the
B
-reduction and trivialization data.

Definition 2.8
.

The
(framed) decorated character stack
is the moduli stack of (framed) decorated local systems on
\mathbb{S}
. We denote the decorated character stack by
\operatorname{Ch}(\mathbb{S})
and the framed one by
\operatorname{Ch}_{\bullet}(\mathbb{S})
.

The stack
\operatorname{Ch}_{\bullet}(\mathbb{S})
carries a natural action of the group
\mathcal{G}_{M}=G^{\left|M_{G}\right|}\times T^{\left|M_{T}\right|}
, which changes trivialization at marked points.
In particular, we have

\operatorname{Ch}(\mathbb{S})\simeq\operatorname{Ch}_{\bullet}(\mathbb{S})/\mathcal{G}_{M}.

We refer the reader to
[
IO23
]
for a comprehensive discussion of character stacks.

Example 2.9
.

The decorated character stacks corresponding to decorated surfaces on Figure

, read left to right as follows:
G/N
,

,
\mathrm{pt}/N
,
\mathrm{pt}/B
, where
\mathrm{pt}
denotes a point. For the reader’s convenience, let us briefly review how to arrive at this description, starting from Definition
2.7
. Suppose that
\mathcal{L}
is a framed decorated local system on the surface shown in the left pane of Figure
2
. Choose a path connecting the two marked points and intersecting the blue domain wall at a single point
p
. Parallel transporting the trivializations at the two marked points along this path, we obtain a distinguished basepoint
e_{p}
in the fiber at
p
of the
G\times T
local system
\mathcal{L}_{G}\times\mathcal{L}_{T}
on the wall. The data of a
B
-reduction of the latter local system is that of a
B
-orbit
Bf_{p}
in this fiber. Since the fiber is a
G\times T
torsor, there is a well-defined
B
-coset in
G\times T
given by
(g,t)B
, where
(g,t)\in G\times T
is the unique element such that
(g,t)f_{p}=e_{p}
. Applying the same construction to an isomorphic decorated framed local system
\mathcal{L}^{\prime}\simeq\mathcal{L}
evidently results in the same such coset. Finally, since
T
normalizes
N
, we can identify
(G\times T)/B\simeq G/N
via
(g,t)B\mapsto gt^{-1}N
.
The other three stacks are obtained from
G/N
via taking further quotients by
G
on the left and
T
on the right.

Example 2.10
.

Let
\pi\colon G/N\to G/B
denote the natural projection. Then the decorated character stacks corresponding to surfaces shown on Figure
3
are non-canonically isomorphic to the ones parametrizing the following data:

(1)

pairs
(g,\bar{F})\in G\times G/N
, such that
g\pi(\bar{F})=\pi(\bar{F})
;

(2)

pairs
(g,F)\in G\times G/B
, such that
gF=F
;

(3)

pairs
(g,\bar{F})\in G\times G/N
, such that
g\bar{F}=\bar{F}
. This forces
g
to be unipotent;

(4)

pairs
(g,F)\in G\times G/B
, such that
gF=F
and
g
is unipotent.

For the reader’s convenience, we again sketch how to obtain the explicit description of the moduli space (1).
In the left pane of Figure
3
, choose a line segment connecting the two marked points, intersecting the domain wall at a single point
p
. Using parallel transport of the
G
and
T
local systems, we get this way a distinguished point in the fiber over
p
of the
G\times T
-local system on the wall. This distinguished point allows us to associate to the loop around the wall an element
(g,t)\in G\times T
recording the corresponding monodromy, along with a
B
-coset
(h,s)B
in
G\times T
preserved by
(g,t)
: we have
(gh,ts)b=(h,s)
for some
b\in B
. To reconcile this description with that given in (1), note that
in particular the
B
-coset
\bar{F}=hB
in
G
is preserved by
g
, and moreover that the semisimple part of
b
is exactly
t^{-1}
, that is
b=nt^{-1}
with
n\in N
and
ghn=ht
. Since
G/N
is a principal
T
-bundle over
G/B
, the element
t
can be recovered as the monodromy of
hN\in G/N
under
g
: it is the unique element of
T
such that
ghN=htN
. Hence the data in (1) indeed suffice to recover the original framed decorated local system up to isomorphism.

As was noted in
[
JLSS21
]
, the moduli space
(2)
is the quotient of
(1)
by the
T
-action changing the trivialization, whereas
(3)\subset(1)
is the preimage of the identity with respect to a multiplicative
T
-valued moment map
\mu\colon(1)\to T^{*}
. Accordingly,
(4)
is the multiplicative Hamiltonian reduction of
(1)
.

Decorated cylinders and disks.

Definition 2.11
.

A region of a decorated surface
\mathbb{S}
is said to be
boundary
if its intersection with
\partial\mathbb{S}
is non-empty, and the region can be retracted onto this intersection while preserving
\partial\mathbb{S}
. A region is
internal
if it does not intersect
\partial\mathbb{S}
. A
decorated
n
-gon

\mathbb{D}_{n}
is a decorated disk with a single
G
-region and
n
boundary
T
-disks.

One can see boundary
T
-annuli on the first two pictures of Figure
3
, and internal
T
-disks on the second two. Decorated monogons and a decorated triangle are shown respectively on Figures
2
and
4
. Note that a boundary region is necessarily either an annulus or a disk.

Figure 4.
Decorated triangle.

We say that a decorated surface is
simple

[

]
.

-region is boundary if the region is such, and the
G
-region contains no marked points. In the remainder of the article, we will only work with simple decorated surfaces.

Definition 2.12
.

A
marked surface
is an oriented surface
S
together with a subset
M\subset\partial S
of boundary marked points, a subset
P
of interior marked points (which following
[
FG06b
]
we refer to as
punctures
), and a subset
\mathcal{C}=\left\{c_{j}\right\}
of connected components of
\partial S
, each of which comes with a distinguished point
t_{j}\in c_{j}
. We refer to the elements of
\mathcal{C}
as
tacked circles
, to the points
t_{j}
as their
tacks.
A
special point
on a marked surface is one which is either a boundary marked point, a puncture, or a tack. We denote the set of special points by
V=V(S)
.

From a simple decorated surface
\mathbb{S}
one can produce a marked surface
S
using the following recipe:

•

Retract each boundary or interior
T
-disk to the unique marked point it contains, thereby creating respectively a boundary marked point or a puncture on
S
;

•

Retract each boundary
T
-annulus containing a marked point to its intersection with
\partial\mathbb{S}
and replace the marked point with a tack, thereby creating a tacked circle on
S
;

•

Retract each boundary
T
-annulus without marked points to its intersection with
\partial\mathbb{S}
and glue in a disk with an interior marked point, thereby creating a puncture on
S
.

We show an example of a decorated surface and the corresponding marked surface on Figure
5
. In order to graphically disambiguate boundary marked points from tacks, we draw the latter as diamonds.

Figure 5.
Decorated surface and marked surface.

Note that the above recipe does not distinguish the interior marked points on a decorated surface from its boundary circles without marked points, and thus different decorated surfaces can be assigned the same marked surface.

Definition 2.13
.

Given a marked surface
S
, we define its
\mathcal{A}
-lift

\mathbb{S}_{\mathcal{A}}
to be the decorated surface where all punctures are replaced by internal
T
-disks with a single marked point, and its
\mathcal{P}
-lift

\mathbb{S}_{\mathcal{P}}
to be the one where all punctures are replaced by boundary circles without marked points.

Notation 2.14
.

Given a marked surface
S
, we write
S_{\circ}
for the marked surface obtained from
S
by replacing every tacked circle of
S
by a puncture. By construction,
S_{\circ}
is then a marked surface in the sense of
[
FG06b
]
.

Definition 2.15
.

Given a marked surface
S
, we denote by
\operatorname{Diffeo}^{+}(S)
the group of diffeomorphisms of
S
which preserve its orientation, as well as the sets of punctures, marked points, and tacked circles. We write
\operatorname{Diffeo}_{0}^{+}(S)
for the connected component of the identity in
\operatorname{Diffeo}^{+}(S)
. The
mapping class group
of
S
is the quotient

\Gamma_{S}=\operatorname{Diffeo}^{+}(S)/\operatorname{Diffeo}_{0}^{+}(S).

Definition 2.16
.

An
arc
on a marked surface
S
a curve
a\colon[0,1]\rightarrow S
satisfying the following conditions:

•

a(t_{1})=a(t_{2})
implies
\left\{t_{1},t_{2}\right\}=\left\{0,1\right\}
;

•

\partial a\in V(S)
and
(a\smallsetminus\partial a)\cap V(S)=\varnothing
;

•

a
does not retract onto
\partial a
.

We shall only consider arcs up to the action of
\operatorname{Diffeo}_{0}^{+}(S)
and abuse notation by referring to their
\operatorname{Diffeo}_{0}^{+}(S)
-orbits by arcs as well.

Definition 2.17
.

Two arcs are
compatible
if they do not intersect in
S\smallsetminus V(S)
. An
ideal triangulation
of a marked surface
S
is a maximal collection of distinct, pairwise compatible arcs. The arcs of a triangulation cut the surface S into
ideal triangles
and are often referred to as
edges.
A triangle is
self-folded
if two of its edges coincide, and is
special
if one of its edges is a tacked circle. An edge
e
of a triangle is
boundary
if
e\subset\partial S
.

Ideal triangulations are always considered up to the action of
\operatorname{Diffeo}_{0}^{+}(S)
. In what follows, we will often write “triangulation” instead of ‘ideal triangulation’ whenever it does not cause confusion.

Definition 2.18
.

We say that a marked surface
S
is
admissible
if the surface
S_{\circ}
admits an ideal triangulation.

Let
g(S)
denote the genus of
S
. Then it is not hard to see that
S
is admissible if and only if it satisfies one of the following:

•

g(S)>0
and
V(S)\neq\varnothing
;

•

g(S)=0
and
|V(S)|\geqslant 3
;

•

g(S)=0
,
|V(S)|=2
, and
|M(S)|=1
.

Let
\mathbb{S}
be a simple decorated surface,
D
its boundary
T
-disk, and
D^{\prime}
the boundary
T
-disk immediately following
D
as we traverse
\partial S
in the positive direction, note that
D
and
D^{\prime}
may coincide. Consider a framed decorated local system
\mathcal{L}
on
\mathbb{S}
and set
s,s^{\prime}
to be the sections of the associated
G/N
-bundles over the walls
c\subset\partial D
,
c^{\prime}\subset\partial D^{\prime}
. Furthermore, let
\rho(s)
to be the parallel transport of
s
along the path traversing
\partial S
in the positive direction from
c
to
c^{\prime}
, so that
\rho(s)
is a section of the
G/N
bundle over
c^{\prime}
. Recall that a pair of framed flags
\bar{F}_{1},\bar{F}_{2}\in G/N
is said to be in relative position
u\in W
, where
W
is the Weyl group of
G
, if its
G
-coset can be presented as
NtuN\subset G\backslash(G/N)^{2}\simeq N\backslash G/N
for some
t\in T
. The framed decorated local system
\mathcal{L}
is
generic at
D
if
\rho(s)
and
s^{\prime}
are in relative position
w_{0}
, with the latter being the longest element of the Weyl group
W
. We say that
\mathcal{L}
is
generic
if it is such at each boundary
T
-disk. Given a simple decorated surface
\mathbb{S}
, we define
\operatorname{Ch}^{\circ}_{\bullet}(\mathbb{S})\subset\operatorname{Ch}_{\bullet}(\mathbb{S})
to be the substack which parametrizes generic framed decorated local systems. We then set
\operatorname{Ch}^{\circ}(\mathbb{S})=p(\operatorname{Ch}^{\circ}_{\bullet}(\mathbb{S}))
, where
p\colon\operatorname{Ch}_{\bullet}(\mathbb{S})\to\operatorname{Ch}(\mathbb{S})
is the natural projection. We remark that
\operatorname{Ch}^{\circ}(\mathbb{S})
and
\operatorname{Ch}^{\circ}_{\bullet}(\mathbb{S})
are in fact quasi-projective varieties, see
[
IO23
]
.

Remark 2.19
.

If
S
is an admissible marked surface
S
without tacked circles, then the following is known:

a)

Suppose
G
is simply connected, and let
\mathbb{S}_{\mathcal{A}}
be the decorated surface obtained as the
\mathcal{A}
-lift of
S
. Then
\operatorname{Ch}^{\circ}_{\bullet}(\mathbb{S}_{\mathcal{A}})
is (non-canonically) isomorphic to the moduli space
\mathcal{A}_{G,S}
of
twisted
framed local systems on
S
, see Section
4.6
, which carries a natural pre-symplectic form. In
[
FG06b
,
GS19
]
, there was constructed a cluster
K_{2}
-variety
\mathcal{A}_{{\boldsymbol{Q}}(G,S)}
sharing with
\mathcal{A}_{G,S}
a subset of toric charts, on which the pre-symplectic form of
\mathcal{A}_{{\boldsymbol{Q}}(G,S)}
coincides with that of
\mathcal{A}_{G,S}
. It is expected that the ring of functions on the cluster
K_{2}
-variety coincides with that on
\mathcal{A}_{G,S}
, however we do not know of a reference where this statement is proven in full generality.

b)

If
G
is of adjoint type, i.e. has has trivial center, then by
[
GS19
, Lemma 3.13]
, the moduli space
\operatorname{Ch}^{\circ}_{\bullet}(\mathbb{S})
coincides with the moduli space
\mathcal{P}_{G,S}
of
framed
G
-local systems with pinnings
introduced in
loc. cit.
In
[
FG06b
,
GS19
]
, there was constructed a cluster Poisson variety
\mathcal{P}_{{\boldsymbol{Q}}(G,S)}
sharing with
\mathcal{P}_{G,S}
a subset of toric charts, on which the Poisson structure of
\mathcal{P}_{{\boldsymbol{Q}}(G,S)}
coincides with that of
\mathcal{P}_{G,S}
. The isomorphism
\mathcal{O}\big(\mathcal{P}_{{\boldsymbol{Q}}(G,S)}\big)\simeq\mathcal{O}(\mathcal{P}_{G,S})
of coordinate rings was then proven in
[
She22
]
, by showing that the complement to the union of joint toric charts is of codimension at least two in
\mathcal{P}_{G,S}
.

Most important for us will be the class of simple decorated surfaces whose
T
-regions are boundary disks and annuli containing one marked point each. In this case, all special points of the corresponding marked surface are boundary marked points and tacks. As indicated in Example
2.10
, the moduli spaces for all other simple decorated surfaces can be recovered as various kinds of reductions of those for surfaces of this type.

Definition 2.20
.

Let
S
be a marked surface without punctures and
\mathbb{S}
be the corresponding decorated surface. We define the moduli space

\mathcal{P}_{G,S}^{\diamond}=\operatorname{Ch}_{\bullet}^{\circ}(\mathbb{S}).

In this text we will only consider Lie groups of Dynkin type
A_{n}
, whose simply connected form is
SL_{n+1}
and whose adjoint form is
PGL_{n+1}
. For a marked surface
S
without punctures but possibly with tacked circles, in Section
4
we will construct, in the sense of Remark
2.19
, the structure of a non-degenerate cluster ensemble on the moduli spaces
\left(\mathcal{P}_{SL_{n+1},S}^{\diamond},\mathcal{P}_{PGL_{n+1},S}^{\diamond}\right)
. Among the symplectic toric charts in its atlas are those introduced in
[
BK23
]
.

2.2.
Ptolemy groupoids

Definition 2.21
.

Let
S
be a marked surface with a triangulation
\underline{\Delta}
, and
e
be a non-boundary edge in
\underline{\Delta}
. We say that the triangulation
\underline{\Delta}^{\prime}
is related to
\underline{\Delta}
by a
flip

F_{e}
at
e
if
\underline{\Delta}^{\prime}
is obtained from
\underline{\Delta}
by replacing
e
with the other diagonal of the unique 4-gon containing
e
as a diagonal.

Definition 2.22
.

The
Ptolemy complex

\operatorname{Pt}_{2}(S)
of a marked surface
S
is a 2-groupoid, whose objects are ideal triangulations,
3
3

3

Let us point out that our definition of the Ptolemy complex differs from the one given in
[
Tes05
]
, where the objects are ideal triangulations whose edges are labelled.
1-morphisms are sequences of flips, and 2-morphisms are compositions of

•

bigons,

F_{e}^{2}=\operatorname{Id}
;

•

4-gons,

F_{e}F_{f}=F_{f}F_{e}
, where
e
and
f
do not share a vertex;

•

pentagons,
see Figure
6
.

Remark 2.23
.

The Ptolemy complex can be presented in more topological terms. Namely, consider a 3-manifold
M_{S}
defined as
M_{S}=S\times[0,1]/(\partial S\times[0,1])
. Then 1-morphisms in the Ptolemy complex are the 3d-triangulations of
M
, i.e. presentations of
M
as a union of tetrahedra, while pentagon relations are the 2-3 Pachner moves.

Definition 2.24
.

The
Ptolemy groupoid

\operatorname{Pt}(S)
is the 1-truncation of the Ptolemy complex.

Concretely, this means that
\operatorname{Pt}(S)
is the 1-category with the same objects as the Ptolemy complex
\operatorname{Pt}_{2}(S)
, but with 1-morphisms given by isomorphism classes of
1
-morphisms in
\operatorname{Pt}_{2}(S)
.

Figure 6.
Pentagon relation.

Proposition 2.25

(
[
CF99
]
)
.

The Ptolemy complex is connected and simply connected:

(1)

for any pair of objects,
\underline{\Delta}_{1},\underline{\Delta}_{2}
, there exists a 1-morphism
F\colon\underline{\Delta}_{1}\to\underline{\Delta}_{2}
;

(2)

for any pair of 1-morphisms,
F,F^{\prime}\colon\underline{\Delta}_{1}\to\underline{\Delta}_{2}
, there exists a 2-morphism
\Phi\colon F\Rightarrow F^{\prime}
.

Corollary 2.26
.

The Ptolemy groupoid is connected and thin: there exists a unique morphism between any pair of objects.

Definition 2.27
.

Let
\Gamma
be a group acting on a groupoid
\mathrm{G}
via endofunctors
A_{\gamma}
,
\gamma\in\Gamma
. The
enhancement
of
\mathrm{G}
by
\Gamma
is the groupoid
\widehat{\mathrm{G}}
whose objects are the same as those of
\mathrm{G}
, and morphisms
\operatorname{Mor}(O_{1},O_{2})
between objects
O_{1}
and
O_{2}
are the formal compositions
A_{\gamma}F
, where
F\in\operatorname{Mor}(O_{1},A_{\gamma}^{-1}(O_{2}))
. Composition in
\widehat{\mathrm{G}}
is defined by

(2.1)

\displaystyle A_{\gamma_{2}}F_{2}\circ A_{\gamma_{1}}F_{1}=A_{\gamma_{2}\gamma_{1}}A_{\gamma_{1}}^{-1}(F_{2})F_{1},

where
F_{1}\in\operatorname{Mor}(O_{1},A_{\gamma_{1}}^{-1}(O_{2}))
and
F_{2}\in\operatorname{Mor}(O_{2},A_{\gamma_{2}}^{-1}(O_{3}))
.

We apply this construction to the natural action via endofunctors of the mapping class group
\Gamma_{S}
on
\operatorname{Pt}(S)
. Namely, an element
\gamma\in\Gamma_{S}
acts via an endofunctor
A_{\gamma}
defined by
A_{\gamma}(\underline{\Delta})=\gamma(\underline{\Delta})
and
A_{\gamma}(F_{\underline{\Delta}_{2},\underline{\Delta}_{1}})=F_{\gamma(\underline{\Delta}_{2}),\gamma(\underline{\Delta}_{1})}
, where
F_{\underline{\Delta}_{2},\underline{\Delta}_{1}}
is the unique morphism between
\underline{\Delta}_{1}
and
\underline{\Delta}_{2}
. This action of
\Gamma_{S}
evidently descends from one on the Ptolemy complex.

Definition 2.28
.

The
enhanced Ptolemy groupoid

\widehat{\operatorname{Pt}}(S)
is the enhancement of the Ptolemy groupoid
\operatorname{Pt}(S)
by the mapping class group
\Gamma_{S}
.

Corollary 2.29
.

For any object
\underline{\Delta}
of
\widehat{\operatorname{Pt}}(S)
, we have an injective homomorphism

\Gamma_{S}\lhook\joinrel\longrightarrow\operatorname{Aut}(\underline{\Delta})\simeq\pi_{1}\big(\widehat{\operatorname{Pt}}(S)\big),\qquad\gamma\longmapsto A_{\gamma}F_{\gamma^{-1}(\underline{\Delta}),\underline{\Delta}}.

2.3.
Cut and glue functors

In this section,
S
will be a marked surface without puntures, but possibly with tacked circles.

Definition 2.30
.

We say that an arc
a
of a triangulation
\underline{\Delta}
of
S
is a
shadow
of a simple closed curve
c\subset S
if
a
and
c
are isotopic as free loops. An ideal triangulation
\underline{\Delta}
of
S
is called
c
-admissible
if it contains a shadow
a
of
c
which is not a tacked circle.

Note in particular that shadows of a curve
c
need not be isotopic as arcs of the triangulation, i.e. relative to their basepoint, and can indeed be based at different special points on
S
.
In any given triangulation, a simple closed curve
c
can have at most 2 shadows. If
c
has two shadows, they bound a cylinder
C\subset S
covered by a pair of triangles, with
\pi_{1}(C)=\left\langle c\right\rangle
. Note that the shadows of
c
are also shadows of each other.

Definition 2.31
.

A simple closed curve
c\subset S
is said to be
isolated
in a triangulation
\underline{\Delta}
if the latter contains two shadows of
c
. We call the subsurface
C\subset S
bounded by the shadows of
c
an
isolating cylinder
for
c
, and refer to
\underline{\Delta}
as a
c
-isolating triangulation
of
S
.

Consider a collection
\boldsymbol{c}
of non-intersecting, non-homotopic simple closed curves on
S
. We consider the following full sub-groupoids

\operatorname{Pt}^{o}_{\boldsymbol{c}}(S)\subset\operatorname{Pt}_{\boldsymbol{c}}(S)\subset\operatorname{Pt}(S),

where the objects of
\operatorname{Pt}_{\boldsymbol{c}}(S)
are ideal triangulations admissible with respect to at least one curve in
\boldsymbol{c}
and those of
\operatorname{Pt}^{o}_{\boldsymbol{c}}(S)
are ideal triangulations admissible with respect to every curve in
\boldsymbol{c}
.

Let
\tau_{c}
be the Dehn twist along an oriented simple closed curve
c\subset S
. Clearly, the cyclic subgroup
\left\langle\tau_{c}\right\rangle\subset\Gamma_{S}
is independent of the orientation of
c
. Given a collection
\boldsymbol{c}
of simple closed curves on
S
, we write

\Gamma_{S;\boldsymbol{c}}=C_{\Gamma_{S}}\left\langle\tau_{c}\,|\,c\in\boldsymbol{c}\right\rangle

for
the centralizer in
\Gamma_{S}
of the subgroup generated by the Dehn twists
\tau_{c}
,
c\in\boldsymbol{c}
.
Then elements of
\Gamma_{S;\boldsymbol{c}}
preserve the isotopy classes of all curves in
\boldsymbol{c}
. We define
\widehat{\operatorname{Pt}}_{\boldsymbol{c}}(S)
and
\widehat{\operatorname{Pt}}^{o}_{\boldsymbol{c}}(S)
to be the
\Gamma_{S;\boldsymbol{c}}
-enhanced groupoids
\operatorname{Pt}_{\boldsymbol{c}}(S)
and
\operatorname{Pt}^{o}_{\boldsymbol{c}}(S)
respectively.

Now, let
S^{\prime}
be a possibly disconnected marked surface, and
c_{\pm}\in\mathcal{C}(S^{\prime})
be a pair of oriented tacked circles
with tacks
t_{\pm}\in c_{\pm}
. Given an orientation-compatible homeomorphism
\phi\colon c_{+}\to c_{-}
such that
\phi(t_{+})=t_{-}
, we consider an oriented surface
S
obtained from
S^{\prime}
by identifying
c_{+}
and
c_{-}
along
\phi
, and forgetting the data of the tacks
t_{\pm}
. We endow the curve
c\subset S
obtained as the common image of the
c_{\pm}
with the orientation given by that of the curve
c_{+}
.

Let
\operatorname{Pt}_{\phi}(S^{\prime})
be the thin groupoid whose set of objects is obtained from that of
\operatorname{Pt}_{c_{\pm}}(S^{\prime})
by identifying all objects in the same orbit of the cyclic group
\left\langle\tau_{c_{+}}\tau_{c_{-}}\right\rangle
generated by the product of two Dehn twists.
Gluing along
\phi
induces a surjection from
\Gamma_{S^{\prime}}
to
\Gamma_{S;c}
whose kernel is
\left\langle\tau_{c_{+}}\tau_{c_{-}}\right\rangle
, and we set

\Gamma_{S^{\prime};\phi}=\Gamma_{S^{\prime}}/\left\langle\tau_{c_{+}}\tau_{c_{-}}\right\rangle\simeq\Gamma_{S;c}.

This group acts on
\operatorname{Pt}_{\phi}(S^{\prime})
, and we define
\widehat{\operatorname{Pt}}_{\phi}(S^{\prime})
to be the
\Gamma_{S^{\prime};\phi}
-enhanced groupoid
\operatorname{Pt}_{\phi}(S^{\prime})
. The same construction can obviously be carried out for a collection of pairs of tacked circles on
S^{\prime}
.

Proposition 2.32
.

Let
\boldsymbol{c}
be a collection of pairwise non-intersecting, non-homotopic simple closed curves on
S
. Then we have a pair of mutually-inverse
cutting
and
gluing
functors:

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}_{\boldsymbol{c}}

\displaystyle\colon\widehat{\operatorname{Pt}}\vphantom{\operatorname{Pt}}^{o}_{\boldsymbol{c}}(S)\longrightarrow\widehat{\operatorname{Pt}}_{\boldsymbol{\phi}}(S^{\prime}),

\displaystyle\mathcal{G}_{\boldsymbol{\phi}}

\displaystyle\colon\widehat{\operatorname{Pt}}_{\boldsymbol{\phi}}(S^{\prime})\longrightarrow\widehat{\operatorname{Pt}}\vphantom{\operatorname{Pt}}^{o}_{\boldsymbol{c}}(S).

Proof..

We treat the case of a single curve
c
, with the general case being obtained by iterating the construction.
To specify functors as in the statement of the Proposition, it suffices to define
\Gamma_{S;c}
-equivariant maps
\mathcal{C}_{c}
and
\mathcal{G}_{\phi}
on objects of
\operatorname{Pt}_{c}(S)
and
\operatorname{Pt}_{\phi}(S^{\prime})
, since both groupoids are connected and thin. Let
\underline{\Delta}^{\prime}
be an object in
\widehat{\operatorname{Pt}}_{\phi}(S^{\prime})
. Without loss of generality we may assume that it is
c_{+}
-admissible. Then
\underline{\Delta}^{\prime}
contains the isolating cylinder
C
of
c_{+}
, which in turn has 4 arcs:
c_{+}
, its shadow
a_{+}
, and a pair of non-boundary arcs,
e_{1},e_{2}
, where
e_{1}
precedes
e_{2}
as we go around the tack on
c_{+}
in a positive direction defined by the orientation of
S^{\prime}
. Let us identify tacked circles
c_{+}
and
c_{-}
along
\phi
, and retract
C
onto
a_{+}
in such a way that
e_{1}
retracts to the marked point of
a_{+}
, arriving at a triangulation
\underline{\Delta}
in
\widehat{\operatorname{Pt}}_{c}(S)
. We illustrate this on Figure
7
, where the homeomorphism
\phi
is indicated by horizontal yellow segments connecting tacked circles
c_{\pm}
. This procedure yields the same ideal triangulation
\underline{\Delta}
if the initial one
\underline{\Delta}^{\prime}
is replaced by its image under the diagonal product of Dehn twists
\tau_{c_{+}}\tau_{c_{-}}
. Hence it descends to a map on objects of
\operatorname{Pt}_{\phi}(S^{\prime})
given by
\mathcal{G}_{\phi}(\underline{\Delta}^{\prime})=\underline{\Delta}
. This map is
\Gamma_{S;c}
-equivariant, and well-defined on ideal triangulations which are both
c_{+}
- and
c_{-}
-admissible.

a_{-}
a_{+}
c
\mathcal{C}_{c}
\mathcal{G}_{\phi}
a_{-}
c_{-}
c_{+}
a_{+}
e_{1}
e_{2}

Figure 7.
Cutting and gluing functors.

On the other hand, let
\underline{\Delta}
be an object in
\widehat{\operatorname{Pt}}_{c}(S)
, and
a
be a shadow of
c
equipped with an orientation. Cut the surface
S
along
a
, thus obtaining a pair of new boundary arcs
a_{\pm}
. We suppose that the orientation of
c
is chosen such that we may assume that
a_{+}
is a shadow of
c
. We then turn it into a tacked circle and rename it into
c_{+}
. Now, we glue a cylinder
C
with one marked point on each side along
a_{-}
, denote by
c_{-}
the arc of the cylinder which is a new boundary arc, and turn
c_{-}
into a tacked circle, thereby recovering the surface
S^{\prime}
along with a partial triangulation. We complete it to a triangulation
\underline{\Delta}^{\prime}
by taking the unique triangulation of
C
such that
\mathcal{G}_{\phi}(\underline{\Delta}^{\prime})=\underline{\Delta}
, and define the
\Gamma_{S;c}
-equivariant map
\mathcal{C}_{c}
by setting
\mathcal{C}_{c}(\underline{\Delta})=\underline{\Delta}^{\prime}
. This completes the construction of the pair of inverse functors
\mathcal{G}_{\phi}
and
\mathcal{C}_{c}
.
∎

We finish this section with the following observation. Let
\boldsymbol{c}\subset\mathcal{C}(S)
and
\boldsymbol{\phi}
be as in Proposition
2.32
. Given a decomposition
\boldsymbol{c}=\boldsymbol{c}_{1}\sqcup\boldsymbol{c}_{2}
, denote by
S_{\boldsymbol{c}_{i}}
the result of cutting
S
along
\boldsymbol{c}_{i}
, and let
\boldsymbol{\phi}_{i}
be such that
S
is the result of gluing
S_{\boldsymbol{c}_{i}}
by
\boldsymbol{\phi}_{i}
.
Then the equalities

\mathcal{C}_{\boldsymbol{c}}=\mathcal{C}_{\boldsymbol{c}_{1}}\mathcal{C}_{\boldsymbol{c}_{2}}=\mathcal{C}_{\boldsymbol{c}_{2}}\mathcal{C}_{\boldsymbol{c}_{1}}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\mathcal{G}_{\boldsymbol{\phi}}=\mathcal{G}_{\boldsymbol{\phi}_{1}}\mathcal{G}_{\boldsymbol{\phi}_{2}}=\mathcal{G}_{\boldsymbol{\phi}_{2}}\mathcal{G}_{\boldsymbol{\phi}_{1}}

follow from the construction of functors
\mathcal{C}_{c}
and
\mathcal{G}_{\phi}
. To aid the reader in parsing these formulas, we write down the source and target groupoids of thefactors in the composition
\mathcal{C}_{\boldsymbol{c}_{2}}\mathcal{C}_{\boldsymbol{c}_{1}}
. We have

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}_{\boldsymbol{c}_{1}}\colon\widehat{\operatorname{Pt}}_{\boldsymbol{c}}(S)\longrightarrow\widehat{\operatorname{Pt}}\vphantom{\operatorname{Pt}}^{o}_{\boldsymbol{c}_{2};\boldsymbol{\phi}_{1}}(S_{\boldsymbol{c}_{1}}),

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}_{\boldsymbol{c}_{2}}\colon\widehat{\operatorname{Pt}}\vphantom{\operatorname{Pt}}^{o}_{\boldsymbol{c}_{2};\boldsymbol{\phi}_{1}}(S_{\boldsymbol{c}_{1}})\longrightarrow\widehat{\operatorname{Pt}}_{\boldsymbol{\phi}}(S_{\boldsymbol{c}}),

where
\operatorname{Pt}^{o}_{\boldsymbol{c}_{2};\boldsymbol{\phi}_{1}}(S_{\boldsymbol{c}_{1}})
is the full subgroupoid of
\operatorname{Pt}_{\boldsymbol{\phi}_{1}}(S_{\boldsymbol{c}_{1}})
, whose objects are (equivalence classes of) triangulations admissible with respect to every curve in
\boldsymbol{c}_{2}
, and
\widehat{\operatorname{Pt}}\vphantom{\operatorname{Pt}}^{o}_{\boldsymbol{c}_{2};\boldsymbol{\phi}_{1}}(S_{\boldsymbol{c}_{1}})
is the
\Gamma_{S;\boldsymbol{c}}
-enhancement of
\operatorname{Pt}^{o}_{c_{2};\boldsymbol{\phi}_{1}}(S_{\boldsymbol{c}_{1}})
.

2.4.
Umbral moves

In this section we investigate the passage from one
c
-isolating triangulation to another in the Ptolemy groupoid. The key to understanding this is the following local transformation on isolating cylinders.

Definition 2.33
.

Let
C
be an isolating cylinder of
c
,
e_{0}
a non-boundary boundary shadow of
c
, and
e_{1}
,
e_{2}
the internal arcs, labelled in such a way that the cycle
e_{0}\rightarrow e_{1}\rightarrow e_{2}\rightarrow e_{0}
inside each triangle of
C
agrees with the orientation of the surface.
We call the following sequence of flips

U_{C;e_{0}}=F_{e_{2}}F_{e_{1}}F_{e_{0}}

the
umbral move
at
e_{0}
.

e_{0}
e_{1}
e_{2}
F_{e_{0}}
F_{e_{1}}
F_{e_{2}}
U_{c;e_{0}}

Figure 8.
The umbral move
U_{C;e_{0}}
.

The umbral move is illustrated in Figure
8
.

Remark 2.34
.

There is also an
opposite umbral move
given by the sequence of flips

U^{op}_{C;e_{0}}=F_{e_{1}}F_{e_{2}}F_{e_{0}}.

The triangulation
U^{op}_{C;e_{0}}(\underline{\Delta})
is simply the image of
U_{C;e_{0}}(\underline{\Delta})
under the Dehn twist along
c
, and for this reason we choose to work only with the umbral moves themselves and not their opposite counterparts.

Suppose we cut
S
along
c
to produce a surface
S^{\prime}
with two new tacked circles
c_{\pm}
. Given a triangulation
\underline{\Delta}
of
S
containing an isolating cylinder
C
for
c
, write
e_{\pm}
for the corresponding edges bounding
C
. It is straightforward to see that under the cutting functor
\mathcal{C}_{c}
, the umbral move
U_{C;e_{\pm}}
is intertwined with the (unique) umbral move
U_{C_{\pm}}
on
S^{\prime}
for the cylinder
C_{\pm}
having
c_{\pm}\subset\partial S^{\prime}
as one of its boundaries.

Lemma 2.35
.

Any morphism in
\operatorname{Pt}_{\left|c\right|}(S)
can be factored into a sequence of umbral moves and flips preserving the isolating cylinder of
c
.

Proof..

Since the cutting functor intertwines umbral moves on
S
with those on the cut surface
S^{\prime}
, it suffices to prove the statement for objects of the groupoid
\operatorname{Pt}^{o}_{c_{+},c_{-}}(S^{\prime})
associated to
S^{\prime}
.
It is convenient to introduce the surface
S_{c}^{\circ}
obtained from
S^{\prime}
by shrinking the tacked circles
c_{\pm}
to a pair of punctures
p_{\pm}
. Denote by
\operatorname{Pt}^{o}_{\left|p_{\pm}\right|}(S_{c}^{\circ})
the full subgroupoid of
\operatorname{Pt}(S_{c}^{\circ})
, such that in any object of the former both punctures
p_{\pm}
are internal punctures of self-folded triangles.
Recall that any
\underline{\Delta}
in
\operatorname{Pt}^{o}_{c_{+},c_{-}}(S^{\prime})
contains isolating cylinders
C_{\pm}
for both tacked circles
c_{\pm}
. So replacing the
C_{\pm}
by self-folded triangles
\Delta_{\pm}
, we obtain an object
\underline{\Delta}^{\circ}
of
\operatorname{Pt}^{o}_{\left|p_{\pm}\right|}(S_{c}^{\circ})
. In what follows we say that triangles
\Delta_{\pm}
are
isolating
punctures
p_{\pm}
. Denote by
v_{\pm}
the other two vertices of
\Delta_{\pm}
, and let
b_{\pm}
and
d_{\pm}
be their boundary and internal arcs respectively, so that
\partial b_{\pm}=v_{\pm}
, and
\partial d_{\pm}=\left\{v_{\pm},p_{\pm}\right\}
, see Figure
9
.

Now observe that if
\underline{\Delta}_{1},\underline{\Delta}_{2}
are related by performing an umbral move
U_{C_{\pm}}
on
S^{\prime}
, then
\underline{\Delta}^{\circ}_{1},\underline{\Delta}^{\circ}_{2}
are related by the composite
U_{\pm}=F_{d_{\pm}}F_{b_{\pm}}
of two flips on
S_{c}^{\circ}
.
From an object of
\operatorname{Pt}^{o}_{\left|p_{\pm}\right|}(S_{c}^{\circ})
one can recover an object of
\operatorname{Pt}^{o}_{c_{+},c_{-}}(S^{\prime})
up to a power of Dehn twist along
c
, and the latter can be realized using flips preserving the isolating cylinder.
Hence the Lemma will follow if we can prove that
any morphism in
\operatorname{Pt}^{o}_{\left|p_{\pm}\right|}(S_{c}^{\circ})
can be factored into a sequence of moves
F_{\pm}
and flips outside of triangles isolating
p_{\pm}
.

Given a pair of objects
\underline{\Delta},\underline{\Delta}^{\prime}
in
\operatorname{Pt}^{o}_{\left|p_{\pm}\right|}(S_{c}^{\circ})
, let us superimpose arcs
d^{\prime}_{\pm}
with
\underline{\Delta}
. We will assume that the number of intersection points

X_{i}^{j}=(d_{i}\smallsetminus\partial d_{i})\cap(d^{\prime}_{j}\smallsetminus\partial d^{\prime}_{j}),\qquad i,j\in\left\{+,-\right\},

is minimal in the isotopy classes of
d_{i}
and
d^{\prime}_{j}
, and set

X_{i}=X_{i}^{+}\sqcup X_{i}^{-},\qquad X^{j}=X_{+}^{j}\sqcup X_{-}^{j},\qquad\text{and}\qquad X=X_{+}^{+}\sqcup X_{+}^{-}\sqcup X_{-}^{+}\sqcup X_{-}^{-}.

The proof is by induction on
\left|X\right|
. If
\left|X\right|=0
, using flips outside of triangles
\Delta_{\pm}
we can turn
\underline{\Delta}
into a triangulation
\underline{\Delta}^{\prime\prime}
containing triangles
\Delta^{\prime\prime}_{i}
,
i=\pm
, with edges
b_{i},e_{i}^{1},e_{i}^{2}
, such that
\partial e_{i}^{1}=\partial e_{i}^{2}=\left\{v_{i},v^{\prime}_{i}\right\}
and both
e_{i}^{1},e_{i}^{2}
are isotopic to the concatenation of
d_{i}
and
d^{\prime}_{i}
, see Figure
9
. Note that possibly
v_{i}=v^{\prime}_{i}
, in which case the
e_{i}^{1},e_{i}^{2}
are loops. Then the flip
F_{b_{i}}
replaces
b_{i}
with
d^{\prime}_{i}
, and flip
F_{d_{i}}
replaces
d_{i}
with
b^{\prime}_{i}
. Thus, performing moves
U_{\pm}
we change
\underline{\Delta}^{\prime\prime}
into a triangulation containing triangles
\Delta^{\prime}_{\pm}
. The latter can be turned into
\underline{\Delta}^{\prime}
using only flips outside of triangles
\Delta^{\prime}_{\pm}
, which proves the base of induction.

e_{i}^{1}
e_{i}^{2}
d_{i}
b_{i}
v_{i}
p_{i}
v^{\prime}_{i}
F_{i}
e_{i}^{1}
e_{i}^{2}
d^{\prime}_{i}
b^{\prime}_{i}
v_{i}
p_{i}
v^{\prime}_{i}

Figure 9.
Moves
F_{i}
,
i=\pm
on
S_{c}^{\circ}
covered by the umbral moves on
S^{\prime}
.

Note that a self-folded triangle is determined by its internal edge. In the remainder of the proof we shall discuss transformations of the latter and with the understanding that the corresponding self-folded triangles change accordingly. Denote by
x_{i}
the last point in
X^{i}
one encounters traversing
d^{\prime}_{i}
from
v^{\prime}_{i}
to
p_{i}
. First, let
x_{i}\in X_{i}^{i}
for
i=+
or
i=-
. Without loss of generality we assume
x_{+}\in X_{+}^{+}
. Consider (the isotopy class of) the arc
d^{\prime\prime}_{+}
which follows
d_{+}
from
v_{+}
to
x_{+}
and
d^{\prime}_{+}
from
x_{+}
to
p_{+}
. The arc
d^{\prime\prime}_{+}
intersects
d_{\pm}
only at the end-points, and by the base of induction we may replace the pair
(d_{+},d_{-})
with
(d^{\prime\prime}_{+},d_{-})
. Since
\left|d^{\prime\prime}_{+}\cap(d^{\prime}_{+}\cup d^{\prime}_{-})\right|\leqslant\left|X_{+}\smallsetminus x_{+}\right|
we may now apply the induction hypothesis.

It remains to consider the case in which
x_{+}\in X_{-}^{+}
and
x_{-}\in X_{+}^{-}
. Without loss of generality we assume that
\left|X_{+}\right|\geqslant\left|X_{-}\right|
. Consider (the isotopy class of) the arc
d^{\prime\prime}_{+}
which follows
d_{-}
from
v_{-}
to
x_{+}
and
d^{\prime}_{+}
from
x_{+}
to
p_{+}
. As before,
d^{\prime\prime}_{+}
only intersects
d_{\pm}
at the end-points, and we can replace
(d_{+},d_{-})
with
(d^{\prime\prime}_{+},d_{-})
. Observing that
\left|d^{\prime\prime}_{+}\cap(d^{\prime}_{+}\cup d^{\prime}_{-})\right|\leqslant\left|X_{-}\smallsetminus x_{+}\right|
, we can apply the induction hypothesis once again. This completes the proof.
∎

3.
Quantum cluster varieties and cluster modular groupoids

In this section we review the notion of quantum cluster varieties. Our exposition mostly follows that in
[
FG09a
,
FG09b
,
GS19
]
.

3.1.
Seeds, quivers, and quantum tori

In this paper we only use skew-symmetric quantum cluster algebras, which we incorporate in the definition of a seed.

Definition 3.1
.

A
seed
is a datum
\Theta=\left(I,I_{*},\Lambda,(\cdot,\cdot)_{\Lambda},\left\{e_{i}\right\}\right)
where

•

I
is a finite set;

•

I_{*}\subset I
is a subset;

•

\Lambda
is a lattice;

•

(\cdot,\cdot)_{\Lambda}
is a skew-symmetric bilinear
\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{Z}
-valued form on
\Lambda
;

•

\left\{e_{i}\,|\,i\in I\right\}
is a basis for the lattice
\Lambda
, with
(e_{i},e_{j})_{\Lambda}\in\mathbb{Z}
whenever
i\in I\setminus I_{0}
.

The sets
I_{*}
and
I_{m}=I\setminus I_{0}
are called the
frozen
and
mutable
subsets of
I
respectively.

The data of the last bullet point is equivalent to that of an isomorphism
\boldsymbol{e}\colon\mathbb{Z}^{|I|}\simeq\Lambda
. In particular, given a pair of seeds
\Theta,\Theta^{\prime}
with the same data
\left(I,I_{*}\right)
, we get a canonical isomorphism of abelian groups (not necessarily isometry of lattices)
\boldsymbol{e^{\prime}}\boldsymbol{e}^{-1}\colon\Lambda\simeq\Lambda^{\prime}
. In what follows, we will skip the subscript
\Lambda
in the notation
(\cdot,\cdot)_{\Lambda}
when the lattice is clear from the context.

Definition 3.2
.

We say that seeds
\Theta
and
\Theta^{\prime}
are
equivalent
if they have the same label sets
(I,I_{*})
and the canonical isomorphism
\Lambda\simeq\Lambda^{\prime}
is an isometry, that is
(e_{i},e_{j})_{\Lambda}=(e^{\prime}_{i},e^{\prime}_{j})_{\Lambda^{\prime}}
for all
i,j\in I
. We define a
quiver
to be an equivalence class of seeds.

The quiver
Q
associated to a seed
\Theta
can be visualized as a directed graph with vertices labelled by the set
I
and arrows with weights defined by the matrix
\varepsilon=\left(\varepsilon_{ij}\right)
, where
\varepsilon_{ij}=(e_{i},e_{j})
. More precisely, if
\varepsilon_{ij}\in\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{Z}_{>0}
we draw
\lfloor\varepsilon_{ij}\rfloor
solid and
2(\varepsilon_{ij}-\lfloor\varepsilon_{ij}\rfloor)
dashed arrows from vertex
i
to vertex
j
. The vertices corresponding to the subset
I_{*}
are called
frozen
and are drawn as squares, while those corresponding to
I_{m}
are referred to as
mutable
and are drawn as circles.

The pair
\left(\Lambda,(\cdot,\cdot)_{\Lambda}\right)
determines a
quantum torus algebra

\mathcal{T}^{\mathcal{X}}_{\Lambda}
, which is defined to be the free
\mathbb{Z}[q^{\pm 1}]
-module spanned by
\left\{Y_{\lambda}\,|\,\lambda\in\Lambda\right\}
, with the multiplication rule

(3.1)

\displaystyle q^{(\lambda,\mu)}Y_{\lambda}Y_{\mu}=Y_{\lambda+\mu}.

Specializing
q=1
we recover the coordinate ring of the split algebraic torus
\mathbb{T}_{\Lambda}
with character lattice
\Lambda
.

A basis
\left\{e_{i}\right\}
of the lattice
\Lambda
gives rise to a distinguished system of generators for
\mathcal{T}^{\mathcal{X}}_{\Lambda}
, namely
the elements
Y_{i}=Y_{e_{i}}
. This way we obtain a
quantum cluster
\mathcal{X}
-chart

(3.2)

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}^{\mathcal{X}}_{Q}=\mathbb{Z}[q^{\pm 1}]\left\langle Y_{i}^{\pm 1}\,|\,i\in I\right\rangle/\left\langle q^{\varepsilon_{jk}}Y_{j}Y_{k}=q^{\varepsilon_{kj}}Y_{k}Y_{j}\right\rangle\simeq\mathcal{T}^{\mathcal{X}}_{\Lambda}.

The generators
Y_{i}
are called the
quantum cluster
\mathcal{X}
-variables
. We note that this presentation of
\mathcal{T}^{\mathcal{X}}_{Q}
depends only on the quiver and not on the choice of the representative seed.

Given a quantum torus with generators
Y_{1},\ldots,Y_{d}
one can pass back to the lattice representation by using the
Weyl ordering
. First, define an algebra anti-involution
*
by

*Y_{i}=Y_{i},\quad*q=q^{-1}.

Then given any exponent vector
(n_{1},\ldots,n_{d})\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}
, we define the Weyl ordered product

(3.3)

\displaystyle:\!Y_{1}^{n_{1}}\cdots Y_{d}^{n_{d}}\!:

to be the unique
*
-invariant monomial of the form
q^{k}Y_{1}^{n_{1}}\cdots Y_{d}^{n_{d}}
.

3.2.
Compact quantum dilogarithm

Recall the (compact) quantum dilogarithm function

(3.4)

\Psi_{q}(Y)=\prod_{m=0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{1+q^{2m+1}Y}\in\mathbb{Z}((q))[[Y]].

It is related to the infinite
q
-Pochhammer symbol by

\Psi_{q}(Y)=(-qY;q^{2})^{-1}_{\infty}.

The Taylor coefficients of
\Psi_{q}(Y)
are given by series expansions of rational functions of
q
, so we can regard the compact quantum dilogarithm as an element of the ring
\mathbb{Q}(q)[[Y]]
:

(3.5)

\Psi_{q}(Y)=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{q^{n}Y^{n}}{(q^{2}-1)(q^{4}-1)\cdots(q^{2n}-1)}.

Its reciprocal also has a similar Taylor series:

(3.6)

\Psi_{q}(Y)^{-1}=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{q^{n^{2}}Y^{n}}{(1-q^{2})(1-q^{4})\cdots(1-q^{2n})}.

In particular, when thought of as an element of
\mathbb{Q}(q)[[Y]]
,
\Psi_{q}(Y)
satisfies the inversion relation

\Psi_{1/q}(Y)=\frac{1}{\Psi_{q}(Y)}.

It is immediate from the infinite product representation (
3.4
) that the compact quantum dilogarithm satisfies the
q
-difference equation

(3.7)

\Psi_{q}(q^{2}Y)=(1+qY)\Psi_{q}(Y).

Another important property we will need is the
pentagon identity
for
\Psi_{q}
, which takes the form of the following identity in the ring of noncommutative formal power series with
\mathbb{Q}(q)
-coefficients:

UV=q^{2}VU\implies\Psi_{q}(U)\Psi_{q}(V)=\Psi_{q}(V)\Psi_{q}(qUV)\Psi_{q}(U).

Note that the middle factor in the triple product can be written using the Weyl ordering (
3.3
) as
\Psi_{q}(:UV:)
.

We also recall the
q
-binomial theorem in the form

\sum_{n\geqslant 0}\frac{(a;q^{2})_{n}}{(q^{2};q^{2})_{n}}z^{n}=\frac{(az;q^{2})_{\infty}}{(z;q^{2})_{\infty}}\in\mathbb{Q}[[a,z,q]].

3.3.
Quantum cluster varieties

Let
\Theta,\Theta^{\prime}
be seeds representing quivers
Q,Q^{\prime}
with the same label sets
(I,I_{*})
.
We say that the quiver
Q^{\prime}
is the
mutation of
Q
in direction
k\in I_{m}
,
and write
Q^{\prime}=\mu_{k}(Q)
, if the map

(3.8)

\mu_{k}\colon\Lambda\longrightarrow\Lambda^{\prime},\qquad e_{i}\longmapsto\begin{cases}-e^{\prime}_{k}&\text{if}\;i=k,\\
e^{\prime}_{i}+[\varepsilon_{ik}]_{+}e^{\prime}_{k}&\text{if}\;i\neq k\end{cases}

is an isometry, where

[a]_{+}=\max\left\{a,0\right\}.

The
mutation class
of a quiver
Q
, which we denote by
\boldsymbol{Q}
, is the set of all quivers that can be obtained from
Q
by some finite sequence of mutations.

Remark 3.3
.

Consider a seed
\Theta_{0}
with lattice
\Lambda_{0}
. For every other seed
\Theta
mutation equivalent to
\Theta_{0}
we may identify its lattice
\Lambda
with
\Lambda_{0}
via the canonical isomorphism
\boldsymbol{e}_{0}\boldsymbol{e}^{-1}
. Then the seed mutation
\mu_{k}\colon\Theta\to\Theta^{\prime}
can be understood as a change of basis in
\Lambda_{0}
defined by the rule (
3.8
).

To each quiver mutation
\mu_{k}
we associate an isomorphism of quantum tori

(3.9)

\displaystyle\mu^{\prime}_{k}\colon\mathcal{T}^{\mathcal{X}}_{Q}\longrightarrow\mathcal{T}^{\mathcal{X}}_{\mu_{k}(Q)},\qquad Y_{e_{i}}\longmapsto Y_{\mu_{k}(e_{i})}.

The
quantum cluster
\mathcal{X}
-mutation
is the isomorphism

(3.10)

\mu^{q}_{k}\colon\operatorname{Frac}(\mathcal{T}^{\mathcal{X}}_{Q})\longrightarrow\operatorname{Frac}(\mathcal{T}^{\mathcal{X}}_{Q^{\prime}}),\qquad f\longmapsto\Psi_{q}\left(Y_{k}^{\prime}\right)\mu^{\prime}_{k}(f)\Psi_{q}\left(Y_{k}^{\prime}\right)^{-1},

where
\operatorname{Frac}(\mathcal{T}^{\mathcal{X}}_{Q})
denotes the skew fraction field of the Ore domain
\mathcal{T}^{\mathcal{X}}_{Q}
.
The fact that conjugation by
\Psi_{q}\left(Y^{\prime}_{k}\right)
yields a birational automorphism follows form the integrality of the form
\omega_{\Lambda}
and the difference equation (
3.7
). Equivalently, we can rewrite the factorization above as

(3.11)

\displaystyle\mu^{q}_{k}=\mu^{\prime}_{k}\circ\mu^{\sharp},\quad\mu^{\sharp}(f)=\Psi(Y_{k}^{-1})f\Psi(Y_{k}^{-1})^{-1}.

The specialization of (
3.10
) at
q=1
gives a birational map of tori
\mu_{k}:{\mathcal{T}}_{Q}\dashrightarrow\mathcal{T}_{Q^{\prime}}
known as the classical cluster
\mathcal{X}
-mutation in direction
k
.

Remark 3.4
.

As noted above, the requirement that the linear map
\mu_{k}
from (
3.8
) is an isometry depends only on the quivers
Q,Q^{\prime}
and not on the choice of seeds representing them. In particular, we get an equivalent definition if we replace
\mu_{k}
with

(3.12)

\mu^{-}_{k}\colon\Lambda\longrightarrow\Lambda^{\prime},\qquad e_{i}\longmapsto\begin{cases}-e^{\prime}_{k}&\text{if}\;i=k,\\
e^{\prime}_{i}+[\varepsilon_{ki}]_{+}e^{\prime}_{k}&\text{if}\;i\neq k,\end{cases}

obtained from (
3.8
) by postcomposing it with the isometry of
\Lambda^{\prime}
given by
e_{i}^{\prime}\mapsto e_{i}^{\prime}+(e_{k},e_{i})e_{k}^{\prime}
. In terms of
\mu_{k}^{-}
, the quantum cluster transformation
\mu_{k}^{q}
defined by formula (
3.10
) factors as

(3.13)

\mu_{k}^{q}(f)=\mu_{k}^{-}\left(\Psi_{q}(Y_{k})^{-1}f\Psi_{q}(Y_{k})\right).

Definition 3.5
.

Let
Q
be a quiver and
\mathcal{T}^{\mathcal{X}}_{Q}
the corresponding quantum torus. The
universally Laurent ring

\mathbb{L}^{\mathcal{X}}_{Q}
based at
Q
is the subring in
\mathcal{T}^{\mathcal{X}}_{Q}
consisting of elements
X\in\mathcal{T}^{\mathcal{X}}_{Q}
such that for any finite-length sequence of mutable directions
(k_{1},\ldots,k_{l})
, we have

\mu^{q}_{k_{l}}\cdots\mu^{q}_{k_{1}}(X)\in\mathcal{T}^{\mathcal{X}}_{Q^{\prime}},\qquad\text{where}\qquad Q^{\prime}=\mu_{k_{l}}\cdots\mu_{k_{1}}(Q).

Elements of
\mathbb{L}^{\mathcal{X}}_{Q}
are said to be
universally Laurent.

Definition 3.6
.

Consider a pair of quivers
Q,Q^{\prime}
with possibly different label sets
I,I^{\prime}
, lattices
\Lambda,\Lambda^{\prime}
, and bases
\left\{e_{i}\right\},\left\{e^{\prime}_{i}\right\}
. Let
\sigma\colon I\rightarrow I^{\prime}
be a bijection such that
\sigma(I_{m})=I^{\prime}_{m}
. We say that an isometry

\varsigma\colon\Lambda\to\Lambda^{\prime}

is a
quiver permutation
if
\varsigma(e_{i})=e^{\prime}_{\sigma(i)}
for all
i\in I
. If the equality
\varsigma(e_{i})=e^{\prime}_{\sigma(i)}
holds only for the mutable directions
i\in I_{m}
, then
\varsigma
is called a
quiver quasi-permutation.
An isometry
\mu\colon\Lambda\to\Lambda^{\prime}
is a
(quasi-)cluster quiver transformation
if it can be factored into a composition of quiver mutations and quiver (quasi-)permutation.

Remark 3.7
.

The notion of a quasi-cluster quiver transformation is independent of the choice of particular seeds representing the two quivers, as is the case with cluster mutations.

Definition 3.8
.

Let
Q,Q^{\prime}
be a pair of quivers, and
\mathcal{T}^{\mathcal{X}}_{Q},\mathcal{T}^{\mathcal{X}}_{Q^{\prime}}
the corresponding quantum tori. An algebra isomorphism
\mathcal{T}^{\mathcal{X}}_{Q}\to\mathcal{T}^{\mathcal{X}}_{Q^{\prime}}
is a
(quasi-)permutation
if it is given by
Y_{e_{j}}\to Y_{\varsigma(e_{j})}
for some quiver (quasi-)permutation
\varsigma
. A
(quasi-)cluster transformation

\mu\colon\mathcal{T}^{\mathcal{X}}_{Q}\dashrightarrow\mathcal{T}^{\mathcal{X}}_{Q^{\prime}}

is an isomorphism
\operatorname{Frac}(\mathcal{T}^{\mathcal{X}}_{Q})\simeq\operatorname{Frac}(\mathcal{T}^{\mathcal{X}}_{Q^{\prime}})
, which can be factored into a composition of quantum cluster mutations and a (quasi-)permutation.

By Definition
3.5
, an element of the non-commutative Laurent polynomial ring
\mathcal{T}^{\mathcal{X}}_{Q}
is universally Laurent if its image under any finite sequence of quantum cluster mutations remains a (non-commutative) Laurent polynomial. Equivalently, it is universal Laurent if it stays a Laurent polynomial under any quasi-cluster transformation. Note that a quasi-cluster transformation
\mu\colon\mathcal{T}^{\mathcal{X}}_{Q}\dashrightarrow\mathcal{T}^{\mathcal{X}}_{Q^{\prime}}
defines an isomorphism
\mathbb{L}^{\mathcal{X}}_{Q}\simeq\mathbb{L}^{\mathcal{X}}_{Q^{\prime}}
. In particular, if
Q=Q^{\prime}
, then
\mu
is an automorphism of the universally Laurent ring
\mathbb{L}^{\mathcal{X}}_{Q}
.

3.4.
Tropicalization

Given a quiver
Q
, the
universal semifield

\mathbb{P}_{\mathrm{univ}}(Q)\subset\mathbb{Q}_{+}(\boldsymbol{x})
consists of all nonzero rational functions of classical cluster variables
\boldsymbol{x}=\left\{x_{i},\,|\,i\in I\right\}
having subtraction-free expressions. We also write
\mathbb{P}_{\mathrm{trop}}(Q)
for the
tropical semifield
on variables
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{y}}=\left\{\mathrm{y}_{i},\,|\,i\in I\right\}
, which is the abelian multiplicative group freely generated by
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{y}}
endowed with the addition

\prod_{i\in I}{\mathrm{y}}_{i}^{a_{i}}\oplus\prod_{i\in I}\mathrm{y}_{i}^{b_{i}}=\prod_{i\in I}\mathrm{y}_{i}^{\min(a_{i},b_{i})}.

We call
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{y}}
the
tropical
\mathcal{X}
-variables
associated to quiver
Q
. The canonical homomorphism of semifields
\pi_{t}\colon\mathbb{P}_{\mathrm{univ}}(\boldsymbol{x})\to\mathbb{P}_{\mathrm{trop}}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{y}})
, defined by
\pi_{t}(x_{i})=\mathrm{y}_{i}
and
\pi_{t}(a)=1
for all
a\in\mathbb{Q}_{+}
, induces a mutation rule for the tropical
\mathcal{X}
-variables, which can be described as follows. Let us say that a Laurent monomial in
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{y}}
is positive (resp. negative) if it is not 1 and all its exponents are nonnegative (resp. nonpositive).

Proposition 3.9

(
[
DWZ10
,
Nag10
,
Pla11
]
)
.

Let
Q
be a quiver and
(\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{\mathrm{y}})
the corresponding sets of classical and tropical
\mathcal{X}
-variables. Then for any quiver
Q^{\prime}
mutation equivalent to
Q
, each Laurent monomial
\mathrm{y}_{i}^{\prime}=\pi_{t}(x^{\prime}_{i})
in
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{y}}
is either positive or negative.

By this sign-coherence, once we have fixed an initial quiver
Q_{0}
we get a well-defined
tropical sign

\sigma=\sigma_{Q_{0}}
of each tropical cluster variable
\mathrm{y}_{i}
, defined by
\sigma(\mathrm{y}_{i})=1
if
\mathrm{y}_{i}
is a positive Laurent monomial in tropical cluster variables of
Q_{0}
, and
-1
otherwise. Now if
Q
is a quiver mutation equivalent to
Q_{0}
, and
Q^{\prime}=\mu_{k}(Q)
, the mutation rule for tropical
\mathcal{X}
-variables can be conveniently expressed in terms of the tropical sign via

\mathrm{y}^{\prime}_{i}=\begin{cases}(\mathrm{y}_{k})^{-1},&i=k\\
\mathrm{y}_{i}(\mathrm{y}_{k})^{[\sigma(\mathrm{y}_{k})\varepsilon_{ki}]_{+}},&i\neq k.\end{cases}

In fact, the question of whether two composites of quantum mutations coincide can be decided just based on the corresponding tropical cluster transformations.

Theorem 3.10

(
[
FG09a
,
Kel11
,
KN11
]
)
.

Write
c^{q},d^{q}
for quantum cluster transformations obtained as composites of cluster mutations (
3.10
),
c
and
d
for their
q=1
specializations, and
c_{t},d_{t}
for their tropical counterparts. Then

c^{q}=d^{q}\iff c=d\iff c_{t}=d_{t}.

Remark 3.11
.

Thanks to Theorem
3.10
, it follows that a cluster in the cluster atlas is completely determined by its set of tropical variables.

The following lemma is useful for computing the effect of sequences of mutations at the level of tropical cluster variables.

Lemma 3.12
.

Let
Q
be a quiver mutation equivalent to a reference quiver
Q_{0}
,
\mathrm{y}
be the tropical cluster variables of
Q
, and
i_{0},\ldots,,i_{l+1}
be a sequence of vertices such that

(1)

\sigma(\mathrm{y}_{i})=1
for all
r\geqslant 1
, where
\sigma
is the tropical sign with respect to the quiver
Q_{0}
;

(2)

for each
1\leq r\leq l
, the only outward pointing arrows from vertex
i_{r}
in the quiver
\mu_{i_{r-1}}\circ\cdots\circ\mu_{i_{1}}(Q)
are to vertices
i_{r-1}
and
i_{r+1}
.

Then in the quiver
Q^{\prime}=\mu_{i_{l}}\circ\cdots\circ\mu_{i_{1}}(Q)
we have

\mathrm{y}^{\prime}_{i_{0}}=\mathrm{y}_{i_{0}}\mathrm{y}_{i_{1}},\qquad\mathrm{y}^{\prime}_{i_{s}}=\mathrm{y}_{i_{s+1}},\qquad\mathrm{y}^{\prime}_{i_{l}}=\prod_{r=1}^{l}\mathrm{y}_{i_{r}}^{-1},\qquad\mathrm{y}^{\prime}_{i_{l+1}}=\prod_{r=1}^{l+1}\mathrm{y}_{i_{r}}

for all
1\leqslant s<l
, while no other tropical variables are affected.

3.5.
Compatible pairs,
\mathcal{A}
-variables, and central characters.

In this section we recall the enhancement cluster seeds to compatible pairs, which will allow us to work with cluster
\mathcal{A}
-variables.

Given a lattice
\Lambda
with skew-form
(\cdot,\cdot)
and a ring
R\supset\mathbb{Z}
, we define the
R
-module

\Lambda_{R}=\Lambda\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}R,

and endow it with an
R
-valued skew-form obtained from
(\cdot,\cdot)_{\Lambda}
by extension of scalars. We abuse notation and denote the skew-form on
\Lambda_{R}
by the same symbol
(\cdot,\cdot)
.

Given a seed
\Theta=\left(I,I_{*},\Lambda,(\cdot,\cdot)_{\Lambda},\left\{e_{i}\right\}\right)
be a seed, we write

\Lambda^{\mathrm{mut}}=\bigoplus_{i\in I_{m}}\mathbb{Z}e_{i}\subseteq\Lambda.

Definition 3.13
.

Let
\Theta=\left(I,I_{*},\Lambda,(\cdot,\cdot)_{\Lambda},\left\{e_{i}\right\}\right)
be a seed. An extension of
\Theta
to a
compatible pair
is the data of a lattice
\Xi
such that

\Lambda\subseteq\Xi\subset\Lambda_{\mathbb{Q}},

together with a basis
\left\{\xi_{i}\right\}_{i\in I}
for
\Xi
satisfying

(3.14)

(e_{i},\xi_{j})=\delta_{ij}\qquad\text{for all}\qquad i\in I_{m},\;j\in I.

A compatible pair determines an integer
I\times I

ensemble matrix

b=(b_{ij})
by expanding the basis
\left\{e_{j}\right\}
into the basis
\left\{\xi_{i}\right\}
:

(3.15)

e_{j}=\sum_{i\in I}b_{ij}\xi_{i},\qquad j\in I.

We also obtain a
\mathbb{Q}
-valued skew-symmetric
I\times I
matrix
\kappa
, whose entries are the values of the form on the basis
\left\{\xi_{i}\right\}
:

\kappa_{ij}=(\xi_{i},\xi_{j}),\qquad i,j\in I.

Remark 3.14
.

Here are some remarks on how the definition above fits in with related ones in the literature.

(1)

Condition (
3.14
) implies that the form
(\cdot,\cdot)
defines perfect pairing

\bigoplus_{i\in I^{m}}\mathbb{Z}\xi_{i}\otimes\Lambda^{\mathrm{mut}}\rightarrow\mathbb{Z},

and moreover that

\left(\Lambda^{\mathrm{mut}}_{\mathbb{Q}}\right)^{\perp}\cap\Xi=\bigoplus_{j\in I^{*}}\mathbb{Z}\xi_{j}.

In particular, the existence of a compatible pair implies that
\Lambda^{\mathrm{mut}}
has trivial intersection with the kernel
Z_{\Lambda}
of the form
(\cdot,\cdot)
on
\Lambda_{\mathbb{Q}}
.

(2)

Denote by
\tilde{b}
be the
I\times I_{m}
submatrix of
b
. Evaluating the functional
(\cdot,\xi_{k})
on both sides of (
3.15
), we see that

\tilde{b}^{t}\kappa=\left[\mathrm{Id}_{I_{m}}|0\right].

In particular, for any
d\in\mathbb{Z}
such that the matrix
d\kappa
has integer entries,
(\tilde{b},d\kappa)
is a compatible pair in the sense of
[
BZ05
]
with all multipliers given by
d_{j}=d
.

(3)

Now evaluating the functional
(e_{i},\cdot)
on both sides of (
3.15
), with
i\in I_{m}
, we get that

b_{ij}=\varepsilon_{ij}\qquad\text{for all}\qquad i\in I_{m},\;j\in I.

Hence the rows of any ensemble matrix labelled by non-frozen directions coincide with the corresponding rows of the
\varepsilon
-matrix.

Definition 3.15
.

Compatible pairs
(\Theta,\left\{\xi_{i}\right\})
and
(\Theta^{\prime},\left\{\xi^{\prime}_{i}\right\})
are
equivalent
if the underlying seeds
\Theta,\Theta^{\prime}
are the induced isometry
\Xi\rightarrow\Xi^{\prime}
sends
\xi_{i}
to
\xi_{i}^{\prime}
. A quiver
Q

enhanced to a compatible pair

(Q,\left\{\xi_{i}\right\})
is the class of pairs equivalent to
(\Theta,\left\{\xi_{i}\right\})
.

Consider a quiver
Q
enhanced to a compatible pair
(Q,\left\{\xi_{i}\right\})
. Given an integer
k
, such that
k\kappa_{ij}\in\mathbb{Z}
for all
i,j\in I
(unless otherwise specified, we will always choose the minimal such
k
), we define the quantum torus

\mathcal{T}^{\mathcal{A}}_{Q}=\mathbb{Z}[q^{\pm 1/d}]\langle Y_{\pm\xi_{i}}\,|\,i\in I\rangle/\langle q^{\kappa_{jk}}Y_{\xi_{j}}Y_{\xi_{k}}=q^{\kappa_{kj}}Y_{\xi_{k}}Y_{\xi_{j}}\rangle.

As before, the algebra
\mathcal{T}^{\mathcal{A}}_{Q}
only depends on the enhanced quiver rather than on a particular seed. Note that the quantum torus
\mathcal{T}^{\mathcal{X}}_{Q}
is a
\mathbb{Z}[q^{\pm 1}]
-subalgebra of
\mathcal{T}^{\mathcal{A}}_{Q}
.

Definition 3.16
.

The elements
Y_{\xi_{j}}
of
\mathcal{T}^{\mathcal{A}}_{Q}
are called the
quantum cluster
\mathcal{A}
-variables
4
4

4

Here we follow the terminology of
[
FG06b
]
. Note however that it is common in cluster literature to refer to our
\mathcal{A}
- and
\mathcal{X}
-variables as the
\mathrm{x}
- and
\mathrm{y}
-variables respectively.
associated to the compatible pair
(Q,\left\{\xi_{i}\right\})
.

Recall the notion of two seeds being related by mutation defined in (
3.8
). In the case that the seeds
\Theta,\Theta^{\prime}
are both enhanced to compatible pairs, we impose the additional requirement that under the extension of the map
\mu_{k}\colon\Xi\to\Xi^{\prime}
from (
3.8
) we have

(3.16)

\mu_{k}(\xi_{i})=\begin{cases}-\xi^{\prime}_{k}+\sum_{j\neq k}[b_{jk}]_{+}\xi_{j}^{\prime}&\text{if}\;i=k,\\
\xi^{\prime}_{i}&\text{if}\;i\neq k.\end{cases}

One easily computes that the latter is equivalent to the following standard relation between the ensemble matrices:

(3.17)

b^{\prime}_{ij}=\begin{cases}-b_{ij},&\text{if}\;\;k=i\;\;\text{or}\;\;k=j,\\
b_{ij}+\frac{|b_{ik}|b_{kj}+b_{ik}|b_{kj}|}{2},&\text{otherwise.}\end{cases}

Since the pairing between
\Xi
and
\mathbb{Z}\left\langle e_{i}\,|\,i\in I_{m}\right\rangle
takes integer values by (
3.14
), the quantum mutation maps (
3.10
) extend to well-defined isomorphisms

(3.18)

\mu^{q}_{k}\colon\operatorname{Frac}(\mathcal{T}^{\mathcal{A}}_{Q})\longrightarrow\operatorname{Frac}(\mathcal{T}^{\mathcal{A}}_{\mu_{k}(Q)}).

We can use formula (
3.10
) to compute the evolution of the quantum
\mathcal{A}
-variables under mutation:

(3.19)

\displaystyle\mu_{k}^{q}(Y_{\xi_{k}})

\displaystyle=\Psi_{q}(Y_{e_{k}^{\prime}})Y_{\mu_{k}(\xi_{k})}\Psi_{q}(Y_{e_{k}^{\prime}})^{-1}

\displaystyle=Y_{\mu_{k}(\xi_{k})}+Y_{\mu_{k}(\xi_{k})+e_{k}^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=Y_{-\xi_{k}^{\prime}+\xi^{+}_{k}}+Y_{-\xi_{k}^{\prime}+\xi^{-}_{k}},

where

\xi_{k}^{\pm}=\sum_{j\in I}[\pm b_{jk}]_{+}\xi^{\prime}_{j}.

This matches the standard mutation rule for quantum
\mathcal{A}
-variables given in formula (4.23) of
[
BZ05
]
.

Remark 3.17
.

In the classical
q\rightarrow 1
limit, the
Y_{\xi_{k}}
become coordinates
A_{k}
on the cluster chart of a cluster
K_{2}
-variety, also known as the cluster
\mathcal{A}
-variety. Similarly, generators
Y_{e_{k}}
become coordinates
X_{k}
on the cluster chart of a cluster Poisson variety, also called the cluster
\mathcal{X}
-variety. The classical limit
\mu_{k}^{q=1}
of the mutation map
\mu_{k}^{q}
is expressed in terms of classical
\mathcal{A}
-coordinates as the involutive birational transformation

(3.20)

\mu_{k}^{q=1}(A^{\prime}_{i})=\begin{cases}A_{i}\quad&i\neq k\\
A_{k}^{-1}\left(\prod\limits_{b_{jk}>0}A_{j}^{b_{jk}}+\prod\limits_{b_{jk}<0}A_{j}^{-b_{jk}}\right)\quad&i=k,\end{cases}

and the classical ensemble map reads

X_{i}=\prod_{r\in I}A_{r}^{\varepsilon_{ri}}.

Using (
3.18
), we can now define the
K_{2}
-version of the universally Laurent ring, the
quantum upper cluster algebra

\mathbb{L}^{\mathcal{A}}_{Q}\subset\mathcal{T}^{\mathcal{A}}_{Q}
, which consists of all elements
A\in\mathcal{T}^{\mathcal{A}}_{Q}
that stay Laurent polynomials under any finite sequence of quantum cluster mutations. As before, for any pair of mutation equivalent quivers
Q,Q^{\prime}
we have a canonical isomorphism
\mathbb{L}^{\mathcal{A}}_{Q}\simeq\mathbb{L}^{\mathcal{A}}_{Q^{\prime}}
of their quantum upper cluster algebras. Note that the universally Laurent algebra
\mathbb{L}^{\mathcal{X}}_{Q}
is contained in
\mathbb{L}^{\mathcal{A}}_{Q}
as a
\mathbb{Z}[q^{\pm 1}]
-subring, and we have the equality
\mathbb{L}^{\mathcal{X}}_{Q}=\mathbb{L}^{\mathcal{A}}_{Q}
if and only if
\det(b)=\pm 1
. By the quantum Laurent phenomenon, see
[
BZ05
, Corollary 5.2]
, all quantum
\mathcal{A}
-variables (from all cluster charts) are elements of the upper cluster algebra. The subalgebra of
\mathbb{L}^{\mathcal{A}}_{Q}
generated by them is called the
quantum cluster algebra.

Berenstein and Zelevinsky prove the quantum Laurent phenomenon by means of the following useful criterion for when an element of
\mathcal{T}^{\mathcal{A}}_{Q}
lies in
\mathbb{L}^{\mathcal{A}}_{Q}
. Given a quiver
Q
, let
Q^{[k]}
denote the one obtained from
Q
by replacing its frozen subset
I_{*}
with
I_{*}^{[k]}=I\smallsetminus k
