. In other words, in
Q^{[k]}
we freeze all basis vectors but the one labelled
k
. Then the
upper bound

\mathcal{U}_{Q}^{\mathcal{A}}
is defined as

\mathcal{U}_{Q}^{\mathcal{A}}=\bigcap_{k\in I_{m}}\mathbb{L}^{\mathcal{A}}_{Q^{[k]}}\subset\mathcal{T}^{\mathcal{A}}_{Q}.

Theorem 3.18

(
[
BZ05
]
, Theorem 5.1)
.

The upper bound
\mathcal{U}_{Q}^{\mathcal{A}}
depends only on the mutation class of the pair
(Q,\left\{\xi_{i}\right\})
: we have

\mathbb{L}^{\mathcal{A}}_{Q}=\mathcal{U}_{Q}^{\mathcal{A}}.

Hence an element
f\in\mathcal{T}^{\mathcal{A}}_{Q}
is universally Laurent if and only if it remains Laurent under all 1-step mutations.

We record the following standard corollary of this Theorem:

Corollary 3.19
.

For any quiver
Q
(which need not admit an extension to a compatible pair), we have

\mathbb{L}^{\mathcal{X}}_{Q}=\bigcap_{k\in I_{m}}\mathbb{L}^{\mathcal{X}}_{Q^{[k]}}.

Proof..

Consider the principal coefficient extension
\widehat{Q}
of quiver
Q
, obtained by adding a frozen vertex
k_{*}
for every vertex
k
of
Q
along with an arrow
k_{*}\to k
. Then the quiver
\widehat{Q}
admits a compatible pair such that
(e_{i},\xi_{j})=\delta_{ij}
for all
i,j\in I
. In particular it satisfies the assumption of Theorem
3.18
with
\det(\hat{b})=1
, so we have the following chain of equalities:

\mathbb{L}^{\mathcal{X}}_{\widehat{Q}}=\mathbb{L}^{\mathcal{A}}_{\widehat{Q}}=\bigcap_{k\in I_{m}}\mathbb{L}^{\mathcal{A}}_{\widehat{Q}^{[k]}}=\bigcap_{k\in I_{m}}\mathbb{L}^{\mathcal{X}}_{\widehat{Q}^{[k]}}.

Since
\mathbb{L}^{\mathcal{X}}_{Q}
is a subring of
\mathbb{L}^{\mathcal{X}}_{\widehat{Q}}
and
\mathbb{L}^{\mathcal{X}}_{Q}\cap\mathbb{L}^{\mathcal{X}}_{\widehat{Q}^{[k]}}=\mathbb{L}^{\mathcal{X}}_{Q^{[k]}}
, the Corollary follows.
∎

3.6.
Coordinate and cluster modular groupoids.

We now recall the
cluster modular groupoid
associated to a cluster variety.

Notation 3.20
.

Given a quiver
Q
we denote by
{\boldsymbol{Q}}
the class of quivers that may be obtained from
Q
by quasi-cluster quiver transformations. In particular, quivers from
{\boldsymbol{Q}}
are not required to have identical label sets
I
.

Definition 3.21
.

The
(enhanced) quasi-cluster modular groupoid

\operatorname{Cl}_{\boldsymbol{Q}}
is the groupoid whose objects are quivers
Q\in\boldsymbol{Q}
(enhanced to compatible pairs)
and whose morphisms are quasi-cluster transformations of cluster tori. Composition of morphisms is given by composition of birational transformations. The
quasi-cluster modular group

\Gamma_{\boldsymbol{Q}}=\pi_{1}(\operatorname{Cl}_{\boldsymbol{Q}})
, is the automorphism group of an object in
\operatorname{Cl}_{\boldsymbol{Q}}
.

In other words, one can think of morphisms in
\operatorname{Cl}_{{\boldsymbol{Q}}}
as formal composites of quiver mutations and quiver quasi-permutations, where we identify two formal composites if they induce the same birational isomorphism of tori.

Since all seeds we consider are enhanced with compatible pairs, in what follows omit the word “enhanced” (as well as the prefix “quasi-”) and simply write
cluster modular groupoid
and
cluster modular group.

Remark 3.22
.

(1)

Any element of the cluster modular group defines an automorphism of the quantum upper cluster algebra
\mathbb{L}^{\mathcal{A}}_{Q}
and the universally Laurent ring
\mathbb{L}^{\mathcal{X}}_{Q}
.

(2)

We can also consider the subgroupoid
\operatorname{Cl}_{{\boldsymbol{Q}};I}
whose objects are quivers with a
fixed
label set
I
, and morphisms are quasi-cluster transformations obtained as composites of mutations and quasi-permutations between quivers with identical label sets. Since the groupoids
\operatorname{Cl}_{{\boldsymbol{Q}}}
and
\operatorname{Cl}_{{\boldsymbol{Q}};I}
have the same fundamental groups, the question of which to work with is largely a question of convenience for the application at hand.

The following gives another way to think about the cluster modular groupoid. We formulate it in the context of enhanced quivers, although the same construction works for ordinary ones using the
\mathcal{X}
-torus in place of the
\mathcal{A}
one.

Definition 3.23
.

Let
Q
be a quiver with label set
I
, and
\mathcal{T}^{\mathcal{A}}_{Q}
the corresponding quantum torus with its generators
\left\{A_{i}\,|\,i\in I\right\}
. A
cluster
in
\mathcal{T}^{\mathcal{A}}_{Q}
is a collection of elements

\mathcal{B}_{\mu}=\left\{\mu(A^{\prime}_{i})\,|\,i\in I_{Q^{\prime}}\right\},

where
\mu\colon\mathcal{T}^{\mathcal{A}}_{Q^{\prime}}\dashrightarrow\mathcal{T}^{\mathcal{A}}_{Q}
is a quasi-cluster transformation. We define the
coordinate groupoid

\widetilde{\operatorname{Cl}}_{Q}
based at the quiver
Q
to be the connected, simply connected groupoid whose objects are clusters in
\mathcal{T}^{\mathcal{A}}_{Q}
.

As with universally Laurent rings, a quasi-cluster transformation
\mu\colon\mathcal{T}^{\mathcal{A}}_{Q_{1}}\dashrightarrow\mathcal{T}^{\mathcal{A}}_{Q_{2}}
defines an isomorphism of coordinate groupoids
\widetilde{\operatorname{Cl}}_{Q_{1}}\simeq\widetilde{\operatorname{Cl}}_{Q_{2}}
. We emphasize that this isomorphism is not canonical, and depends on the choice of the transformation
\mu
.

For each
Q\in{\boldsymbol{Q}}
there is a canonical functor

\pi\colon\widetilde{\operatorname{Cl}}_{Q}\longrightarrow\operatorname{Cl}_{\boldsymbol{Q}}

defined as follows. The objects
\mathcal{B}_{\mu}
of
\widetilde{\operatorname{Cl}}_{Q}
are labelled by quasi-cluster transformations
\mu\colon\mathcal{T}^{\mathcal{A}}_{Q^{\prime}}\dashrightarrow\mathcal{T}^{\mathcal{A}}_{Q}
, and we set
\pi(\mathcal{B}_{\mu})=Q^{\prime}
. At the level of morphisms,
\pi
sends the unique morphism between objects
\mathcal{B}_{\mu_{1}}
and
\mathcal{B}_{\mu_{2}}
to the quasi-cluster transformation
\mu_{2}\mu_{1}^{-1}\colon\mathcal{T}^{\mathcal{A}}_{Q_{1}}\dashrightarrow\mathcal{T}^{\mathcal{A}}_{Q_{2}}
. This way the composition law in the cluster atlas gets identified with composition of quasi-cluster transformations, making
\pi
into a functor.

Remark 3.24
.

This approach to the cluster modular groupoid is natural in the case when the upper cluster algebra
\mathbb{L}^{\mathcal{A}}_{Q}
quantizes a ring of functions on some variety
X
. In this case, each object
\mathcal{B}
of the cluster atlas corresponds to a distinct toric coordinate chart
\phi_{\mathcal{B}}\colon U_{\mathcal{B}}\to(\mathbb{C}^{\times})^{d}
on
X
, and so we can think of the objects of the coordinate groupoid
\widetilde{\operatorname{Cl}}_{Q}
as being labelled by these coordinate systems.

4.
Cluster coordinates on decorated character varieties

In this section we introduce a non-degenerate compatible pair related to the moduli spaces
\mathcal{P}^{\diamond}_{SL_{n+1},S}
and
\mathcal{P}^{\diamond}_{PGL_{n+1},S}
.

More precisely, we explain in
4.3
how to construct enhanced quivers
Q_{\Gamma}
indexed by certain planar bicolored graphs
\Gamma
on
S
. We show in Proposition
4.22
that the enhanced quivers associated to any two such graphs are related by a sequence of quasi-cluster transformations, and hence
our construction gives a well-defined pair of cluster varieties associated to
S
.
Moreover, in
4.6
we associate to each such graph
\Gamma
a collection of functions
\{A_{\ell}(\Gamma)\},\{Y_{\ell}(\Gamma)\}
on the moduli spaces
\mathcal{P}^{\diamond}_{SL_{n+1},S}
and
\mathcal{P}^{\diamond}_{PGL_{n+1},S}
respectively. In Propositions
4.31
and
4.32
we show that the functions associated to graphs related by a quasi-cluster transformation satisfy the same relations as the generators of the classical cluster
\mathcal{A}
- and
\mathcal{X}
-tori for the corresponding quivers.

Since our class of graphs is preserved by the action of the mapping class group, we obtain functors

\mathcal{Q}\colon\widehat{\operatorname{Pt}}(S)\longrightarrow\operatorname{Cl}_{\boldsymbol{Q}}

from the enhanced Ptolemy groupoid
\widehat{\operatorname{Pt}}(S)
to the cluster modular groupoid
\operatorname{Cl}_{\boldsymbol{Q}}
of the compatible pair, where the quiver class
\boldsymbol{Q}=\boldsymbol{Q}(S)
depends on the marked surface
S
and the group
G
. The description of these functors, along with some examples, is given in
4.8
.

4.1.
Bicolored graphs

Let
S
be a marked surface with a set
\mathcal{C}
of tacked circles, a set
P
of punctures, and a set
M
of marked points.

Definition 4.1
.

A
bicolored graph

\Gamma
on
S
is an embedded graph
\Gamma\subset S
with black and white vertices in
S\smallsetminus(P\cup\partial S)
and 1-valent boundary vertices, which avoid all special points of
S
.

We consider such graphs up to ambient isotopies
\gamma
, such that for each time-slice
\gamma_{t}
there is a color-preserving isomorphism
\gamma_{t}(\Gamma)\simeq\Gamma
of abstract graphs.

We say that an edge of
\Gamma
is
white
or
black
if both of its vertices are. We also refer to white and black edges as
monochrome
. Similarly, an edge of
\Gamma
is
bicolored
if one of its vertices is black while the other one is white, and is
boundary
if it has a boundary vertex.
Faces
of
\Gamma
are the connected components of
S\smallsetminus\Gamma
. A face
f
is
proper
if it is homeomorphic to a disk without marked points or punctures, and is incident to a bi-colored edge. A proper face is
boundary
if its intersection with
\partial S
is non-empty. Any non-boundary proper face is said to be
internal.
We denote the set of proper faces, edges, and vertices of
\Gamma
by
F(\Gamma)
,
E(\Gamma)
, and
V(\Gamma)
respectively. In what follows we only work with proper faces, thus we abuse notation and write face instead of proper face unless otherwise specified.

Definition 4.2
.

Two bicolored graphs are
equivalent
if one can be obtained from the other by applying the following transformations and their inverses:

•

collapse a monochrome edge to a single vertex of the same color;

•

replace a 2-valent vertex and the two edges incident to it by a single edge.

Figure 10.
Bi-colored graphs of rank 3 in ideal triangles.

A bi-colored graph
\Gamma\subset S
is
trivalent
if each of its non-boundary vertices is such. Evidently, every equivalence class of bi-colored graphs contains a bipartite one and a trivalent one, and we will often use those in the sequel. We also note that there is a natural bijection between the sets of proper faces of equivalent graphs.

Example 4.3
.

Given an ideal triangulation
\underline{\Delta}
of a marked surface and a positive integer
n
, we define a bicolored graph
\Gamma_{\underline{\Delta}}\subset S
as follows. We place the graph shown on the left of Figure
10
inside each non-special triangle,
5
5

5

In the Figure we have
n=3
. The definition of this graph can be found, for example, in
[
Gon17
]
, however we trust the reader to be able to picture the graph in question for an arbitrary number
n
.
where
n+1
is the number of boundary vertices on each edge, and the graph on the right inside each special triangle in such a way that the pairs of boundary vertices on every internal edge of
\underline{\Delta}
match, see Figure
11
. The number
n
is the
rank
of the graph.

Figure 11.
Graph
\Gamma_{\underline{\Delta}}
on an annulus, one boundary of which is a tacked circle while the other contains 2 marked points.

Let
\Gamma\subset S
be a trivalent bicolored graph on a marked surface
S
. Assume further that the graph
\Gamma
in the vicinity of a tacked circle
c
can be described as follows. First, there is a cycle
o
in
\Gamma
homotopic to
c
, which consists of white edges
6
6

6

We denote edges of
\Gamma
by the letter
l
to distinguish them from basis elements of the lattice we associate to
\Gamma
in the sequel.

l_{j}=(w_{j},w_{j+1})
for
1\leqslant j<k
, and bicolored edges
l_{+}=(b,w_{1})
,
l_{-}=(w_{k},b)
, so that
w_{j+1}
follows
w_{j}
as we traverse
o
in the positive direction. Second, the restriction of
\Gamma
to the open annulus bounded by
o
and
c
consists of the unique boundary edge connecting
b
to
c
, as shown on Figure
12
.

Notation 4.4
.

Assume that the graph
\Gamma\subset S
is as described above in the vicinity of every tacked circle
c\in\mathcal{C}(S)
. We denote by
\Gamma_{\circ}
the bicolored graph obtained from
\Gamma
by replacing each vertex
b=b(c)
and the three edges incident to it by a single white edge
l_{k}=(w_{k},w_{1})
.

Definition 4.5
.

Assume that the bicolored graph
\Gamma
satisfies the above conditions. Then the
positive shift

\sigma_{c}
is a transformation of
\Gamma
consisting of sliding the vertex
b
past
w_{1}
in the positive direction, see Figure
12
. The
negative shift
is the transformation
\sigma_{c}^{-1}
.

\sigma_{c}

Figure 12.
Positive shift at a tacked circle.

Let
\Gamma=\Gamma_{\underline{\Delta}}
for some ideal triangulation
\underline{\Delta}
of
S
, as in Example
4.3
,
c\in\mathcal{C}(S)
be a tacked circle, and
\Delta
the special triangle of
\underline{\Delta}
containing
c
. Then the positive shift
\sigma_{c}
corresponds to the flip of
\underline{\Delta}
at the edge of
\Delta
, which follows
c
as we traverse
\Delta
in the positive direction. In that case, for the Dehn twist
\tau_{c}
we have
\sigma_{c}^{k}(\underline{\Delta})=\tau_{c}(\underline{\Delta})
.

Now, let us consider the universal cover
\pi\colon\widetilde{S}\to S
. Clearly, each marked point or puncture on
S
lifts to a marked point on
\widetilde{S}
.
Each tacked circle
c\in C(S)
lifts to a
tacked boundary component
of
\widetilde{S}
. We denote the set of such components by
C(\widetilde{S})
, and observe that each of them contains infinitely many tacks. The ideal triangulations and bicolored graphs on
S
are in bijection with those on
\widetilde{S}
which are invariant with respect to the deck group
\pi_{1}(S)
. From now on we will only consider deck invariant triangulations and graphs on
\widetilde{S}
. Now, given a bicolored graph
\Gamma
on
\widetilde{S}
, and a tacked circle
c\in C(S)
, we define

\sigma_{c}(\Gamma)=\pi^{-1}\left(\sigma_{c}(\pi(\Gamma))\right).

Definition 4.6
.

A non-boundary quadrilateral face
f
of a bipartite graph
\Gamma\subset S
is
admissible
if no two faces
\tilde{f},\tilde{f}^{\prime}\in\pi^{-1}(f)
share an edge, and none of the vertices of
f
is connected to a tacked circle by an edge. A face
f
of a bipartite graph
\Gamma\subset\widetilde{S}
is admissible if its projection
\pi(f)
is.

Definition 4.7
.

Let
f
be an admissible face of a trivalent graph
\Gamma\subset\widetilde{S}
, and
f=\pi(\tilde{f})
be its projection. A
square move

\mu_{f}
at the face
f
is the transformation of
\pi(\Gamma)
, which changes the color of every vertex of
f
, see Figure
13
. As before, we set

\mu_{f}(\Gamma)=\pi^{-1}\left(\mu_{f}(\pi(\Gamma))\right).

f
\mu_{f}
f

Figure 13.
Square move at a face
f\in F(\Gamma)
.

Definition 4.8
.

A bicolored graph
\Gamma\subset S
is
ideal
if for some ideal triangulation
\underline{\Delta}
of
S
it is related to
\Gamma_{\underline{\Delta}}
by a sequence of square moves and shifts at tacked circles. A graph
\Gamma\subset\widetilde{S}
is
ideal
if its projection
\pi(\Gamma)\subset S
is.

Remark 4.9
.

As explained in
[
FG06b
]
, alternatively see Section
4.8
here, for any pair of ideal triangulations
\underline{\Delta},\underline{\Delta}^{\prime}
of
S
, the graphs
\Gamma_{\underline{\Delta}}
and
\Gamma_{\underline{\Delta}^{\prime}}
of the same rank are related to each other by a sequence of square moves and shifts at tacked circles.

In what follows we will only consider ideal bicolored graphs. Note that any such graph has the local form described before Notation
4.4
in the vicinity of each tacked circle. Let
\underline{\Delta}
be an ideal triangulation of a marked surface
S
. Recall that
S_{\circ}
is obtained from
S
by replacing every tacked circle with a puncture, and let
\underline{\Delta}_{\circ}
be the triangulation of
S_{\circ}
obtained from
\underline{\Delta}
by collapsing each special triangle onto one of its non-boundary edges. Then it is easy to see that
\Gamma_{\circ}=\Gamma_{\underline{\Delta}_{\circ}}
. For
\Gamma\subset\widetilde{S}
, we set

\Gamma_{\circ}=\pi^{-1}\left(\pi(\Gamma)_{\circ}\right).

4.2.
Zig-zag paths

Definition 4.10
.

A
zig-zag path
, or a
zig-zag
for short, on a bicolored graph
\Gamma\subset\widetilde{S}
is a path that follows edges of
\Gamma
turning maximally left at each black vertex, and maximally right at each white one.

When drawing zig-zags we will always push them away from the vertices of
\Gamma
so that they turn clock-wise around black vertices, counter-clockwise around white ones, and cross every bi-colored edge they follow. We will often draw projections of zig-zags onto
\pi(\Gamma)
rather than the zig-zags themselves. Abusing notation we still refer to these projections as
zig-zags,
see Figure
14

Denote by
\mathcal{W}_{\circ}
the union of all zig-zags of the graph
\Gamma_{\circ}
on the universal cover
\widetilde{S}_{\circ}
of
\widetilde{S}
. A connected component
R
of
\widetilde{S}_{\circ}\smallsetminus\mathcal{W}_{\circ}
is called a
k
-gon
if it is a disk which contains at most one marked point, and whose boundary has non-trivial intersection with exactly
k
zig-zags. We call each such intersection a
side
of
R
and let it inherit the orientation of the zig-zag it belongs to. Then the following properties hold for any ideal bicolored graph
\Gamma\subset\widetilde{S}
of rank
n
, see
[
Gon17
]
.

(1)

Each connected component of
\widetilde{S}_{\circ}\smallsetminus\mathcal{W}_{\circ}
is a polygon. No zig-zags on
\widetilde{S}_{\circ}
self-intersect or form non-oriented bigons.

(2)

For any zig-zag
z
the surface
\widetilde{S}_{\circ}\smallsetminus z
has two connected components, one of which has exactly one marked point
m
. We will say that
z

goes around

m
.

(3)

For each marked point
m
on
\widetilde{S}_{\circ}
there exist exactly
n+1
non-intersecting zig-zags
z_{0}(m),\dots,z_{n}(m)
that go around it. We assume the notation that the index of the zig-zags increases as we move away from
m
. Moreover, any zig-zag
z
on
\widetilde{S}_{\circ}
is of the form
z=z_{i}(m)
for some marked point
m
and some
0\leqslant i\leqslant n
.

Remark 4.11
.

Let us make the following observations.

•

Projections of zig-zags may intersect and self-intersect. For example, this happens when
S
is a punctured torus.

•

The zig-zags
z_{0}(m)
do not intersect the rest of
\mathcal{W}_{\circ}
. Erasing these zig-zags we recover an
ideal
A_{n}
-web
in the sense of
[
Gon17
]
, see the right pane of Figure
14
.

Figure 14.
Left pane: zig-zags on an ideal bicolored graph
\Gamma_{\underline{\Delta}}
of rank 3. Right pane: the corresponding ideal
A_{3}
-web.

The zig-zags on a bicolored graph
\Gamma\subset\widetilde{S}
are almost identical to the zig-zags on
\Gamma_{\circ}\subset\widetilde{S}_{\circ}
. Indeed, the only ones that can possibly differ are those that contain edges present in
\Gamma_{\circ}
, but not in
\Gamma
. If
l=(w_{-},w_{+})
is such an edge, it is replaced in
\Gamma
by a trivalent black vertex
b
, edges
l_{\pm}=(b,w_{\pm})
, and a boundary edge
l_{0}=(b,v)
with
v
lying on a tacked boundary component
c\in\mathcal{C}(\widetilde{S})
. The two zig-zags in
\Gamma_{\circ}
containing
l
are
z_{0}(m_{c})
and
z_{n}(m)
, where
m_{c}
is the marked point on
\widetilde{S}_{\circ}
corresponding to
c
on
\widetilde{S}
. Replacing edges
\gamma(l)\subset z_{0}(m_{c})
,
\gamma\in\pi_{1}(S)
, with pairs
\gamma(l_{-}),\gamma(l_{+})
we obtain a zig-zag on
\Gamma
, which we denote by
z_{0}(c)
. Similarly, for all
1\leqslant j<n
we write
z_{j}(c)
for the zig-zags
z_{j}(m_{c})
considered as zig-zags on
\Gamma
. Now, let
t
be the tack preceding
v
as we traverse
c
in the positive direction. The zig-zag
z_{n}(m)
is split into a family of zig-zags on
\Gamma
including the two denoted
\check{z}_{\pm}(t)
, which contain edges
l_{\pm},l_{0}
. Note that
\check{z}_{-}(t)
terminates at the vertex
v=v(t)
, while
\check{z}_{+}(t)
originates at it. Let
l_{1},\dots,l_{k}
be all the edges of
z_{n}(m)
, which are not present in
\Gamma
, and
t_{1},\dots,t_{k}
be the corresponding tacks on
\widetilde{S}
. Assuming that
l_{j}
precedes
l_{j+1}
for all
1\leqslant j<k
as one follows
z_{n}(m)
, we have
\check{z}_{+}(t_{j})=\check{z}_{-}(t_{j+1})
. We will abuse notation and denote by
z_{n}(m)
(or
z_{n}(c^{\prime})
if
m=m_{c^{\prime}}
for some tacked boundary component
c^{\prime}\in\mathcal{C}(S)
) the union of zig-zags
\check{z}_{-}(t_{1})\cup\check{z}_{+}(t_{1})\cup\dots\cup\check{z}_{+}(t_{k})
on
\Gamma
arising from that zig-zag on
\Gamma_{\circ}
. We will also abbreviate notation
\check{z}_{+}(t)
to
\check{z}(t)
. The thick paths in the left pane of Figure
14
are (the projections of) the two parts of the zig-zag
z_{3}(m)
, where
m
is (a preimage of) the bottom marked point.

Lemma 4.12
.

Let
t
be a tack on the boundary component
c
of
\widetilde{S}
, and
\Gamma\subset\widetilde{S}
be a bipartite graph. Then

(1)

the zig-zag
\check{z}(t)
shares a single black vertex
b_{j}(t)
, a single white vertex
w_{j}(t)
, and a single edge
l_{j}(t)=(b_{j},w_{j})
with each of the zig-zags
z_{j}(c)
;

(2)

at the intersection point of
\check{z}(t)
and
z_{j}(c)
over the edge
l_{j}
, the respective tangent vectors to
z_{j}(c),\check{z}(t)
form a positively-oriented frame.

Proof..

Both statements of the Lemma hold for
\Gamma=\Gamma_{\underline{\Delta}}
and are invariant under shifts
\sigma_{c}
and square moves, as can be seen from Figures
12
and
13
.
∎

Here is a useful consequence of Lemma
4.12
:

Lemma 4.13
.

Suppose that
t,t^{\prime}
are two tacks on boundary components
c,c^{\prime}
of
\widetilde{S}
, and let
l
be an edge of a bicolored graph
\widetilde{\Gamma}\subset\widetilde{S}
such that
l=l_{i}(t)=l_{j}(t^{\prime})
. Then
t=t^{\prime}
and
i=j
.

Proof..

The hypothesis of the Lemma implies
l=\check{z}(t)\cap z_{i}(c)=\check{z}(t^{\prime})\cap z_{j}(c^{\prime})
. Since there are exactly two zig-zags over each edge of
\widetilde{\Gamma}
, we conclude that the set
\{z_{i}(c),z_{j}(c^{\prime}),\check{z}(t),\check{z}(t^{\prime})\}
contains only two distinct elements. Now suppose that
c\neq c^{\prime}
. Then we cannot have
z_{i}(c)=z_{j}(c^{\prime})
or
\check{z}(t)=\check{z}(t^{\prime})
, since elements of the former pair encircle different boundary components, and those of the latter two originate on different boundary components. Hence the only possibility is that
\check{z}(t^{\prime})=z_{i}(c)
and
\check{z}(t)=z_{j}(c^{\prime})
. But this too is ruled out by Lemma
4.12
, which says that pairs
(z_{i}(c),\check{z}(t))
and
(\check{z}(t^{\prime}),z_{j}(c^{\prime}))
have respectively positively- and negatively-oriented frames. Therefore we must have
c=c^{\prime}
, which implies that
i=j
since the zig-zags encircling the same boundary component do not intersect one another, and the Lemma follows.
∎

We now introduce a few more notations that will be frequently used later on. Given a bicolored graph
\Gamma\subset\widetilde{S}
and a tacked boundary component
\tilde{c}\in\mathcal{C}(\widetilde{S})
, let
Z_{i}(\tilde{c})
be the collection of faces of
\Gamma
, which lie between the zig-zags
z_{i-1}(\tilde{c})
and
z_{i}(\tilde{c})
. Whenever it does not cause confusion, we denote the region of
S
covered by the union of all faces in
Z_{i}(\tilde{c})
by the same symbol. Choosing a tack
\tilde{t}\in\tilde{c}
we define
\tilde{f}_{i}^{-}(\tilde{t})\in Z_{i}(\tilde{c})
to be the face incident to the edge
l_{i}(\tilde{t})
, and
\tilde{f}_{i}^{+}(\tilde{t})\in Z_{i}(\tilde{c})
to be the face incident to the edge
l_{i-1}(\tilde{t})
. We then set
f_{i}^{\pm}(c)=\pi(\tilde{f}_{i}^{\pm}(\tilde{t}))
, where
c=\pi(\tilde{c})
, and observe that these faces of
\pi(\Gamma)
depend only on the tacked circle
c\in\mathcal{C}(S)
and not on a particular lift
\tilde{t}
of its tack. Finally, we define
Z_{i}^{\pm}(\tilde{t})
to be the subset of those faces of
Z_{i}(\tilde{c})
which lie to the right of the zig-zag
\check{z}_{\pm}(\tilde{t})
, so that
\tilde{f}_{i}^{+}(\tilde{t})\in F_{i}^{+}(\tilde{t})
and
\tilde{f}_{i}^{\pm}(\tilde{t})\in F_{i}^{-}(\tilde{t})
. In Figure
15
we shade the region
Z_{i}^{+}(\tilde{t})
in green.

\tilde{f}_{i+1}^{+}
\tilde{f}_{i}^{-}
z_{i}
\check{z}(\tilde{t})
\tilde{f}_{i-1}^{-}
\tilde{f}_{i}^{+}
z_{i-1}

Figure 15.
Faces in
Z_{i}^{+}(\tilde{t})
for a bipartite graph. Dashed edges indicate a possibly non-zero number of edges of
\widetilde{\Gamma}
.

4.3.
Quivers from ideal bicolored graphs

To each ideal bipartite graph
\Gamma\subset S
of rank
n
we now associate a quiver
Q_{\Gamma}
with seed

(4.1)

\Theta_{\Gamma}=\left(I_{\Gamma},I^{*}_{\Gamma},\Lambda_{\Gamma},(\cdot,\cdot)_{\Gamma},\left\{e_{\ell}\right\}\right).

The first two ingredients are

I_{\Gamma}=I_{\Gamma_{\circ}}\sqcup I_{\mathcal{C}(S)}\qquad\text{and}\qquad I^{*}_{\Gamma}=I^{*}_{\Gamma_{\circ}}\sqcup I_{\mathcal{C}(S)},

where

I_{\Gamma_{\circ}}=F(\Gamma)
,
I^{*}_{\Gamma_{\circ}}
is the set of proper boundary faces of
\Gamma
, and

I_{\mathcal{C}(S)}=\mathcal{C}(S)\times\left\{1,\dots,n\right\}.

Remark 4.14
.

Slightly more formally, we really think of elements of
I_{\Gamma_{\circ}}
as pairs
(\Gamma,f)
consisting of an ideal graph
\Gamma
on
S
(considered as usual up to isotopy), together with a 1-cycle
\partial f
on
\Gamma
. In particular, given a mapping class
\tau:S\rightarrow S
the label sets
I_{\Gamma}
and
I_{\tau(\Gamma)}
will generally be
different
, in which case the two graphs will correspond to distinct objects in the cluster modular groupoid.

We will define the form
(\cdot,\cdot)_{\Gamma}
by specifying its values of pairs of elements of the basis
\left\{e_{\ell}\right\}
. To that end let us introduce an auxilliary basis
\left\{\dot{e}_{\ell}\right\}
with the same label set
(I_{\Gamma},I_{\Gamma}^{*})
. The graph
\Gamma
inherits the structure of a fat graph from the orientation of
S
, that is each vertex of
\Gamma
is endowed with a cyclic orientation of edges incident to it. This in turn allows one to identify the lattice
\Lambda_{\Gamma_{\circ}}
with the integral first homology of the conjugate surface for
\Gamma_{\circ}
, and take the restriction
(\cdot,\cdot)_{\Gamma_{\circ}}
of the form
(\cdot,\cdot)_{\Gamma}
onto
\Lambda_{\Gamma_{\circ}}
to be the intersection pairing, see
[
GK13
]
. Choosing
\dot{e}_{f}
to be the cycle
\partial f
in
\Lambda_{\Gamma_{\circ}}
for every
f\in I_{\Gamma_{\circ}}
, we can depict the corresponding quiver
\dot{Q}_{\Gamma_{\circ}}
as follows. Place a vertex
v_{f}
inside each proper face
f
of
\Gamma_{\circ}
. Then, for each pair of faces
f_{1},f_{2}
sharing a bicolored or a boundary edge
l
draw respectively a solid or a dashed arrow
a
of
\dot{Q}_{\Gamma_{\circ}}
between
v_{f_{1}}
and
v_{f_{2}}
so that the white vertex of
l
stays on the right as we follow
a
, see Figure
16
. Thus, the pairing
(\cdot,\cdot)_{\Gamma_{\circ}}
admits the following combinatorial description:

(4.2)

(\dot{e}_{f},\dot{e}_{g})_{\Gamma_{\circ}}=s_{f,g}+\frac{1}{2}d_{f,g},

where
s_{f,g}
is the number of solid arrows pointing from
v_{f}
to
v_{g}
minus the number of those pointing from
v_{g}
to
v_{f}
, and
b_{f,g}
is defined similarly, but with solid arrows replaced by the dashed ones. We remark that the quiver
\dot{Q}_{\Gamma_{\circ}}
coincides with those of a cluster chart on the moduli spaces
\mathcal{P}_{PGL_{n},S}
and
\mathcal{A}_{SL_{n},S}
, see
[
FG06b
,
GS19
]
.

A
B
C

Figure 16.
Quiver
\dot{Q}_{\Gamma}
from the bi-colored graph on a triangle.

Let us now describe the form
(\cdot,\cdot)_{\Gamma_{\circ}}
in terms of the graph
\widetilde{\Gamma}=\pi^{-1}(\Gamma)
on the universal cover
\widetilde{S}
of
S
. The graph
\widetilde{\Gamma}
gives rise to a pair of abelian groups

\Lambda_{\widetilde{\Gamma}}=\bigoplus_{\tilde{f}\in F(\widetilde{\Gamma})}\mathbb{Z}\langle e_{\tilde{f}}\rangle\qquad\text{and}\qquad\widehat{\Lambda}_{\widetilde{\Gamma}}=\prod_{\tilde{f}\in F(\widetilde{\Gamma})}\mathbb{Z}\langle e_{\tilde{f}}\rangle

together with a bilinear pairing

(\cdot,\cdot)_{\widetilde{\Gamma}}\colon\widehat{\Lambda}_{\widetilde{\Gamma}}\times\Lambda_{\widetilde{\Gamma}}\to\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{Z},

given by the same formula (
4.2
). Since each face
\tilde{f}\in F(\widetilde{\Gamma})
has only finitely many bicolored or boundary edges, the pairing
(\cdot,\cdot)_{\widetilde{\Gamma}}
is well-defined. Note that the pairing
(\cdot,\cdot)_{\widetilde{\Gamma}}
is
\pi_{1}(S)
-invariant: for all
\tilde{f},\tilde{g}\in F(\widetilde{\Gamma})
and
\gamma\in\pi_{1}(S)
we have

\big(\dot{e}_{\tilde{f}},\dot{e}_{\tilde{g}}\big)_{\widetilde{\Gamma}}=\big(\dot{e}_{\gamma(\tilde{f})},\dot{e}_{\gamma(\tilde{g})}\big)_{\widetilde{\Gamma}}.

Moreover, for any
f,g\in F(\Gamma)
and any choice of lifts
\tilde{f}\in\pi^{-1}(f)
,
\tilde{g}\in\pi^{-1}(g)
we have

(4.3)

(\dot{e}_{f},\dot{e}_{g})_{\Gamma}=\sum_{\tilde{h}\in\pi^{-1}(g)}(\dot{e}_{\tilde{f}},\dot{e}_{\tilde{h}})_{\widetilde{\Gamma}}=\sum_{\tilde{h}\in\pi^{-1}(f)}(\dot{e}_{\tilde{h}},\dot{e}_{\tilde{g}})_{\widetilde{\Gamma}}.

We now specify the value of the form
(\cdot,\cdot)_{\Gamma}
on a pair of vectors in
\left\{\dot{e}_{\ell}\right\}
in the situation when one or both of them is a frozen vector
\dot{e}_{c,i}
associated to a tacked circle
c\in C(S)
. For each lift
\tilde{t}
of the tack on
c
and
1\leqslant i\leqslant n
, define the vector

(4.4)

\dot{e}_{\tilde{t},i}=\sum_{\tilde{f}\in Z_{i}^{+}(\tilde{t})}\tilde{f}\in\widehat{\Lambda}_{\widetilde{\Gamma}}.

The element
\dot{e}_{\tilde{t},i}
defines a linear functional

(\dot{e}_{\tilde{t},i},\cdot)_{\widetilde{\Gamma}}\colon\Lambda_{\widetilde{\Gamma}}\rightarrow\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{Z},

and we have
(\dot{e}_{\tilde{t},i},\dot{e}_{\tilde{f}})_{\widetilde{\Gamma}}=0
unless
\tilde{f}\in\left\{\tilde{f}_{i-1}^{-}(\tilde{t}),\tilde{f}_{i}^{\pm}(\tilde{t}),\tilde{f}_{i+1}^{+}(\tilde{t})\right\}
. Now for any
f\in F(\Gamma)
we declare

(4.5)

\left(\dot{e}_{c,i},\dot{e}_{f}\right)_{\Gamma}=-\left(\dot{e}_{f},\dot{e}_{c,i}\right)_{\Gamma}=\sum_{\tilde{f}\in\pi^{-1}(f)}(\dot{e}_{\tilde{t},i},\dot{e}_{\tilde{f}})_{\widetilde{\Gamma}}.

Formula (
4.5
) can be expressed more explicitly as:

(4.6)

\left(\dot{e}_{c,i},\dot{e}_{f}\right)_{\Gamma}=\delta_{f,f_{i+1}^{+}(c)}-\delta_{f,f_{i}^{+}(c)}-\delta_{f,f_{-}^{-}(c)}+\delta_{f,f_{i-1}^{-}(c)},

or equivalently

\left(\dot{e}_{c,i},\dot{e}_{f}\right)_{\Gamma}=\begin{cases}1&\text{if}\;\;f\in\left\{f_{i+1}^{+},f_{i-1}^{-}\right\},\\
-1&\text{if}\;\;f\in\left\{f_{i}^{+},f_{i}^{-}\right\}\;\;\text{and}\;\;f_{i}^{+}\neq f_{i}^{-},\\
-2&\text{if}\;\;f=f_{i}^{+}=f_{i}^{-},\\
0&\text{otherwise},\end{cases}

where we write
f_{j}^{\pm}
for
f_{j}^{\pm}(c)
.
For a tacked circle
c
and a pair of integers
1\leqslant i,j\leqslant n
, we declare

(4.7)

(\dot{e}_{c,i},\dot{e}_{c,j})_{\Gamma}=\pm\frac{1}{2}\delta_{i\pm 1,j}.

Finally, for distinct tacked circles
c,c^{\prime}\in C(S)
and lifts
\tilde{t},\tilde{t}^{\prime}\in\partial\widetilde{S}
of their tacks we define

(4.8)

(\dot{e}_{c,i},\dot{e}_{c^{\prime},j})_{\Gamma}=\sum_{\tilde{f}\sim Z_{j}^{+}(\tilde{t}^{\prime})}\left(\dot{e}_{\tilde{t},i},\dot{e}_{\tilde{f}}\right)_{\widetilde{\Gamma}},

where the sum is taken over all faces in
\pi^{-1}\big(\pi\big(Z_{j}^{+}(t^{\prime})\big)\big)
. In order to show that the pairing
(\cdot,\cdot)_{\Gamma}
is skew-symmetric we only need the following result.

Lemma 4.15
.

Formula (
4.8
) gives rise to a well-defined skew-symmetric pairing on
\Lambda_{\mathcal{C}(S)}
.

Proof..

For the duration of the proof we abbreviate
Z=Z_{i}^{+}(\tilde{t})
and
Z^{\prime}=Z_{j}^{+}(\tilde{t}^{\prime})
. Let
z_{i-1}
,
z_{i}
,
\check{z}
be the zig-zags bounding
Z
, and
z^{\prime}_{j-1}
,
z^{\prime}_{j}
,
\check{z}^{\prime}
be those bounding
Z^{\prime}
. Write
\check{s}_{i}
for the segment of the zig-zag
\check{z}
between the black vertices
b_{i-1}
,
b_{i}
in the notations of Lemma
4.12
, and define the segment
\check{s}^{\prime}_{j}
in a similar way. Formula (
4.6
) implies that the expression

(4.9)

\sum_{\tilde{f}\in Z^{\prime}}(\dot{e}_{\tilde{t},i},\dot{e}_{\tilde{f}})_{\widetilde{\Gamma}}

vanishes, unless at least one of the edges
l_{i},l_{i-1}
belongs to the boundary of the region
Z^{\prime}
.

Assume first that
l_{i}\subset\check{s}^{\prime}_{j}
. Since each edge belongs to exactly two zig-zags, we have
\check{z}^{\prime}\in\{z_{i},\check{z}\}
. On the other hand,
\check{z}\neq\check{z}^{\prime}
since
c\neq c^{\prime}
, and we conclude that
z_{i}=\check{z}^{\prime}
. Then
\tilde{f}_{i}^{-}\in Z^{\prime}
,
\tilde{f}_{i+1}^{+}\notin Z^{\prime}
, as can be seen from Figure
15
, and the pair of faces contributes
-1
to (
4.9
) by the formula (
4.6
). Note that in this case the edge
l_{i-1}
does not lie on the boundary of
Z^{\prime}
. Indeed, equality
z_{i}=\check{z}^{\prime}
ensures that
l_{i-1}\not\subset\check{z}^{\prime}
, thus if
l_{i-1}
belonged to the said boundary we would have had either
\check{z}\in\{z^{\prime}_{j-1},z^{\prime}_{j}\}
, which is ruled out by the second part of Lemma
4.12
, or
z_{i-1}\in\{z^{\prime}_{j-1},z^{\prime}_{j}\}
, which contradicts Lemma
4.13
. Therefore, the pair
\tilde{f}_{i}^{+},\tilde{f}_{i-1}^{-}
does not contribute to (
4.9
), which takes value
-1
. By a similar argument, condition
l_{i-1}\subset\check{s}^{\prime}_{j}
implies that the sum (
4.9
) takes value
1
.

Now assume that
l_{i}
or
l_{i-1}
is an edge in
z^{\prime}_{j}\cup z^{\prime}_{j-1}
. Recall that neither of the zig-zags
z_{i}
,
z_{i-1}
coincides with
z^{\prime}_{j}
or
z^{\prime}_{j-1}
. Thus we have
\check{z}\in\{z^{\prime}_{j},z_{j-1}^{\prime}\}
, and both edges
l_{i},l_{i-1}
belong to exactly one of the zig-zags
z^{\prime}_{j},z^{\prime}_{j-1}
, which coincides with
\check{z}
. By Lemma
4.12
, we have
\check{z}^{\prime}\notin\left\{z_{i},z_{i-1}\right\}
, so unless the zig-zag
\check{z}^{\prime}
intersects
\check{z}
over an edge within the segment
\check{s}_{i}
, the contribution to (
4.9
) from the pair
\tilde{f}_{i-1}^{-},\tilde{f}_{i}^{+}
cancels that from the pair
\tilde{f}_{i}^{-},\tilde{f}_{i+1}^{+}
. Hence
l^{\prime}_{j}\subset\check{s}_{i}
or
l^{\prime}_{j-1}\subset\check{s}_{i}
. Arguing as before we arrive at

(4.10)

\sum_{\tilde{f}\in Z^{\prime}}(\dot{e}_{Z},\dot{e}_{\tilde{f}})_{\widetilde{\Gamma}}=\begin{cases}1&\text{if}\;\;l_{i-1}\subset\check{s}^{\prime}_{j}\;\;\text{or}\;\;l^{\prime}_{j}\subset\check{s}_{i},\\
-1&\text{if}\;\;l_{i}\subset\check{s}^{\prime}_{j}\;\;\text{or}\;\;l^{\prime}_{j-1}\subset\check{s}_{i},\\
0&\text{otherwise.}\end{cases}

Formula (
4.10
) shows that the pairing
(\cdot,\cdot)_{\Gamma}
on
\Lambda_{\mathcal{C}(S)}
is skew-symmetric if well-defined and it remains to address the latter issue.

As we have just seen, the pairing (
4.9
) vanishes unless
\check{z}_{i}\in\{z^{\prime}_{j-1},z^{\prime}_{j}\}
or
\check{z}^{\prime}_{j}\in\{z_{i-1},z_{i}\}
. A lift of any of the zig-zags
\pi(\check{z}^{\prime})
,
\pi(z^{\prime}_{j-1})
,
\pi(z^{\prime}_{j})
determines that of the region
\pi(Z^{\prime})
, and therefore there exist at most four regions
Z^{\prime}
in the same deck orbit for which (
4.9
) is nonzero. In fact, there are at most two, since the option
\check{z}_{i}=z^{\prime}_{j}
ensures that
\pi(\check{z}_{i})\neq\pi(z^{\prime}_{j-1})
, for otherwise the zig-zags
z^{\prime}_{j}
and
z^{\prime}_{j-1}
would have been deck invariant, and similarly for
\check{z}^{\prime}_{j}
. This finishes the proof of the Lemma.
∎

To finish the construction of the quiver defined by the seed (
4.1
) we now express the basis
\left\{e_{\ell}\right\}
in terms of
\left\{\dot{e}_{\ell}\right\}
. For any
c\in\mathcal{C}(S)
and
1\leqslant i\leqslant n
, we set
e_{c,i}=\dot{e}_{c,i}
. If
f\in F(\Gamma)
is such that there are no faces
\tilde{f}_{1},\tilde{f}_{2}\in\pi^{-1}(f)
sharing a bicolored edge, we define
e_{f}=\dot{e}_{f}
. Now assume that
\tilde{f}_{1}
and
\tilde{f}_{2}
share an edge, and consider
\gamma\in\pi_{1}(S)
such that
\gamma(\tilde{f}_{1})=\tilde{f}_{2}
. Note that the region on
\widetilde{S}
covered by the orbit
\gamma^{n}(\tilde{f}_{1})
,
n\in\mathbb{Z}
is bounded by a pair of zig-zags
z_{i-1}(b)
,
z_{i}(b)
, where
\pi(b)
is either a puncture or a tacked circle on
S
. For
1\leqslant j\leqslant n
, denote by
Z_{j}
the region lying between the zig-zags
z_{j-1}(b),z_{j}(b)
, and by
n_{j}
the number of distinct faces in
\Gamma
arising as projections of those in
Z_{j}
. It is easy to see from the definition of a zig-zag that
n_{j}\leqslant n_{j+1}
for all
j
. Let
k
be the maximal integer for which
n_{k}=1
, and note that
k\geqslant i
. Then for any
1\leqslant j\leqslant k
, there is a face
f_{j}\in F(\Gamma)
, such that
\pi(\tilde{f})=f_{j}
for all
\tilde{f}\in Z_{j}
. Furthermore, there is a unique face
f_{k+1}\in F(\Gamma)
, such that any of its preimages shares bicolored edges with a pair of distinct faces
\tilde{f}_{k},\tilde{f}^{\prime}_{k}\in Z_{k}
. Then we set
e_{f_{j}}=\dot{e}_{f_{j}}+\dots+\dot{e}_{f_{k+1}}
for all
1\leqslant j\leqslant k
, which determines the value of the form
(\cdot,\cdot)_{\Gamma}
on the basis
\left\{e_{\ell}\right\}
and completes the construction of the quiver
Q_{\Gamma}
.

We now enhance the quiver
Q_{\Gamma}
to a compatible pair. Recall that to each boundary arc
a
on
S
, we associated a collection of
n
frozen vectors. If this boundary arc is a tacked circle
c
, we denoted the vectors by
e_{c,i}
, otherwise we labelled the vectors by the boundary faces
f
adjacent to
a
. We will denote faces of the latter kind by
e_{a,i}
, where the index
i
decreases as we traverse
a
in accordance with the orientation induced by that of
S
, so that in
Q_{\underline{\Delta}}
we have

(e_{a,i},e_{a,i+1})=\frac{1}{2}

for any boundary arc
a
and any
1\leqslant i<n
. Now each frozen direction
\ell\in I_{\Gamma}^{*}
has two components: the boundary arc and the index of the frozen variable on it. Given
\ell=(a,i)
we write
\ell[1]=a
and
\ell[2]=i
. Let
\widehat{C}
be the matrix given by

\widehat{C}_{\ell_{1},\ell_{2}}=\begin{cases}\frac{1}{2}\delta_{\ell_{1}[1],\ell_{2}[1]}\mathfrak{A}_{\ell_{1}[2],\ell_{2}[2]},&\text{if}\;\;\ell_{1},\ell_{2}\in I_{\Gamma}^{*},\\
0,&\text{otherwise},\end{cases}

where
\mathfrak{A}
is the Cartan matrix of the group
G=SL_{n+1}
. Note that the matrix

(4.11)

b=\varepsilon+\widehat{C},

has integer entries.

Let
\tilde{t}
be a tack on a lift
\tilde{c}\in\mathcal{C}(\widetilde{S})
of the tacked circle
c\in\mathcal{C}(S)
, and
\tilde{t}^{\prime}
the next tack we encounter on
\tilde{c}
as we traverse it according to its orientation. Denote by
Z_{i}(\tilde{c})
the set of faces lying between the zig-zags
z_{i-1}(\tilde{c})
,
z_{i}(\tilde{c})
,
\check{z}(\tilde{t})
, and
\check{z}(\tilde{t}^{\prime})
. We then set

(4.12)

\alpha_{c,i}=\sum_{\tilde{f}\in Z_{i}(\tilde{c})}\dot{e}_{\pi(f)}

If
\tilde{a}
is a boundary arc incident to and oriented towards a marked point
\tilde{m}
, we denote by
Z_{i}(\tilde{m})
the set of faces lying between the zig-zags
z_{i-1}(\tilde{m})
and
z_{i}(\tilde{m})
. Then we set

(4.13)

\alpha_{a,i}=-\sum_{\tilde{f}\in Z_{i}(\tilde{x})}\dot{e}_{\pi(f)}

Lemma 4.16
.

If
S
has only tacked circles and no punctures, the matrix (
4.11
) defines an ensemble matrix in the sense of Section
3.5
, and thereby enhances the quiver
Q_{\Gamma}
to a compatible pair.

Proof..

First let us show that
b
is invertible over
\mathbb{Q}
.
Let
b_{\circ}
be the submatrix of
b
obtained by crossing out the rows and columns labelled by vectors
e_{c,i}
, for
c
a tacked circle. It was shown in
[
GS19
]
that the
\alpha_{c,i}
form a
\mathbb{Q}
-basis of
\ker(b_{\circ})
. Using (
4.6
) one checks that

(\alpha_{c,i},e_{c,j})=\mathfrak{A}_{ij},

and thus the kernel of
b
is trivial provided
S
has only tacked circles and no punctures.

Now we define
\Xi\subset\Lambda_{\Gamma}\otimes\mathbb{Q}
to be the lattice spanned by

(4.14)

\displaystyle\xi_{j}=\sum_{k\in I}(b^{-1})_{kj}e_{k},\quad j\in I.

Since
b
itself is an integer matrix, we have by definition
\Lambda\subset\Xi
, so it only remains to verify the condition
(e_{i},\xi_{j})=\delta_{ij}
for
i\notin I_{\Gamma}^{*}
. But this follows by evaluating the functional
(e_{i},\cdot)
on both sides of (
4.14
) and using the fact that
\varepsilon_{ik}=b_{ik}
for
i\notin I_{\Gamma}^{*}
.
∎

Convention 4.17
.

Recall that the quantum torus associated to the
\Xi
-lattice in a compatible pair is not in general defined over
\mathbb{Z}[q^{\pm 1}]
, but rather over a ring obtained by adjoining the required fractional powers of
q
. For the quantum tori associated to the
\Xi
-lattice in the compatible pair associated to
(\mathcal{P}^{\diamond}_{SL_{n+1},S},\mathcal{P}^{\diamond}_{G_{n+1},S})
in Lemma
4.16
, we will always assume that the base ring contains
(-q)^{\frac{n}{2}}
. Note that this assumption is void exactly when the element
s_{SL_{n+1}}=(-1)^{n}
in the definition of a twisted local system is the identity.

Remark 4.18
.

Let
\{a_{j}\}_{j\in\mathbb{Z}/k\mathbb{Z}}
be the boundary arcs comprising a connected component
B\in\pi_{0}(\partial S)
of the boundary of
S
and listed in agreement with its orientation. As was shown in
[
GS19
]
, elements of the form

\sum_{j\in\mathbb{Z}/k\mathbb{Z}}^{k}(\alpha_{a_{2j},i}+\alpha_{a_{2j+1},n+1-i})

form a
\mathbb{Q}
-basis of
\ker(\varepsilon)
.

Remark 4.19
.

One easily computes that for
b
a tacked circle or a boundary arc

\alpha_{b,i}=-\sum_{j=1}^{n}\mathfrak{A}_{ij}\xi_{b,j}=\xi_{b,i-1}-2\xi_{b,i}+\xi_{b,i+1},

where
\xi_{0}=\xi_{n+1}=0
, and the ensemble map reads

e_{\ell}=\begin{cases}\sum_{i\in I}\varepsilon_{i\ell}\xi_{i},&\ell\notin I_{\Gamma}^{*},\\
-\frac{1}{2}\alpha_{\ell}+\sum_{i\in I}\varepsilon_{i\ell}\xi_{i},&\ell\in I_{\Gamma}^{*}.\end{cases}

We finish this section by introducing vectors
\bar{e}_{c,i}
which will be convenient for calculations later in the paper. Let
\tilde{t}
be a tack on a lift
\tilde{c}\in\mathcal{C}(\widetilde{S})
of the tacked circle
c\in\mathcal{C}(S)
. Denote by
U_{c,i}
the set of faces lying between the zig-zags
z_{i-1}(\tilde{c})
,
z_{i}(\tilde{c})
,
\check{z}_{+}(\tilde{t})
, and
\check{z}_{-}(\tilde{t})
. We then set

(4.15)

\bar{e}_{c,i}=\sum_{\tilde{f}\in U_{c,i}}\dot{e}_{\pi(\tilde{f})}-e_{c,i}.

For example, in the notations of Figure
21
we have

\displaystyle\bar{e}_{c,1}

\displaystyle=e_{f_{12}}-e_{c,1},

\displaystyle\bar{e}_{c,2}

\displaystyle=e_{f_{13}}+e_{f_{6}}+e_{f_{11}}-e_{c,2},

\displaystyle\bar{e}_{c,3}

\displaystyle=e_{f_{14}}+e_{f_{7}}+e_{f_{2}}+e_{f_{5}}+e_{f_{10}}-e_{c,3}.

It is easy to see that for all
1\leqslant i<n
we have

(\bar{e}_{c,i},\bar{e}_{c,i+1})_{\Gamma}=-\frac{1}{2}.

Notation 4.20
.

From this place onwards unless specified otherwise
c
in the frozen index
(c,i)\in I_{\Gamma}^{*}
is a tacked circle.

4.4.
Depicting quivers from triangulations

Given an ideal triangulation
\underline{\Delta}
of a marked surface
S
, we now describe the quiver
Q_{\underline{\Delta}}=Q_{\Gamma_{\underline{\Delta}}}
in a more pictorial way. An arc of
\underline{\Delta}
is
special
if it precedes a tacked circle as we traverse a special triangle in a positive direction. For example, the right arc connecting the tack with the bottom marked point on Figure
11
is special. Note that any special arc is shared by a special and a non-special triangle and is oriented towards the tack in the former, and away from the tack in the latter.

Figure 17.
Quiver for a triangle with notches.

Inside each non-special triangle
\Delta
we draw the quiver
Q
described in the beginning of this section, see Figure
16
. Now, for each special arc
a
of
\Delta
oriented away from the tack
t
of the tacked circle
c
, we enhance the quiver
Q
with
n
additional frozen vertices
v_{c,j}
and draw dashed arrows
v_{c,j}\dashrightarrow v_{c,j+1}
for all
1\leqslant j<n
. Note that every arrow of the quiver
Q
is parallel to one of the arcs of
\Delta
. Consider the
n
rhombi formed by the arrows of
Q
, which are adjacent to the arc of
\Delta
preceding
a
and whose diagonals are parallel to the arc of
\Delta
following
a
. Let us number these rhombi from 1 to
n
, starting with the one which contains the tack
t
as a vertex. In the left pane of Figure
17
, the edge
AB
is special and is oriented away from the tack
A
, the 3 rhombi are adjacent to the edge
CA
which precedes
AB
, and their diagonals are parallel to the edge
BC
which follows
AB
. We now draw an arrow from each of the vertices of the diagonal of the
j
-th rhombus to
v_{c,j}
, and draw an edge from
v_{c,j}
to each of the remaining vertices of the
j
-th rhombus (note, however, that the first rhombus only contains three vertices of
Q
, since the forth one is the tack
t
). Finally, let us assume that
\Delta
contains special arcs
a,a^{\prime}
, oriented away from the tacks
t,t^{\prime}
of tacked circles
c,c^{\prime}
, and
a^{\prime}
follows
a
as we travers
\Delta
. In that case we draw an additional solid arrow
v_{c,1}\to v_{c^{\prime},n}
, see the right pane of Figure
17
. In what follows, the resulting quiver is denoted by
Q_{\Delta}
.

.

Now, we are ready to describe the quiver
\dot{Q}_{\underline{\Delta}}
. For each pair of non-special triangles
\Delta,\Delta^{\prime}
in
\underline{\Delta}
sharing an arc
a
, we
amalgamate
the quivers
Q_{\Delta}
and
Q_{\Delta^{\prime}}
along
a
, see e.g.
[
FG06a
]
. Namely, we identify the vertices of
\Delta
and
\Delta^{\prime}
which lie on
a
, unfreeze the resulting
n
vertices, and add up arrows between them, which in the case at hand amounts to simply erasing the dashed arrows. Furthermore, if
\Delta
and
\Delta^{\prime}
are two (not necessarily distinct) non-special triangles containing arcs
a,a^{\prime}
of the same special triangle, we amalgamate the quivers
Q_{\Delta}
and
Q_{\Delta^{\prime}}
along the vertices which lie on
a
and
a^{\prime}
. For example, the triangulation on Figure
11
gives rise to the quiver shown on Figure
18
, which is obtained by amalgamating a pair of non-special triangles by both a joint arc and a self-folded triangle.
The quivers
\dot{Q}_{\underline{\Delta}}
have previously appeared in
[
BK23
]
in a similar context of extending the Poisson moduli space
\mathcal{P}_{G,S}
to a symplectic one.

\dot{Q}_{\underline{\Delta}}
(on the left) and
Q_{\underline{\Delta}}
(on the right) for an annulus with one special and one non-special triangle.

Note that unless we have a non-special triangle sharing two edges with a special one, the bases
\left\{e_{i}\right\}
and
\left\{\dot{e}_{i}\right\}
coincide, and hence
Q_{\underline{\Delta}}=\dot{Q}_{\underline{\Delta}}
. Thus, it remains to treat the case of an annulus consisting of one special and one non-special triangle. Denote by
c
the tacked circle of the former, choose a lift
\tilde{t}
of its tack, and let
f_{1}
be the unique face in the projection of the region
Z_{1}(\tilde{t})
. In that case the basis
\left\{e_{i}\right\}
is obtained from
\left\{\dot{e}_{i}\right\}
by replacing the vector
\dot{e}_{f_{1}}
with
e_{f_{1}}=\dot{e}_{f_{1}}+\dot{e}_{f_{2}}
. We show quivers
\dot{Q}_{\underline{\Delta}}
and
Q_{\underline{\Delta}}
on Figure
19
, where the vertices
v_{f_{1}}
,
v_{f_{2}}
are the mutable vertices in the first and second top rows respectively, adjacent to the frozen ones.

We record one more lemma about quivers associated to triangulations.

Lemma 4.21
.

Suppose that
S
is a marked surface without punctures, but possibly with tacked circles, and let
\underline{\Delta}
be a triangulation of
S
. Then the only permutation automorphism of the quiver
Q_{\underline{\Delta}}
which is the identity on all frozen vectors
e_{i}
,
i\in I_{\Gamma}^{*}
is the trivial permutation.

Proof..

Any such automorphism
\sigma
restricts to the subquiver
Q_{\underline{\Delta}_{\circ}}
, obtained from
Q_{\underline{\Delta}}
by erasing all frozen vertices from tacked circles, and we denote its restriction by
\sigma_{\circ}
. Let
\Delta
be a triangle in
\underline{\Delta}_{\circ}
. Then
\sigma_{\circ}
is trivial on the subquiver
Q_{\Delta}
of
Q_{\underline{\Delta}_{\circ}}
if
\Delta
is not self-folded, and may only be permuting a pair of nodes in
Q_{\Delta}
if it is. Arguing by induction on the number of trinagles in
\underline{\Delta}_{\circ}
, we conclude that
\sigma_{\circ}
is a composition of permutations of pairs of nodes in the self-folded triangles of
\underline{\Delta}
. However, the presence of tacked boundary nodes in
Q_{\underline{\Delta}}
guarantees that
\sigma
is isometry if and only if this composition is a trivial permutation. This concludes the proof.
∎

4.5.
Mutations and shifts

Consider an ideal bicolored graph
\Gamma
on
S
and a proper quadrilateral face
f\in F(\Gamma)
.

Proposition 4.22
.

Let
\Gamma^{\prime}
be the bicolored graph obtained from
\Gamma
by performing a square move at the face
f
. Then the following formula defines an isometry of lattices

(4.16)

\hat{\mu}_{f}\colon\Lambda_{\Gamma}\longrightarrow\Lambda_{\Gamma^{\prime}},\qquad e_{\ell}\longmapsto\begin{cases}-e^{\prime}_{\ell}&\text{if}\;\;\ell=f,\\
e^{\prime}_{\ell}&\text{if}\;\;\ell=(c,i)\text{ and }f=f_{i-1}^{-}(\Gamma),\\
e^{\prime}_{\ell}+\varepsilon_{\ell f}e^{\prime}_{f}&\text{if}\;\;\ell=(c,i)\text{ and }f=f_{i}^{+}(\Gamma),\\
e^{\prime}_{\ell}+[\varepsilon_{\ell f}]_{+}e^{\prime}_{f}&\text{otherwise.}\end{cases}

where we use the natural bijection
F(\Gamma)\simeq F(\Gamma^{\prime})
to identify the index sets labelling the bases
\{e_{\ell}\}
in
\Lambda_{\Gamma}
and
\{e^{\prime}_{\ell}\}
in
\Lambda_{\Gamma^{\prime}}
.

Proof..

We need to show that

(4.17)

(e_{\ell_{1}},e_{\ell_{2}})_{\Gamma}=(\hat{\mu}_{f}(e_{\ell_{1}}),\hat{\mu}_{f}(e_{\ell_{2}}))_{\Gamma^{\prime}}

for any pair
\ell_{1},\ell_{2}\in I_{\Gamma}
. When
\ell_{1},\ell_{2}\in F(\Gamma)
the result follows from inspecting Figure
20
, while disregarding the shaded regions. The Figure shows a neighborhood of an admissible face
\tilde{f}\in\pi^{-1}(f)
on trivalent graphs
\widetilde{\Gamma}
,
\widetilde{\Gamma}^{\prime}
, as well as that of the vertex
v_{f}
, which is shaded with pink, of the quivers
Q_{\Gamma}
,
Q_{\Gamma^{\prime}}
. Note that the colors of the vertices of
\widetilde{\Gamma}
which are not incident to the face
\tilde{f}
can be arbitrary, and we only illustrate one of several possible options. The bases
\left\{e^{\prime}_{\ell}\right\}
and
\left\{\dot{e}^{\prime}_{\ell}\right\}
coincide unless a pair of faces neighboring
\tilde{f}
and situated on the same side of either of the two zig-zags have the same projection in
F(\Gamma)
. In the latter case one can easily verify that formula (
4.17
) holds for the basis
\left\{e_{\ell}\right\}
, but not for
\left\{\dot{e}_{\ell}\right\}
.

\mu_{\tilde{f}}

Figure 20.
Square move and quiver mutation.

Now we consider the case
\ell_{1}=e_{c,i}\in I_{\mathcal{C}(S)}
and
\ell_{2}=\ell\in I_{\Gamma_{\circ}}
. First, assume that there is no lift
\tilde{f}\in\pi^{-1}(f)
which is simultaneously incident to one of the zig-zags
z_{i-1}(\tilde{c})
,
z_{i}(\tilde{c})
, and
\check{z}(\tilde{t})
for a lift
\tilde{c}
of
c
and a tack
\tilde{t}
on
\tilde{c}
. In that case the location of the intersection points of the zig-zags
z_{j}(c)
and
\check{z}(\tilde{t})
remain unchanged and thus
(e^{\prime\prime}_{c,i},e^{\prime\prime}_{\ell})=(e_{c,i},e_{\ell})
for all
\ell\in F(\Gamma)
. In particular, by (
4.6
) we have
(e^{\prime\prime}_{c,i},e^{\prime\prime}_{f})=(e_{c,i},e_{f})=0
, which in turn implies that the two sides of (
4.17
) are equal.
Now suppose that
\tilde{f}
is incident to
\check{z}(\tilde{t})
and to exactly one of the zig-zags
z_{i-1}(c)
,
z_{i}(c)
. In this case, thanks to Lemma
4.12
the neighborhood of
\widetilde{f}
corresponds to one of the two sides of Figure
20
. If the red zig-zag in the Figure is
z_{i}(c)
, then the square face
f
on the left of Figure
20
is the special face
f_{i+1}^{+}
, while the square face on the right is
f_{i}^{-}
. Similarly, if the red zig-zag is
z_{i-1}(c)
, then the square face on the left is
f_{i}^{+}
and that on the right
f_{i-1}^{-}
. If the face
\ell
is not a neighbor of
f
, then we have
\varepsilon_{\ell f}=0
and
\hat{\mu}_{f}(e_{\ell})=e^{\prime\prime}_{\ell}
. We also have
(e^{\prime\prime}_{c,i},e^{\prime\prime}_{\ell})=(e_{c,i},e_{\ell})
since the square move does not modify intersections of red and green zig-zags over any edge of
\ell
. Then since
\hat{\mu}_{f}(e_{i,c})
differs from
e^{\prime\prime}_{i,c}
by a multiple of
e^{\prime\prime}_{f}
and
(e^{\prime\prime}_{f},e^{\prime\prime}_{\ell})=0
, we have
(\hat{\mu}_{f}(e_{i,c}),\hat{\mu}_{f}(e_{\ell}))=(e^{\prime\prime}_{i,c},e^{\prime\prime}_{\ell})=(e_{i,c},e_{\ell})
so the equality (
4.17
) holds. It remains to consider the cases in which the face
\ell
coincides with
f
or is one of the faces adjacent to it. These follow from a straightforward inspection of Figure
20
, which we leave to the reader. Thereby we establish the isometry (
4.17
) when exactly one of the arguments corresponds to a proper face.

Finally, the case
\ell_{1},\ell_{2}\in I_{\mathcal{C}(S)}
when
\ell_{1}[1]=\ell_{2}[1]
once again follows from inspecting the Figure
20
, and the remaining case
\ell_{1}[1]\neq\ell_{2}[1]
is a consequence of the formula (
4.8
) and the definition of the basis
\left\{e_{\ell}\right\}
.
∎

In other words, when the graph
\Gamma^{\prime}
is obtained from
\Gamma
by a square move at a special face of the latter of the form
f=f_{i}^{+}(c)
with
i\leq n
or
f=f_{i}^{-}
with
i\leq n-1
, the quiver
Q_{\Gamma^{\prime}}
does not coincide with the mutated quiver
\mu_{f}(Q_{\Gamma})
but instead with the quiver obtained by applying to the latter a quasi-permutation. Indeed, let’s write
\{e_{l}^{\prime\prime}\}
for the basis associated to
\mu_{f}(Q_{\Gamma})
and consider the quiver quasi-permutation

\varsigma_{f}\colon\mu_{f}(Q_{\Gamma})\to Q_{\Gamma^{\prime}}

defined by

(4.18)

\varsigma_{f}(e^{\prime\prime}_{\ell})=\begin{cases}e^{\prime}_{\varphi(\ell)}-e^{\prime}_{\varphi(f)},&\text{if }\ell=(c,i)\text{ and}\;\;f\in\{f_{i}^{+}(\Gamma^{\prime}),f_{i-1}^{-}(\Gamma^{\prime})\},\\
e^{\prime}_{\varphi(\ell)},&\text{otherwise,}\end{cases}

where
\varphi
denotes the natural bijection
I_{\Gamma}\simeq I_{\Gamma^{\prime}}
associated to the square move at
f
.
Then we have

Corollary 4.23
.

The isometry
\hat{\mu}_{f}\colon\Lambda_{\Gamma}\longrightarrow\Lambda_{\Gamma^{\prime}}
factors as

(4.19)

\hat{\mu}_{f}=\varsigma_{f}\circ\mu_{f},

where
\mu_{f}\colon Q_{\Gamma}\to\mu_{f}(Q_{\Gamma})
is the quiver mutation isometry defined by (
3.8
).

Remark 4.24
.

To avoid confusion let us note that in our setup there need not exist a bicolored graph
\Gamma^{\prime\prime}
that
\mu_{f}(Q_{\Gamma})=Q_{\Gamma^{\prime\prime}}
in the notations of Corollary
4.23
.

The following Lemma is straightforward to check using the rule (
3.17
) for mutation of ensemble matrices:

Lemma 4.25
.

The quiver quasi-permutation
\varsigma_{f}
admits the following expression in terms of the bases
\left\{\xi^{\prime}\right\},\left\{\xi^{\prime\prime}\right\}
for
\Xi_{\Gamma^{\prime}}
and
\Xi_{\mu_{f}(Q)}
:

(4.20)

\varsigma_{f}(\xi^{\prime\prime}_{\ell})=\begin{cases}\xi^{\prime}_{\varphi(f)}+\xi^{\prime}_{c,i},&\text{if}\;\;\ell=f\in\{f_{i}^{+}(c,\Gamma^{\prime}),f_{i-1}^{-}(c,\Gamma^{\prime})\}\;\;\text{for some }1\leq i\leq n,\\
\xi^{\prime}_{\varphi(\ell)},&\text{otherwise.}\end{cases}

Let
\Gamma^{\prime}=\sigma_{c}^{\pm 1}(\Gamma)
be the bicolored graph obtained from
\Gamma
by performing a shift
\sigma_{c}^{\pm 1}
at a tacked circle
c
. Using the natural identification
F(\Gamma)\simeq F(\Gamma^{\prime})
we can consider subsets
S_{i}^{\pm}(c)\subset F(\Gamma)
defined as follows:

(4.21)

S_{i}^{+}(c)=\pi(\sigma_{c}(Z_{i}^{+})\smallsetminus Z_{i}^{+})\qquad\text{and}\qquad S_{i}^{-}(c)=\pi(Z_{i}^{+}\smallsetminus\sigma_{c}^{-1}(Z_{i}^{+})),

where
Z_{i}^{+}=Z_{i}^{+}(t)
for some tack
t\in\pi^{-1}(c)
. The next result is an immediate consequence of the definition of vectors
e_{c,i}
and the form
(\cdot,\cdot)_{\Gamma}
.

Lemma 4.26
.

The following formula defines a quiver quasi-permutation
\varsigma_{c}^{\pm 1}\colon\Lambda_{\Gamma}\to\Lambda_{\Gamma^{\prime}}
:

(4.22)

\varsigma_{c}^{\pm 1}(e_{\ell})=\begin{cases}e^{\prime}_{\varphi(\ell)}\mp\sum_{f\in S_{i}^{\pm}(c)}\dot{e}^{\prime}_{\varphi(f)},&\text{if}\;\;\ell=(c,i),\\
e^{\prime}_{\varphi(\ell)},&\text{otherwise,}\end{cases}

where we write
\varphi
for the natural bijection
I_{\Gamma}\simeq I_{\Gamma^{\prime}}
associated to the shift at
c
.

Corollary 4.27
.

At the level of
\xi
-bases, the quiver quasi-permutation
\varsigma_{c}^{\pm 1}
reads

(4.23)

\varsigma_{c}^{\pm 1}(\xi_{\ell})=\begin{cases}\xi^{\prime}_{\varphi(\ell)}\pm\xi^{\prime}_{c,i},&\text{if}\;\;\ell\in S_{i}^{\pm}(c),\\
\xi^{\prime}_{\varphi(\ell)},&\text{otherwise.}\end{cases}

4.6.
Cluster
K_{2}
-coordinates

Let
T^{\prime}S
be the unit tangent bundle to a surface
S
. A
twisted
SL_{n+1}
-local system
on
S
is a local system on
T^{\prime}S
with monodromy
(-1)^{n}
around any loop which rotates a vector
v\in T^{\prime}S
by
2\pi
. A choice of a spin structure on
S
gives rise to an isomorphism between the spaces of local systems and twisted local systems, see
[
FG06b
, Section 2.3]
. A
twisted decorated local system
on a stratified surface
\mathbb{S}
is a twisted
G
-local system on
\mathbb{S}_{G}
, a twisted
T
-local system on
\mathbb{S}_{T}
, a
B
-reduction of the
G\times T
-local system along the walls, and a trivialization at each marked point. Now we describe cluster
K_{2}
-coordinates on the (classical, rather than quantum) moduli space of twisted decorated
SL_{n+1}
-local systems along the same lines as the procedure of
[
FG06b
,
Gon17
]
.

As before, let
\pi\colon\widetilde{S}\to S
be the universal cover of a marked surface
S
, and
\Gamma
an ideal bicolored graph of rank
n
on
S
. For each marked point
m
on
\widetilde{S}
, we define
R_{j}(m)\subset\widetilde{S}
to be the region to the right of the zig-zag
z_{j}(m)
. Now let
t,t^{\prime}
be a pair of tacks on a tacked boundary component
c\subset\partial\widetilde{S}
, so that
t^{\prime}
directly follows
t
as we traverse
c
in the positive direction. In this case we define
R_{j}(t)
to be the unique region bounded by
\check{z}(t)
,
\check{z}(t^{\prime})
, and
z_{j}(c)
. Note that
t
is the unique tack on
\widetilde{S}
contained in
R_{j}(t)
. Given a face
f\in F(\widetilde{\Gamma})
and marked point or tacked circle
x
on
\widetilde{S}
, we define the
codistance

d(f,x)
to be the cardinality of the set
\{1\leqslant j\leqslant n\,|\,f\in R_{j}(x)\}
.
The following result is a trivial modification of a similar statement for
\widetilde{S}_{\circ}
, see
[
Gon17
]
.

Proposition 4.28
.

For any
f\in F(\widetilde{\Gamma})
, we have

\sum_{x}d(f,x)=n+1,

where the sum is taken over all special points
x
in
\widetilde{S}
.

We first construct the cluster
K_{2}
-coordinates in the case that the underlying surface
S
is a disk. We define its ‘lifted boundary” to be the section
\partial S\rightarrow T^{\prime}S
given by taking the outward normal vector
v_{z}
to each point
z\in\partial S
on the boundary of
S
. Given a marked point
x\in\partial S
, let
x^{+}
be the first point of
\mathbb{S}_{B}\cap\partial S
one sees when traversing
\partial S
according to its orientation starting from
x
, and write
\hat{x}=(x^{+},v_{x^{+}})
for the corresponding point on the lifted boundary. As in the discussion from Example
2.9
, from the decoration we produce an
N
-orbit in the fiber
\mathcal{L}_{G;\hat{x}}
of the
G
-local system at
\hat{x}
. Now let
V=\mathbb{C}^{n+1}
be an
n+1
dimensional vector space with a volume form
\Omega\in\det V^{*}
, so that
G\simeq\mathrm{Stab}(\Omega)\subset GL(V)
.
If we choose a trivialization of the fiber
\mathcal{L}_{G;\hat{x}}
, the data of an
N
-orbit in that fiber becomes that of a
framed flag
: a pair
\bar{F}=(F,\nu)
consisting of a complete flag
F=(F_{i}\subset V),~\operatorname{dim}F_{i}=i
in
V
, together with a choice of nonzero vectors
\nu_{i}(\bar{F})\in\det F_{i}
such that
\langle\Omega,\nu_{n+1}(\bar{F})\rangle=1
.

Now given a face
f\in F(\Gamma)
, choose arbitrarily a marked point
x\in\partial S
with
d(f,x)>0
. This choice linearizes the cyclic order on the set of all marked points (induced by the orientation of
\partial S
) into a total order on the set of boundary marked points so that
x=x_{1}
is the minimal element. We write
\{x=x_{1}<x_{2}<\ldots<x_{m}\}
for the set of all marked points with nonzero codistance from
f
, with
\hat{x}_{k}
the corresponding points on the lifted boundary. Let
\hat{y}\in T^{\prime}S
be a point in the unit tangent bundle projecting to an interior point
y\in S
of the face
f
, and whose tangent direction coincides with the outward normal
v_{x^{+}_{1}}
to
\partial S
at
x_{1}
.
Then we can produce a collection of
m
framed flags
\rho_{f}(\bar{F}_{\hat{x}_{i}})
at
\hat{y}
by parallel transporting the
N
-orbits
\bar{F}_{\hat{x}_{i}}
from the fibers over
\hat{x}_{i}
to the fiber over
\hat{y}
along the path in
T^{\prime}S
that rotates the tangent vectors
v_{x^{+}_{i}}
clockwise to
v_{x^{+}_{1}}
.

Then if we choose a trivialization of the fiber over
\hat{y}
and set

(4.24)

\dot{A}_{f}=\left\langle\Omega,\nu_{d_{1}}(\rho_{f}(\bar{F}_{\hat{x}_{1}}))\wedge\dots\wedge\nu_{d_{k}}(\rho_{f}(\bar{F}_{\hat{x}_{k}}))\right\rangle,\qquad d_{j}=d(f,x_{j}),

the resulting function
\dot{A}_{f}
depends on neither the choice of trivialization at
\hat{y}
nor the arbitrary choice of point
x_{1}
, and so gives a well-defined function on the moduli space
\mathcal{P}_{G,S}^{\diamond}
canonically associated to the face
f
. For brevity, we will often abuse notation by making the following abbreviation for this function:

(4.25)

\displaystyle\left\langle\Omega,\nu_{d_{1}}(\rho_{f}(\bar{F}_{\hat{x}_{1}}))\wedge\dots\wedge\nu_{d_{k}}(\rho_{f}(\bar{F}_{\hat{x}_{k}}))\right\rangle\equiv[\nu_{d_{1}}({x_{1}})\wedge\dots\wedge\nu_{d_{k}}({x_{k}})].

For general
S
we reduce to the case above by lifting a twisted decorated local system on
S
to a
\pi_{1}(S)
-equivariant twisted decorated local system on
T^{\prime}\widetilde{S}
. Given a face
f\in F(\Gamma)
, we choose its lift
\tilde{f}\in\pi^{-1}(f)
and any disk
D\subset\widetilde{S}
on the universal cover
\widetilde{S}
, which contains all special points
x
of
\widetilde{S}
such that the codistance
d(\tilde{f},x)
is nonzero. We then set
\dot{A}_{f}=\dot{A}_{\tilde{f}}
, where the latter is defined by the formula (
4.24
) with
\widetilde{S}
replaced by the disk
D
. The function
\dot{A}_{f}
is again well-defined in the sense that it does not depend on the lift
\tilde{f}
of
f
or the choice of the disk
D
; we refer the reader to
[
FG06b
]
for further details.

The functions
A_{f}
are related to the
\dot{A}_{f}
defined above as follows. As before, if
f\in F(\Gamma)
is such that there are no faces
\tilde{f}_{1},\tilde{f}_{2}\in\pi^{-1}(f)
sharing a bicolored edge, we define
A_{f}=\dot{A}_{f}
. Recall that if there exist such
\tilde{f}_{1},\tilde{f}_{2}
sharing an edge, then both faces belong to the region
Z_{j}(b)
, with
1\leqslant j\leqslant n
and
b
a puncture or a tacked circle, which only contains faces in
\pi^{-1}(f)
. Moreover, there exists a maximal number
k
, such that
Z_{i}(b)
only contains preimages of the same face
f_{i}
for all
i\leqslant k
, and there is a unique face
f_{k+1}\in F(\Gamma)
such that each of its preimages shares bicolored edges with a pair of distinct faces
\tilde{f}_{k},\tilde{f}^{\prime}_{k}\in Z_{k}
. Then for all
1\leqslant j\leqslant k+1
we define
A_{f_{j}}
via
\dot{A}_{f_{j}}=A_{f_{j}}\dot{A}_{f_{j-1}}
. In other words, we have

(4.26)

\displaystyle\dot{A}_{f_{j}}=\prod_{s=1}^{j}A_{f_{s}},\qquad A_{f_{j}}=\dot{A}_{f_{j}}/\dot{A}_{f_{j-1}}.

For more detail, see Example
4.30
.

Recall that a tacked circle
c\in\mathcal{C}(S)
represents a boundary component of the decorated surface
\mathbb{S}
, surrounded by a boundary
T
-annulus with a single boundary
T
-marked point at the tack
t
of
c
. Given an ideal graph
\Gamma
on the corresponding marked surface
S
, let us draw the
(G,T)
domain wall on
S
in such a way that it lies in a small neighborhood of
c
and does not intersect (projections of) any zig-zags except those emanating from or terminating at
t
. Consider the point
p_{t}
at which the domain wall meets (the projection of) the zig-zag
\check{z}(t)
. The
T
-local system at this point has a canonical trivialization, given by transporting the one at
t
along
\check{z}(t)
. Further choosing a trivialization of the
G
-local system at
p_{t}
, we can identify its monodromy around the domain wall with an element
g_{t}
of
G
, and the
B
-reduction of the
G\times T
-local system is identified with a framed flag
\bar{F}=(F,\nu)
. Then for each
1\leqslant i\leqslant n
, we define a coordinate
A_{c,i}
via

(4.27)

\displaystyle\nu_{i}(g_{t}\bar{F})=A_{c,n+1-i}^{-1}\nu_{i}(\bar{F}),

and note that
A_{c,i}
is independent of the choice of
G
-trivialization at
p_{t}
. Since
g_{t}(F)=F
, the flag
F
induces an order on the eigenvalues
\left\{\lambda_{i}\right\}
of the holonomy
g_{t}
around the loop encircling
c
by requiring that

(4.28)

\displaystyle A_{c,i}=\lambda_{1}\cdots\lambda_{i}.

g^{-1}\bar{F}_{2}

\bar{F}_{2}

Example 4.29
.

Figure
21
depicts the universal cover
\widetilde{\Gamma}
of a bicolored graph shown in the left pane of Figure
14
with the quiver
Q_{\Gamma}
appearing in Figure
18
. Framed flags at the tacks and marked points are labelled by
\bar{F}_{j}h^{n}
and
g^{n}\bar{F}_{j}
, where
\bar{F}_{j}\in G/N
and
(g,h)
is the monodromy of the
G\times T
local system around the tacked circle. Then the cluster
K_{2}
-coordinates are the functions, for example,

A_{f_{11}}=\langle\Omega,\nu_{2}(\bar{F}_{0})\wedge\nu_{1}(\bar{F}_{2})\wedge\nu_{1}(\bar{F}_{1})\rangle

and

\displaystyle A_{f_{14}}

\displaystyle=\langle\Omega,\nu_{1}(\bar{F}_{0})\wedge\nu_{1}(\bar{F}_{1})\wedge\nu_{2}(g^{-1}\bar{F}_{2})\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle\Omega,\nu_{1}(g\bar{F}_{0})\wedge\nu_{1}(g\bar{F}_{1})\wedge\nu_{2}(\bar{F}_{2})\rangle

\displaystyle=\lambda_{4}\langle\Omega,\nu_{1}(\bar{F}_{0})\wedge\nu_{1}(g\bar{F}_{1})\wedge\nu_{2}(\bar{F}_{2})\rangle,

where
\lambda_{4}
is the ‘last’ eigenvalue of the monodromy
g
.

Example 4.30
.

The following example illustrates how the relation (
4.28
) between the frames
\{\dot{A}_{\ell}\}
and
\{A_{\ell}\}
reconciles the discrepancy between the Plücker relations for face functions
\dot{A}_{\ell}
and the combinatorial cluster mutation formula for the
\mathcal{A}
-variables
A_{\ell}
.

For
G=SL_{2}
, consider the cluster coordinate systems associated to the two triangulations shown in Figure

which are related by the flip at the edge labelled 2 in the Figure.

In Figure
23
, we show their unrollings to the universal cover. In terms of the shorthand notation (
4.25
), the cluster coordinates associated to the edges of the triangulation in the left pane of Figure
22
are

\displaystyle A_{1}=[\nu_{1}(t_{1})\wedge\nu_{1}(b_{1})],\quad A_{2}=[\nu_{1}(b_{0})\wedge\nu_{1}(c_{0})],

\displaystyle A_{3}=[\nu_{1}(t_{0})\wedge\nu_{1}(b_{0})],\quad A_{4}=[\nu_{1}(t_{0})\wedge\nu_{1}(c_{0})],

and following (
4.27
) the frozen coordinate
A_{c}
is defined by

\nu_{1}(c_{0})=A_{c}\nu_{1}(c_{1}).

For the triangulation in the right pane, there is a nontrivial relation between the
\dot{A}_{\ell}
and
A_{\ell}
frames owing to the fact that each face on the universal cover projecting to face
4
shares a pair of bicolored edges with faces projecting to
2
(and vice-versa).
Indeed, since

[\nu_{1}(t_{1})\wedge\nu_{1}(c_{0})]=A_{c}[\nu_{1}(t_{1})\wedge\nu_{1}(c_{1})]=A_{c}A_{4},

the 3-term Plücker relation

[\nu_{1}(t_{0})\wedge\nu_{1}(t_{1})][\nu_{1}(b_{0})\wedge\nu_{1}(c_{0})]=[\nu_{1}(t_{0})\wedge\nu_{1}(b_{1})][\nu_{1}(t_{1})\wedge\nu_{1}(c_{0})]+[\nu_{1}(t_{0})\wedge\nu_{1}(c_{0})][\nu_{1}(b_{0})\wedge\nu_{1}(t_{1})]

for face functions reads

A_{2}\dot{A}^{\prime}_{2}=A_{4}(A_{1}+A_{3}A_{c}).

So in accordance with the recipe (
4.26
), it is not
\dot{A}^{\prime}_{2}
but rather the function

A_{2}^{\prime}=\dot{A}^{\prime}_{2}/A_{4}=\frac{[\nu_{1}(t_{0})\wedge\nu_{1}(t_{1})]}{[\nu_{1}(t_{0})\wedge\nu_{1}(c_{0})]}

that satisfies the combinatorial exchange relation

A_{2}A_{2}^{\prime}=A_{1}+A_{3}A_{c}

dictated by the quiver in Figure
22
.

Figure 24.
Mutation at vertex 3 in a self-folded triangle for

.

Now let us give an example for
G=SL_{3}
, in which we begin with a single self-folded triangle and perform a square move at the face labelled 3, see Figure
24
. In the self-folded triangulation we have

\dot{A}_{1}=A_{1}=[\nu_{1}(b_{1})\wedge\nu_{2}(c_{1})],\quad\dot{A}_{2}=[\nu_{1}(b_{1})\wedge\nu_{1}(b_{0})\wedge\nu_{1}(c_{1})],\quad A_{2}=\dot{A}_{2}/A_{1},

and

A_{3}=\dot{A}_{3}=[\nu_{1}(c_{1})\wedge\nu_{2}(b_{1})].

The square move at face 3 corresponds to a Plücker relation

A_{3}\dot{A}^{\prime}_{3}=\dot{A}_{2}(A_{c,2}A_{5}+A_{4}),

and so the variable satisfying the cluster exchange relation is

A_{3}^{\prime}=\dot{A}^{\prime}_{3}/\dot{A}^{\prime}_{2}.

In the final cluster, we have

\displaystyle\dot{A}_{1}^{\prime}

\displaystyle=[\nu_{1}(b_{1})\wedge\nu_{2}(c_{1})]

\displaystyle\dot{A}_{2}^{\prime}

\displaystyle=[\nu_{1}(b_{2})\wedge\nu_{1}(b_{1})\wedge\nu_{1}(c_{2})]

(4.29)

\displaystyle\dot{A}_{3}^{\prime}

\displaystyle=[\nu_{1}(b_{3})\wedge\nu_{1}(b_{2})\wedge\nu_{1}(b_{1})].

The next two results describe the transformation of the coordinates
A_{f}
under square moves and shifts at tacked circles. Just as in the case without tacked circles, the new coordinates are related to the old ones by quasi-cluster transformations. Let us illustrate precisely what we mean by this when performing a square move at face
f
of
\Gamma
to produce a new graph
\Gamma^{\prime}
. In this case we have two collections of functions
\{A_{\ell}(\Gamma)\},~\{A^{\prime}_{\ell}=A_{\ell}(\Gamma^{\prime})\}
on the moduli space
\mathcal{P}^{\diamond}_{SL_{n+1},S}
, obtained respectively from the combinatorial data of the graphs
\Gamma,\Gamma^{\prime}
. If we identify the
\{A_{\ell}(\Gamma)\}
with the generators of the coordinate ring
\mathcal{T}^{\mathcal{A}}_{Q_{\Gamma}}
of the
\mathcal{A}
-torus for
Q_{\Gamma}
and the
\{A_{\ell}(\Gamma^{\prime})\}
with the corresponding generators of the coordinate ring
\mathcal{T}^{\mathcal{A}}_{Q_{\Gamma^{\prime}}}
, a quasicluster transformation (
3.20
) from
Q_{\Gamma}
to
Q_{\Gamma}^{\prime}
gives a map
\mathbb{C}(\{A_{\ell}(\Gamma)\})\rightarrow\mathbb{C}(\{A_{\ell}(\Gamma^{\prime})\})
, i.e. a birational transformation of the moduli space
\mathcal{P}^{\diamond}_{SL_{n+1},S}
. The assertion of the next Proposition is that there exists a quasicluster transformation such that the corresponding birational map is the identity. In other words, the functions on the moduli space associated to
\{A_{\ell}(\Gamma)\},~\{A^{\prime}_{\ell}=A_{\ell}(\Gamma^{\prime})\}
to the two graphs satisfy the algebraic relations dictated by a certain quasicluster transformation.

Proposition 4.31
.

Let
\Gamma^{\prime}
be the bicolored graph obtained from
\Gamma
by performing a square move at the face
f
, and let
Q_{\Gamma},Q_{\Gamma^{\prime}}
be the corresponding quivers. Identify the functions
\{A_{\ell}(\Gamma)\}
with the generators
\{A_{\ell}\}
of
\mathcal{T}^{\mathcal{A}}_{Q_{\Gamma}}
, and the
\{A_{\ell}(\Gamma^{\prime})\}
with the generators
\{A_{\ell}^{\prime}\}
of
\mathcal{T}^{\mathcal{A}}_{Q_{\Gamma}}
. Write
\{A_{\ell}^{\prime\prime}\}
for the cluster
K_{2}
coordinate frame associated to the mutated quiver
\mu_{f}(Q_{\Gamma})
. Then as functions on
\mathcal{P}^{\diamond}_{SL_{n+1},S}
, we have

A_{f}(\Gamma)=(\varsigma_{f}\circ\mu_{f})(A_{f}(\Gamma)),

where
\mu_{f}\colon\mathcal{T}^{\mathcal{A}}_{Q_{\Gamma}}\rightarrow\mathcal{T}^{\mathcal{A}}_{\mu_{f}(Q_{\Gamma})}
is the classical
K_{2}
-mutation (
3.20
), and
\varsigma_{f}\colon\mathcal{T}^{\mathcal{A}}_{\mu_{f}(Q_{\Gamma})}\rightarrow\mathcal{T}^{\mathcal{A}}_{Q_{\Gamma^{\prime}}}
is the quasi-permutation on
\mathcal{A}
-tori induced by (
4.20
):

(4.30)

\varsigma_{f}(A^{\prime\prime}_{\ell})=\begin{cases}A_{f}(\Gamma^{\prime})A_{c,i}(\Gamma^{\prime}),&\text{if}\;\;\ell=f\in\{f_{i}^{+}(c,\Gamma^{\prime}),f_{i-1}^{-}(c,\Gamma^{\prime})\}\;\;\text{for some }1\leq i\leq n,\\
A_{\ell}(\Gamma^{\prime}),&\text{otherwise}.\end{cases}

We have a similar result in the case of shifting a tack:

Proposition 4.32
.

Let
\Gamma^{\prime}=\sigma_{c}^{\pm 1}(\Gamma)
be the bicolored graph obtained from
\Gamma
by performing a shift
\sigma_{c}^{\pm 1}
at a tacked circle
c
. Then we have

A_{\ell}(\Gamma)=\varsigma_{c}^{\pm 1}(A_{\ell}(\Gamma)),

where
\varsigma_{c}
is the quasi-permutation on
\mathcal{A}
-tori induced by (
4.23
).

Proposition
4.31
follows from the classical 3-term Plücker relation in exactly the same way as the corresponding result in
[
FG06b
]
(see also Lemma 3.12 in
[
Gon17
]
), while Proposition
4.32
is immediate from the definition of the functions
A_{\ell}
.

This completes the construction of the cluster ensemble associated to the moduli spaces
(\mathcal{P}^{\diamond}_{SL_{n+1},S},~\mathcal{P}^{\diamond}_{PGL_{n+1},S})
and the verification of its compatibility with the coordinate systems described at the beginning of this Section.
We mention one Corollary that we will use later:

Corollary 4.33
.

Let
\Gamma
be an ideal bicolored graph on a marked surface
S
, and
\boldsymbol{\mu}_{1},\boldsymbol{\mu}_{2}
be compositions of square moves and shifts at tacked circles, such that
\boldsymbol{\mu}_{1}(\Gamma)=\Gamma^{\prime}=\boldsymbol{\mu}_{2}(\Gamma)
and the composite bijections
\varphi_{1},\varphi_{2}:I_{\Gamma}\rightarrow I_{\Gamma^{\prime}}
are identical. Then we have an equality
\boldsymbol{\mu}_{1}^{q}=\boldsymbol{\mu}_{2}^{q}
of the corresponding quantum quasi-cluster transformations.

Proof..

Since the functions
A_{\ell}
associated to each face are completely determined by the bicolored graph, and moreover the identification between face sets of
\Gamma,\Gamma^{\prime}
induced by
\boldsymbol{\mu}_{1},\boldsymbol{\mu}_{2}
coincide, we have that
A_{\ell}(\boldsymbol{\mu}_{1}(\Gamma))=A_{\ell}(\boldsymbol{\mu}_{2}(\Gamma))
for all
\ell\in I_{\Gamma}
. Thus the classical quasi-cluster transformations
\boldsymbol{\mu}_{1},\boldsymbol{\mu}_{2}
are identical, which by Theorem
3.10
implies that of the quantum quasi-cluster transformations.
∎

Remark 4.34
.

It is crucial here that we consider the bicolored graphs
\Gamma\hookrightarrow S
up to isotopy and not diffeomorphism/combinatorial equivalence. If we replace the hypothesis that
\boldsymbol{\mu}_{1}(\Gamma)
and
\boldsymbol{\mu}_{2}(\Gamma)
be conjugate under an isotopy by the weaker hypothesis that they are conjugate under a diffeomorphism of
S
, the conclusion of Corollary
4.33
is false.

4.7.
Birational action of the braid group

Suppose that
S
is not a surface of genus
g
with a single tacked circle and no other special points. Then associated to each tacked circle
c
on
S
there is an action of the braid group
B_{n+1}
by Poisson birational transformations on the moduli space
\mathcal{P}^{\diamond}_{SL_{n+1},S}
defined as follows. Consider the open locus in
\mathcal{P}^{\diamond}_{SL_{n+1},S}
consisting of twisted decorated local systems
(\mathcal{L},\beta)
with regular semisimple monodromy
g
around
c
. Let us trivialize the fiber
(V,\Omega)
of the associated rank
n
vector bundle with volume form over the tack by choosing a unimodular basis
(u_{i})
of eigenvectors for
g
:

gu_{i}=\lambda_{n+2-i}u_{i},\quad 1\leq i\leq n+1,

such that the framed flag
\nu
at the tack is given by

\nu=(\nu_{r})_{r=1}^{n+1}\colon\nu_{r}=u_{1}\wedge u_{2}\wedge\cdots\wedge u_{n+1},

with
\langle\nu_{n+1},\Omega\rangle=1
.

Now we define an action of the generator
\sigma_{i}\in B_{n+1}
on this open locus in
\mathcal{P}^{\diamond}_{SL_{n+1},S}
which modifies only the framed flag
\nu
, replacing it by

\sigma_{i}(\nu)=(\nu_{i})_{r=1}^{n+1}\colon\nu_{r}=\sigma_{i}(u_{1})\wedge\sigma_{i}(u_{2})\wedge\cdots\wedge\sigma_{i}(u_{r})

where

(4.31)

\displaystyle\sigma_{i}({u_{j}})=\begin{cases}u_{j+1}(1-\lambda_{i+1}/\lambda_{i})\quad&i+j=n+1\\
-u_{j-1}(1-\lambda_{i+1}/\lambda_{i})^{-1}\quad&i+j=n+2\\
u_{j}\quad&\text{else.}\end{cases}

Lemma 4.35
.

Suppose that
S
is not a surface of genus
g
with a single tacked circle. Then formula (
4.31
) defines an action of the braid group by Poisson birational automorphisms of
\mathcal{P}^{\diamond}_{SL_{n+1},S}
, which factors through the action of the cluster modular group. The projection
\mathcal{P}^{\diamond}_{SL_{n+1},S}\rightarrow\mathcal{P}_{PGL_{n+1},S}
is
B_{n+1}
-equivariant, where the
B_{n+1}
acts on the base factors through the quotient to the Weyl group
S_{n+1}
.

Proof..

It is straightforward to check that the birational transformations (
4.31
) satisfy the braid relations, and clear from the definition that the projection to
\mathcal{P}_{PGL_{n+1},S}
is equivariant. We postpone the proof that the braid group acts by cluster automorphisms (and hence is Poisson) until the introduction of isolating coordinate systems for tacked circles in Definition
5.8
of Section
5
.
∎

4.8.
Functor from Ptolemy to cluster groupoid

Now we can construct the functor

(4.32)

\mathcal{Q}\colon\widehat{\operatorname{Pt}}(S)\longrightarrow\operatorname{Cl}_{\boldsymbol{Q}}.

On the level of objects, for a triangulation
\underline{\Delta}
we set

\mathcal{Q}(\underline{\Delta})=Q_{\underline{\Delta}},

where
Q_{\underline{\Delta}}
is the quiver for the bipartite graph
\Gamma_{\underline{\Delta}}
associated to
\underline{\Delta}
, defined in Section
4.3
.

Recall that arrows in
\widehat{\operatorname{Pt}}(S)
are formal composites
A_{\gamma}\circ F_{\underline{\Delta}_{1},\underline{\Delta}_{2}}
, where
\gamma\in\Gamma_{S}
and
\underline{\Delta}_{1},\underline{\Delta}_{2}
are triangulations, while the morphisms in
\operatorname{Cl}_{\boldsymbol{Q}}
are quasi-cluster transformations. The quasi-cluster transformation assigned to the arrow
F_{\underline{\Delta}_{1},\underline{\Delta}_{2}}
labelled by a pair
(\underline{\Delta}_{1},\underline{\Delta}_{2})
is defined as follows.

First suppose that
\underline{\Delta}_{1}
is obtained from
\underline{\Delta}_{2}
by flipping a single diagonal
d
, and let us abbreviate the the corresponding arrow as

F_{d}:=F_{\underline{\Delta}_{1},\underline{\Delta}_{2}}.

Since our triangulations are defined up to isotopy, the diagonal
d
is uniquely defined. Let us present a quasi-cluster quiver transformation bringing
Q_{\underline{\Delta}_{2}}
to
Q_{\underline{\Delta}_{1}}
by transforming the bicolored graph
\Gamma_{\underline{\Delta}_{2}}
into
\Gamma_{\underline{\Delta}_{1}}
via a sequence of square moves and shifts at tacked circles.

If the diagonal
d
is shared by two non-special triangles, number the vertices of the subquiver corresponding to the quadriateral as shown on Figure
25
. Consider for each
1\leqslant k\leqslant n
the composition

(4.33)

\mu_{d;k}=\prod_{(i,j)\in R(k)}\hat{\mu}_{(i,j)}^{q},

where
\hat{\mu}^{q}_{(i,j)}
is the quasi-cluster transformation corresponding to the quiver transformation (
4.19
), and the set
R(k)
consists of pairs
(i,j)
, such that

\displaystyle i-j

\displaystyle\in\left\{1-k,3-k,\dots,k-1\right\},

\displaystyle i+j

\displaystyle\in\left\{k+1,k+3,\dots,2n+1-k\right\}.

Note that all
k(n-k)
transformations
\hat{\mu}^{q}_{(i,j)}
in (
4.33
) commute, and therefore
\mu_{d;k}
is well-defined. Moreover, each corresponds to performing a square move at the corresponding face of the bicolored graph, and the graph
\mu_{d;n}\dots\mu_{d;1}(\Gamma_{\underline{\Delta}_{2}})
coincides with
\Gamma_{\underline{\Delta}_{1}}
.
Hence we get a quasi-cluster transformation

\mu_{d}=\mu_{d;n}\dots\mu_{d;1}\colon\mathcal{T}^{\mathcal{A}}_{\underline{\Delta}_{2}}\dashrightarrow\mathcal{T}^{\mathcal{A}}_{\underline{\Delta}_{1}},

and we define

\mathcal{Q}(F_{d})=\mu_{d}.

The transformation
\mu_{d}
is a minor generalization of the one described in
[
FG06b
]
. The latter can be recovered from
\mu_{d}
by replacing each factor
\hat{\mu}^{q}_{(i,j)}
with
\mu^{q}_{(i,j)}
.
