A flip.

On the other hand, suppose
c
is a tacked circle contained in the special triangle
\Delta_{c}
, and
d_{\pm}
be the remaining two sides of
\Delta_{c}
, so that
d_{+}
follows
d_{-}
as we go around
c
in the positive direction. To the flips
F_{d_{\pm}}
we
associate the quasi-permutations

\mu_{d_{\pm}}=\varsigma_{c}^{\pm 1}\colon\mathcal{T}_{\underline{\Delta}}^{q}\longrightarrow\mathcal{T}_{F_{d_{\pm}}(\underline{\Delta})}^{q}

defined by the quiver quasi-permutation (
4.23
).
This completes the definition of
\mathcal{Q}(F_{\underline{\Delta}_{1},\underline{\Delta}_{2}})
for two triangulations related by a single flip.

Now given a general pair
\underline{\Delta}_{1},\underline{\Delta}_{2}
, choose any sequence
\boldsymbol{d}=(d_{1},\dots d_{k})
of flips such that
\underline{\Delta}_{1}=F_{d_{k}}\cdots F_{d_{1}}(\underline{\Delta}_{2})
. Given any two such sequences
\boldsymbol{d},\boldsymbol{d}^{\prime}
, both cluster transformations

\mathcal{Q}(F_{\boldsymbol{d}})=\mathcal{Q}(F_{d_{k}})\circ\cdots\circ\mathcal{Q}(F_{d_{1}}),\quad\mathcal{Q}(F_{\boldsymbol{d}^{\prime}})=\mathcal{Q}(F_{d^{\prime}_{k}})\circ\cdots\circ\mathcal{Q}(F_{d^{\prime}_{1}})

arise from composites of square moves and shifts which transform
\Gamma_{\underline{\Delta}_{2}}
into
\Gamma_{\underline{\Delta}_{1}}
. Moreover, by Lemma
4.21
the bijections on quiver label sets
I_{\underline{\Delta}_{1}}\rightarrow I_{\underline{\Delta}_{2}}
induced by both cluster transformations must be identical. Hence we can apply Corollary
4.33
to conclude that the cluster transformations
\mathcal{Q}(F_{\boldsymbol{d}}),\mathcal{Q}(F_{\boldsymbol{d}^{\prime}})
are equal. This proves that the assignment

\mathcal{Q}(F_{\underline{\Delta}_{1},\underline{\Delta}_{2}})=\mathcal{Q}(F_{d_{k}})\circ\cdots\circ\mathcal{Q}(F_{d_{1}})

is well-defined and functorial.

Now given a general morphism
A_{\gamma}F_{\underline{\Delta}_{1},\underline{\Delta}_{2}}
, we define

(4.34)

\mathcal{Q}(A_{\gamma}F_{\underline{\Delta}_{1},\underline{\Delta}_{2}})=\varsigma_{\gamma}\circ\mathcal{Q}(F_{\underline{\Delta}_{1},\underline{\Delta}_{2}}),

where
\varsigma_{\gamma}
is the quasi-permutation morphism corresponding to the identification of graphs

\gamma:\Gamma_{\underline{\Delta}_{1}}\simeq\Gamma_{\gamma(\underline{\Delta}_{1})}

induced by the diffeomorphism
\gamma
. This definition evidently respects the multiplication rule (
2.1
), and thus we obtain a functor as promised.

In particular, if we fix a quiver
Q
then by
4.21
we get a homomorphism

\Gamma_{S}\rightarrow\operatorname{Aut}_{\operatorname{Cl}_{\boldsymbol{Q}}}(Q)

from the mapping class group of
S
to the automorphism group of the object
Q
in the cluster modular groupoid.

Example 4.36
.

Let
S
be a torus with a single tacked circle
c
, so that
\Gamma_{S}\simeq B_{3}
is the universal central extension of
SL(2,\mathbb{Z})
. We compute the corresponding representation associated to the group
G=SL_{2}
.

We indicate the induced bijection
\varphi:I_{\Gamma_{\underline{\Delta}}}\rightarrow I_{\Gamma_{\underline{\Delta}^{\prime}}}
by again labelling the proper faces of
\Gamma_{\underline{\Delta}^{\prime}}
by the same alphabet
\{1,2,3\}
. Then in accordance with Lemma
4.22
, the corresponding classical cluster transformation reads

\displaystyle\varsigma_{c}^{+}\colon Y_{(c,1)}\mapsto Y^{\prime}_{(c,1)}(Y_{2}^{\prime})^{-1},\quad Y_{i}\mapsto Y_{i}^{\prime},\quad i=1,2,3.

But now observe that the element
\rho
of the mapping class group of
S
induced by the 120 degree counterclockwise rotation satisfies
\rho(\underline{\Delta}^{\prime})=\underline{\Delta}
and thus returns us to the original bicolored graph. Hence it induces a quasi-permutation morphism

\varsigma_{\rho}\colon Y_{1}^{\prime}\mapsto Y_{3},\quad Y_{3}^{\prime}\mapsto Y_{2},\quad Y_{2}^{\prime}\mapsto Y_{1},\quad Y^{\prime}_{c,1}\mapsto Y_{c,1}

So the birational automorphism of
\mathcal{T}_{Q(\underline{\Delta})}
by which the mapping class
\rho
acts is

\rho_{Q_{\underline{\Delta}}}=\varsigma_{\rho}\circ\varsigma_{c}^{+},

\rho_{Q_{\underline{\Delta}}}(Y_{1})=Y_{3},\quad\rho_{Q_{\underline{\Delta}}}(Y_{2})=Y_{1},\quad\rho_{Q_{\underline{\Delta}}}(Y_{3})=Y_{2},\quad\rho_{Q_{\underline{\Delta}}}(Y_{(c,1)})=\frac{Y_{(c,1)}}{Y_{1}}.

Note that we have

\rho^{3}_{Q_{\underline{\Delta}}}(Y_{(c,1)})=\frac{Y_{(c,1)}}{Y_{1}Y_{2}Y_{3}}=\frac{Y_{(c,1)}}{A_{(c,1)}},\quad\rho^{3}_{Q_{\underline{\Delta}}}(Y_{i})=Y_{i},\quad i=1,2,3.

On the other hand, consider the triangulation
\underline{\Delta}^{\prime\prime}
obtained from the initial one
\underline{\Delta}
by flipping the diagonal dual to the face of
\Gamma
numbered 3 in Figure
26
, which amounts to performing the square move at the face
f=f_{2}^{+}(\Gamma_{\underline{\Delta}})
. We illustrate the graph
\Gamma_{\underline{\Delta}^{\prime\prime}}
in Figure
29
where we again indicate the induced bijection
\varphi:I_{\Gamma_{\underline{\Delta}}}\rightarrow I_{\Gamma_{\underline{\Delta}^{\prime\prime}}}
by labelling the proper faces of
\Gamma_{\underline{\Delta}^{\prime\prime}}
by the same alphabet
\{1,2,3\}
.

Black-white graph
\Gamma_{\underline{\Delta}^{\prime\prime}}
.

The corresponding cluster transformation
\hat{\mu}_{f}
reads

\hat{\mu}_{f}\colon Y_{1}\mapsto Y^{\prime}_{1}/(1+1/Y_{3}^{\prime})^{2},\quad Y_{2}\mapsto Y^{\prime}_{2}(1+Y_{3}^{\prime})^{2},\quad Y_{3}\mapsto 1/Y^{\prime}_{3},\quad Y_{(c,1)}\mapsto Y_{(c,1)}^{\prime}/(1+1/Y_{3}^{\prime}).

This time we observe that the element
\tau
of the mapping class group given by the Dehn twist along the
(0,1)
-curve on the torus satisfies
\tau(\underline{\Delta}^{\prime\prime})=\underline{\Delta}
, and so induces a quasi-permutation morphism

\varsigma_{\tau}\colon Y_{2}^{\prime}\mapsto Y_{3},\quad Y_{3}^{\prime}\mapsto Y_{2},\quad Y_{1}^{\prime}\mapsto Y_{1},\quad Y^{\prime}_{(c,1)}\mapsto Y_{(c,1)}.

So the birational automorphism of
\mathcal{T}_{Q(\underline{\Delta})}
by which the mapping class
\tau
acts is

\tau_{Q_{\underline{\Delta}}}=\varsigma_{\tau}\circ\hat{\mu}_{f},\qquad

Y_{1}\mapsto Y_{1}/(1+1/Y_{2})^{2},\quad Y_{2}\mapsto Y_{3}(1+Y_{2})^{2},\quad Y_{3}\mapsto 1/Y_{2},\quad Y_{(c,1)}\mapsto Y_{(c,1)}/(1+1/Y_{2})

The element

S=\rho_{Q_{\underline{\Delta}}}\circ\tau_{Q_{\underline{\Delta}}}^{-1}

satisfies

S^{2}=\rho_{Q_{\underline{\Delta}}}^{3},

so these birational transformations indeed give a representation of the braid group
B_{3}
.

5.
Special cluster charts for isolating cylinders

We will construct the algebra isomorphism
\eta_{c}
in Theorem
Main Theorem
using special cluster coordinates on the moduli spaces
\mathcal{P}_{G,S}
and
\mathcal{P}^{\diamond}_{G,S^{\prime}}
(for
G
of type
A_{n}
) which are adapted to a
c
-isolating triangulation
\underline{\Delta}
and its image
\underline{\Delta}^{\prime}
under the cutting functor. When
n>1
, these special charts differ from those associated to the triangulations
\underline{\Delta},\underline{\Delta}^{\prime}
, but this difference is concentrated only at the cluster coordinates inside the isolating cylinders. The main advantage of the charts introduced here is that they allow for a “separation of variables”, simplifying to the extent possible the action of the Dehn twist
\tau_{c}
by cluster transformations.

5.1.
Cyclic Weyl words and quiver mutations

We start by recalling a dictionary between (cyclic) double Weyl words and their transformations on the one hand and certain classes of quivers and quiver mutations on the other. We refer the reader to
[
BFZ05
,
FG06a
]
for further details and relation to the cluster coordinate charts on the double Bruhat cells. Our exposition follows that in
[
SS17
]
.

Consider two alphabets
\mathfrak{A}_{\pm}=\left\{\pm 1,\dots,\pm n\right\}
and the union
\mathfrak{A}=\mathfrak{A}_{+}\sqcup\mathfrak{A}_{-}
. We introduce the notation

(5.1)

\overline{a}=a\mp(n+1)\qquad\text{for}\qquad a\in\mathfrak{A}_{\pm},

and note that
\overline{\mathfrak{A}}_{\pm}=\mathfrak{A}_{\mp}
. A
double Weyl word
is an element of the free monoid generated by
\mathfrak{A}
modulo the
shuffle relations

(5.2)

\dots,a,b,\ldots=\dots,b,a,\dots\qquad\text{unless}\qquad a\in\left\{\pm b,b\pm 1\right\}.

Here and in what follows we separate letters of a double Weyl word by commas. A
cyclic double Weyl word
is a double Weyl word considered up to a cyclic shift of its letters. We will make use of the following transformations on (cyclic) double Weyl words: the
braid move

(5.3)

\dots,a,b,a,\ldots\longleftrightarrow\dots,b,a,b,\dots\qquad\text{for}\qquad a=b\pm 1,

the
shuffle

(5.4)

\dots,a,-a,\ldots\longleftrightarrow\dots,-a,a,\dots

and the
merge

(5.5)

\dots,a,a,\ldots\longrightarrow\dots,a,\dots

The
length

l(\mathbf{i})
of a (cyclic) double Weyl word
\mathbf{i}
is the number of its letters. A (cyclic) word is
(cyclically) irreducible
if its length cannot be reduced by applying relations (
5.2
) and transformations (
5.3
) – (
5.5
).

We now construct a quiver for each double Weyl word. Consider
n
horizontal lines numbered 1 to
n
. For each letter
a
in the alphabet
\mathfrak{A}
define an elementary quiver
Q_{a}
as shown on Figure
30
(omitting all vertices on lines labelled 0 and
n+1
along with all adjacent arrows, so that the quivers
Q_{\pm 1}
and
Q_{\pm n}
have 3 vertices each). Given a word
\mathbf{i}
, we read it left to right and draw quivers
Q_{a}
for each
a\in\mathfrak{A}
. We then amalgamate adjacent vertices in each of the rows if they belong to different elementary quivers and denote the result by
Q_{\mathbf{i}}
. It is easy to see that the quiver
Q_{\mathbf{i}}
is invariant under applying relation (
5.2
) to the sequence of letters of
\mathbf{i}
, and is hence well-defined.

Q_{i}=
i-1
i
i+1
Q_{-i}=

1\leqslant i\leqslant n
, there is at least two letters
a,b
in

. We construct a quiver for

in a similar way to the one for the non-cyclic words, except now we replace the

n
circles on a cylinder. Equivalently, we first construct a quiver for any non-cyclic representative of
\mathbf{i}
and then amalgamate the left-most and the right-most vertex on each line. For example, the mutable subquiver in the left pane of Figure

is the quiver
Q_{\mathbf{i}}
, where
\mathbf{i}=(\mathbf{i}_{-},\mathbf{i}_{+})
is a cyclic word and

(5.6)

(5.7)

\displaystyle\mathbf{i}_{-}

\displaystyle=(\overline{4},\overline{3},\overline{2},\overline{1},\overline{4},\overline{3},\overline{2},\overline{4},\overline{3},\overline{4}).

), (

) now correspond to quiver mutations, see Figures

and

where mutation is performed at the pink vertex. Similarly, the transformation
5.5
corresponds to the quiver mutation on Figure

after we have erased the pink vertex in the quiver on the right.

5.2.
Reduction of cyclic Weyl words

In this subsection we describe certain reductions of cyclic Weyl words, which were explained to us by M. Gekhtman. In what follows these reductions yield sequences of quiver mutations which we use over and over again.

Given a letter
a\in\mathfrak{A}
, let introduce a notation

a^{*}=-\overline{a},

and let

-\mathbf{i}=(-i_{1},\dots,-i_{p}),\qquad\overline{\mathbf{i}}=(\overline{i}_{1},\dots,\overline{i}_{p}),\qquad\mathbf{i}^{*}=(i_{1}^{*},\dots,i_{p}^{*}),\qquad\mathbf{i}^{t}=(i_{p},\dots,i_{1})

for any word Weyl word
\mathbf{i}=(i_{1},\dots,i_{p})
. We also define

\mathbf{i}_{[i,j]}=\begin{cases}(i,i+1,\dots,j)&\text{if}\quad 0<i\leqslant j,\\
(i,i-1,\dots,j)&\text{if}\quad 0<j\leqslant i,\\
0&\text{otherwise.}\end{cases}

so that, for example,
\overline{\mathbf{i}}_{[i,j]}=\mathbf{i}_{[\overline{j},\overline{i}]}
. Now, let us consider words

\mathbf{i}_{c}=\mathbf{i}_{[1,n]},\qquad\mathbf{i}_{w_{0}}=\left(\mathbf{i}_{[n,1]},\mathbf{i}_{[n,2]},\dots,\mathbf{i}_{[n,n]}\right),

where notations
c
and
w_{0}
stand respectively for a Coxeter and the longest word in the Weyl group of
SL_{n+1}
. For
n=4
, the words
\mathbf{i}_{w_{0}}
and
\overline{\mathbf{i}}_{w_{0}}
are given by formulas (
5.6
) and (
5.7
) respectively. Given a collection
u_{1},\dots,u_{k}
of symbols in
\left\{c,w\right\}
we set

(5.8)

\mathbf{i}_{u_{1},\dots,u_{k}}=(\mathbf{i}_{u_{1}},\dots,\mathbf{i}_{u_{k}})\qquad\text{and}\qquad\mathbf{i}_{-u}=-\mathbf{i}_{u},\qquad\mathbf{i}_{\overline{u}}=\overline{\mathbf{i}}_{u},\qquad\mathbf{i}_{u^{*}}=\mathbf{i}_{u}^{*}.

In particular, in our notations

\mathbf{i}_{\overline{w}_{0},c^{*},c}=\left(\overline{\mathbf{i}}_{w_{0}},\mathbf{i}_{c}^{*},\mathbf{i}_{c}\right),

and the following equalities hold:

\mathbf{i}^{t}_{w_{0}}=\mathbf{i}_{w_{0}},\qquad\mathbf{i}^{t}_{c}=\mathbf{i}_{c}^{*}.

We also observe that the word
\mathbf{i}_{-c,c}
is
Coxeter,
that is it contains every letter from
\mathfrak{A}
exactly once. It is easy to see that every Coxeter word is cyclically irreducible, and that every two cyclic Coxeter words are related via shuffles and cyclic shifts. In the remainder of this subsection we describe three cyclic Weyl word reductions

(5.9)

\displaystyle\Phi_{1}\colon\mathbf{i}_{\overline{w}_{0},w_{0}}\longmapsto\mathbf{i}_{\overline{w}_{0},c},

(5.10)

\displaystyle\Phi_{2}\colon\mathbf{i}_{\overline{w}_{0},c}\longmapsto\mathbf{i}_{-c,c},

(5.11)

\displaystyle\Phi_{3}\colon\mathbf{i}_{\overline{w}_{0},c^{*},c}\longmapsto\mathbf{i}_{\overline{w}_{0},c^{*}}.

5.2.1.
Word transformation
\Phi_{1}

First, set

\mathbf{i}_{\overline{w}_{0},w_{0}}^{(j,k)}=\left(\overline{\mathbf{i}}_{w_{0}},\mathbf{i}_{[1,j]},\mathbf{i}_{[n-k,j+1]},\mathbf{i}_{[n,j+2]},\dots,\mathbf{i}_{[n,n]}\right),

so that

\mathbf{i}_{\overline{w}_{0},w_{0}}^{(0,0)}=\mathbf{i}_{\overline{w}_{0},w_{0}},\qquad\mathbf{i}_{\overline{w}_{0},w_{0}}^{(j,n-j-1)}=\mathbf{i}_{\overline{w}_{0},w_{0}}^{(j+1,0)},\qquad\mathbf{i}_{\overline{w}_{0},w_{0}}^{(n-1,0)}=\mathbf{i}_{\overline{w}_{0},c}.

We factor the transformation
\Phi_{1}
into a composite of
n-1
“waves”,

(5.12)

\Phi_{1}=\Phi_{1}^{(n-1)}\dots\Phi_{1}^{(1)}\qquad\Phi_{1}^{(j)}=\Phi_{1}^{(j,n-j)}\dots\Phi_{1}^{(j,1)},

so that

\Phi_{1}^{(j+1,k+1)}\colon\mathbf{i}_{\overline{w}_{0},w_{0}}^{(j,k)}\longmapsto\mathbf{i}_{\overline{w}_{0},w_{0}}^{(j,k+1)},

and refer to
\Phi_{1}^{(j)}
as the
j
-th “wave” of
\Phi_{1}
, and to
\Phi_{1}^{(j,k)}
as the
k
-th step of the
j
-th wave.

Let us now describe the transformation
\Phi_{1}^{(j+1,k+1)}
. Using that

\mathbf{i}_{\overline{w}_{0},w_{0}}^{(j,k)}=\left(\overline{\mathbf{i}}_{w_{0}},\mathbf{i}_{[1,j]},n-k,\mathbf{i}_{[n-k-1,j+1]},\mathbf{i}_{[n,j+2]},\dots,\mathbf{i}_{[n,n]}\right),

we first commute the letter
n-k
all the way to the left through the word
\overline{\mathbf{i}}_{w_{0}}
, which consists of applying equivalences
5.2
together with exactly
k+1
shuffles
(k-n,n-k)\mapsto(n-k,k-n)
. Then, we perform the cyclic shift that erases the letter
n-k
on the very left and writes it on the far right, to obtain

(5.13)

\left(\overline{\mathbf{i}}_{w_{0}},\mathbf{i}_{[1,j]},\mathbf{i}_{[n-k-1,j+1]},\mathbf{i}_{[n,j+2]},\dots,\mathbf{i}_{[n,n]},n-k\right).

The part of the latter word which reads
\left(\mathbf{i}_{[n,j+2]},\dots,\mathbf{i}_{[n,n]},n-k\right)
can be rewritten as follows using only (
5.2
):

(5.14)

\left(\mathbf{i}_{[n,j+2]},\dots,\mathbf{i}_{[n,n+1-k]},\mathbf{i}_{[n,n+2-k]},\dots,\mathbf{i}_{[n,n]},n-k\right)=\\
\left(\mathbf{i}_{[n,j+2]},\dots,\mathbf{i}_{[n,n+1-k]},n-k,\mathbf{i}_{[n,n+2-k]},\dots,\mathbf{i}_{[n,n]}\right).

We shall now restrict our attention to the subword

(5.15)

\left(\mathbf{i}_{[n,n-k]},\mathbf{i}_{[n,n+1-k]},n-k\right).

Using (
5.2
) again we rewrite it as

\left(\mathbf{i}_{[n,n+1-k]},\mathbf{i}_{[n,n+2-k]},n-k,n+1-k,n-k\right),

and then apply a braid move to get

\left(\mathbf{i}_{[n,n+1-k]},\mathbf{i}_{[n,n+2-k]},n+1-k,n-k,n+1-k\right).

The latter is a particular case of the word

\left(\mathbf{i}_{[n,s]},\mathbf{i}_{[n,s+1]},s,\mathbf{i}_{[s-1,t-1]},\mathbf{i}_{[s,t]}\right)

for
s=t=n+1-k
. We can once again rewrite it as

\left(\mathbf{i}_{[n,s+1]},\mathbf{i}_{[n,s+2]},s,s+1,s,\mathbf{i}_{[s-1,t-1]},\mathbf{i}_{[s,t]}\right)

and use a braid move to get

\left(\mathbf{i}_{[n,s+1]},\mathbf{i}_{[n,s+2]},s+1,s,s+1,\mathbf{i}_{[s-1,t-1]},\mathbf{i}_{[s,t]}\right)=\left(\mathbf{i}_{[n,s+1]},\mathbf{i}_{[n,s+2]},s+1,\mathbf{i}_{[s,t-1]},\mathbf{i}_{[s+1,t]}\right).

Proceeding by induction on
s
we arrive at

\left(n,n-1,n,n-1,\mathbf{i}_{[n-2,t-1]},\mathbf{i}_{[n-1,t]}\right),

use a braid move once again, to get

\left(n,n,n-1,n,\mathbf{i}_{[n-2,t-1]},\mathbf{i}_{[n-1,t]}\right)=\left(n,n,\mathbf{i}_{[n-1,t-1]},\mathbf{i}_{[n,t]}\right),

and finally, apply the merge
(n,n)\mapsto n
to obtain

\left(n,\mathbf{i}_{[n-1,t-1]},\mathbf{i}_{[n,t]}\right)=\left(\mathbf{i}_{[n,t-1]},\mathbf{i}_{[n,t]}\right).

Recall that
t=n+1-k
and thus the word (
5.15
) has been reduced to
\left(\mathbf{i}_{[n,n-k]},\mathbf{i}_{[n,n+1-k]}\right)
. Re-inserting it into the word (
5.14
) and then (
5.13
) we arrive at

\mathbf{i}_{\overline{w}_{0},w_{0}}^{(j,k+1)}=\left(\overline{\mathbf{i}}_{w_{0}},\mathbf{i}_{[1,j]},\mathbf{i}_{[n-k-1,j+1]},\mathbf{i}_{[n,j+2]},\dots,\mathbf{i}_{[n,n]}\right).

To summarize, the transformation
\Phi_{1}^{(j+1,k+1)}
consists of

•

k+1
shuffles
(k-n,n-k)\mapsto(n-k,k-n)
;

•

k
braid moves
((s+1)^{*},s^{*},(s+1)^{*})\mapsto(s^{*},(s+1)^{*},s^{*})
for
s=k,\dots,2,1
;

•

a merge
(n,n)\mapsto n
.

Example 5.1
.

We spell out the transformation
\Phi_{1}
in case
n=4
. Below we write
\mathbf{i}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle t}}{{\mapsto}}\mathbf{i}^{\prime}
if, up to equivalences (
5.2
), the transformation from
\mathbf{i}
to
\mathbf{i}^{\prime}
is
t
, where
t=b
denotes a braid move (
5.3
),
t=s^{k}
stands for
k
shuffles (
5.4
), and
t=m
indicates a merge (
5.5
). Then we have

\displaystyle\Phi_{1}^{(1,1)}\colon\quad\mathbf{i}_{\overline{w}_{0},w_{0}}^{(0,0)}

\displaystyle=(\overline{\mathbf{i}}_{w_{0}},4,3,2,1,4,3,2,4,3,4)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle s}}{{\longmapsto}}(4,\overline{\mathbf{i}}_{w_{0}},3,2,1,4,3,2,4,3,4)

\displaystyle=(\overline{\mathbf{i}}_{w_{0}},3,2,1,4,3,2,4,3,4,4)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle m}}{{\longmapsto}}(\overline{\mathbf{i}}_{w_{0}},3,2,1,4,3,2,4,3,4)=\mathbf{i}_{\overline{w}_{0},w_{0}}^{(0,1)},

\displaystyle\Phi_{1}^{(1,2)}\colon\quad\mathbf{i}_{\overline{w}_{0},w_{0}}^{(0,1)}

\displaystyle=(\overline{\mathbf{i}}_{w_{0}},3,2,1,4,3,2,4,3,4)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle s^{2}}}{{\longmapsto}}(3,\overline{\mathbf{i}}_{w_{0}},2,1,4,3,2,4,3,4)

\displaystyle=(\overline{\mathbf{i}}_{w_{0}},2,1,4,3,2,4,3,4,3)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle b}}{{\longmapsto}}(\overline{\mathbf{i}}_{w_{0}},2,1,4,3,2,4,4,3,4)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle m}}{{\longmapsto}}(\overline{\mathbf{i}}_{w_{0}},2,1,4,3,2,4,3,4)=\mathbf{i}_{\overline{w}_{0},w_{0}}^{(0,2)},

\displaystyle\Phi_{1}^{(1,3)}\colon\quad\mathbf{i}_{\overline{w}_{0},w_{0}}^{(0,2)}

\displaystyle=(\overline{\mathbf{i}}_{w_{0}},2,1,4,3,2,4,3,4)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle s^{3}}}{{\longmapsto}}(2,\overline{\mathbf{i}}_{w_{0}},1,4,3,2,4,3,4)

\displaystyle=(\overline{\mathbf{i}}_{w_{0}},1,4,3,2,4,3,4,2)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle b}}{{\longmapsto}}(\overline{\mathbf{i}}_{w_{0}},1,4,3,4,3,2,3,4)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle b}}{{\longmapsto}}(\overline{\mathbf{i}}_{w_{0}},1,4,4,3,4,2,3,4)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle m}}{{\longmapsto}}(\overline{\mathbf{i}}_{w_{0}},1,4,3,2,4,3,4)=\mathbf{i}_{\overline{w}_{0},w_{0}}^{(0,3)}=\mathbf{i}_{\overline{w}_{0},w_{0}}^{(1,0)}.

The second wave
\Phi_{1}^{(2)}
reads

\displaystyle\Phi_{1}^{(2,1)}\colon\quad\mathbf{i}_{\overline{w}_{0},w_{0}}^{(1,0)}

\displaystyle=(\overline{\mathbf{i}}_{w_{0}},1,4,3,2,4,3,4)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle s}}{{\longmapsto}}(4,\overline{\mathbf{i}}_{w_{0}},1,3,2,4,3,4)

\displaystyle=(\overline{\mathbf{i}}_{w_{0}},1,3,2,4,3,4,4)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle m}}{{\longmapsto}}(\overline{\mathbf{i}}_{w_{0}},1,3,2,4,3,4)=\mathbf{i}_{\overline{w}_{0},w_{0}}^{(1,1)},

\displaystyle\Phi_{1}^{(2,2)}\colon\quad\mathbf{i}_{\overline{w}_{0},w_{0}}^{(1,1)}

\displaystyle=(\overline{\mathbf{i}}_{w_{0}},1,3,2,4,3,4)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle s^{2}}}{{\longmapsto}}(3,\overline{\mathbf{i}}_{w_{0}},1,2,4,3,4)

\displaystyle=(\overline{\mathbf{i}}_{w_{0}},1,2,4,3,4,3)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle b}}{{\longmapsto}}(\overline{\mathbf{i}}_{w_{0}},1,2,4,4,3,4)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle m}}{{\longmapsto}}(\overline{\mathbf{i}}_{w_{0}},1,2,4,3,4)=\mathbf{i}_{\overline{w}_{0},w_{0}}^{(1,2)}=\mathbf{i}_{\overline{w}_{0},w_{0}}^{(2,0)}.

And finally, the wave
\Phi_{1}^{(3)}
becomes

\displaystyle\Phi_{1}^{(3,1)}\colon\quad\mathbf{i}_{\overline{w}_{0},w_{0}}^{(2,0)}

\displaystyle=(\overline{\mathbf{i}}_{w_{0}},1,2,4,3,4)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle s}}{{\longmapsto}}(4,\overline{\mathbf{i}}_{w_{0}},1,2,3,4)

\displaystyle=(\overline{\mathbf{i}}_{w_{0}},1,2,3,4,4)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle m}}{{\longmapsto}}(\overline{\mathbf{i}}_{w_{0}},1,2,3,4)=\mathbf{i}_{\overline{w}_{0},w_{0}}^{(2,1)}=\mathbf{i}_{\overline{w}_{0},w_{0}}^{(3,0)}.

5.2.2.
Word transformation
\Phi_{2}

For
0\leqslant j\leqslant n-1
and
0\leqslant k\leqslant n-1-j
we now set

\mathbf{i}_{\overline{w}_{0},c}^{(j,k)}=\left(\overline{\mathbf{i}}_{[n,n]},\ldots,\overline{\mathbf{i}}_{[j+2,n]},\overline{\mathbf{i}}_{[j+1,n-k]},\overline{\mathbf{i}}_{[j,1]},\mathbf{i}_{c}\right),

so that
\mathbf{i}_{\overline{w}_{0},c}^{(j,n-1-j)}=\mathbf{i}_{\overline{w}_{0},c}^{(j+1,0)}
and
\mathbf{i}_{\overline{w}_{0},c}^{(n-1,0)}=\mathbf{i}_{-c,c}
. We then factor

(5.16)

\Phi_{2}=\Phi_{2}^{(n-1)}\dots\Phi_{2}^{(1)},\qquad\Phi_{2}^{(j)}=\Phi_{2}^{(j,n-j)}\dots\Phi_{2}^{(j,1)},

so that

\Phi_{2}^{(j+1,k+1)}\colon\mathbf{i}_{\overline{w}_{0},c}^{(j,k)}\longmapsto\mathbf{i}_{\overline{w}_{0},c}^{(j,k+1)}.

The steps of
\Phi_{2}
are similar to those of
\Phi_{1}
, namely, the transformation
\Phi_{2}^{(j+1,k+1)}
consists of

•

a shuffle
(-k-1,k+1)\mapsto(k+1,-k-1)
;

•

k
braid moves
(-s-1,-s,-s-1)\mapsto(-s,-s-1,-s)
for
s=k,\dots,2,1
;

•

a merge
(-1,-1)\mapsto-1
.

5.2.3.
Word transformation
\Phi_{3}

Let us set

\mathbf{i}_{\overline{w}_{0},c^{*},c}^{(j,k)}=\left(\overline{\mathbf{i}}_{w_{0}},\mathbf{i}_{c}^{*},\mathbf{i}_{[1,n-j-1]},n-j+k\right),

so that
\mathbf{i}_{\overline{w}_{0},c^{*},c}^{(0,0)}=\mathbf{i}_{\overline{w}_{0},c^{*},c}
and
\mathbf{i}_{\overline{w}_{0},c^{*},c}^{(n,0)}=\mathbf{i}_{\overline{w}_{0},c^{*}}
, and factor

(5.17)

\Phi_{3}=\Phi_{3}^{(n)}\dots\Phi_{3}^{(1)},\qquad\Phi_{3}^{(j)}=\Phi_{3}^{(j,j)}\dots\Phi_{3}^{(j,1)}.

The transformation
\Phi_{3}^{(j+1,k+1)}
reads as follows. Setting
s=n-j+k
, we first apply
n+1-s
shuffles to move the letter
s
all the way to the right through
\overline{\mathbf{i}}_{w_{0}}
. Then, if
k\neq j
we apply the braid move
(s,s+1,s)\mapsto(s+1,s,s+1)
and arrive at
\mathbf{i}_{\overline{w}_{0},c^{*},c}^{(j,k+1)}
, whereas if
k=j
we merge
(n,n)\mapsto n
and arrive at
\mathbf{i}_{\overline{w}_{0},c^{*},c}^{(j+1,0)}
.

Example 5.2
.

Let us spell out the transformation
\Phi_{3}
for
n=2
. In that case we have

\Phi_{3}^{(1,1)}\colon\quad\mathbf{i}_{\overline{w}_{0},c^{*},c}^{(0,0)}=(\overline{\mathbf{i}}_{w_{0}},2,1,1,2)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle s}}{{\longmapsto}}(\overline{\mathbf{i}}_{w_{0}},2,2,1,1)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle m}}{{\longmapsto}}(\overline{\mathbf{i}}_{w_{0}},2,1,1)=\mathbf{i}_{\overline{w}_{0},c^{*},c}^{(1,0)},

and the second wave
\Phi_{3}^{(2)}
reads

\displaystyle\Phi_{3}^{(2,1)}\colon\quad\mathbf{i}_{\overline{w}_{0},c^{*},c}^{(1,0)}

\displaystyle=(\overline{\mathbf{i}}_{w_{0}},2,1,1)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle s^{2}}}{{\longmapsto}}(\overline{\mathbf{i}}_{w_{0}},1,2,1)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle b}}{{\longmapsto}}(\overline{\mathbf{i}}_{w_{0}},2,1,2)=\mathbf{i}_{\overline{w}_{0},c^{*},c}^{(1,1)},

\displaystyle\Phi_{3}^{(2,2)}\colon\quad\mathbf{i}_{\overline{w}_{0},c^{*},c}^{(1,1)}

\displaystyle=(\overline{\mathbf{i}}_{w_{0}},2,1,2)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle s}}{{\longmapsto}}(\overline{\mathbf{i}}_{w_{0}},2,2,1)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle m}}{{\longmapsto}}(\overline{\mathbf{i}}_{w_{0}},2,1)=\mathbf{i}_{\overline{w}_{0},c^{*},c}^{(2,0)}=\mathbf{i}_{\overline{w}_{0},c^{*}}.

5.3.
Quiver transformations

We now use the dictionary from Section
5.1
between cyclic double Weyl words and quivers on a cylinder to turn reductions (
5.9
) – (
5.11
) of the former into transformations of the latter, which we denote by the same symbols.

We write
Q_{\mathbf{i}}
for the quiver defined by the cyclic Weyl word
\mathbf{i}
. If
\mathbf{i}=\mathbf{i}_{u_{1},\dots,u_{k}}
in the sense of notation (
5.8
), we denote the corresponding quiver by
Q_{u_{1},\dots,u_{k}}^{n}
, where
n
stands for the number of letters in the alphabet
\mathfrak{A}_{+}
and will be omitted when is clear from the context. For example, the unfrozen part of the quiver in the left pane of Figure
34
is
Q_{\overline{w}_{0},w_{0}}^{4}
. Note that the quivers
Q_{-\mathbf{i}^{t}}
,
Q_{\mathbf{i}^{*}}
,
Q_{\overline{\mathbf{i}}^{t}}
are all isomorphic to
Q_{\mathbf{i}}
, as they can be obtained from the latter by reflecting it respectively across the vertical axes, across the horizontal axes, and across the origin.

5.3.1.
Quiver transformation
\Phi_{1}

Consider the quiver
Q_{\mathrm{cyl}}^{n}\simeq Q_{\underline{\Delta}}
for a triangulation
\underline{\Delta}
of a cylinder with a single marked point on each boundary component, see the left side of Figure
7
. Let us further fix an oriented simple closed curve
c
on the cylinder generating its fundamental group.

We arrange the nodes of
Q_{\mathrm{cyl}}^{n}
into a square grid with
n+2
rows and
n+1
columns and label the nodes by their coordinates, so the vector pointing in the first coordinate direction corresponds to the chosen orientation of
c
, and two coordinate directions form a positive frame on
S
. The setup is illustrated in the left pane of Figure
34
for
Q_{\mathrm{cyl}}^{4}
. Note that the mutable part of
Q_{\mathrm{cyl}}^{n}
is
Q_{\overline{w}_{0},w_{0}}^{n}
.
We define the quiver transformation
\Phi_{1}
via factorization (
5.12
) into individual steps. After each step of
\Phi_{1}
we will rearrange the nodes and relabel them according to their new position, thereby establishing the bijection between the indexing sets of the two quivers. To indicate the embedding of the quiver as a planar graph on the cylinder, we draw with bent lines arrows going around its back-side. Then the quiver transformation
\Phi_{1}^{(j,k+1)}
reads as follows:

•

mutate at nodes
(x,n-k)
as
x
runs from
n+1
to
n+1-k
;

•

mutate at nodes
(n-k,y)
as
y
runs from
n-k
to
n
.

The dependence on the number
j
of the wave appears in the following repositioning and relabelling of the nodes:

•

move the node
(n-k,y)
to position
(n-k,y+1)
for all
y\geqslant n-k
;

•

move the node
(x,n-k)
to position
(x-1,n-k)
for all
n+1-k\leqslant x\leqslant n+1
;

•

move the node
(j,n-k)
to position
(n+1,n-k)
;

•

rename nodes according to their new position.

In particular, in the process of applying the wave
\Phi_{1}^{(j)}
we are losing nodes positioned at
(j,y)
as
y
runs from
n
down to
j+1
, and acquiring those positioned at
(x,n+j+1)
as
x
runs from
n
to
j+1
.

Example 5.3
.

The step
\Phi_{1}^{(1,3)}
consists of consecutive mutations at the shaded nodes on the right pane of Figure
34
, starting with
(5,2)
and ending at
(2,4)
. We then move nodes
(2,y)
upward by 1 for all
2\leqslant y\leqslant 5
, move nodes
(x,2)
left by 1 for all
3\leqslant x\leqslant 5
, move the node
(1,2)
to position
(5,2)
, and finish by relabeling nodes according to their new position.

We denote by
Q_{\mathrm{cone}}^{n}
the result of applying
\Phi_{1}
to (the mutable part of) the quiver
Q_{\mathrm{cyl}}^{n}
. Figure
35
shows
Q_{\mathrm{cone}}^{4}
, where positions of vertices have been changed without relabelling. Following the quiver through the steps of the transformation
\Phi_{1}
, one can see that the bottom
n
mutable rows of
Q_{\mathrm{cone}}^{n}
form a subquiver
Q_{\overline{w}_{0},c}^{n}
, while the top
n-2
mutable rows of
Q_{\mathrm{cone}}^{n}
form
Q_{\overline{c},w_{0}}^{n-2}
.

5.3.2.
Quiver transformation
\Phi_{2}

Let us reposition the nodes of the subquiver, formed by the bottom
n+1
rows of
Q_{\mathrm{cone}}^{n}
. Move the node
(n+1,y)
to position
(y,y)
for all
1\leqslant y\leqslant n
, the node
(x,y)
to position
(x+1,y)
if
0\leqslant y\leqslant x\leqslant n
, and relabel all nodes according to their new positions. The resulting subquiver
Q_{\mathrm{frust}}^{n}
is shown for
n=4
on the left pane of Figure
36
. We define the quiver transformation
\Phi_{2}
on
Q_{\mathrm{frust}}^{n}
via factorization (
5.16
), where the step
\Phi_{2}^{(j,k)}
reads

•

mutate at vertex
(k,k)
;

•

mutate at vertices
(k+1,y)
as
y
runs from
k
to 1.

As before, the dependence on
j
appears in the relabelling of the nodes, which follows
\Phi_{2}^{(j,k)}
:

•

shift vertices
(k+1,y)
to the position
(k+1,y-1)
for all
y\leqslant k
;

•

shift the vertex
(k,k)
to the position
(k+1,k)
;

•

shift the vertex
(n+2-j,k)
to the position
(k,k)
;

•

rename vertices according to their new position.

In particular, in the process of applying the wave
\Phi_{1}^{(j)}
we are losing nodes positioned at
(n+2-j,y)
as
y
runs from
1
up to
n-j
, and acquiring nodes positioned at
(x,-j)
as
x
runs from
2
to
n+1-j
.

Example 5.4
.

The step
\Phi_{2}^{(1,3)}
consists of consecutive mutations at the shaded nodes on the right pane of Figure
36
, starting with
(3,3)
and ending at
(4,1)
. We then move the nodes
(4,y)
down by 1 for all
0\leqslant y\leqslant 3
, move the node
(3,3)
to position
(4,3)
, move the node
(5,3)
to the position
(3,3)
, and finish by relabeling nodes according to their new position.

Now we have all the necessary ingredients to define the isolating cluster subordinate to a
c
-isolating ideal triangulation.

Definition 5.5
.

Suppose that
\underline{\Delta}
is an isolating triangulation for an oriented simple closed curve
c
on a surface
S
, so that
\underline{\Delta}
has the local form shown in the left pane of Figure
7
. We define the
c
-isolating quiver

Q_{\underline{\Delta};c}=\Phi_{c}(Q_{\underline{\Delta}})
subordinate to
\underline{\Delta}
to be the result of applying to
Q_{\underline{\Delta}}
the composition of mutations

(5.18)

\Phi_{c}=\Phi_{2}\circ\Phi_{1}

within the subquiver
Q_{\mathrm{cyl}}
of
Q_{\underline{\Delta}}
.

The adjacency matrix of the subquiver
\Phi_{c}(Q_{\mathrm{cyl}}^{n})
is represented graphically in Figure
37
for the
n=4
case.
Following the quiver
Q_{\mathrm{frust}}^{n}
through the steps of
\Phi_{2}
, one can see that the bottom
n-2
rows of
Q_{\underline{\Delta};c}^{n}
form the quiver
Q_{\overline{w}_{0},c^{*}}^{n-2}
, while the rows 1 to
n
form the quiver
Q_{\overline{c},c}^{n}
.

Notation 5.6
.

Let
Q_{\underline{\Delta};c}
be the
c
-isolating quiver subordinate to an ideal triangulation
\underline{\Delta}
. We introduce convenient aliases for certain vertices of
Q_{\underline{\Delta};c}
as follows:

e_{s_{i}}=e_{(i,i)},\qquad e_{t_{i}}=e_{(i+1,i)},\qquad 1\leq i\leq n

e_{h_{+}}=e_{(n,n+1)},\qquad e_{h_{-}}=e_{(2,0)}.

Note that we have

(e_{s_{i}},e_{t_{j}})=\mathfrak{A}_{ij}

where
\mathfrak{A}
is the Cartan matrix of type
A_{n}
.
We write
\Lambda_{S_{\geq 0}}
for the rank
2(n+1)
-sublattice of
\Lambda_{\underline{\Delta};c}
spanned by the
e_{h_{\pm}}
together with the
e_{s_{i}},e_{t_{i}}
, and
\Lambda_{S_{<0}}
for the sublattice spanned by all other
e
-basis vectors for
\Lambda_{S}
. Hence we have a non-orthogonal direct sum decomposition

(5.19)

\displaystyle\Lambda_{S}(Q_{\underline{\Delta};c})=\Lambda_{S_{\geq 0}}(Q_{\underline{\Delta};c})\bigoplus\Lambda_{S_{<0}}(Q_{\underline{\Delta};c}).

We also write
\Lambda_{S_{\leq 0}}
for the sublattice spanned by
\Lambda_{S_{<0}}
together with the
e_{h_{\pm}}
, and
\Lambda_{S_{>0}}
for the span of the complementary set of
e
-basis vectors for
\Lambda_{S}
. We set

\mathcal{T}_{\leq 0}(\underline{\Delta};c)=\mathcal{T}(\Lambda_{S_{\geq 0}}(Q_{\underline{\Delta};c})),\quad\mathcal{T}_{<0}(\underline{\Delta};c)=\mathcal{T}(\Lambda_{S_{<0}}(Q_{\underline{\Delta};c}))

and define analogously the quantum tori
\mathcal{T}_{\geq 0}(\underline{\Delta};c),\mathcal{T}_{>0}(\underline{\Delta};c)
.

The notation above is illustrated in the following picture:

Remark 5.7
.

It is easy to check that in the compatible pair associated to
Q_{\underline{\Delta};c}
we have the relations

(5.20)

\displaystyle\xi_{s_{n}}-\xi_{t_{n}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=1}^{n}\frac{j}{n+1}(e_{s_{j}}+e_{t_{j}}),

(5.21)

\displaystyle\xi_{s_{1}}-\xi_{t_{1}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=1}^{n}\left(1-\frac{j}{n+1}\right)(e_{s_{j}}+e_{t_{j}}),

and that the vectors

z_{+}=e_{v_{+}}+\xi_{s_{n}}-\xi_{t_{n}}\qquad\text{and}\qquad z_{-}=e_{v_{-}}+\xi_{s_{1}}-\xi_{t_{1}}

are skew orthogonal to
\Lambda_{S_{\geq 0}}
.

for

We now define a quiver transformation

(5.22)

\Phi_{c_{+}}=\Phi_{3}\circ\Phi_{c_{+}}^{(0)}

of
Q_{\underline{\Delta}^{\prime}}
which modifies only the subquiver
Q_{\mathrm{sf}}^{n}(+)
. The preliminary wave
\Phi_{c_{+}}^{(0)}
consists of

•

apply transformation (
4.16
) at nodes
(i,i)
for
1\leqslant i\leqslant n-1
;

•

move the node
(n+1,y)
to position
(n+1,y+1)
for all
1\leqslant y\leqslant n
;

•

move the node
(n,y)
to position
(n,y+1)
for
y=n-1
and
y=n
;

•

move the node
(n-1,n-1)
to position
(n,n-1)
;

•

relabel nodes according to their new position.

Observing that the bottom
n-2
mutable rows of
\Phi_{c_{+}}^{(0)}(Q_{\mathrm{sf}}^{n})
form the subquiver
Q_{\overline{w}_{0},c^{*},c}^{n}
, we now apply the transformation
\Phi_{3}
, which admits factorization (
5.17
). The step
\Phi_{3}^{(j,k)}
reads as follows:

•

apply (
4.16
) at nodes
(x,n-2-j+k)
as
x
runs from
n-1-j+k
to
n
;

•

if
k\neq j
move the node
(n,n-2-j+k)
to position
(n-1-j+k,n-1-j+k)
;

•

if
k=j
move the nodes
(n+1,y)
and
(n,y)
with
y\geqslant n-2
up by 1;

•

move the node
(x,n-2-j+k)
right by 1 for all
n-2-j+k\leqslant x\leqslant n-1
;

•

relabel nodes according to their new position.

The same procedure applied to the isolating cylinder for
c_{-}
defines a cluster transformation
\Phi_{c_{-}}
.

Definition 5.8
.

We define the
c_{\pm}
-isolating quiver

Q_{\underline{\Delta}^{\prime};c_{\pm}}=\Phi_{c_{+}}\circ\Phi_{c_{-}}(Q_{\underline{\Delta}^{\prime}})

subordinate to a
c_{\pm}
-isolating ideal triangulation
\underline{\Delta}^{\prime}
to be the result of applying the commuting cluster transformations
\Phi_{c_{+}}
and
\Phi_{c_{-}}
to the quiver
Q_{\underline{\Delta}^{\prime}}
. If
c_{+}
is a single tacked circle on
S
and
\underline{\Delta}^{\prime}
a
c_{+}
-isolating triangulation, we refer to

Q_{\underline{\Delta}^{\prime};c_{+}}=\Phi_{c_{+}}(Q_{\underline{\Delta}^{\prime}})

as a
c_{+}
-isolating quiver subordinate to
\underline{\Delta}^{\prime}
.

The local adjacency matrix of
Q_{\underline{\Delta}^{\prime};c_{\pm}}
near
c_{+}
is represented graphically in the left pane of Figure
40
for the
n=4
case.

Remark 5.9
.

The transformation
\hat{\mu}_{f}
defined by (
4.16
) differs from the mutation
\mu_{f}
when
f\in\left\{f_{i-1}^{-}(c),f_{i}^{+}(c)\right\}
for some tacked circle
c
and
1\leqslant i\leqslant n
. Let
Q_{\Gamma}
be the quiver defined by the ideal bicolored graph
\Gamma
, and
k
be the largest such number that
\dot{e}_{c,i}\neq e_{c,i}
. Then for each
1\leqslant i\leqslant k
we have
f_{i}^{+}=f_{i}^{-}
and the node
(c,i)
(where
c
is still a tacked circle rather than the coordinate of the node) is 2-valent with arrows
f_{i}^{-}\to(c,i)\to f_{i+1}^{+}
. The node
(c,k+1)
is 3-valent with an arrow
f_{k+1}^{+}\to(c,k+1)
and an oriented triangle formed by vertices
f_{k+1}^{-}
,
(c,k+1)
,
f_{k+2}^{+}
listed in that order. Finally, the nodes
(c,j)
with
k+2\leqslant j\leqslant n
are 4-valent with an oriented triangle formed by vertices
f_{j}^{-}
,
(c,j)
,
f_{j+1}^{+}
and a pair of arrows
f_{j}^{+}\to(c,j)\to f_{j-1}^{-}
. Moreover, in the latter case there is an arrow
f_{j}^{+}\to f_{j-1}^{-}
. This analysis allows one to identify the nodes
v
of the quiver
Q_{\Gamma}
such that
\mu_{v}\neq\hat{\mu}_{v}
without having to draw the bicolored graph
\Gamma
. For example, in the quiver
\Phi_{3}^{(0)}(Q_{\mathrm{sf}}^{4})
on the right pane of Figure
39
we have
k=2
, for
2\leqslant y\leqslant 5
nodes
(5,y)=(c,6-y)
where
c
is the unique tacked circle, and

\displaystyle(4,5)

\displaystyle=f_{1}^{+}=f_{1}^{-},

\displaystyle(4,3)

\displaystyle=f_{3}^{+},

\displaystyle(3,2)

\displaystyle=f_{3}^{-},

\displaystyle(1,1)

\displaystyle=f_{5}^{+},

\displaystyle(4,4)

\displaystyle=f_{2}^{+}=f_{2}^{-},

\displaystyle(2,2)

\displaystyle=f_{4}^{+},

\displaystyle(2,1)

\displaystyle=f_{4}^{-}.

Notation 5.11
.

Given a pair of tacked circles
c_{\pm}
on
S^{\prime}
, let
Q_{\underline{\Delta}^{\prime};c_{\pm}}
be the
c_{\pm}
-isolating quiver subordinate to an ideal triangulation
\underline{\Delta}
. We introduce convenient aliases for certain vertices of
Q_{\underline{\Delta}^{\prime};c_{\pm}}
as follows:

e_{v^{\pm}_{i}}\equiv e_{(n,2n-i)^{\pm}},\qquad 1\leq i\leq n+1

e_{(c_{\pm},i)}\equiv e_{(n+1,2n-i)^{\pm}},\qquad 1\leq i\leq n.

In plain language, in order to match with the notations of Section
4
, the new labeling of the mutable vertices in the top
n+1
rows of
Q_{\underline{\Delta}^{\prime};c_{\pm}}
by
\left\{v_{i}^{\pm}\right\}
=has the property that the index
i
increases as we move further away from the boundary circles
c_{\pm}
.

Remark 5.12
.

Recall the bases
\left\{e_{\ell}\right\}
and
\left\{\dot{e}_{\ell}\right\}
introduced in Section
4.3
. The pairings between the vectors for each of these bases are represented graphically in Figure
40
. Inspecting the bicolored graph
\Gamma_{\underline{\Delta};c_{\pm}}
, one observes that
\dot{e}_{v_{i}^{\pm}}=\alpha_{c_{\pm},i}
, where the vectors
\alpha_{c_{\pm},i}
are defined by (
4.12
). Thus we have
(\dot{e}_{v_{i}^{\pm}},e_{c,j})=\mathfrak{A}_{ij}
, where
\mathfrak{A}
is the Cartan matrix of type
A_{n}
. Moreover, for all
1\leqslant i\leqslant n
we have
e_{v_{i}^{\pm}}=\dot{e}_{v_{i}^{\pm}}+\ldots+\dot{e}_{v_{n+1}^{\pm}}
and
e_{c_{\pm},i}=\dot{e}_{c_{\pm},i}
.

5.4.
Action of the braid group in an isolating cluster.

Now that we have introduced isolating clusters for tacked circles we can finish the proof of Proposition
4.35
that the braid group acts by cluster Poisson transformations. Indeed, under the hypothesis of the Lemma, given a tacked circle
c
there exists a
c
-isolating cluster
Q_{\underline{\Delta}^{\prime},c}
. Let
b
be the special point which is connected to
c
in the corresponding triangulation
\underline{\Delta}^{\prime}
, and let
c_{i},b_{i}
be their preimages on the universal cover so that the special triangles are
(c_{i},c_{i+1},b_{i})
.
Then we have (cf. Example
4.30
)

(5.23)

\displaystyle\dot{A}_{v_{i}}=[\nu_{1}(b_{i})\wedge\nu_{1}(b_{i-1})\wedge\cdots\wedge\nu_{1}(b_{1})\wedge\nu_{n+1-i}(c_{i})],

and so an easy computation shows that

(5.24)

\displaystyle\sigma_{i}(A_{v_{j}})=\begin{cases}\frac{A_{v_{i+1}}A_{c,i+1}}{A_{c,i}}\quad&j=i\\
\frac{A_{v_{i}}A_{c,i}}{A_{c,i+1}}\quad&j=i+1\\
A_{v_{j}}\quad&\text{else}.\end{cases}

Hence in a
c
-isolating cluster the braid group acts by a quasicluster automorphism, which proves the action factors through the cluster modular group.

In a
c
-isolating cluster, the quasi-permutation implementing the action of the braid
\sigma_{i}
acts on the
e
-basis by

(5.25)

\displaystyle\sigma_{i}(e_{c,j})=\begin{cases}e_{c,i-1}+e_{c,i}-\dot{e}_{v_{i}}\quad&j=i-1\\
-e_{c,i}+\dot{e}_{v_{i}}\quad&j=i\\
e_{c,i}+e_{c,i+1}\quad&j=i+1\\
e_{c,j}\quad&\text{else.}\end{cases}

Note that the inverse transformation is given by

\sigma^{-1}_{i}(e_{c,j})=\begin{cases}e_{c,i-1}+e_{c,i}\quad&j=i-1\\
-e_{c,i}-\dot{e}_{v_{i}}\quad&j=i\\
e_{c,i}+e_{c,i+1}+\dot{e}_{v_{i}}\quad&j=i+1\\
e_{c,j}\quad&\text{else,}\end{cases}

and we have

\sigma^{2}_{i}(e_{c,j})=e_{c,j}-(\alpha_{i},\alpha_{j})\dot{e}_{v_{i}}.

Similarly, for the ‘oppositely oriented’ frozen basis vectors
\{\overline{e}_{c,i}\}
from (
4.15
) we have

(5.26)

\displaystyle\sigma_{i}(\overline{e}_{c,j})=\begin{cases}\overline{e}_{c,i-1}+\overline{e}_{c,i}\quad&j=i-1\\
-\overline{e}_{c,i}-\dot{e}_{v_{i}}\quad&j=i\\
\overline{e}_{c,i}+\overline{e}_{c,i+1}+\dot{e}_{v_{i}}\quad&j=i+1\\
\overline{e}_{c,j}\quad&\text{else.}\end{cases}

5.5.
Dehn twists.

Let
c
be a simple closed curve on a marked surface
S
. As usual we write
S^{\prime}
for the result of cutting
S
along
c
, and
\phi\colon c_{+}\to c_{-}
for the homeomorphism defining the gluing
S^{\prime}
of back into
S
.

Now suppose that there exists a
c
-isolating ideal triangulation of
S
, and let
Q_{\underline{\Delta};c}
be the corresponding
c
-isolating cluster as defiend in the previous section. We write
Q_{\underline{\Delta}^{\prime};c}
for the the
c_{\pm}
-isolating cluster subordinate to the ideal triangulation
\underline{\Delta}^{\prime}
obtained by applying the cutting functor
\mathcal{C}_{c}
to
\underline{\Delta}
.

Proposition 5.13
.

The following diagram in the cluster modular groupoid is commutative:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Q_{\underline{\Delta}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Q_{\underline{\Delta}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Q_{\underline{\Delta};c}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Q_{\underline{\Delta};c}}
\leftarrow
\rightarrow
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{Q}(\tau_{c})}
\leftarrow
\rightarrow
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Phi_{c}}
\leftarrow
\rightarrow
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Phi_{c}}
\leftarrow
\rightarrow
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\boldsymbol{\mu}_{c}}

Here
\mathcal{Q}(\tau_{c})
denotes the image of the Dehn twist
\tau_{c}
under the functor (
4.32
),
\Phi_{c}
is the cluster transformation denoted by the same symbol as that of the corresponding quiver, and the cluster transformation

(5.27)

\boldsymbol{\mu}_{c}=\prod_{j=1}^{n}(s_{j},t_{j})\circ\prod_{j=1}^{n}\mu_{s_{j}},

consists of
n
commuting mutations at the vertices
s_{1},\ldots,s_{n}
of
Q_{\underline{\Delta},c}
, followed by
n
commuting transpositions
(s_{j},t_{j})
.

Proof..

Using Theorem
3.10
, the proposition can be proved directly by a straightforward comparison of tropical cluster transformations. Alternatively, we can give a less computational proof using Corollary
4.33
. Indeed, we have
Q_{\underline{\Delta}}=Q_{\Gamma_{\underline{\Delta}}}
, and it is simple to check that
Q_{\underline{\Delta};c}=Q_{\Gamma_{\underline{\Delta};c}}
, where for
n=4
the bicolored graph
\Gamma_{\underline{\Delta};c}
is shown on Figure
42
. Since all mutations in the transformation
\Phi_{c}
are 4-valent and can be interpreted as admissible face mutations, by Corollary
4.33
the Dehn twist
\tau_{c}
is realized in
Q_{\underline{\Delta};c}
by a sequence of face mutations turning the graph
\Gamma_{\underline{\Delta};c}
into
\tau_{c}^{-1}(\Gamma_{\underline{\Delta};c})
, followed by a permutation of nodes turning
\tau_{c}^{-1}(\Gamma_{\underline{\Delta};c})
back into
\Gamma_{\underline{\Delta};c}
. The latter coincides with the cluster transformation
\boldsymbol{\mu}_{c}
, so the proposition follows.
∎

\Gamma_{\underline{\Delta};c}
.

Let us now look at the corresponding picture on the cut surface
S^{\prime}
. As can be easily seen from the bicolored graph
\Gamma_{\underline{\Delta}_{+};c_{+}}
on Figure
41
, the Dehn twist
\tau_{c_{\pm}}
is realized in the quiver
Q_{\underline{\Delta}^{\prime};c_{\pm}}
via a single shift at
c_{\pm}
in the sense of Definition
4.5
: we have

\tau_{c_{\pm}}=\sigma_{c_{\pm}}.

The cluster transformation for the shift
\sigma_{c_{\pm}}
is expressed via the quasi-permutation
\varsigma_{c_{\pm}}
described in Lemma
4.22
, which in
Q_{\underline{\Delta}^{\prime};c_{\pm}}
simplifies to

(5.28)

\displaystyle\tau_{c_{\pm}}=\varsigma_{c_{\pm}}(e_{\ell})=\begin{cases}e^{\prime}_{\iota(\ell)}-\dot{e}^{\prime}_{\iota(v_{i}^{\pm})},&\text{if}\;\;\ell=(c_{\pm},i),\\
e^{\prime}_{\iota(\ell)},&\text{otherwise,}\end{cases}

where
\iota
is the natural bijection between the set of faces of the bicolored graph

\Gamma_{\underline{\Delta}^{\prime};c_{\pm}}=\Gamma_{\underline{\Delta}_{+};c_{+}}\sqcup\Gamma_{\underline{\Delta}_{-};c_{-}}

and that of its image under the shift
\sigma_{c_{\pm}}
.

5.6.
Fundamental Hamiltonians of an isolatable simple closed curve.

We now use the quiver
Q_{\underline{\Delta};c}
to describe a commutative subalgebra of the universal Laurent ring algebra
\mathbb{L}^{\mathcal{A}}_{\underline{\Delta};c}
, invariant under the action of the Dehn twist
\tau_{c}
. This subalgebra is generated by certain elements
H_{1}(c),\ldots,H_{n}(c)
, which we call the
fundamental Hamiltonians
associated to an orientation of the simple closed curve
c
.

To construct the fundamental Hamiltonians we consider two augmentations of the quiver
Q_{\underline{\Delta};c}
. The first of these, which we denote by
\widehat{Q}_{\underline{\Delta};c}
, is the quiver obtained from
Q_{\underline{\Delta};c}
by adding a mutable vertex
a
together with a frozen vertex
A
, and arrows
t_{1}\to a\to s_{1}
and
a\to A
.

For the second augmentation, which denote by
\widehat{Q}_{\underline{\Delta};c}^{\prime}
, we add a mutable vertex
b
and a frozen vertex
B
, with arrows
t_{n}\to b\to s_{n}
, and an arrow
B\to b
.

We illustrate the subquivers of
\widehat{Q}_{\underline{\Delta};c}
and
\widehat{Q}_{\underline{\Delta};c}^{\prime}
containing the new nodes along with those of
Q_{S_{\geq 0}}
on the left and the right panes of Figure
43
respectively. We have a non-orthogonal direct sum decomposition

(5.29)

\Lambda_{\widehat{Q}_{\underline{\Delta};c}^{\prime}}\simeq\Lambda_{Q_{\underline{\Delta};c}}\oplus\mathbb{Z}\left\langle e_{b},e_{B}\right\rangle.

Although we do not extend
\widehat{Q}_{\underline{\Delta};c}^{\prime}
to a compatible pair, the compatible pair
(\Lambda_{\underline{\Delta};c},\Xi_{\underline{\Delta};c})
induces a skew form on the lattice
\Xi_{{Q}_{\underline{\Delta};c}}\oplus\mathbb{Z}\left\langle e_{b},e_{B}\right\rangle
.
There is a non-isometric isomorphism of lattices

(5.30)

\Xi_{{Q}_{\underline{\Delta};c}}\oplus\mathbb{Z}\left\langle e_{b},e_{B}\right\rangle\simeq\Xi_{{Q}_{\underline{\Delta};c}}\oplus\mathbb{Z}\left\langle e_{u},e_{B}\right\rangle,

where

e_{u}=e_{b}+\xi_{s_{n}}-\xi_{t_{n}}.

Using (
5.20
) we see that the direct summands in the right hand side are orthogonal with respect to the form
(\cdot,\cdot)_{\widehat{Q}_{\underline{\Delta};c}^{\prime}}
and that
(e_{B},e_{u})=1
.
