Subquiver of
\widehat{Q}_{\underline{\Delta};c}
(on the left) and that of
\widehat{Q}_{\underline{\Delta};c}^{\prime}
(on the right) for
n=4
.

Now consider the cluster transformation
\mu_{\mathrm{Baxter}}
defined as the composition of the following
2n+1
mutations starting from the quiver
\widehat{Q}_{\underline{\Delta};c}
:

(5.31)

\mu_{\mathrm{Baxter}}=\mu_{s_{n}}\mu_{t_{n}}\ldots\mu_{s_{2}}\mu_{t_{2}}\mu_{s_{1}}\mu_{t_{1}}\mu_{a}.

If
I,I^{\prime}
are the label sets of the quivers
\widehat{Q}_{\underline{\Delta};c},\widehat{Q}_{\underline{\Delta};c}^{\prime}
, then there is a permutation morphism
\iota\colon I\to I^{\prime}
between the quivers
\mu_{\mathrm{Baxter}}(\widehat{Q}_{\underline{\Delta};c})
and
\widehat{Q}_{\underline{\Delta};c}^{\prime}
given by

(5.32)

\displaystyle a\longmapsto t_{1}\longmapsto s_{1}\longmapsto t_{2}\longmapsto s_{2}\longmapsto\ldots\longmapsto t_{n}\longmapsto s_{n}\longmapsto b,\qquad A\longmapsto B,

and identity on all other labels. We set
\widetilde{\mu}_{\mathrm{Baxter}}=\iota\circ\mu_{\mathrm{Baxter}}
, and denote the corresponding quasi-cluster transformation by the same symbol.

As is clear from inspecting the quiver, we have
(e_{A},e_{\ell})\leq 0
for all mutable directions
\ell
in
\widehat{Q}_{\underline{\Delta};c}
. It follows that the quantum cluster
\mathcal{X}
-variable
Y_{A}=Y_{e_{A}}
remains Laurent under all 1-step mutations and thus lies in the universal Laurent ring
\mathbb{L}_{\widehat{Q}_{\underline{\Delta};c}}
. Hence
\widetilde{\mu}_{\mathrm{Baxter}}(Y_{A})\in\mathbb{L}_{\widehat{Q}_{\underline{\Delta};c}^{\prime}}
. On the other hand, by the decomposition (
5.29
) and the isomorphism (
5.30
), we have an embedding of algebras

\mathbb{L}_{\widehat{Q}_{\underline{\Delta};c}^{\prime}}\subset\mathbb{L}_{{Q}_{\underline{\Delta};c}}^{\mathcal{A}}\otimes\mathbb{Z}[q^{\pm 1}]\left\langle Y_{u}^{\pm 1},Y_{B}^{\pm 1}\right\rangle,

where
Y_{u}Y_{B}=q^{2}Y_{B}Y_{u}
.
Since the two tensor factors commute, the coefficients of
\widetilde{\mu}_{\mathrm{Baxter}}(Y_{A})
, considered as a Laurent polynomial in
\left\{Y_{u},Y_{B}\right\}
, are elements of the upper cluster algebra
\mathbb{L}_{\underline{\Delta};c}
.

The mutation sequence
\mu_{\mathrm{Baxter}}
and its generalizations are studied in
[
SS18
,
SS19b
]
under the name of the
Baxter operator
. In particular, it follows from Proposition
6.22
recalled in Section
6.4
that
\widetilde{\mu}_{\mathrm{Baxter}}(Y_{A})
can be written as

\widetilde{\mu}_{\mathrm{Baxter}}(Y_{A})=\sum_{k=0}^{n+1}H_{k}(\underline{\Delta};c)Y_{e_{B}+ke_{u}}\in\mathbb{L}_{Q_{\underline{\Delta};c}}^{\mathcal{A}}\otimes\mathbb{Z}[q^{\pm 1}]\left\langle Y_{u}^{\pm 1},Y_{B}^{\pm 1}\right\rangle,

with
H_{k}(c)=H_{k}(\underline{\Delta};c)\in\mathbb{L}_{Q_{\underline{\Delta};c}}^{\mathcal{A}}
.

Definition 5.14
.

The
fundamental quantum Hamiltonians
associated to the simple closed curve
c
are defined to be the coefficients
H_{k}(c)\in\mathbb{L}^{\mathcal{A}}_{\underline{\Delta};c}
of
Y_{e_{B}+ke_{u}}
of the polynomial
\widetilde{\mu}_{\mathrm{Baxter}}(Y_{A})
.

Example 5.15
.

For
G=SL_{2}
, the only nontrivial fundamental Hamiltonian is given by

\displaystyle H_{1}(c)

\displaystyle=Y_{\xi_{t_{1}}-\xi_{s_{1}}}+Y_{\xi_{t_{1}}-\xi_{s_{1}}+e_{s_{1}}}+Y_{\xi_{t_{1}}-\xi_{s_{1}}+e_{s_{1}}+e_{t_{1}}}

(5.33)

\displaystyle=Y_{\xi_{t_{1}}-\xi_{s_{1}}}+Y_{\xi_{h_{+}}+\xi_{h_{-}}-\xi_{s_{1}}-\xi_{t_{1}}}+Y_{\xi_{s_{1}}-\xi_{t_{1}}}.

The next Lemma is a consequence of Proposition
6.22
in Section
6.4
.

Lemma 5.16
.

The fundamental Hamiltonians
H_{k}(c)
generate a commutative subalgebra in
\mathbb{L}^{\mathcal{A}}_{\underline{\Delta};c}
.

In Section
7
we will prove the following theorem justifying the claim, implicit in the notation, that the
H_{k}(c)
depend only on the curve
c
and not on the choice of ideal triangulation used in the definition above.

Theorem 5.17
.

The elements
H_{k}(c)
are independent of the choice of isolating triangulation for
c
: if
\underline{\Delta},\underline{\Delta}^{\prime}
are two triangulations containing an isolating cylinder for
c
and

\mu\colon Q_{\underline{\Delta};c}\longrightarrow Q_{\underline{\Delta}^{\prime};c}

is the cluster transformation between the corresponding special coordinate systems, then we have

\mu(H_{k}(\underline{\Delta};c))=H_{k}(\underline{\Delta}^{\prime};c).

Remark 5.18
.

In a companion paper
[
SS25
]
we will give an alternative construction of the elements
H_{k}(c)
which identifies them with
q
-deformations of the traces of the monodromy around
c
in the
k
-th fundamental representation of
SL_{n+1}(\mathbb{C})
. This construction gives a separate proof of Theorem
5.17
, also explained in
[
SS25
]
.

6.
Algebraic Whittaker transform

The topic of this section is the local version of the gluing isomorphism
\eta_{c}
in Theorem
Main Theorem
.
This local picture amounts to an algebraic version of the spectral transform for the type-
A_{n}

q
-difference open Toda chain, which we will use to identify the commutative subalgebra of
\mathbb{L}_{G,S}
generated by the fundamental Hamiltonians
H_{k}(c)
for an isolatable simple closed curve
c
with the character ring of
G
. For convenience, we will prove the main results for the group
G=GL_{n+1}
, and then deduce from these all the needed statements for the semisimple groups
G=SL_{n+1}
and
PGL_{n+1}
.

6.1.
The Toda chain cluster algebra

In this section we recall some known results about the
Q
-system cluster algebra associated to the group
GL_{n+1}
, which is also known as the
GL_{n+1}
Toda chain cluster algebra. Let
\mathrm{P}\simeq\mathbb{Z}^{n+1}
be the weight lattice for
GL_{n+1}
, with standard basis
\{\varepsilon_{i}\}_{i=0}^{n}
and basis of fundamental weights

\omega_{i}=\sum_{r\leq i}\varepsilon_{i},\quad 1\leq i\leq n+1.

We write
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle
for the standard Euclidean form on
\mathrm{P}
for which
\{\varepsilon_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n+1}
is an orthonormal basis, and
consider the double lattice

\Lambda=\Lambda_{GL_{n+1}}\simeq\mathrm{P}\oplus\mathrm{P}

equipped with the skew-form

(6.1)

\displaystyle((\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}),(\mu_{1},\mu_{2}))_{\Lambda}=\langle\lambda_{1},\mu_{2}\rangle-\langle\lambda_{2},\mu_{1}\rangle.

For
\lambda\in\mathrm{P}
we set

p_{\lambda}=(\lambda,0),\qquad x_{\lambda}=(0,\lambda)

and write
P_{\lambda}
,
X_{\lambda}
for the corresponding generators of the quantum torus
\mathcal{T}_{GL_{n+1}}
associated to the double lattice. Then the nontrivial commutation relations in
\mathcal{T}_{GL_{n+1}}
take the form

P_{\lambda}X_{\mu}=q^{-2\langle\lambda,\mu\rangle}X_{\mu}P_{\lambda},\quad\lambda,\mu\in\mathrm{P}.

We will also make the abbreviations

P_{i}=P_{\varepsilon_{i}},\quad X_{i}=X_{\varepsilon_{i}}\quad\text{for}\quad i=1,\ldots,n+1.

Consider the following pair of bases in
\Lambda
:

(6.2)

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\xi}

\displaystyle=\{\xi_{s_{i}},\xi_{t_{i}}\}_{i=1}^{n+1},\qquad\xi_{s_{i}}=p_{\omega_{i}}+x_{\omega_{i}},\qquad\xi_{t_{i}}=x_{\omega_{i}},

(6.3)

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{e}

\displaystyle=\{e_{s_{i}},e_{t_{i}}\}_{i=1}^{n+1},\qquad e_{s_{i}}=x_{-\alpha_{i}},\quad e_{t_{i}}=p_{\alpha_{i}}+x_{\alpha_{i}},

where
\alpha_{i}=\varepsilon_{i}-\varepsilon_{i+1}
is the
i
-th simple root, and
\alpha_{n+1}=x_{n+1}
. Then
(\boldsymbol{e},\boldsymbol{\xi})
defines a compatible pair with
\Xi=\Lambda
, where the frozen directions are
I_{0}=\{s_{n+1},t_{n+1}\}
.

Remark 6.1
.

Note that the map sending the basis vectors
\{e_{s_{i}},e_{t_{i}}\}_{i=1}^{n}
from the quiver
Q_{\underline{\Delta};c}
defined in Notation
5.6
to the correspondingly labelled ones in the lattice
\Lambda_{GL_{n+1}}
is an isometry. In particular, we can use this embedding to regard
\mathcal{T}_{S_{>0}}
as a subalgebra in the quantum torus for the
GL_{n+1}
Toda chain.

As in the case of
Q_{\underline{\Delta};c}
, there is an element
\tau
of the cluster modular group given by mutating in all directions
\{s_{j}\}_{j=1}^{n}
and then applying the generalized permutation

t_{n+1}\mapsto s_{n+1},\quad s_{n+1}\mapsto t_{n+1}+2s_{n+1},\quad\text{and}\quad s_{j}\leftrightarrow t_{j}\quad\text{for all}\quad 1\leqslant j\leqslant n.

By (
3.13
), the quantum cluster transformation implementing its action on the universal Laurent ring can be expressed as

(6.4)

\displaystyle\tau=\gamma\circ\mathrm{Ad}\left(\prod_{j=1}^{n}\Psi(X_{-\alpha_{j}})^{-1}\right),

where
\gamma
is the automorphism of
\mathcal{T}_{GL_{n+1}}
defined on the generators by

\gamma(P_{i})=P_{i},\quad\gamma(X_{i})=qP_{i}X_{i},\quad i=1,\ldots,n+1.

We write
\mathbb{L}_{Toda}(GL_{n+1})
for the universal Laurent ring corresponding to this compatible pair, and
\mathcal{A}_{Toda}(GL_{n+1})
for the corresponding quantum cluster algebra. By the results of Goodearl and Yakimov (
[
GY21
]
, Theorem B), it is known that in fact

\mathcal{A}_{Toda}(GL_{n+1})=\mathbb{L}_{Toda}(GL_{n+1}).

Moreover, by the same Theorem both are isomorphic as
\mathbb{Z}[q^{\pm 1}]
-algebras to the localization of the ring
A_{q^{2}}(\mathfrak{n}\left((s_{0}s_{1})^{n+1}\right))\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}[q^{\pm 2}]}\mathbb{Z}[q^{\pm 1}]
at a pair of generalized minors, where
A_{q^{2}}(\mathfrak{n}(w))
is the dual canonical form of the quantum coordinate ring of the unipotent cell associated to the affine Weyl group element
w
in the loop group
LSL_{2}
. Under this isomorphism, the two inverted generalized minors correspond to the frozen variable
A_{s+1},A_{t+1}
, while the

\tau^{m}(A_{t_{1}})
with
-n\leq m\leq n+1
correspond to the dual PBW generators of the quantum coordinate ring. Hence
\mathbb{L}_{Toda}(GL_{n+1})
is generated as a
\mathbb{Z}[q^{\pm 1}]
-algebra by
\{\tau^{m}(A_{t_{1}})\}_{m\in\mathbb{Z}}
together with the inverses of the two frozen variables.

6.2.
Residue algebra

In this section we define an explicit subalgebra
\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{res}}
of rational
q
-difference operators on the maximal torus
T
of
GL_{n+1}
which will serve as the “spectral” description of the cluster algebra
\mathbb{L}_{Toda}(GL_{n+1})
.

We define the algebra of
q
-difference operators on
T
to be the quantum torus

(6.5)

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}_{q}(T)=\mathbb{Z}[q^{\pm 1}]\left\langle w_{i}^{\pm 1},D_{i}^{\pm}\,\big|\,1\leqslant i\leqslant n+1\right\rangle

with defining relations

D_{i}w_{j}=q^{2\delta_{ij}}w_{j}D_{i},\qquad w_{i}w_{j}=w_{j}w_{i},\qquad D_{i}D_{j}=D_{j}D_{i}.

The
w_{i}
generate a commutative subalgebra which we identify with the group ring
\mathcal{O}(T)
of the weight lattice
\mathrm{P}
of
GL_{n+1}
. The action of the invertible generators
w_{i}
by conjugation defines an
internal grading
on
\mathcal{D}_{q}(T)
by
\mathrm{P}
. We write
\mathcal{D}^{\mu}_{q}(T)
for the
\mu
-graded piece with respect to this grading, with the sign convention that

A\in\mathcal{D}^{\mu}_{q}(T)\iff Aw_{\lambda}=q^{2\langle\lambda,\mu\rangle}w_{\lambda}A\quad\text{for all }\lambda\in P.

In concrete terms,
\mathcal{D}^{\mu}_{q}(T)
consists of all elements of the form
f(\boldsymbol{w})D_{1}^{\mu_{1}}\cdots D_{n+1}^{\mu_{n+1}}
where
f
is an arbitrary Laurent polynomial in
\boldsymbol{w}
. For a general element
A\in\mathcal{D}_{q}(T)
we write
A_{\mu}
for its degree
\mu
component, so that

A=\sum_{\mu}A_{\mu},\quad A_{\mu}\in\mathcal{D}^{\mu}_{q}(T).

The algebra
\mathcal{D}_{q}(T)
has a standard representation on
\mathcal{O}(T)
in which the subalgebra
\mathcal{O}(T)
acts on itself by multiplication operators, and the generators
D_{i}
act by

(6.6)

\displaystyle(D_{i}\circ f)(\boldsymbol{w})=f(w_{1},\ldots,q^{2}w_{i},\ldots,w_{n+1}).

Hence as a left
\mathcal{D}_{q}(T)
module, this representation is identified with the quotient of
\mathcal{D}_{q}(T)
by the left ideal
\langle D_{i}-1\rangle_{i=1}^{n+1}
. We will sometimes abuse notation by writing
Af
instead of
A\circ f
whenever it is clear from the context that we mean the action of
\mathcal{D}_{q}(T)
on
\mathcal{O}(T)
rather than the multiplication in
\mathcal{D}_{q}(T)
.

Now consider the Ore localization

(6.7)

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}_{q}(T)_{\mathrm{loc}}=\mathcal{D}_{q}(T)\big[(1-q^{2k}w_{\alpha})^{-1}\,\big|\,\alpha\in\Delta_{+},\;k\in\mathbb{Z}\big],

where for a positive root
\alpha=\varepsilon_{i}-\varepsilon_{j}
we set
w_{\alpha}=w_{i}/w_{j}
. Since the elements we invert commute with all of
\mathcal{O}(T)
, the localized ring also inherits an internal grading whose pieces we denote by
\mathcal{D}^{\mu}_{q}(T)_{\mathrm{loc}}
. Given
\alpha\in\Delta_{+}
and
k\in\mathbb{Z}
we introduce the following notation for the divisors at which we localize in (
6.7
):

(6.8)

\displaystyle d_{\alpha,k}=\left\{w_{\alpha}=q^{-2k}\right\}.

We write
\mathcal{O}(T)_{\mathrm{loc}}
for the subring of
\mathcal{D}_{q}(T)_{\mathrm{loc}}
given by its internal-degree zero piece. This subring is commutative and we can identify it with the ring of rational functions on
T
regular away from the union of the
d_{\alpha,k}
. We again have a standard difference operator representation of the algebra
\mathcal{D}_{q}(T)_{\mathrm{loc}}
on
\mathcal{O}(T)_{\mathrm{loc}}
.

Associated to each divisor
d_{\alpha,k}
we have an increasing
order
filtration

(6.9)

\ldots\subset\mathcal{D}_{q}(T)_{\mathrm{loc}}^{\alpha,k;-1}\subset\mathcal{D}_{q}(T)_{\mathrm{loc}}^{\alpha,k;0}\subset\mathcal{D}_{q}(T)_{\mathrm{loc}}^{\alpha,k;1}\subset\ldots

of
\mathcal{D}_{q}(T)_{\mathrm{loc}}
by left
\mathcal{O}(T)_{\mathrm{loc}}
-modules

\mathcal{D}_{q}(T)_{\mathrm{loc}}^{\alpha,k;m}=(1-q^{2k}w_{\alpha})^{m}\mathcal{D}_{q}(T)\big[(1-q^{2\ell}w_{\beta})^{-1}\,\big|\,\beta\neq\alpha,\;\ell\neq k].

So associated to each divisor
d_{\alpha,k}
is a graded vector space

\operatorname{gr}_{\alpha,k}(\mathcal{D}_{q}(T)_{\mathrm{loc}})=\bigoplus_{m\in\mathbb{Z}}\operatorname{gr}_{\alpha,k}^{m}(\mathcal{D}_{q}(T)_{\mathrm{loc}})

where

\operatorname{gr}_{\alpha,k}^{m}(\mathcal{D}_{q}(T)_{\mathrm{loc}})=\mathcal{D}_{q}(T)_{\mathrm{loc}}^{\alpha,k;m}/\mathcal{D}_{q}(T)_{\mathrm{loc}}^{\alpha,k;m+1},

along with natural projections

(6.10)

\pi_{\alpha,k;m}\colon\mathcal{D}_{q}(T)_{\mathrm{loc}}^{\alpha,k;m}\longrightarrow\operatorname{gr}_{\alpha,k}^{m}(\mathcal{D}_{q}(T)_{\mathrm{loc}}).

Note that the order filtration associated to any
d_{\alpha,k}
is compatible with and completely independent from the internal grading defined earlier, so each order-filtered piece can be further decomposed by internal degree:

\mathcal{D}^{\mu}_{q}(T)_{\mathrm{loc}}^{\alpha,k;m}=\mathcal{D}_{q}(T)_{\mathrm{loc}}^{\alpha,k;m}\cap\mathcal{D}_{q}^{\mu}(T)_{\mathrm{loc}},

\pi_{\alpha,k;m}\colon\mathcal{D}_{q}^{\mu}(T)_{\mathrm{loc}}^{\alpha,k;m}\longrightarrow\operatorname{gr}_{\alpha,k}^{m}(\mathcal{D}_{q}^{\mu}(T)_{\mathrm{loc}}).

Definition 6.2
.

We say that an element
A\in\mathcal{D}_{q}(T)_{\mathrm{loc}}
has a
pole of order
m
at the divisor
d_{\alpha,k}
if
A\in\mathcal{D}_{q}(T)_{\mathrm{loc}}^{\alpha,k;-m}
and
\pi_{\alpha,k;-m}(A)\neq 0
. For
A\in\mathcal{D}_{q}(T)_{\mathrm{loc}}^{\alpha,k;-1}
we use the shorthand

\pi_{\alpha,k;-1}(A)=\operatorname{res}_{\alpha,k}(A),

and refer to
\operatorname{res}_{\alpha,k}(A)
as the
residue
of
A
at
d_{\alpha,k}
. Similarly, for
A\in\mathcal{D}_{q}(T)_{\mathrm{loc}}^{\alpha,k;0}
we abbreviate

\pi_{\alpha,k;0}(A)=A|_{d_{\alpha,k}},

and say that
A|_{d_{\alpha,k}}
is the
restriction
of
A
to
d_{\alpha,k}
.

Given a weight
\mu\in\mathrm{P}
consider the automorphism
\operatorname{Ad}_{D^{\mu}}
of
\mathcal{D}_{q}(T)_{\mathrm{loc}}
given by

(6.11)

\displaystyle\operatorname{Ad}_{D^{\mu}}\colon A\longmapsto A^{[\mu]}=D^{\mu}AD^{-\mu},\quad D^{\mu}=D_{1}^{\mu_{1}}\cdots D_{n+1}^{\mu_{n+1}}.

Note that unlike
\operatorname{Ad}_{w_{\mu}}
, the automorphism
\operatorname{Ad}_{D^{\mu}}
does not respect the order filtrations on
\mathcal{D}_{q}(T)_{\mathrm{loc}}
and is not even diagonalizable. Instead, the isomorphisms (
6.11
) satisfy

(6.12)

\operatorname{Ad}_{D^{\mu}}\circ\pi_{\alpha,k;n}=\pi_{\alpha,k+\left\langle\alpha,\mu\right\rangle;n}\circ\operatorname{Ad}_{D^{\mu}}.

and therefore induce isomorphisms of
\mathbb{Z}[q^{\pm 1}]
-modules between associated graded factors for order filtrations associated to different divisors:

(6.13)

\operatorname{Ad}_{D^{\mu}}\colon\operatorname{gr}_{\alpha,k}^{m}(\mathcal{D}_{q}(T)_{\mathrm{loc}})\longrightarrow\operatorname{gr}_{\alpha,k+\left\langle\alpha,\mu\right\rangle}^{m}(\mathcal{D}_{q}(T)_{\mathrm{loc}}),

which are compatible with the internal grading.

There is a natural action of the Weyl group
W=S_{n+1}
by automorphisms of the algebra
\mathcal{D}_{q}(T)_{\mathrm{loc}}
induced by the reflection representation on the weight lattice, or equivalently by the permutation action on the coordinates
\boldsymbol{w}=(w_{1},\ldots,w_{n+1})
and
\boldsymbol{D}=(D_{1},\ldots,D_{n+1})
. We write
\mathcal{D}_{q}(T)^{W}_{\mathrm{loc}}
for the subalgebra of
W
-invariants with respect to this action. Note that
A\in\mathcal{D}_{q}(T)_{\mathrm{loc}}^{W}
if and only if
s_{\alpha}(A_{\mu})=A_{s_{\alpha}(\mu)}
for every
\mu\in\mathrm{P}
and
\alpha\in\Delta_{+}
. The Weyl group action induces isomorphisms of
\mathbb{Z}[q^{\pm 1}]
-modules

s_{\alpha}\colon\operatorname{gr}_{\alpha,k}^{m}(\mathcal{D}_{q}^{\mu}(T)_{\mathrm{loc}})\longrightarrow\operatorname{gr}_{\alpha,-k}^{m}(\mathcal{D}_{q}^{s_{\alpha}(\mu)}(T)_{\mathrm{loc}}),

which satisfy

(6.14)

s_{\alpha}\circ\pi_{\alpha,k;m}=\pi_{\alpha,-k;m}\circ s_{\alpha}.

Lemma 6.3
.

For any Weyl-invariant element
A\in\mathcal{D}_{q}(T)_{\mathrm{loc}}^{W}
, any divisor
d_{\alpha,k}
, any choice of internal degree
\mu\in\mathrm{P}
, and any choice of
d_{\alpha,k}
-order
m\in\mathbb{Z}
, we have

(6.15)

\pi_{\alpha,k;m}\left(A_{\mu}+(-1)^{m+1}A_{s_{\alpha(\mu)}}^{[k\alpha]}D^{\left\langle\alpha,\mu\right\rangle\alpha}\right)=0.

Proof..

Write

\pi_{\alpha,k;m}(A_{\mu})=(1-q^{2k}w_{\alpha})^{m}a_{\mu;m}(\boldsymbol{w})D^{\mu}+\mathcal{D}_{q}^{\mu}(T)_{\mathrm{loc}}^{\alpha,k;m+1}

where
a_{\mu;m}\in\mathcal{D}_{q}^{0}(T)_{\mathrm{loc}}^{\alpha,k;0}
is a rational function on
T
regular at
d_{\alpha,k}
. Then we have

\displaystyle\pi_{\alpha,k;m}\left((-1)^{m+1}A_{s_{\alpha(\mu)}}^{[k\alpha]}D^{\left\langle\alpha,\mu\right\rangle\alpha}\right)

\displaystyle=(-1)^{m+1}\pi_{\alpha,k;n}\left(\operatorname{Ad}_{D^{k\alpha}}(s_{\alpha}(A_{\mu}))\right)D^{\left\langle\alpha,\mu\right\rangle\alpha}

\displaystyle=(-1)^{m+1}\operatorname{Ad}_{D^{k\alpha}}(s_{\alpha}(\pi_{\alpha,k;m}\left(A_{\mu}\right)))D^{\left\langle\alpha,\mu\right\rangle\alpha}

\displaystyle=(1-q^{-2k}w_{\alpha}^{-1})^{m}\operatorname{Ad}_{D^{k\alpha}}(s_{\alpha}(a_{\mu;m}(\boldsymbol{w})))D^{\mu}+\mathcal{D}_{q}^{\mu}(T)_{\mathrm{loc}}^{\alpha,k;m+1}

\displaystyle=(-1)^{m}\pi_{\alpha,k;m}(A_{\mu}),

The second equality follows from (
6.12
) and (
6.14
), and the last one from the relation

\operatorname{Ad}_{D^{k\alpha}}(s_{\alpha}(f(\boldsymbol{w})))\big|_{d_{\alpha,k}}=f(\boldsymbol{w})\big|_{d_{\alpha,k}},

which holds for any
f\in\mathcal{D}_{q}^{0}(T)_{\mathrm{loc}}^{\alpha,k;0}
.
∎

The following definition and lemma are modelled on the constructions of
[
GKV97
]
, in particular their Definition 1.3 and Theorem 1.4.

Definition 6.4
.

We define
\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{res}}
to be the subspace of
\mathcal{D}_{q}(T)_{\mathrm{loc}}^{W}
consisting of elements
A
such that

(1)

for each
\alpha\in\Delta_{+}
and
k\in\mathbb{Z}
the element
A
has at most a simple pole at
d_{\alpha,k}
;

(2)

for all
\mu\in\mathrm{P}
,
\alpha\in\Delta_{+}
, and
k\in\mathbb{Z}
the corresponding residues satisfy

(6.16)

\operatorname{res}_{\alpha,k}\left(A_{\mu}+A_{s_{\alpha}(\mu)+k\alpha}D^{(\left\langle\alpha,\mu\right\rangle-k)\alpha}\right)=0.

Remark 6.5
.

Because
A
is required to be invariant under the action of the Weyl group, it would suffice to impose the condition (
6.16
) for a single positive root
\alpha\in\Delta_{+}
.

Note that for
k=0
the condition (
6.16
) is a particular case of (
6.15
), and hence is automatically satisfied. Moreover, combining (
6.15
) and (
6.16
) we obtain

(6.17)

\operatorname{res}_{\alpha,k}\left(D^{k\alpha}A_{\nu}-A_{\nu+k\alpha}\right)=0

for any
A\in\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{res}}
,
\nu\in\mathrm{P}
,
\alpha\in\Delta_{+}
, and
k\in\mathbb{Z}
.

Lemma 6.6
.

The subspace
\mathcal{D}_{res}
is closed under multiplication, and hence forms a subalgebra in
\mathcal{D}_{q}(T)_{loc}^{W}
.

Proof..

First we show that the product of two elements of
\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{res}}
has at most simple poles at each divisor
d_{\alpha,k}
.
For this we need to show that for any pair of elements
A,B\in\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{res}}
, any
\alpha\in\Delta_{+}
, and any
k\in\mathbb{Z}
we have

(6.18)

\pi_{\alpha,k;-2}(AB)=0.

We can express the component of weight
\nu\in\mathrm{P}
of the product
AB
as

(AB)_{\nu}=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{\mu\in P}K_{\alpha,k;\mu}\qquad\text{where}\qquad K_{\alpha,k;\mu}=A_{\mu}B_{\nu-\mu}+A_{s_{\alpha}(\mu)+k\alpha}B_{\nu-s_{\alpha}(\mu)+k\alpha}.

Observing that

\pi_{\alpha,k;-2}\left(A_{\mu}B_{\nu-\mu}\right)=\operatorname{res}_{\alpha,k}(A_{\mu})\operatorname{res}_{\alpha,k-\left\langle\alpha,\mu\right\rangle}(B_{\nu-\mu})

we obtain

\pi_{\alpha,k;-2}\left(K_{\alpha,k;\mu}\right)=\operatorname{res}_{\alpha,k}(A_{\mu})\operatorname{res}_{\alpha,k-\left\langle\alpha,\mu\right\rangle}(B_{\nu-\mu})\\
+\operatorname{res}_{\alpha,k}(A_{s_{\alpha}(\mu)+k\alpha})\operatorname{res}_{\alpha,\left\langle\alpha,\mu\right\rangle-k}(B_{\nu-s_{\alpha}(\mu)-k\alpha}).

Adding and subtracting the product

\operatorname{res}_{\alpha,k}(A_{s_{\alpha}(\mu)+k\alpha})D^{(\left\langle\alpha,\mu\right\rangle-k)\alpha}\operatorname{res}_{\alpha,k-\left\langle\alpha,\mu\right\rangle}(B_{\nu-\mu})\\
=\operatorname{res}_{\alpha,k}(A_{s_{\alpha}(\mu)+k\alpha})\operatorname{res}_{\alpha,\left\langle\alpha,\mu\right\rangle-k}\left(D^{(\left\langle\alpha,\mu\right\rangle-k)\alpha}B_{\nu-\mu}\right)

we arrive at the sum

\operatorname{res}_{\alpha,k}\left(A_{\mu}+A_{s_{\alpha}(\mu)+k\alpha}D^{(\left\langle\alpha,\mu\right\rangle-k)\alpha}\right)\operatorname{res}_{\alpha,k-\left\langle\alpha,\mu\right\rangle}\left(B_{\nu-\mu}\right)\\
+\operatorname{res}_{\alpha,k}\Big(A_{s_{\alpha}(\mu)+k\alpha}\Big)\operatorname{res}_{\alpha,\left\langle\alpha,\mu\right\rangle-k}\left(B_{\nu-s_{\alpha}(\mu)-k\alpha}-D^{(\left\langle\alpha,\mu\right\rangle-k)\alpha}B_{\nu-\mu}\right).

But the first summand vanishes by (
6.16
) and the second one by (
6.17
), and therefore (
6.18
) holds.

It now remains to show that the condition (
6.16
) holds for the product
AB
, that is

\operatorname{res}_{\alpha,k}\left((AB)_{\nu}+(AB)_{s_{\alpha}(\nu)+k\alpha}D^{(\left\langle\alpha,\nu\right\rangle-k)\alpha}\right)=0

for all
\nu\in\mathrm{P}
,
\alpha\in\Delta_{+}
, and
k\in\mathbb{Z}
. As before, we write

(AB)_{\nu}+(AB)_{s_{\alpha}(\nu)+k\alpha}D^{(\left\langle\alpha,\nu\right\rangle-k)\alpha}=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{\mu\in P}L_{\alpha,k;\mu},

where

L_{\alpha,k;\mu}=A_{\mu}\left(B_{\nu-\mu}+B_{s_{\alpha}(\nu)-\mu+k\alpha}D^{(\left\langle\alpha,\nu\right\rangle-k)\alpha}\right)\\
+A_{s_{\alpha}(\mu)+k\alpha}\left(B_{\nu-s_{\alpha}(\mu)-k\alpha}+B_{s_{\alpha}(\nu)-s_{\alpha}(\mu)}D^{(\left\langle\alpha,\nu\right\rangle-k)\alpha}\right)

By (
6.16
) we have

\displaystyle\operatorname{res}_{\alpha,k-\left\langle\alpha,\mu\right\rangle}\left(B_{\nu-\mu}+B_{s_{\alpha}(\nu)-\mu+k\alpha}D^{(\left\langle\alpha,\nu\right\rangle-k)\alpha}\right)=0,

\displaystyle\operatorname{res}_{\alpha,\left\langle\alpha,\mu\right\rangle-k}\left(B_{\nu-s_{\alpha}(\mu)-k\alpha}+B_{s_{\alpha}(\nu)-s_{\alpha}(\mu)}D^{(\left\langle\alpha,\nu\right\rangle-k)\alpha}\right)=0,

and therefore

\operatorname{res}_{\alpha,k}(L_{\alpha,k;\mu})=\operatorname{res}_{\alpha,k}\left(A_{\mu}\right)\left(B_{\nu-\mu}+B_{s_{\alpha}(\nu)-\mu+k\alpha}D^{(\left\langle\alpha,\nu\right\rangle-k)\alpha}\right)\Big|_{d_{\alpha,k-\left\langle\alpha,\mu\right\rangle}}\\
+\operatorname{res}_{\alpha,k}\left(A_{s_{\alpha}(\mu)+k\alpha}\right)\left(B_{\nu-s_{\alpha}(\mu)-k\alpha}+B_{s_{\alpha}(\nu-\mu)}D^{(\left\langle\alpha,\nu\right\rangle-k)\alpha}\right)\Big|_{d_{\alpha,\left\langle\alpha,\mu\right\rangle-k}}.

It follows from (
6.15
) that

D^{(\left\langle\alpha,\mu\right\rangle-k)\alpha}\left(B_{\nu-\mu}+B_{s_{\alpha}(\nu)-\mu+k\alpha}D^{(\left\langle\alpha,\nu\right\rangle-k)\alpha}\right)\Big|_{d_{\alpha,k-\left\langle\alpha,\mu\right\rangle}}\\
=\left(B_{\nu-s_{\alpha}(\mu)-k\alpha}+B_{s_{\alpha}(\nu-\mu)}D^{(\left\langle\alpha,\nu\right\rangle-k)\alpha}\right)\Big|_{d_{\alpha,\left\langle\alpha,\mu\right\rangle-k}},

and thus
\operatorname{res}_{\alpha,k}(L_{\alpha,k;\mu})
is equal to the product

\operatorname{res}_{\alpha,k}\left(A_{\mu}+A_{s_{\alpha}(\mu)+k\alpha}D^{(\left\langle\alpha,\mu\right\rangle-k)\alpha}\right)\left(B_{\nu-\mu}+B_{s_{\alpha}(\nu)-\mu+k\alpha}D^{(\left\langle\alpha,\nu\right\rangle-k)\alpha}\right)\Big|_{d_{\alpha,k-\left\langle\alpha,\mu\right\rangle}},

whose first factor vanishes by (
6.16
). This concludes the proof.
∎

Remark 6.7
.

Because of the Weyl invariance condition in Definition
6.4
, the algebra
\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{res}}
no longer carries an internal grading. Nonetheless, the internal grading on
\mathcal{D}_{q}(T)_{loc}
induces a filtration

\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{res}}=\bigcup_{\mu\in P^{+}}\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{res}}^{\leq\mu},\qquad\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{res}}^{\leq\mu}=\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{res}}\cap\mathcal{D}_{q}^{\leq\mu}(T)_{loc},

where

\mathcal{D}_{q}^{\leq\mu}(T)_{loc}=\bigoplus_{\nu\leq\mu}\mathcal{D}_{q}^{\nu}(T)_{loc}

and the dominance order relation
\nu\leq\mu
means that
\mu-\nu
is a linear combination of simple roots with non-negative coefficients.

The main point of the conditions in Definition
6.4
transpires from the following lemma:

Lemma 6.8
.

The action of the
\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{res}}
on
\mathcal{O}(T)_{\mathrm{loc}}
preserves the subspace
\mathcal{O}(T)^{W}\subset\mathcal{O}(T)_{\mathrm{loc}}
of symmetric Laurent polynomials.

Proof..

For any
A\in\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{res}}
and
f\in\mathcal{O}(T)^{W}
it is clear that
A\circ f
can have at worst simple poles at each divisor
d_{\alpha,k}
, so it suffices to check that
\operatorname{res}_{\alpha,k}(A\circ f)=0
for all
\alpha\in\Delta_{+}
and
k\in\mathbb{Z}
. Writing

A\circ f=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{\mu\in P}\left(A_{\mu}+A_{s_{\alpha}(\mu)+k\alpha}\right)\circ f,

and observing by (
6.15
) that

\big(D^{\mu}\circ f\big)\big|_{d_{\alpha,k}}=\big(D^{s_{\alpha}(\mu)+k\alpha}\circ f\big)\big|_{d_{\alpha,k}},

we arrive at

\operatorname{res}_{\alpha,k}(A\circ f)=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{\mu\in P}\operatorname{res}_{\alpha,k}\big(A_{\mu}D^{-\mu}+A_{s_{\alpha}(\mu)+k\alpha}D^{(k-\left\langle\alpha,\mu\right\rangle)\alpha}\big)\big(D^{\mu}\circ f\big)\big|_{d_{\alpha,k}}.

The latter expression vanishes by (
6.16
), which concludes the proof.
∎

Let us introduce

\Delta(\boldsymbol{w})=\prod_{\alpha\in\Delta_{+}}\Psi_{q}(-q^{-1}w_{\alpha}).

Given a dominant weight
\mu\in\mathrm{P}^{+}
, we define an element
\widetilde{D}^{\mu}\in\mathcal{D}_{q}(T)_{loc}
by

\widetilde{D}^{\mu}=\Delta(\boldsymbol{w})D^{\mu}\Delta(\boldsymbol{w})^{-1}=d_{\mu}(\boldsymbol{w})D^{\mu},

where

d_{\mu}(\boldsymbol{w})=\prod_{\alpha\in\Delta_{+}}\prod_{k=1}^{\left\langle\alpha,\mu\right\rangle}\left(1-q^{2(k-1)}w_{\alpha}\right)^{-1}.

Recall that a weight
\mu\in\mathrm{P}^{+}
is
minuscule
if
\left\langle\alpha,\mu\right\rangle\leqslant 1
for any
\alpha\in\Delta_{+}
, so that there is no dominant weight strictly less than
\mu
in the dominance order. For
GL_{n+1}
, minuscule weights are those of the form

\omega=\omega_{k}+l\omega_{n+1},\quad 0\leq k\leq n,~l\in\mathbb{Z}

where
\omega_{j}=\sum_{i=1}^{j}\varepsilon_{i}
are the fundamental weights (and so
\omega_{0}=0
and
\omega_{n+1}
is the determinant character).
In particular, for a minuscule weight
\mu
we have

d_{\mu}(\boldsymbol{w})=\prod_{\left\langle\alpha,\mu\right\rangle=1}\left(1-w_{\alpha}\right)^{-1}.

Definition 6.9
.

An element of
\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{res}}
is said to be
minuscule
if it is contained in
\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{res}}^{\leq\omega}
for some minuscule weight
\omega
.

Any minuscule element has the form

\mathcal{R}_{\omega}[f]=\sum_{[\sigma]\in W/\mathrm{Stab}(\omega)}\sigma\left(f(\boldsymbol{w})\widetilde{D}^{\omega}\right),\quad f\in\mathcal{O}(T)^{\mathrm{Stab}(\omega)},

where we sum over coset representatives for the stabilizer
\mathrm{Stab}(\omega)\simeq S_{k}\times S_{n+1-k}
in
W\simeq S_{n+1}
of the minuscule weight
\omega=\omega_{k}+l\omega_{n+1}
. We abbreviate

\mathcal{R}_{\omega}=\mathcal{R}_{\omega}[1],

and say that the minuscule element
\mathcal{R}_{\omega}[f]
is
dressed
by the function
f
.

Example 6.10
.

Let us identify

(6.19)

\mathcal{O}(T)^{\mathrm{Stab}(\omega)}\simeq\mathbb{Z}\left[w_{1}^{\pm 1},\ldots,w_{k}^{\pm 1}\right]^{S_{k}}\otimes\mathbb{Z}\left[w_{k+1}^{\pm 1},\ldots,w_{n+1}^{\pm 1}\right]^{S_{n+1-k}}.

Then for
f=e_{k}\otimes 1
, where
e_{j}
denotes the
j
-th elementary symmetric functions, we have

\mathcal{R}_{\omega_{k}}[e_{k}\otimes 1]=\sum_{|I|=k}\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}i\in I\\
j\notin I\end{subarray}}\frac{1}{1-w_{j}/w_{i}}\prod_{i\in I}w_{i}D_{i}.

Remark 6.11
.

It is not hard to check directly that the elements
\mathcal{R}_{\omega}
generate a commutative subalgebra in
\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{res}}
as
\omega
runs over the set of all minuscule weights. In Section
6.4
we will construct a basis in
\mathbb{Z}[q^{\pm 1}]
consisting of joint eigenvectors for this subalgebra.

Lemma 6.12
.

The algebra
\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{res}}
is generated over
\mathbb{Z}[q^{\pm 1}]
by the minuscule elements.

Proof..

Given a general element
A
of
\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{res}}
, let us consider the finite set of summands
A_{\mu}
of
A
having maximal (in dominance order) internal degree as elements of
\mathcal{D}_{q}(T)_{\mathrm{loc}}
. Since
A
is Weyl-invariant, this gives us a finite set of dominant weights
\mu_{i}\in\mathrm{P}^{+}
. Define the total
height
of
A
to be the sum of the heights
\langle\rho,\mu_{i}\rangle
of each of these weights. Now fix one such weight
\mu
.
The residue conditions (
6.16
) imply that the summand
A_{\mu}
is regular except possibly at the divisors
d_{\alpha,k}
with
0\leqslant k<\left\langle\alpha,\mu\right\rangle
, at which
A
may have simple poles. Indeed, let us first assume that
k\geqslant\left\langle\alpha,\mu\right\rangle
, which implies
s_{\alpha}(\mu)+k\alpha\geqslant\mu
. Then the condition (
6.16
) together with the dominance-maximality of
A_{\mu}
among the summands of
A
implies that
\operatorname{res}_{\alpha,k}(A_{\mu})=0
. On the other hand, if
k<0
then
s_{\alpha}(\mu)+k\alpha=s_{\alpha}(\mu-k\alpha)
belongs to the
W
-orbit of the element
\mu-k\alpha>\mu
. By the
W
-invariance of
A
we have
A_{s_{\alpha}(\mu)+k\alpha}=s_{\alpha}(A_{\mu-k\alpha})=0
, and hence once again
\operatorname{res}_{\alpha,k}(A_{\mu})=0
by (
6.16
). This shows the summand
A_{\mu}
can indeed only have (simple) poles at the divisors
d_{\alpha,k}
with
0\leqslant k<\left\langle\alpha,\mu\right\rangle
.

It follows that
A_{\mu}
has the form
f(\boldsymbol{w})\widetilde{D}^{\mu}
for some
f\in\mathcal{O}(T)
. But since
\mathcal{O}(T)
is generated as an algebra by the subrings of
S_{j}\times S_{n+1-j}
-symmetric functions, there exists a linear combination of products of minuscule elements of the form

{\mathcal{R}_{\omega_{n+1}}^{|\mu|-\sum_{j}\left\langle j\alpha_{j},\mu\right\rangle}[f_{n+1}]\prod_{j=1}^{n}\mathcal{R}_{\omega_{j}}^{\left\langle\alpha_{j},\mu\right\rangle}[f_{j}]}

whose leading term coincides with
A_{\mu}
, and with all other summands having degree strictly less than
\mu
in dominance order. So subtracting this sum of products of minuscules from
A
we obtain a new element
A^{\prime}\in\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{res}}
with strictly smaller total height. Hence by induction it follows that
\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{res}}
is generated by the minuscule elements.
∎

6.3.
The algebra
\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{res}}
as a Coulomb branch

Using Lemma
6.12
we can identify the algebra
\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{res}}
with the quantized BFN Coulomb branch ring
K_{G[\![z]\!]\rtimes{\mathbb{C}}^{\times}_{2:1}}(\mathrm{Gr}_{GL_{n+1}})
associated to the pair
(G,N)=(GL_{n+1},0)
, following the setup described in
[
BFN16b
, Appendix A]
. Here
\mathrm{Gr}_{GL_{n+1}}=G(\!(z)\!)/G[\![z]\!]
refers to the affine Grassmannian of
GL_{n+1}
, and the ring structure is defined by convolution, see
[
BFN16a
,
CW18
]
for details.

As in
[
FT17
]
, we work equivariantly with respect to the double cover
\mathbb{C}^{\times}_{2:1}
of the loop rotation torus, and identify the corresponding equivariant parameter with the parameter
q
from the previous section.

Remark 6.13
.

In this paper, we use the Fock–Goncharov “integral” convention for quantization parameters, so that the quantum torus associated to the mutable sub-lattice
\Lambda_{\mathrm{mut}}
is an algebra over
\mathbb{Z}[q^{\pm 1}]
, rather than
\mathbb{Z}[q^{\pm\frac{1}{2}}]
as would be the case in the convention of Berenstein–Zelevinsky
[
BZ05
]
. This means that our
q
in the definition of
\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{res}}
(and later in the definition of
\mathbb{L}_{\mathrm{Toda}}(GL_{n+1})
) corresponds to the parameter
\nu
of
[
FT17
]
and
[
GY21
]
, and to the parameter
q^{-1}
in
[
CW18
]
. The authors of the latter paper work equivariantly with respect to a 4-fold cover
\mathbb{C}^{\times}_{4:1}
of the loop rotation group, and denote by
q^{\frac{1}{2}}
the equivariant parameter for that cover. Recall from Convention
4.17
that we lift to a when we define the quantum tori for the moduli space
\mathcal{P}^{\diamond}_{SL_{n+1},S}
.

Recall the
k
-th fundamental coweight
\omega_{k}=\sum_{r=1}^{k}\epsilon_{r}
for
G=GL_{n+1}
, and
\mathrm{Gr}^{\omega_{k}}\subset\mathrm{Gr}_{GL_{n+1}}
the corresponding closed
G[\![z]\!]
-orbit. These orbits are isomorphic to finite dimensional Grassmannians of
k
-dimensional quotients of
\mathbb{C}^{n+1}
, and each has an equivariant line bundle
\mathcal{L}_{\omega_{k}}
given by the top exterior power of the tautological vector bundle
\mathcal{V}
of quotients
\mathbb{C}^{n+1}/V
. There is a similar tautological vector bundle
\widetilde{\mathcal{V}}
whose fibers the subspaces of
\mathbb{C}^{n+1}
given by the kernel of the quotient map. For any function of
n+1
variables
f
symmetric under
S_{k}\times S_{n+1-k}
, there is a class
f(\mathcal{V},\widetilde{\mathcal{V}})
defined in the usual way, with the
i
-th elementary symmetric polynomial corresponding to
\Lambda^{i}\mathcal{V}
.

As shown in
[
BFN16a
,
BFN16b
,
FT17
]
, the localization theorem in equivariant
K
-theory provides an embedding of the convolution algebra
K_{G[\![z]\!]\rtimes\mathbb{C}^{\times}_{2:1}}(\mathrm{Gr}_{GL_{n+1}})
into a localization of the Coulomb branch ring associated to the pair
(T,0)
, where
T
is the maximal torus
T
of
GL_{n+1}
. This latter ring coincides with the algebra
\mathcal{D}_{q}(T)_{\mathrm{loc}}
defined in the previous section.

Lemma 6.14
.

(1)

Localization in equivariant
K
-theory defines an isomorphism of algebras
K_{G[\![z]\!]\rtimes\mathbb{C}^{\times}_{2:1}}(\mathrm{Gr}_{GL_{n+1}})\simeq\mathcal{D}_{res}
.

(2)

The algebra
\mathcal{D}_{res}
is generated over
\mathbb{Z}[q^{\pm 1}]
by the minuscule elements
D_{\omega_{n+1}}^{\pm 1}
and
\{\mathcal{R}_{1}[w_{1}^{m}]\,\big|\,m\in\mathbb{Z}\}
.

Proof..

In Lemma 2.18 and Corollary 2.21 of
[
CW18
]
, it is proven that the classes
\{[\mathcal{L}_{\omega_{1}}^{\otimes m}]\}_{m\in\mathbb{Z}}
, together with the inverse of the class
[\mathcal{O}_{\mathrm{Gr}_{\omega_{n+1}}}]
, generate
K_{G[\![z]\!]\rtimes\mathbb{C}^{\times}_{2:1}}(\mathrm{Gr}_{GL_{n+1}})
as an algebra over
\mathbb{Z}[q^{\pm 1}]
. Next, the image under the localization map of any class supported on a closed orbit
\mathrm{Gr}_{\omega_{k}}
can be computed explicitly as in Section 8(i) of
[
FT17
]
. In particular, the image of the class
[\mathcal{L}_{\omega_{k}}^{\otimes m}]
coincides with the minuscule element
\mathcal{R}_{k}[w_{1}^{m}]
, while the class
[\mathcal{O}_{\mathrm{Gr}_{\omega_{n+1}}}]
is mapped to
D_{\omega_{n+1}}\in\mathcal{D}_{res}
. Thus it follows that the image of the Coulomb branch ring under the localization map is contained in
\mathcal{D}_{res}
. On the other hand, letting
f
range over all
S_{k}\times S_{n+1-k}
-symmetric polynomials in the bundles
\mathcal{V},\widetilde{\mathcal{V}}
it follows that the image of
K_{G[\![z]\!]\rtimes\mathbb{C}^{\times}_{2:1}}(\mathrm{Gr}_{GL_{n+1}})
contains all minuscule elements of
\mathcal{D}_{res}
, which generate the latter as an algebra by Lemma
6.12
. This shows that the image of the Coulomb branch ring under localization is precisely
\mathcal{D}_{res}
, and hence that the latter is generated by the elements listed in point (2).
∎

A more conceptual proof of part (1) of the previous lemma for any reductive group
G
will be given in a forthcoming paper by D. Klyuev.

6.4.
Algebraic Whittaker transform

In this section we formulate an algebraic version of the Whittaker transform for the
GL_{n+1}
q-difference open Toda chain, which will identify the algebras
\mathbb{L}_{Toda}
and
\mathcal{D}_{res}
. It can be read as an algebraic counterpart to the analytic construction from
[
SS18
]
.

Let
\mathscr{D}^{n+1}
and
\mathscr{D}^{n+1;\mathrm{int}}
be respectively the spaces of all
\mathbb{Q}(q)
- and
\mathbb{Z}[q^{\pm 1}]
-valued functions on the
GL_{n+1}
weight lattice
\mathrm{P}
. When the underlying weight lattice is fixed, we shall omit the lower index and write
\mathscr{D}=\mathscr{D}^{n+1}
. Denote by
\mathscr{F}
and
\mathscr{F}^{\mathrm{int}}
their subspaces of finitely-supported functions. The following formulas define faithful representation of the quantum torus
\mathcal{T}_{GL_{n+1}}
of the initial seed of the Toda chain cluster algebra on
\mathscr{F}
:

(6.20)

(P_{\epsilon_{i}}\phi)(\mu)=-q^{-1}\phi(\mu-\varepsilon_{i}),\qquad(X_{\epsilon_{i}}\phi)(\mu)=(-q)^{2\mu_{i}+\rho_{i}}\phi(\mu),

where

\rho=(0,-1,\ldots,-n).

In particular, for a simple root
\alpha_{i}=\varepsilon_{i}-\varepsilon_{i+1}
we have

(6.21)

(X_{\alpha_{i}}\phi)(\mu)=-q^{2(\mu_{i}-\mu_{i+1})+1}\phi(\mu).

Let

(6.22)

\displaystyle\mathscr{V}^{\mathrm{int}}=\left\{\phi\in\mathscr{F}^{\mathrm{int}}\,\big|\,\mu\notin\mathrm{P}^{+}\implies\phi(\mu)=0\right\}

be the
\mathbb{Z}[q^{\pm 1}]
-submodule of functions vanishing outside the dominant cone
\mathrm{P}^{+}\subset\mathrm{P}
. We identify elements of the cone
\mathrm{P}^{+}=\{\lambda_{1}\geq\lambda_{2}\geq\cdots\geq\lambda_{n+1}\}
with integer partitions in the usual way. Clearly the space
\mathscr{V}^{\mathrm{int}}
is not preserved by the
\mathcal{T}_{GL_{n+1}}
-action on
\mathscr{F}
, since the action involves all translation operators
P_{\lambda}
, not just those corresponding to dominant
\lambda
.

Lemma 6.15
.

The
\mathbb{Z}[q^{\pm 1}]
-submodule
\mathscr{V}^{\mathrm{int}}\subset\mathscr{F}^{\mathrm{int}}
is preserved by the action of the Toda universal Laurent ring
\mathbb{L}_{Toda}(GL_{n+1})
, and defines a faithful representation of
\mathbb{L}_{Toda}(GL_{n+1})
.

Proof..

The condition for a function
\phi\in\mathscr{F}
to lie in
\mathscr{V}^{\mathrm{int}}
has the form

\mu_{i}-\mu_{i+1}<0\implies\phi(\mu)=0.

Given
A\in\mathbb{L}_{Toda}
, we expand it as

(6.23)

\displaystyle A=\sum_{\nu\in\mathrm{P}}f_{\nu}(X)P_{\nu},

so that

(A\cdot\phi)(\mu)=\sum_{\nu\in\mathrm{P}}(-q)^{|\nu|}f_{\nu}\left((-q)^{2\mu+\rho}\right)\phi(\mu-\nu).

The preservation of
\mathscr{V}^{\mathrm{int}}
by
\mathbb{L}_{Toda}
will follow if we can show that for all
\mu,\nu\in\mathrm{P}
such that
\mu\notin\mathrm{P}^{+}
and
\mu-\nu\in\mathrm{P}^{+}
we have
f_{\nu}\left((-q)^{2\mu+\rho}\right)=0
.
The automorphism part of mutation in direction
s_{i}
consists of conjugation by
\Psi(Y_{-s_{i}})=\Psi(X_{\alpha_{i}})
. Hence if
\langle\alpha_{i},\nu\rangle=\nu_{i}-\nu_{i+1}\geq 0
we see that
\operatorname{Ad}_{\Psi(X_{\alpha_{i}})}(P_{\nu})
remains a Laurent polynomial, while

(6.24)

\nu_{i}<\nu_{i+1}\implies\Psi(X_{\alpha_{i}})P_{\nu}\Psi(X_{\alpha_{i}})^{-1}=\left(\prod_{k=0}^{\nu_{i+1}-\nu_{i}-1}\frac{1}{1+q^{2k+1}X_{\alpha_{i}}}\right)P_{\nu}.

Hence
A
remains Laurent under mutation at
s_{i}
if and only if for each
\nu
with
\nu_{i}<\nu_{i+1}
, the coefficient
f_{\nu}
in (
6.23
) has the form

(6.25)

f_{\nu}(X)=g_{\nu}(X)\prod_{k=0}^{\nu_{i+1}-\nu_{i}-1}\left(1+q^{2k+1}X_{\alpha_{i}}\right)\qquad\text{with}\qquad g_{\nu}(X)\in\mathbb{Z}[q^{\pm 1}][X^{\pm 1}].

Now let
\mu,\nu\in\mathrm{P}
be such that
\mu\notin\mathrm{P}^{+}
and
\mu-\nu\in\mathrm{P}^{+}
. Then there is some
i
such that
\mu_{i}-\mu_{i+1}<0
, and the condition
\mu-\nu\in\mathrm{P}^{+}
says that
\mu_{i}-\mu_{i+1}+\nu_{i+1}-\nu_{i}\geq 0
, so we have

\nu_{i}-\nu_{i+1}\leq\mu_{i}-\mu_{i+1}<0

and by (
6.25
) we see that
\prod_{k=0}^{\mu_{i+1}-\mu_{i}-1}\left({1+q^{2k+1}X_{\alpha_{i}}}\right)
divides
f_{\nu}(X)
. So
f_{\nu}(X)
corresponds to a multiplication operator by a Laurent polynomial in the functions
(-q)^{2\mu_{i}+\rho_{i}}
divisible by

\prod_{k=1}^{\mu_{i+1}-\mu_{i}}\left({1-q^{2(\mu_{i}-\mu_{i+1}+k)}}\right)

which implies
f_{\nu}\left((-q)^{2\mu+\rho}\right)=0
as claimed. The faithfulness follows by observing that no nonzero element of
\mathcal{T}_{GL_{n+1}}
can annihilate all of
V
, which is proved by a standard argument used, for example, in
[
AC+24
, Lemma 4.1]
.
∎

The module
\mathscr{V}^{\mathrm{int}}
has a basis consisting of delta-functions
\{\delta_{\lambda}\}_{\lambda\in\mathrm{P}^{+}}
supported at a single partition
\lambda\in\mathrm{P}^{+}
:

\delta_{\lambda}(\mu)=\begin{cases}1&\text{if}\;\;\lambda=\mu,\\
0&\text{otherwise.}\end{cases}

We are going to identify
\mathscr{V}^{\mathrm{int}}
with the
\mathbb{Z}[q^{\pm 1}]
-module of symmetric Laurent polynomials in
w_{1},\ldots,w_{n+1}
using the basis
\{W_{\lambda}\}_{\lambda\in\mathrm{P}^{+}}
of
q
-Whittaker polynomials
, which can be constructed recursively as follows.

First we set up some notations for various rings of iterated formal Taylor and Laurent series. Given an ordered list of commuting indeterminates
\boldsymbol{w}=(w_{1},\ldots,w_{l})
, we consider the iterated formal Laurent series rings defined inductively by

\mathcal{K}_{w_{1}}=\mathbb{Q}(q)((w_{1})),\quad\mathcal{K}_{w_{1},\ldots,w_{l}}=\mathcal{K}_{w_{1},\ldots,w_{l-1}}((w_{l})).

Equivalently, elements of
\mathcal{K}_{\boldsymbol{w}}
are formal infinite
\mathbb{Q}(q)
-linear combinations

\psi=\sum_{I=(i_{1},\ldots,i_{l})\in\mathbb{Z}^{l}}c_{I}w_{1}^{i_{1}}\cdots w_{l}^{i_{l}},\quad c_{I}\in\mathbb{Q}(q).

where the set
I
is well-ordered with respect to the reverse-lexicographic order on
\mathbb{Z}^{l}
. Examples of elements of
\mathcal{K}_{\boldsymbol{w}}
are the series expansions of rational functions in the regime
|w_{l}|<\ldots<|w_{2}|<|w_{1}|<1
. We also consider
\mathcal{K}^{\mathrm{int}}_{\boldsymbol{w}}
where we replace the initial choice of coefficient field
\mathbb{Q}(q)
by the ring
\mathbb{Z}[q^{\pm 1}]
. Finally, we write

\mathcal{K}_{\boldsymbol{w};z}=\mathcal{K}_{\boldsymbol{w}}[[z]].

Consider the space

\mathscr{D}_{\boldsymbol{w}}=\mathscr{D}\otimes_{\mathbb{Q}(q)}\mathcal{K}_{\boldsymbol{w}}

of all, not necessarily finitely supported,
\mathcal{K}_{\boldsymbol{w}}
-valued functions on
\mathrm{P}
along with its
\mathbb{Z}[q^{\pm 1}]
-submodule of all
\mathcal{K}_{\boldsymbol{w}}^{\mathrm{int}}
-valued functions

\mathscr{D}_{\boldsymbol{w}}^{\mathrm{int}}\subset\mathscr{D}_{\boldsymbol{w}}.

Note that the action (
6.20
) of the quantum torus
\mathcal{T}_{GL_{n+1}}
on
\mathscr{F}
extends naturally to its action on
\mathscr{D}_{\boldsymbol{w}}
.

If
z
is a formal indeterminate, write
\mathcal{T}_{z}
for the ring of power series in
z
with coefficients in
\mathcal{T}_{GL_{n+1}}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}[q^{\pm 1}]}\mathbb{Q}(q)
. When
z
is a distinct indeterminate from the
\boldsymbol{w}
, elements of
\mathcal{T}_{z}
give well-defined operators on
\mathscr{D}_{\boldsymbol{w};z}
.
On the other hand, because elements of
\mathscr{D}_{\boldsymbol{w}}
do not have finite support, there is no well-defined action of
\mathcal{T}_{w_{k}}
on
\mathscr{D}_{\boldsymbol{w}}
. For example, one cannot make sense of the action of
\sum_{m\geqslant 0}w_{k}^{m}P_{j}^{m}
on the distribution
\psi(\lambda)=w_{k}^{\lambda_{j}}
. Let us set
\boldsymbol{w}=(w_{1},\ldots,w_{n+1})
and consider the subspace
\mathscr{D}^{(n,1)}_{\boldsymbol{w}}\subset\mathscr{D}^{n+1}_{\boldsymbol{w}}
consisting of distributions
\psi
for which there exists a function
M_{\psi}\colon\mathbb{Z}\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}
such that

\psi(\lambda)=\sum_{m>M_{\psi}(\lambda_{n+1})}w_{n+1}^{m}g_{\lambda,m}(w_{1},\ldots,w_{n}).

In other words, we require that for each integer
d
, the set of all exponents of
w_{n+1}
in
\psi(\lambda)
has a lower bound, as
\lambda
ranges over all partitions with
\lambda_{n+1}=d
. In particular,
\mathscr{D}^{(n,1)}_{\boldsymbol{w}}
contains functions of the form

\psi(\lambda_{1},\ldots,\lambda_{n+1})=f(\lambda_{n+1})g(\lambda_{1},\ldots,\lambda_{n})\quad\text{where}\quad f\in\mathscr{D}^{1}_{w_{n+1}}\quad\text{where}\quad g\in\mathscr{D}^{n}_{w_{1},\ldots,w_{n}}.

Note that the subalgebra
\mathcal{T}^{(n,1)}_{w_{n+1}}\subset\mathcal{T}_{w_{n+1}}
of elements commuting with
X_{n+1}
has a well-defined action on
\mathscr{D}^{(n,1)}_{\boldsymbol{w}}
. Similarly, we consider the subspace
\mathscr{D}^{(n,1)}_{\boldsymbol{w};z}\subset\mathscr{D}_{\boldsymbol{w};z}
consisting of functions
\psi
such that

\psi(\lambda)=\sum_{k\geqslant 0}\sum_{m>M_{\psi}(k,\lambda_{n+1})}z^{k}w_{n+1}^{m}g_{\lambda,m,k}(w_{1},\ldots,w_{n}).

Then
\mathscr{D}^{(n,1)}_{\boldsymbol{w};z}
has a well-defined action of the algebra
\mathcal{T}^{(n,1)}_{w_{n+1};z}
, which consists of expressions

A(w_{n+1},z)=\sum_{k\geqslant 0}z^{k}A_{k}(w_{n+1})F_{k}(P_{n+1})

where
A_{k}(w_{n+1})\in\mathcal{T}^{(n,1)}_{w_{n+1}}
and
F_{k}(P_{n+1})\in\mathbb{Q}(q)[P_{n+1}^{\pm 1}]
.

Definition 6.16
.

Define the element
R_{n}(z)\in\mathcal{T}^{(n,1)}_{z}
to be

(6.26)

\displaystyle R_{n}(z)

\displaystyle=\prod_{j=1}^{\begin{subarray}{c}n\\
\longrightarrow\end{subarray}}\Psi(zP_{j})\Psi(zq^{-1}X_{\alpha_{j}}P_{j})

(6.27)

\displaystyle=\Psi(zP_{1})\Psi(zq^{-1}X_{\alpha_{1}}P_{1})\cdots\Psi(zP_{n})\Psi(zq^{-1}X_{\alpha_{n}}P_{n}).

The
(GL_{n},GL_{n+1})
-
recursion operator
is defined by

(6.28)

\displaystyle\mathscr{R}_{n+1\leftarrow n}\colon\mathscr{D}^{n}_{w_{1},\ldots,w_{n}}\longrightarrow\mathscr{D}^{n+1}_{w_{1},\ldots,w_{n+1}},

\displaystyle\mathscr{R}_{n+1\leftarrow n}(\psi)=R_{n}(w_{n+1})\cdot(w_{n+1}^{\lambda_{n+1}}\psi).

We can compute the Taylor coefficients of
R_{n}(z)
using the following lemma:

Lemma 6.17
.

Let
U,V
generate a quantum torus
UV=q^{2}VU
, and let
z_{1},z_{2}
be formal invertible indeterminates commuting with
U
,
V
, and each other. Then

(6.29)

\displaystyle(z_{1}V;q^{2})^{-1}_{\infty}(-z_{2}UV;q^{2})^{-1}_{\infty}

\displaystyle=\sum_{m\geq 0}(-1)^{m}{q^{-m(1+m)}}\frac{(z_{1}^{-1}z_{2}U;q^{-2})_{m}}{(q^{-2};q^{-2})_{m}}z_{1}^{m}V^{m}.

Proof..

Taylor expanding both
q
-Pochhammer symbols and using that
(UV)^{k}=q^{k(1-k)}U^{k}V^{k}
, the finite sum computing the coefficient of
V^{m}
in their product is evaluated using the
q
-binomial theorem to yield the formula in the Lemma.
∎

Recalling the
q
-binomial coefficients

\binom{m}{k}_{q^{2}}=\frac{(q^{2};q^{2})_{m}}{(q^{2};q^{2})_{k}(q^{2};q^{2})_{m-k}},

it follows from the Lemma that for
\psi\in\mathscr{D}_{n;(w_{1},\ldots,w_{n})}
we have

(6.30)

(R_{n}(z)\cdot\psi)(\lambda)=\sum_{r_{1},\ldots,r_{n}\geq 0}z^{\sum_{j}r_{j}}\prod_{j=1}^{n}\binom{\lambda_{j}-\lambda_{j+1}}{r_{j}}_{q^{2}}\psi(\lambda_{1}-r_{1},\ldots,\lambda_{n}-r_{n}).

Remark 6.18
.

Since the
q
-binomial coefficients lie in
\mathbb{Z}[q^{\pm 1}]
, it follows from the formula (
6.30
) above that
\mathscr{R}_{n+1\leftarrow n}
maps
\mathscr{D}_{w_{1},\ldots,w_{n}}^{n;\mathrm{int}}
to
\mathscr{D}_{w_{1},\ldots,w_{n+1}}^{n+1;\mathrm{int}}
.

Definition 6.19
.

The
GL_{n+1}
Whittaker kernel is the element of
\mathscr{D}_{w_{1},\ldots,w_{n+1}}^{(n,1);\mathrm{int}}
given by

W^{(n+1)}=\mathscr{R}_{n+1\leftarrow n}\circ\mathscr{R}_{n\leftarrow n-1}\circ\cdots\circ\mathscr{R}_{2\leftarrow 1}(w_{1}^{\lambda_{1}}).

Lemma 6.20
.

If
\lambda\in\mathrm{P}^{+}
the series
W^{(n+1)}(\lambda)
is computed by the following subtraction-free finite sum

W^{(n+1)}(\lambda)=\sum_{\nabla\in GZ_{n+1}}\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq i\leq n\\
1\leq j\leq i\end{subarray}}\binom{\lambda^{(i+1)}_{j}-\lambda^{(i+1)}_{j+1}}{\lambda^{(i+1)}_{j}-\lambda^{(i)}_{j}}_{q^{2}}\prod_{k=1}^{n+1}w_{k}^{|\lambda^{(k)}|-|\lambda^{(k-1)}|}

over the set

GZ(\lambda)=\left\{(\lambda_{j}^{(a)})_{1\leq a\leq n+1}^{1\leq j\leq a}\,\Big|\,\lambda_{j}^{(a)}\geq\lambda_{j}^{(a-1)}\geq\lambda_{j+1}^{(a)},\;\lambda_{j}^{(n+1)}=\lambda_{j}\right\}

of all Gelfand-Zeitlin arrays with top row given by the partition
\lambda
. In particular, for dominant
\lambda
the element
W(\lambda)
lies in the semiring
\mathbb{Z}_{\geqslant 0}[q^{\pm 1}][\boldsymbol{w}]
.

Proof..

The second-to-top row in any array
\nabla\in GZ(\lambda)
gives a partition
\mu
which interlaces with
\lambda
, so that

GZ(\lambda)=\bigcup_{\mu~\text{interlacing }\lambda}GZ(\mu).

We can parametrize the set of such interlacing partitions as
\mu=(\lambda_{1}-r_{1},\lambda_{2}-r_{2},\ldots,\lambda_{n}-r_{n})
where
0\leq r_{j}\leq\lambda_{j}-\lambda_{j+1}
, and these parts are exactly the arguments of
\psi
in (
6.30
). Since
\lambda
is dominant each
\lambda_{j}-\lambda_{j+1}\geq 0
and so the Gaussian binomial coefficients in (
6.30
) vanish whenever
r_{j}>\lambda_{j}-\lambda_{j+1}
, and hence the Lemma follows by induction on
n
.
∎

Remark 6.21
.

When
\lambda\notin\mathrm{P}^{+}
is not dominant, the series computing
W^{(n+1)}(\lambda)
does not truncate and hence is a genuine formal Laurent series in
w_{2}/w_{1},\ldots,w_{n+1}/w_{n}
.

Consider the involution on
\mathcal{K}_{\boldsymbol{w}}
defined by

(6.31)

\overline{q}=q^{-1},\quad\overline{w}_{j}=w_{j},\qquad 1\leq j\leq n+1.

The natural pairing between
\mathscr{F}
and
\mathscr{D}
extends to a
\mathcal{K}_{\boldsymbol{w}}
-valued pairing

(6.32)

(\cdot,\cdot)_{\mathscr{F}}\colon\mathscr{F}\otimes_{\mathbb{Q}(q)}\mathscr{D}_{\boldsymbol{w}}\longrightarrow\mathcal{K}_{\boldsymbol{w}},\qquad(f,\psi)_{\mathscr{F}}=\sum_{\lambda\in\mathrm{P}}f(\lambda)\overline{\psi(\lambda)},

which is
\mathbb{Q}(q)
-linear in the first factor and anti-linear with respect to (
6.31
) in the second. Note that the sum in (
6.32
) is finite since
f
is compactly supported, and that we have

(6.33)

(T_{i}f,\psi)=(f,T_{i}^{-1}\psi),\qquad(q^{2\lambda_{i}}f,\psi)=(f,q^{-2\lambda_{i}}\psi),

where
T_{i}f(\lambda)=f(\lambda+\epsilon_{i})
is the translation operator.

Given a new formal indeterminate
z
, we extend the involution (
6.31
) to
\mathcal{K}_{\boldsymbol{w};z}
by declaring
\overline{z}=z
. Write
\mathscr{F}_{z}
for the space of
\mathbb{Q}(q)[[z]]
-valued functions on
\mathrm{P}
such that the the coefficient of each power of
z
is a
\mathbb{Q}(q)
-valued function with finite support.
Then (
6.32
) extends naturally to a well-defined
\mathcal{K}_{\boldsymbol{w};z}
-valued pairing
(\cdot,\cdot)_{\mathscr{F}_{z}}
between
\mathscr{F}_{z}
and
\mathscr{D}_{\boldsymbol{w};z}
.
Using (
6.33
) and the relation

\Psi_{q}(z)^{-1}=\Psi_{q^{-1}}(z)\in\mathbb{Q}(q)[[z]]

it is easy to check that for any
g\in\mathscr{D}_{\boldsymbol{w};z}
and
f\in\mathscr{F}_{z}
we have

\displaystyle\left(f,\Psi_{q}(zP_{j})^{-1}g\right)_{\mathscr{F}_{z}}

\displaystyle=\left(\Psi_{q}(zP^{-1}_{j})f,g\right)_{\mathscr{F}_{z}},

\displaystyle\left(f,\Psi_{q}(zq^{-1}X_{\alpha_{j}}P_{j+1}^{-1})g\right)_{\mathscr{F}_{z}}

\displaystyle=\left(\Psi_{q}(zq^{-1}X_{-\alpha_{j}}P_{j+1})^{-1}f,g\right)_{\mathscr{F}_{z}}.

Consider the lattice
\Lambda_{GL_{n+1};\zeta}
obtained by extending
\Lambda_{GL_{n+1}}
by a rank 1 lattice
\mathbb{Z}\zeta
. We equip it with a basis (
6.3
) of
\Lambda_{GL_{n+1}}
to which we adjoin the vector
e_{h_{-}}=-\zeta-p_{\varepsilon_{1}}
, and identify the quantum torus element
Y_{\zeta}
with the indeterminate
z
. Now recall from (
5.31
) the mutation sequence
\mu_{\mathrm{Baxter}}
used to define the fundamental Hamiltonians. Then the inverse of the automorphism part of the cluster transformation obtained by applying
\mu_{\mathrm{Baxter}}
to the extended quiver, with
e_{h}
playing the role of
a
, yields the
Baxter operator

\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{n+1}(z)=\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}^{+}_{n+1}(z)
given by

(6.34)

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}^{+}_{n+1}(z)=\Psi(zP_{1})^{-1}\prod_{j=1}^{\begin{subarray}{c}n\\
\longrightarrow\end{subarray}}\Psi(zq^{-1}X_{-\alpha_{j}}P_{j+1})^{-1}\Psi(zP_{j+1})^{-1}\in\mathcal{T}_{z}.

The analogous construction where we instead adjoin
e_{h_{+}}=-\zeta+p_{\varepsilon_{n+1}}
yields the
opposite
Baxter operator

(6.35)

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}^{-}_{n+1}(z)=\Psi(zP^{-1}_{n+1})^{-1}\prod_{j=1}^{\begin{subarray}{c}n\\
\longleftarrow\end{subarray}}\Psi(q^{-1}zX_{-\alpha_{j}}P^{-1}_{j})^{-1}\Psi(zP^{-1}_{j})^{-1}\in\mathcal{T}_{z}.

The inductive proof of the following Proposition is the same as that of the corresponding result in
[
SS18
]
, except that the computations with compact quantum dilogarithms are performed in the completed quantum torus for the extended quiver.

Proposition 6.22
.

Abbreviating
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}^{\pm}(z)=\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}^{\pm}_{n+1}(z)
, the Baxter operators have the following properties:

(1)

For commuting indeterminates
z,w
and any
\epsilon_{1},\epsilon_{2}\in\left\{\pm\right\}
we have

\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}^{\epsilon_{1}}(z)\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}^{\epsilon_{2}}(w)=\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}^{\epsilon_{2}}(w)\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}^{\epsilon_{1}}(z).

(2)

The series
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}^{\pm}(z)
are preserved by the automorphism
\tau
from (
6.4
):

\tau\left(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}(z)^{\pm}\right)=\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}^{\pm}(z).

(3)

The series
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}(z)
satisfies the difference equation

(6.36)

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}(q^{-1}z)=\mathcal{H}(z)\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}(qz),\qquad{\mathcal{H}}(z)=\sum_{k=0}^{n+1}z^{k}{H}_{k}

where the formal adjoints of the fundamental Hamiltonians
H_{k}
with respect to (
6.32
) act by the Hamiltonians of the
q
-difference open Toda chain:

(6.37)

\displaystyle H^{\star}_{k}=(-q)^{k}\sum_{|I|=k}\prod_{m\in G(I)}^{k}(1-q^{2(\lambda_{m}-\lambda_{m+1})})T_{\sum_{i\in I}\varepsilon_{i}},\qquad

where for
k
-subset
I\subseteq\{1,\ldots n+1\}
we write

G(I)=\left\{1\leq m\leq n~|~m\notin I~\text{and }m+1\in I\right\}.

(4)

In
\mathcal{T}_{GL_{n+1}}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}[q^{\pm 1}]}\mathbb{Z}((q))[[z]]
we have the infinite product formula

(6.38)

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}(z)=\prod_{m\geq 0}{\mathcal{H}}(q^{2m+1}z),

where the coefficient of
z^{k}
is a polynomial in
H_{1},\ldots,H_{n+1}
times the series expansion of a rational function in
q
.

It follows from Proposition
6.22
that the action of
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}^{\pm}(z)
on
\mathscr{F}_{z}
preserves the subspace
\mathscr{V}_{z}
of functions vanishing outside the dominant cone of partitions. Moreover, since the automorphism
\tau
preserves
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}(z)
, it follows that it also fixes each fundamental Hamiltonian. Using this observation it is straightforward to derive the following lemma, which is a special case of a more general result by Di Francesco and Kedem (see
[
DFK16
]
, Theorem 3.8):

Lemma 6.23
.

For all
m\in\mathbb{Z}
, we have

\left[\tau^{m}(A_{t_{1}}),H_{\pm 1}\right]=\pm(q-q^{-1})\tau^{m\pm 1}(A_{t_{1}}),\\

where

H_{-k}=H_{n+1-k}H_{n+1}^{-1}.

Using the formulas (
6.33
) we see that

(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{n+1}(z)f,g)_{\mathscr{F}_{z}}=(f,\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}^{\star}_{n+1}(z)g)_{\mathscr{F}_{z}},

where the conjugate
GL_{n+1}
Baxter operator
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}^{\star}_{n+1}(z)
is given by

(6.39)

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}^{\star}_{n+1}(z)=\Psi(zP_{n+1}^{-1})\prod_{j=1}^{\begin{subarray}{c}n\\
\longleftarrow\end{subarray}}\Psi(zq^{-1}X_{\alpha_{j}}P_{j+1}^{-1})\Psi(zP_{j}^{-1}).

Note that we have a recursive description

\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}^{\star}_{n+1}(z)=T_{n+1}(z)\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}^{\star}_{n}(z)

where

T_{n+1}(z)=\Psi(zP_{n+1}^{-1})\Psi(zq^{-1}X_{\alpha_{n}}P_{n+1}^{-1}),

and the recursion operator
R_{n}(z)
can be expressed as

R_{n}(z)=\left(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{n}^{-}(z)\right)^{\star}\Psi(qzX_{\alpha_{n}}P_{n}).

The following Lemma is straightforward to derive using the pentagon identity and the recursive descriptions of
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}^{\star}(z)
and
R(w)
:

Lemma 6.24
.

In
\mathcal{T}^{(n,1)}_{w_{n+1};z}
we have

\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{n+1}^{\star}(z)R_{n}(w_{n+1})=R_{n}(w_{n+1})T_{n+1}(z)\Psi(zw_{n+1}X_{\alpha_{n}})\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{n}^{\star}(z).

Another important lemma is the following:

Lemma 6.25
.

For any
g^{(n)}\in\mathscr{D}^{n}_{w_{1},\ldots,w_{n};z}
, we have

\Psi(zq^{-1}X_{\alpha_{n}}P_{n+1}^{-1})^{-1}\cdot w_{n+1}^{\lambda_{n+1}}g^{(n)}=\Psi(zw_{n+1}X_{\alpha_{n}})w_{n+1}^{\lambda_{n+1}}g^{(n)}.

Proof..

Before starting the proof, note that both sides are well-defined since each operator factor is clearly in
\mathcal{T}^{(n,1)}_{w_{n+1};z}
and
w_{n+1}^{\lambda_{n+1}}g^{(n)}
is in
\mathscr{D}^{(n,1)}_{w_{1},\ldots,w_{n+1};z}
as we can take bounding function
M(k,\lambda_{n+1})=\lambda_{n+1}
. Now as in the proof of Lemma
6.17
, we use

UV=q^{2}VU\implies(UV)^{k}=q^{k(k-1)}U^{k}V^{k}

to bring all
P_{n+1}^{-1}
factors to the right, where they act on
w_{n+1}^{\lambda_{n+1}}g^{(n)}
by
w_{n+1}
. So comparing the formulas (
3.5
) and (
3.6
) for the series expansions of the
q
-Pochhammer and its reciprocal, the Lemma follows.
∎

Now recall that the Whittaker kernel
W^{(n+1)}
is an element of
\mathscr{D}_{w_{1},\ldots,w_{n+1}}^{(n,1)}
and hence of
\mathscr{D}_{w_{1},\ldots,w_{n+1};z}^{(n,1)}
.

Proposition 6.26
.

The Whittaker kernel diagonalizes the conjugate Baxter operator: we have

(6.40)

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}^{\star}_{n+1}(z)W^{(n+1)}=W^{(n+1)}\prod_{j=1}^{n+1}\Psi(-qzw_{j}).

Proof..

We prove the proposition by induction over the rank, combining Lemmas
6.24
and
6.25
. We have

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}^{\star}_{n+1}(z)W^{(n+1)}

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}^{\star}_{n+1}(z)R_{n}(w_{n+1})\cdot w_{n+1}^{\lambda_{n+1}}W^{(n)}

\displaystyle=R_{n}(w_{n+1})T_{n+1}(z)\Psi(zw_{n+1}X_{\alpha_{n}})\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{n}^{\star}(z)\cdot w_{n+1}^{\lambda_{n+1}}W^{(n)}.

By the induction hypothesis,
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{n}^{\star}(z)
acts on
W^{(n)}
with eigenvalue
\prod_{j=1}^{n}\Psi(-qzw_{j})
. On the other hand, using Lemma
6.25
we have

\displaystyle R_{n}(w_{n+1})T_{n+1}(z)\Psi(zw_{n+1}X_{\alpha_{n}+})\cdot w_{n+1}^{\lambda_{n+1}}W^{(n)}

\displaystyle=R_{n}(w_{n+1})\Psi(zP_{n+1}^{-1})\cdot w_{n+1}^{\lambda_{n+1}}W^{(n)}

\displaystyle=\Psi(-qzw_{n+1})R_{n}(w_{n+1})\cdot w_{n+1}^{\lambda_{n+1}}W^{(n)}

\displaystyle=\Psi(-qzw_{n+1})W^{(n+1)},

and the Proposition follows.
∎

Recall the
z
-expansion (
6.36
) of the Baxter operator in the
{H}_{k}
. From it we deduce that their adjoints act on the Whittaker kernel by the multiplication operators

(6.41)

\displaystyle H^{\star}_{k}\cdot W=(-q)^{k}e_{k}(\boldsymbol{w})W,\qquad 1\leq k\leq n+1,

where
e_{k}(\boldsymbol{w})
is the
k
-th elementary symmetric function in the alphabet
\boldsymbol{w}
. In other words, we see that
H_{k}^{\star}
is the operator defining the
k
-th Pieri rule for the Whittaker kernel.

Example 6.27
.

Writing
W_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}=W(\boldsymbol{\lambda})
, the two Pieri rules for
GL_{2}
read

\displaystyle(w_{1}+w_{2})W_{\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}}

\displaystyle=\left(T_{\lambda_{1}}+\big(1-q^{2(\lambda_{1}-\lambda_{2})}\big)T_{\lambda_{2}}\right)W_{\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}}

\displaystyle=W_{\lambda_{1}+1,\lambda_{2}}+\big(1-q^{2(\lambda_{1}-\lambda_{2})}\big)W_{\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}+1},

and

w_{1}w_{2}W_{\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}}=W_{\lambda_{1}+1,\lambda_{2}+1}.

Corollary 6.28
.

For any dominant weight
\lambda\in\mathrm{P}^{+}
, the Laurent polynomial
W^{(n+1)}(\lambda)

coincides with the standard definition of the
q
-Whittaker polynomial in
(n+1)
-variables, i.e.

W^{(n+1)}(\lambda)=P^{(n+1)}_{\lambda}(q,0,\boldsymbol{w})

where
P_{\lambda}
is Macdonald’s
P
-polynomial.
In particular, for
\lambda
dominant
W(\lambda)
is symmetric in
\boldsymbol{w}
and the set
\{{W(\lambda)}\}_{\lambda\in\mathrm{P}^{+}}
forms a
\mathbb{Z}[q^{\pm 1}]
-basis for the ring of symmetric Laurent polynomials in
(w_{1},\ldots,w_{n+1})
with coefficients in
\mathbb{Z}[q^{\pm 1}]
.

Proof..

The identification follows since both collections of polynomials are uniquely characterized by the Pieri rules (
6.41
) with initial condition
W_{\emptyset}=1
; the remaining claims are standard.
∎

Definition 6.29
.

We define the
algebraic Whittaker transform
by

(6.42)

\displaystyle\mathcal{W}\colon\mathscr{V}^{\mathrm{int}}\longrightarrow\mathbb{Z}[q^{\pm 1}][\boldsymbol{w}^{\pm 1}]^{sym},\qquad f\longmapsto\sum_{\lambda\in\mathrm{P}}f(\lambda)\overline{W(\lambda)}.

Using the embeddings
\mathscr{V}^{\mathrm{int}}\hookrightarrow\mathscr{F}^{\mathrm{int}}\hookrightarrow\mathscr{F}
and
\mathbb{Z}[q^{\pm 1}][\boldsymbol{w}^{\pm 1}]^{sym}\hookrightarrow\mathscr{D}_{w_{1},\ldots,w_{n+1}}
, we can write it equivalently as

(6.43)

\displaystyle\mathcal{W}(f)=(f,W)_{\mathscr{F}}.

By Corollary
6.28
,
\mathcal{W}
is an isomorphism of
\mathbb{Z}[q^{\pm 1}]
-modules as it takes the basis
\{\delta_{\lambda}\}_{\lambda\in P^{+}}
in
\mathscr{V}^{\mathrm{int}}
to the basis
\{\overline{W(\lambda)}\}_{\lambda\in\mathrm{P}^{+}}
in
\mathbb{Z}[q^{\pm 1}][\boldsymbol{w}^{\pm 1}]^{sym}
. The Pieri rules (
6.41
) become the following intertwining relation for the Toda Hamiltonians under algebraic Whittaker transform:

(6.44)

\displaystyle\mathcal{W}\circ H_{k}=(-q)^{-k}e_{k}(\boldsymbol{w})\circ\mathcal{W},\quad\quad 1\leq k\leq n+1.

Moreover, by Lemma
6.26
it follows that the natural extension of
\mathcal{W}
to an isomorphism of
\mathbb{Q}(q)[[z]]
-modules

\mathcal{W}_{z}\colon\mathscr{V}_{z}\longrightarrow\mathbb{Q}(q)[\boldsymbol{w}^{\pm 1}]^{sym}[[z]]

satisfies the intertwining relation

(6.45)

\mathcal{W}_{z}\circ\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}(z)=\prod_{j=1}^{n+1}\Psi(-qzw^{-1}_{j})^{-1}\circ\mathcal{W}_{z}.

Since the
q
-Whittaker polynomials are
t=0
specializations of Macdonald polynomials, they also satisfy a dual set of difference equations with respect to the variables
\boldsymbol{w}
: we have

(6.46)

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}_{\omega_{k}}\cdot\overline{W}_{\lambda}=q^{2\sum_{r=1}^{k}\lambda_{r}}\overline{W_{\lambda}},\quad 1\leq k\leq n+1,

where we recall
\mathcal{R}_{\omega_{k}}=\mathcal{R}_{\omega_{k}}[1]
is the following minuscule element of
\mathcal{D}_{res}
:

\mathcal{R}_{\omega_{k}}=\sum_{|I|=k}\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}i\in I\\
j\notin I\end{subarray}}\frac{1}{1-w_{j}/w_{i}}\prod_{i\in I}D_{i}.

They also satisfy the “raising operator” identities describing the action of the excited operators

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}_{\omega_{k}}[e_{k}\otimes 1]=\sum_{|I|=k}\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}i\in I\\
j\notin I\end{subarray}}\frac{1}{1-w_{j}/w_{i}}\prod_{i\in I}w_{i}D_{i},

which read

\mathcal{R}_{\omega_{k}}[e_{k}\otimes 1]\cdot\overline{W}_{\lambda}=q^{2\sum_{r=1}^{k}\lambda_{r}}\overline{W}_{\lambda+\omega_{k}},\quad 1\leq k\leq n+1.

Hence we have intertwining relations

(6.47)

\displaystyle\mathcal{W}\circ A_{t_{k}}

\displaystyle=(-q)^{k(1-k)/2}\mathcal{R}_{\omega_{k}}\circ\mathcal{W},

(6.48)

\displaystyle\mathcal{W}\circ A_{s_{k}}

\displaystyle=(-1)^{k}(-q)^{k(1-k)/2}\mathcal{R}_{\omega_{k}}[e_{k}\otimes 1]\circ\mathcal{W}.

Consider the algebra automorphism
\widetilde{\gamma}
of
D_{q}(T)
defined on generators by

\widetilde{\gamma}(w_{i})=w_{i},\quad\widetilde{\gamma}(D_{i})={-}w_{i}D_{i},\quad i=1,\ldots,n+1.

It is easy to see that
\widetilde{\gamma}
preserves
\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{res}}
.

Proposition 6.30
.

There is a unique algebra isomorphism

(6.49)

\displaystyle\mathbb{W}\colon\mathbb{L}_{Toda}\longrightarrow\mathcal{D}_{res}

such that

(6.50)

\displaystyle\mathbb{W}(H_{k})

\displaystyle=(-q)^{-k}e_{k}(\boldsymbol{w})

(6.51)

\displaystyle\mathbb{W}(Y_{\xi_{t_{k}}})

\displaystyle=(-q)^{k(1-k)/2}\mathcal{R}_{\omega_{k}},

(6.52)

\displaystyle\mathbb{W}(Y_{\xi_{s_{k}}})

\displaystyle=(-1)^{k}(-q)^{k(1-k)/2}\mathcal{R}_{\omega_{k}}[e_{k}\otimes 1]

It satisfies the intertwining relations

(6.53)

\displaystyle\mathbb{W}\circ\tau

\displaystyle=\widetilde{\gamma}\circ\mathbb{W}

(6.54)

\displaystyle\mathbb{W}\circ\mathrm{Ad}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}^{+}(z))

\displaystyle=\prod_{j=1}^{n+1}\mathrm{Ad}(\Psi(-q^{-1}zw_{j})^{-1})\circ\mathbb{W},

(6.55)

\displaystyle\mathbb{W}\circ\mathrm{Ad}(\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}^{-}(z))

\displaystyle=\prod_{j=1}^{n+1}\mathrm{Ad}(\Psi(-qzw^{-1}_{j})^{-1})\circ\mathbb{W}.

where in the last two identities we abuse notation and use the same symbol for
\mathbb{W}
and its extension to the
z
-completions.

Proof..

Using the isomorphism
\mathcal{W}\colon V\simeq\mathbb{Z}[q^{\pm 1}][\boldsymbol{w}^{\pm 1}]^{sym}
we can identify both
\mathcal{D}_{res}
and
\mathbb{L}_{Toda}
as subrings of the endomorphism ring
\mathrm{End}_{\mathbb{Z}[q^{\pm 1}]}(\mathbb{Z}[q^{\pm 1}][\boldsymbol{w}^{\pm 1}]^{sym})
. We claim that these subrings are identical. Indeed, comparing the relations from Lemma
6.23
and the evident commutation relations between
e_{1}(\boldsymbol{w}^{\pm 1})
and
\mathcal{R}_{1}
, it follows that the action of
\tau^{m}(A_{t_{1}})
coincides up to multiplication by a power of
q
with that of the minuscule operator
\mathcal{L}_{\omega_{k}}^{\otimes m}
. Similarly, the frozen
\mathcal{A}
-variables
X_{\omega_{n+1}}
and
P_{\omega_{n+1}}
are intertwined respectively with powers of
q
times
\mathcal{R}_{{n+1}}=D_{1}\cdots D_{n+1}
and
e_{n+1}(\boldsymbol{w})
. Since the
\tau^{m}(A_{t_{1}}),X_{\pm\omega_{n+1}},P_{\pm\omega_{n+1}}
generate
\mathbb{L}_{Toda}
by the results of Goodearl and Yakimov from Section
6.1
, while the
\mathcal{L}_{\omega_{k}}^{\otimes m}
together with
\mathcal{R}_{{n+1}}
and its inverse generate
\mathcal{D}_{res}
by Lemma
6.14
, the claim follows and the construction above defines an isomorphism
\mathbb{W}\colon\mathbb{L}_{Toda}\simeq\mathcal{D}_{res}
. With (
6.50
) already having been established, it remains to prove that the relations (
6.53
) hold. Since
the
\tau^{m}(A_{s_{1}})
generate
\mathbb{L}_{Toda}
(which is a torsion-free
\mathbb{Z}[q^{\pm 1}]
-module), the relation involving
\tau
follows from the commutation relations in Lemma
6.23
. Finally, the relations involving the Baxter operators follow from the intertwining relation (
6.45
) and its analog for
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}^{-}(z)
.
∎

7.
Changing an isolating cylinder

7.1.
Bi-fundamental Baxter operators

In this section we define
bi-fundamental Baxter operators
via sequences of mutations, presented as transformations of cyclic Weyl words, as in Section
5
. For
0\leqslant k\leqslant n
and
2\leqslant j\leqslant 2(n+k)
, let us set

\mathbf{i}_{n,k;j}=\begin{cases}\mathbf{i}_{[\max(1,\lfloor j/2\rfloor-k),\min(\lfloor j/2\rfloor,n)]}&\text{if $j$ is even,}\\
\mathbf{i}_{[\min(\lfloor j/2\rfloor,n),\max(1,\lfloor j/2\rfloor+1-k)]}&\text{if $j$ is odd.}\end{cases}

We also set
\mathbf{i}_{n,k;j}=\varnothing
for
j<2
or
j>2(n+k)
.
Now, for
0\leqslant j\leqslant 2(n+k)+1
consider cyclic words

\mathbf{i}_{n,k}^{(j)}=\bar{\mathbf{i}}_{c}\mathbf{i}_{n,k;j}\mathbf{i}_{c}^{t}\mathbf{i}_{n,k;j+1},

where
\mathbf{i}_{c}=(1,\ldots,n)
. For example, for
n=3
,
k=2
we obtain the following sequence of cyclic Weyl words

\displaystyle\mathbf{i}_{n,k}^{(0)}=\mathbf{i}_{\bar{c},c^{t}},

\displaystyle\mathbf{i}_{n,k}^{(1)}=\mathbf{i}_{\bar{c},c^{t},1},

\displaystyle\mathbf{i}_{n,k}^{(2)}=\mathbf{i}_{\bar{c},1,c^{t},1},

\displaystyle\mathbf{i}_{n,k}^{(3)}=\mathbf{i}_{\bar{c},1,c^{t},1,2},

\displaystyle\mathbf{i}_{n,k}^{(4)}=\mathbf{i}_{\bar{c},1,2,c^{t},2,1},

\displaystyle\mathbf{i}_{n,k}^{(5)}=\mathbf{i}_{\bar{c},2,1,c^{t},1,2,3},

\displaystyle\mathbf{i}_{n,k}^{(6)}=\mathbf{i}_{\bar{c},1,2,3,c^{t},3,2},

\displaystyle\mathbf{i}_{n,k}^{(7)}=\mathbf{i}_{\bar{c},3,2,c^{t},2,3},

\displaystyle\mathbf{i}_{n,k}^{(8)}=\mathbf{i}_{\bar{c},2,3,c^{t},3},

\displaystyle\mathbf{i}_{n,k}^{(9)}=\mathbf{i}_{\bar{c},3,c^{t},3},

\displaystyle\mathbf{i}_{n,k}^{(10)}=\mathbf{i}_{\bar{c},3,c^{t}},

\displaystyle\mathbf{i}_{n,k}^{(11)}=\mathbf{i}_{\bar{c},c^{t}}.

Note that the length of words
\mathbf{i}_{n,k}^{(j)}
increases as
j
runs from 1 to
2k+1
, stays constant for
2k+1\leqslant j\leqslant 2n
, and decreases as
j
runs from
2n+1
to
2(n+k)+1
. The maximal length is
2(n+k)+1
if
k<n
and is
2(n+k)
for
k=n
.

We now define word transformations
\Phi_{n,k}^{(j+1)}\colon\mathbf{i}_{n,k}^{(j)}\to\mathbf{i}_{n,k}^{(j+1)}
for all
0\leqslant j\leqslant 2(n+k)
. For
2k+1\leqslant j\leqslant 2n-1
the transformation
\Phi_{n,k}^{(j+1)}
consists of two commuting steps. The first step transforms the word
\mathbf{i}_{n,k;j+1}\mathbf{i}_{\bar{c}}
into
\mathbf{i}_{\bar{c}}\mathbf{i}_{n,k;j+1}
, and consists of
\mathrm{len}(\mathbf{i}_{n,k;j+1})
shuffles. The second one transforms
\mathbf{i}_{n,k;j}\mathbf{i}_{c}^{t}
into
\mathbf{i}_{c}^{t}\mathbf{i}_{n,k;j+2}
, and consists of
\mathrm{len}(\mathbf{i}_{n,k;j})
braid moves. It is easy to see that when
j
is odd, the mutations within each step commute as well. Now, for
j\geqslant 2n
we define
\Phi_{n,k}^{(j+1)}
is a similar way, except for one of the braid moves is replaced with a
(n,n)\to n
merge. For
j\leqslant 2k
the definition is similar again, but one of the braid moves is replaced with an
insertion

1\to(1,1)
, inverse to the merge
(1,1)\to 1
. Finally, in the case
n=k
, the transformation
\Phi_{n,k}^{(2n+1)}
contains both an insertion
1\to(1,1)
and a merge
(n,n)\to n
. Finally, we set

\Phi_{n,k}=\Phi_{n,k}^{2(n+k)+1}\circ\ldots\circ\Phi_{n,k}^{(1)}.

Quiver

(on the left) and its image under
\boldsymbol{\mu}_{3,2}
(on the right).

Using the dictionary between Weyl words and quivers described in Section
5
,we can now present the transformation
\Phi_{n,k}
as a sequence of mutations
\boldsymbol{\mu}_{n,k}
. Let
Q_{n,k}
be the quiver with
2n+2k+1
vertices, which contains
Q^{n}_{\mathrm{Toda}}
and
Q^{k}_{\mathrm{Toda}}
as subquivers, and such that its remaining vertex is connected to the bottom two vertices of
Q^{n}_{\mathrm{Toda}}
and the top two vertices of
Q^{k}_{\mathrm{Toda}}
as shown on Figure
44
. Then
\boldsymbol{\mu}_{n,k}
mutates the subquiver
Q^{k}_{\mathrm{Toda}}
from the bottom of the
Q^{n}_{\mathrm{Toda}}
to its top, producing a quiver isomorphic to
Q_{n,k}
.

Definition 7.1
.

We call
\boldsymbol{\mu}_{n,k}
the
bi-fundamental Baxter mutation sequence.

Remark 7.2
.

The mutation sequence (
5.31
) is recovered as
\boldsymbol{\mu}_{n,0}
.

To help us visualize the bi-fundamental Baxter operator, consider Figure
45
. We start by positioning the nodes of
Q_{n,k}
, as shown on the left pane of the Figure. After we have applied the bi-fundamental Baxter operator
\boldsymbol{\mu}_{n,k}
the nodes will reach their final positions indicated on the right pane. After applying the subsequence
\boldsymbol{\mu}_{n,k}^{(j)}
, corresponding to the word transformation
\Phi_{n,k}^{(j)}
, we move the node with the smallest label, which has not yet reached its final position, one step along the path formed by yellow arrows. If this position its occupied, we move the node occupying it by one step as well, and so on. For example, after the very first mutation at node
5
we shall be moving nodes 1 up to 6. This way, after we have applied
\boldsymbol{\mu}_{n,k}^{(j)}\circ\ldots\circ\boldsymbol{\mu}_{n,k}^{(1)}
, the Weyl word spelled by the arrows in the
n
horizontal lines containing 4 nodes each is
\mathbf{i}_{n,k}^{(j)}
.

.

Remark 7.3
.

Tracking the mutation sequence
\boldsymbol{\mu}_{n,k}
, one notices that when we are about to mutate at a node labelled
i
, the only two nodes with arrows pointing to it are those with labels
i\pm 1
.

The sequence
\mu_{n,k}
can also be encoded in the following neat combinatorial way. Consider the parallelogram on Figure
46
made of copies of vertices of
Q_{n,k}
. Then the sequences of mutations in
\mu_{n,k}
can be obtained by reading the columns of Figure
46
left to right. We number the columns, so that the left most one has humber 1. Mutations within odd columns commute. Within each even column, highlighted by dashed rectangles, we read circled vertices bottom to top, and the non-circled ones top to bottom. Sequences of mutations at circled and non-circled vertices at a given even column commute as well. Mutations at circled vertices of column
j
correspond to insertions, braid moves, and merges in the word transformation
\Phi_{n,k}^{(j)}
, while those at the non-circled ones correspond to shuffles. For example, we can factor the bi-fundamental Baxter operator
\boldsymbol{\mu}_{3,2}
into 11
columns

\boldsymbol{\mu}_{3,2}=\boldsymbol{\mu}_{3,2}^{(11)}\circ\ldots\circ\boldsymbol{\mu}_{3,2}^{(1)},

each itself factored as

\mu_{3,2}^{(j)}=\mu_{3,2}^{(j),\bullet}\circ\mu_{3,2}^{(j),\circ}

where

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\mu}_{3,2}^{(1)}

\displaystyle=\mu_{5},

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\mu}_{3,2}^{(2)}

\displaystyle=\mu_{6}\circ\mu_{4},

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\mu}_{3,2}^{(3)}

\displaystyle=\mu_{5}\circ\mu_{7}\mu_{3},

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\mu}_{3,2}^{(4)}

\displaystyle=\mu_{4}\mu_{8}\circ\mu_{6}\mu_{2},

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\mu}_{3,2}^{(5)}

\displaystyle=\mu_{3}\mu_{7}\circ\mu_{9}\mu_{5}\mu_{1},

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\mu}_{3,2}^{(6)}

\displaystyle=\mu_{2}\mu_{6}\mu_{10}\circ\mu_{8}\mu_{4},

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\mu}_{3,2}^{(7)}

\displaystyle=\mu_{5}\mu_{9}\circ\mu_{11}\mu_{7}\mu_{3},

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\mu}_{3,2}^{(8)}

\displaystyle=\mu_{4}\mu_{8}\circ\mu_{10}\mu_{6},

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\mu}_{3,2}^{(9)}

\displaystyle=\mu_{7}\circ\mu_{9}\mu_{5},

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\mu}_{3,2}^{(10)}

\displaystyle=\mu_{6}\circ\mu_{8},

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\mu}_{3,2}^{(11)}

\displaystyle=\mu_{7}.

Remark 7.4
.

Using above observations on the commutativity of certain mutations in the sequence
\boldsymbol{\mu}_{n,k}
, it is easy to see that we can refactor
\boldsymbol{\mu}_{n,k}
as

\boldsymbol{\mu}_{n,k}=\tilde{\boldsymbol{\mu}}_{n,k}^{2n+2k+1}\circ\ldots\circ\tilde{\boldsymbol{\mu}}_{n,k}^{(1)},

where

\tilde{\boldsymbol{\mu}}_{n,k}^{(j)}=\boldsymbol{\mu}_{n,k}^{(j+1),\bullet}\boldsymbol{\mu}_{n,k}^{(j),\circ},

and
\boldsymbol{\mu}_{n,k}^{(2n+2k+2),\bullet}=1
. Let us also notice that by reflecting the parallelogram on Figure
46
across the horizontal axis, see Figure

, and applying the same recipe, we obtain equivalent factorization of the same cluster transformation
\boldsymbol{\mu}_{n,k}
.
