In the remainder of this section, we make extensive use of tropical cluster transformations. We start with the following useful result.

Lemma 7.5
.

Let
\mathrm{y}_{i}
denote the tropical cluster variables of the quiver
Q_{n,k}
, and
\mathrm{y}^{\prime}_{i}
be the image of
\mathrm{y}_{i}
under the tropicalization of
\boldsymbol{\mu}_{n,k}^{-1}
. Then we have

\mathrm{y}^{\prime}_{i}=\begin{cases}\mathrm{y}_{i+2n+1}&\text{if}\;\;i<2k+1,\\
\mathrm{y}_{[1,2(n+k)+1]}^{-1}&\text{if}\;\;i=2k+1,\\
\mathrm{y}_{i-2k-1}&\text{if}\;\;i>2k+1,\end{cases}

where
\mathrm{y}_{[j,k]}=\prod_{r=j}^{k}\mathrm{y}_{r}
.

Proof..

Denote by
\mathrm{y}_{i}^{(j)}
the image of
\mathrm{y}_{i}
under the tropicalization of the first
j
columns of
\boldsymbol{\mu}_{n,k}^{-1}
, so that
\mathrm{y}^{(0)}=\mathrm{y}
and
\mathrm{y}^{(2n+2k+1)}=\mathrm{y}^{\prime}
.
Chasing the tropical variables through the above description of the bi-fundamntal Baxter operator we find that

\mathrm{y}_{i}^{(j)}=\begin{cases}\mathrm{y}_{i}&\text{if $j<|2n+1-i|$,}\\
\mathrm{y}_{\left[n+\frac{i-j+1}{2},n+\frac{i+j+1}{2}\right]}&\text{if $i-j$ is odd, $|2n+1-i|\leqslant j\leqslant 2(n+k)-|2k+1-i|$,}\\
\mathrm{y}_{\left[n+1+\frac{i-j}{2},n+\frac{i+j}{2}\right]}^{-1}&\text{if $i-j$ is even, $|2n+1-i|\leqslant j-1\leqslant 2(n+k)-|2k+1-i|$,}\\
\mathrm{y}_{2n+1+i}&\text{if $i\leqslant 2k$, $j\geqslant 2n+1+i$,}\\
\mathrm{y}_{i-2k-1}&\text{if $i\geqslant 2k+2$, $j\geqslant 2n+4k+3-i$,}\end{cases}

which yields the desired result.
∎

Definition 7.6
.

Let
\sigma_{n,k}
be the unique, non-trivial for
n=k
, permutation identifying quivers
Q_{n,k}
and
\boldsymbol{\mu}_{n,k}(Q_{n,k})
. We define the
Baxter twist
to be the following cluster transformation

\beta_{n,k}=\sigma_{n,k}\circ\boldsymbol{\mu}_{n,k}.

Corollary 7.7
.

The Baxter twist
\beta_{n,k}
commutes with the Dehn twists
\tau_{n}
,
\tau_{k}
from formula (
5.27
). Moreover, we have

\beta_{n,k}^{2}=\tau_{n}^{-k-1}\tau_{k}^{-n-1}.

Proof..

The statements follow from a computation with tropical variables as in the proof of Lemma
7.5
and Theorem
3.10
.
∎

Recall that a cluster transformation
\boldsymbol{\mu}
is called a
DT-transformation
if its tropicalization
\boldsymbol{\mu}^{t}
satisfies
\boldsymbol{\mu}^{t}(\mathrm{y}_{i})=\mathrm{y}_{1}^{-1}
for all tropical variables
\mathrm{y}_{i}
. The following corollary is proved in the same as the one above.

Corollary 7.8
.

The cluster transformation

\tau_{n}^{-n-1}\tau_{k}^{-k-1}\circ\beta_{n,k}^{-1}=\tau_{n}^{k-n}\tau_{k}^{n-k}\circ\beta_{n,k}

is the DT-transformation for the quiver
Q^{n,k}_{\mathrm{Toda}}
.

Now consider the lattice

\Lambda_{n,k}=\Lambda_{GL_{n+1}}\oplus\Lambda_{GL_{k+1}}\oplus\mathbb{Z}[\zeta],

together with a skew-symmetric bilinear form, such that the three direct summands are orthogonal to each other and the embeddings
\Lambda_{GL_{n+1}}\hookrightarrow\Lambda_{n,k}
and
\Lambda_{GL_{k+1}}\hookrightarrow\Lambda_{n,k}
are isometries. Let

\mathrm{P}_{n,k}\simeq\mathrm{P}_{GL_{n+1}}\oplus\mathrm{P}_{GL_{k+1}}

be the weight lattice for the group
GL_{n+1}\times GL_{k+1}
. For a pair
(\lambda,\mu)\in P_{n,k}
we define the following elements of
\Lambda_{n,k}
:

(7.1)

p_{(\lambda,\mu)}=(p_{\lambda},p_{\mu},0)\qquad x_{(\lambda,\mu)}=(x_{\lambda},x_{\mu},0).

We equip
\Lambda_{n,k}
with the basis, obtained as the union of bases (
6.3
) for
\Lambda_{GL_{n+1}}
and
\Lambda_{GL_{k+1}}
and the vector
e_{h}=p_{(-\epsilon_{1},\epsilon_{k+1})}-\zeta
. The resulting quiver is isomorphic to
Q_{n,k}
.

As in the previous section, let
\mathscr{D}^{n,k}
be the space of all
\mathbb{Q}(q)
-valued functions on
\mathrm{P}_{n,k}
,
\mathscr{F}^{n,k}\subset\mathscr{D}^{n,k}
be its subspace of compactly supported functions, and
\mathscr{V}^{n,k}\subset\mathscr{F}^{n,k}
be the subspace of those vanishing outside of the positive cone, that is

\mathscr{V}^{n,k}=\big\{\phi\in\mathscr{F}^{n,k}\,\big|\,\phi(\lambda,\mu)=0\;\;\text{for any}\;\;(\lambda,\mu)\notin\mathrm{P}^{+}_{n,k}\big\}.

As before, the integral versions of the above spaces are obtained by replacing the
\mathbb{Q}(q)
-valued functions with the
\mathbb{Z}[q^{\pm 1}]
-valued ones. We then set

\mathscr{A}^{n,k}_{\boldsymbol{w},\boldsymbol{u}}=\mathscr{A}^{n,k}\otimes_{\mathbb{Q}(q)}\mathcal{K}_{\boldsymbol{w},\boldsymbol{u}}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\mathscr{A}^{n,k}_{\boldsymbol{w},\boldsymbol{u};z}=\mathscr{A}^{n,k}\otimes_{\mathbb{Q}(q)}\mathcal{K}_{\boldsymbol{w},\boldsymbol{u};z}

where
\mathscr{A}=\mathscr{D},\mathscr{F},\mathscr{V}
.

Write
\mathcal{T}^{n,k}
for the quantum torus defined by
\Lambda_{n,k}
. We denote by
P_{(\lambda,\mu)}
and
X_{(\lambda,\mu)}
its elements corresponding to the vectors (
7.1
). We also write
\mathcal{T}^{n,k}_{z}
for the space of Taylor series in
z
with coefficients in
\mathcal{T}^{n,k}
. Evidently, the action of
\mathcal{T}^{n,k}
on
\mathscr{D}^{n,k}
preserves the subspace
\mathscr{F}^{n,k}
, and the same is true for the action of
\mathcal{T}^{n,k}_{z}
on
\mathscr{D}^{n,k}_{z}
.

Definition 7.9
.

The
bi-fundamental Baxter operator

\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{n,k}\in\mathcal{T}^{n,k}_{z}
is the inverse of the product of quantum dilogarithms that defines the automorphism part
\mu_{n,k}^{\sharp}
of the cluster transformation
\mu_{n,k}
.

Identifying
z
with
Y_{\zeta}
we have
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{n,k}=\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{n,k}(z)\in\mathcal{T}^{n,k}_{z}
. We now write an explicit formula for its conjugate
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}^{\star}_{n,k}(z)
. First, we define

\displaystyle u_{(0,s)}

\displaystyle=p_{(0,\epsilon_{s})}+x_{(0,\alpha_{s})}

\displaystyle\hskip-73.97733pt\text{for}\qquad 1\leqslant s\leqslant k,

\displaystyle u_{(s,0)}

\displaystyle=p_{(\epsilon_{s},0)}-x_{(\alpha_{s-1},0)}

\displaystyle\hskip-73.97733pt\text{for}\qquad 2\leqslant s\leqslant n+1,

and set

A^{+}(a,b\,|\,k)=\prod_{j}^{\longrightarrow}\Psi\left(zY_{a_{(0,j)}-b_{(j-k,0)}}\right),\qquad A^{-}(a,b\,|\,k)=\prod_{j}^{\longleftarrow}\Psi\left(zY_{a_{(0,j)}-b_{(j-k,0)}}\right),

where
a,b\in\left\{u,p\right\}
, we abbreviate
p_{k}=p_{\epsilon_{k}}
, and both products are taken over all
j
such that both indices
j
and
j-k
are within the range. For example, we have

A^{+}(u,u\,|\,k-1)=1\qquad\text{and}\qquad A^{-}(p,p\,|\,k)=\Psi\left(zP_{(-\epsilon_{1},\epsilon_{k+1})}\right).

Then using Remark
7.3
, we can write the adjoint to the bi-fundamental Baxter operator as

\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}^{\star}_{n,k}(z)=\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{n,k}^{\star,(2n+2k+1)}(z)\circ\ldots\circ\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{n,k}^{\star,(1)}(z),

where

\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}_{n,k}^{\star,(j)}(z)=\begin{cases}A^{+}\left(u,u\,|\,k-(j+1)/2\right)A^{-}\left(p,p\,|\,k-(j-1)/2\right)&\text{if $j$ is odd,}\\
A^{+}\left(p,u\,|\,k-j/2\right)A^{-}\left(u,p\,|\,k-j/2\right)&\text{if $j$ is even.}\end{cases}

Using Remark
7.4
we can also rewrite the operator
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}^{\star}_{n,k}(z)
as

(7.2)

\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}^{\star}_{n,k}(z)=\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}}_{n,k}^{\star,(2n+2k+1)}(z)\circ\ldots\circ\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}}_{n,k}^{\star,(1)}(z),

where

\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}}_{n,k}^{\star,(j)}(z)=\begin{cases}A^{+}\left(p,u\,|\,k-(j+1)/2\right)A^{-}\left(p,p\,|\,k-(j-1)/2\right)&\text{if $j$ is odd,}\\
A^{+}\left(u,u\,|\,k-(j+2)/2\right)A^{-}\left(u,p\,|\,k-j/2\right)&\text{if $j$ is even.}\end{cases}

Consider the product

W^{(n+1,k+1)}(\boldsymbol{w},\boldsymbol{u})=W^{(n+1)}(\boldsymbol{w})W^{(k+1)}(\boldsymbol{u})\in\mathscr{D}^{n,k}_{\boldsymbol{w},\boldsymbol{u}}

of the two Whittaker kernels. We are now ready to prove the following generalization of Proposition
6.26
.

Proposition 7.10
.

The Whittaker kernel
W^{(n,k)}(\boldsymbol{w},\boldsymbol{u})
diagonalizes the conjugate bi-fundamental Baxter operator:

(7.3)

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}^{\star}_{n,k}(z)W^{(n+1,k+1)}(\boldsymbol{w},\boldsymbol{u})=W^{(n+1,k+1)}(\boldsymbol{w},\boldsymbol{u})\prod_{r=1}^{n+1}\prod_{s=1}^{k+1}\Psi(zw_{r}/u_{s}).

Proof..

We shall prove the equality (
7.3
) by induction on
n
. For
n=0
the desired result is equivalent to that of Proposition
6.26
. Now recall the equality

W^{(n+1)}(\boldsymbol{w})=R_{n}(w_{n+1})w_{n+1}^{\lambda_{n+1}}W^{(n)}(\boldsymbol{w}^{\prime}),

where
\boldsymbol{w}^{\prime}=(w_{1},\ldots,w_{n})
, as well as factorization (
7.2
). Applying pentagon relation over and over again one commutes each factor of
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}^{\star}_{n,k}
to the right past
R_{n}(w_{n+1})
arriving at the relation

\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}^{\star}_{n,k}(z)R_{n}(w_{n+1})=R_{n}(w_{n+1})\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}^{\star}_{0,k}(zP_{(-\epsilon_{n+1},0)})F_{n,k}\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}^{\star}_{n-1,k}(z),

where the factor
F_{n,k}
preserves the product
w_{n+1}^{\lambda_{n+1}}W^{(n,k+1)}(\boldsymbol{w}^{\prime},\boldsymbol{u})
thanks to Lemma
6.25
. Thus, we have

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}^{\star}_{n,k}(z)W^{(n+1,k+1)}(\boldsymbol{w},\boldsymbol{u})

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}^{\star}_{n,k}(z)R_{n}(w_{n+1})w_{n+1}^{\lambda_{n+1}}W^{(n,k+1)}(\boldsymbol{w}^{\prime},\boldsymbol{u})

\displaystyle=R_{n}(w_{n+1})\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}^{\star}_{0,k}(zP_{(-\epsilon_{n+1},0)})F_{n,k}\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}^{\star}_{n-1,k}(z)w_{n+1}^{\lambda_{n+1}}W^{(n,k+1)}(\boldsymbol{w}^{\prime},\boldsymbol{u}).

Applying the induction hypothesis, we arrive at

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}^{\star}_{n,k}(z)W^{(n+1,k+1)}(\boldsymbol{w},\boldsymbol{u})

\displaystyle=R_{n}(w_{n+1})w_{n+1}^{\lambda_{n+1}}W^{(n,k+1)}(\boldsymbol{w}^{\prime},\boldsymbol{u})\prod_{r=1}^{n+1}\prod_{s=1}^{k+1}\Psi(zw_{r}/u_{s})

\displaystyle=W^{(n+1,k+1)}(\boldsymbol{w},\boldsymbol{u})\prod_{r=1}^{n+1}\prod_{s=1}^{k+1}\Psi(zw_{r}/u_{s}).

∎

Corollary 7.11
.

We have the following equalitites:

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}^{\star}_{n,k}(z)W^{(n+1,k+1)}(\boldsymbol{w},\boldsymbol{u})

\displaystyle=\prod_{j=1}^{n+1}\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}^{\star}_{0,k}(zP_{(-\epsilon_{j},0)})W^{(n+1,k+1)}(\boldsymbol{w},\boldsymbol{u})

\displaystyle=\prod_{j=1}^{k+1}\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}^{\star}_{n,0}(zP_{(0,\epsilon_{j})})W^{(n+1,k+1)}(\boldsymbol{w},\boldsymbol{u}).

Corollary 7.12
.

The coefficient of
z^{k}
in the power series
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{Q}}^{\star}_{n,k}(z)
can be expressed as a polynomial in the
GL_{n+1}\times GL_{k+1}
Toda Hamiltonians
H_{j}^{(n)}H_{l}^{(k)}
with coefficients in
\mathbb{Q}(q)
.

7.2.
Refined umbral moves

Let
c
be a closed simple curve on a marked surface
S
,
\underline{\Delta}
a
c
-isolating triangulation of
S
,
e\in\underline{\Delta}
a non-boundary shadow of
c
, and
C\subset\underline{\Delta}
the
c
-isolating cylinder. Denote by
S^{\prime}
the result of cutting
S
along
c
. As usual, we let
c_{\pm}\subset S^{\prime}
be the new pair of tacked circles, consider the triangulation
\Delta^{\prime}=\mathcal{C}_{c}(\Delta)
of
S^{\prime}
, and denote by
C_{\pm}
the isolating cylinders of
c_{\pm}
. Furthermore, we assume that the arc
e_{+}\in\underline{\Delta}^{\prime}
corresponding to
e\in\underline{\Delta}
is a shadow of
c_{+}
, and thus belongs to
C_{+}
. The goal of this section is to prepare ground for the proof of Theorem
8.34
, which states that Whittaker transforms
\mathbb{W}_{c}
intertwine cluster transformations, which relate different
c
- or
c_{\pm}
-isolating clusters on
S
and
S^{\prime}
. To that end we introduce
refined umbral moves
and factor them in a way, which will allow us to commute them through the Whittaker transform.

Definition 7.13
.

The
refined umbral moves
are the compositions

F_{c;e}=\Phi_{F_{C;e}(\underline{\Delta});c}\circ F_{C;e}\circ\Phi_{\underline{\Delta};c}^{-1},\qquad\qquad F_{c_{+};e_{+}}=\Phi_{F_{C_{+};e_{+}}(\underline{\Delta}^{\prime});c_{+}}\circ F_{C_{+};e_{+}}\circ\Phi_{\underline{\Delta}^{\prime};c_{+}}^{-1},

where
F_{C;e}
,
F_{C_{+};e_{+}}
are the umbral moves, see Definition
2.33
,
\Phi_{\underline{\Delta};c}
is the transformation defined in (
5.18
), and
\Phi_{\underline{\Delta}^{\prime};c_{+}}
is the one defined in (
5.22
).
Opposite refined umbral moves
are defined in a similar fashion.

First, let us recall that each umbral move is a composition of 3 flips. Consider edges
e,e^{1},e^{2}
of
\underline{\Delta}
, and
e_{+},e_{+}^{1},e_{+}^{2}
of
\underline{\Delta}^{\prime}
such that

F_{C;e}=F_{e^{2}}F_{e^{1}}F_{e}\qquad\text{and}\qquad F_{C;e_{+}}=F_{e_{+}^{2}}F_{e_{+}^{1}}F_{e_{+}}.

For
1\leqslant k\leqslant 6
define quivers

\displaystyle Q_{c;e;1}

\displaystyle=Q_{F_{C;e}(\underline{\Delta});c},

\displaystyle Q_{c;e;2}

\displaystyle=Q_{F_{C;e}(\underline{\Delta})},

\displaystyle Q_{c;e;3}

\displaystyle=Q_{F_{e^{1}}F_{e}(\underline{\Delta})},

\displaystyle Q_{c;e;4}

\displaystyle=Q_{F_{e}(\underline{\Delta})},

\displaystyle Q_{c;e;5}

\displaystyle=Q_{\underline{\Delta}},

\displaystyle Q_{c;e;6}

\displaystyle=Q_{\underline{\Delta};c}.

We denote the cluster transformations sending
Q_{c;e;k}
to
Q_{c;e;j}
by
\boldsymbol{\mu}_{c;e;j\,\circlearrowleft\,k}
if
j<k
and by
\boldsymbol{\mu}_{c;e;j\,\circlearrowright\,k}
otherwise, so that
\boldsymbol{\mu}_{c;e;j\,\circlearrowleft\,k}=\boldsymbol{\mu}_{c;e;k\,\circlearrowright\,j}^{-1}
. For example we have
\boldsymbol{\mu}_{c;e;2\,\circlearrowleft\,5}=F_{C;e}
and
\boldsymbol{\mu}_{c;e;1\,\circlearrowleft\,6}=F_{c;e}
. We define quivers
Q_{c_{+};e_{+};k}
and transformations
\boldsymbol{\mu}_{c_{+};e_{+};j\,\circlearrowleft\,k}
in a similar fashion. For the case when
S^{\prime}_{+}
is a cylinder with a tack on one boundary component and 2 marked points on the other, we show quivers
Q^{5}_{c_{+};e_{+};k}
,
1\leqslant k\leqslant 6
, on Figures
49
–
54
. Here and in what follows the upper index
n
in the notation for the quiver is the rank of the group
G
, defining the character variety.

We now define quivers
Q_{c;e;k}
and
Q_{c_{+};e_{+};k}
for
7\leqslant k\leqslant 13
, and place the 13 quivers on the 13-hour clock face. Quivers
Q^{5}_{c_{+};e_{+};k}
are shown on Figures
55
–
61
. Quivers
Q^{n}_{c;e;k}
may be obtained in the following way. First, define quivers
Q^{n}_{c;k}
by setting
Q^{n}_{c;k}\simeq\widehat{Q}^{n}_{\mathrm{Toda}}
for
k=7,8,9
or
k=13
and
Q^{n}_{c;k}\simeq\mu_{n,0}(\widehat{Q}^{n}_{\mathrm{Toda}})
for
k=10,11,12
, see Figure
48
. Next, recall quiver
Q^{n}_{\underline{\Delta};c_{-}}
, where
\underline{\Delta}
consists of a single self-folded triangle, see the left pane of Figure
40
. Finally, amalgamate vertices
c_{n}
of
Q^{n}_{c_{+};e_{+};k}
and
(n,n-1)
of
Q^{n}_{\underline{\Delta};c_{-}}
with vertices
h_{\pm}
of
Q^{n}_{c;k}
, and erase vertices labelled
h_{k}
and
d_{k}
in
Q^{n}_{c_{+};e_{+};k}
and the corresponding vertices in
Q^{n}_{\underline{\Delta};c_{-}}
labelled respectively
(n,n-1+k)
and
(n+1,n-1+k)
.

h_{-}
s_{1}
t_{1}
s_{2}
t_{2}
s_{3}
t_{3}
s_{4}
t_{4}
s_{5}
t_{5}
h_{+}
h_{-}
s_{1}
t_{1}
s_{2}
t_{2}
s_{3}
t_{3}
s_{4}
t_{4}
s_{5}
t_{5}
h_{+}

Figure 48.
Quivers
Q^{5}_{c;k}
for
k=7,8,9,13
(on the left) and
k=10,11,12
(on the right).

For the remainder of this section we abbreviate
Q_{c_{+};e_{+};k}
to
Q_{k}
. As above we let
\boldsymbol{\mu}_{j\,\circlearrowleft\,k}
and
\boldsymbol{\mu}_{j\,\circlearrowright\,k}
denote the cluster transformations relating the
Q_{k}
and
Q_{j}
, depending on whether
Q_{k}
precedes or follows
Q_{j}
as we traverse the face of the clock in the clock-wise fashion. Each time, having defined
\boldsymbol{\mu}_{k+1\,\circlearrowright\,k}
we shall also explain how to modify it to obtain
\boldsymbol{\mu}_{c;e;k+1\,\circlearrowright\,k}
.

Recall the quiver
Q_{\Delta}
corresponding to the standard bicolored graph on a single triangle
\Delta
, see Figure
16
. Let us label vertices of
Q_{\Delta}
by a triple of numbers
(i,j,k)
reflecting the co-distance of the corresponding face of the bicolored graph to the three vertices of
\Delta
. In particular, we have
i+j+k=n
for each vertex. Let
A
be the vertex of
\Delta
, co-distance to which is measured by the first number in the triple
(i,j,k)
. Then we define the transformation
\Phi_{A;*}
as follows:

\Phi_{\Delta;A}=\prod_{r=1}^{\begin{subarray}{c}n-1\\
\longrightarrow\end{subarray}}\prod_{s=1}^{\begin{subarray}{c}r\\
\longrightarrow\end{subarray}}\prod_{j=1}^{r-s+1}\mu_{(n-r+s,r-s-j,j)}.

We remark that mutations under the third product commute. Applying
\Phi_{\Delta;A}
to the quiver
Q_{\Delta}
and swapping positions of the nodes
(j,n-j,0)
and
(n-j,0,j)
for all
1\leqslant j\leqslant n
we recover the quiver
Q_{\Delta}
with all arrows inverted. The quiver
Q_{6}
contains a subquiver obtained by amalgamating
Q_{\Delta}
to
\Phi_{c}(Q_{\mathrm{sf}})
along
e
. We define the quiver
Q_{7}=\Phi_{\Delta;A}(Q_{6})
, where
A
is the vertex of
\Delta
opposite the side
e
, that is
\Phi_{\Delta;A}
starts and ends with a mutation at the node
c_{1}
in the notations of Figure
55
. We set
\boldsymbol{\mu}_{7\,\circlearrowright\,6}=\Phi_{\Delta;A}
, and define
\boldsymbol{\mu}_{c;e;7\,\circlearrowright\,6}
in an identical way, replacing
\Phi_{c}(Q_{\mathrm{sf}})
with
\Phi_{c}(Q_{\mathrm{cyl}})
, see Figure
37
.

We now recall the transformation
\Phi_{2}
from Section
5.2.2
and consider its close cousin
\overline{\Phi}_{2}\colon\mathbf{i}_{\overline{w}_{0},c^{*}}\to\mathbf{i}_{-c,c}
. The
(j+1,k+1)
-st step of the latter only differs from that of
\Phi_{2}
in starting with the shuffle
(k+1,-k-1)\mapsto(-k-1,k+1)
instead of its inverse. Note that
Q_{7}
contains a subquiver
Q^{n-1}_{\overline{w}_{0},c^{*}}
, where the upper index shows the number of the letters in the alphabets
\mathfrak{A}_{\pm}
. On Figure
37
this is the subquiver formed by vertices in rows
n+1
to
2n-1
counting from the bottom. We now set
Q_{7;0}=Q_{7}
and define
Q_{7;1}=\overline{\Phi}_{2}(Q_{7;0})
. Then the mutable vertices of
Q_{7;1}
positioned in the rows
n
to
2n-3
form the subquiver
Q^{n-2}_{\overline{w}_{0},c}
, which allows us to apply
\Phi_{2}
. The resulting quiver
Q_{7;2}=\Phi_{2}(Q_{7;1})
again contains a subquiver
Q^{n-3}_{\overline{w}_{0},c^{*}}
in the rows
n-1
to
2n-5
and we may proceed by induction. We then define

Q^{n}_{8}=Q^{n}_{7;n-2}=\boldsymbol{\mu}_{8\,\circlearrowright\,7}(Q^{n}_{7}),

where
\boldsymbol{\mu}_{8\,\circlearrowright\,7}
is an alternating composition of transformations
\overline{\Phi}_{2}
and
\Phi_{2}
, starting with
\overline{\Phi}_{2}
on
n-1
letters and dropping the number of letters by 1 with each factor. The quiver
Q^{5}_{8}
is shown on Figure
56
. The transformation
\boldsymbol{\mu}_{c;e;8\,\circlearrowright\,7}
is identical to
\boldsymbol{\mu}_{8\,\circlearrowright\,7}
.

Quiver
Q^{n}_{8}
contains subquivers
Q^{k}_{\mathrm{Toda}}
for all
1\leqslant k\leqslant n-1
. We define
\boldsymbol{\mu}_{9\,\circlearrowright\,8}
as the following composition of commuting Dehn twists

\boldsymbol{\mu}_{c;e;9\,\circlearrowright\,8}=\boldsymbol{\mu}_{9\,\circlearrowright\,8}=\prod_{k=1}^{n-1}\tau_{k}^{-k-1}.

Let us clarify the shape of quiver
Q^{n}_{9}
: in the notations of Figure
57
we have

\displaystyle(e_{t_{k,1}},e_{r_{k}})

\displaystyle=a_{n}(k)+1,

\displaystyle(e_{t_{k,k-1}},e_{l_{k}})

\displaystyle=a_{n}(k),

\displaystyle(e_{s_{k,1}},e_{r_{k}})

\displaystyle=-a_{n}(k),

\displaystyle(e_{s_{k,k-1}},e_{l_{k}})

\displaystyle=1-a_{n}(k),

where

a_{n}(k)=\begin{cases}k-1&\text{if $n-k$ is even,}\\
1&\text{if $n-k$ is odd.}\\
\end{cases}

Note that
Q^{n}_{9}
still contains subquivers
Q^{k}_{\mathrm{Toda}}
for
1\leqslant k\leqslant n-1
and recall that bi-fundamental Baxter operators
\boldsymbol{\mu}_{n,k}
. We then set

\boldsymbol{\mu}_{10\,\circlearrowright\,9}=\boldsymbol{m}_{10\,\circlearrowright\,9}\circ\prod_{j=1}^{n+1}\boldsymbol{\mu}^{-1}_{n-1,0}(h_{j})\circ\prod_{k=1}^{n-1}\boldsymbol{\mu}_{k,k-1}^{-1}

where the Baxter mutation sequence
\boldsymbol{\mu}^{-1}_{n-1,0}(h_{j})
is applied to the subquiver formed by
Q^{n-1}_{\mathrm{Toda}}
the node
h_{j}
, and we understand
h_{n+1}=c_{n}
. The quasi-permutation
\boldsymbol{m}_{10\,\circlearrowright\,9}
is characterized by being identity on all cluster variables but the
Y_{d_{j}}
and the condition

\boldsymbol{m}_{10\,\circlearrowright\,9}(Y_{d_{j}}^{(10)})=Y_{d_{j}}^{(9)},

where
Y_{\ell}^{(k)}
denotes the cluster variable labelled by the node
\ell
in
Q_{k}
. In turn, the quiver
Q^{n}_{c;e;9}
contains
Q^{k}_{\mathrm{Toda}}
for
1\leqslant k\leqslant n
and we set

\boldsymbol{\mu}_{c;e;10\,\circlearrowright\,9}=\prod_{k=1}^{n}\boldsymbol{\mu}_{k,k-1}^{-1}.

As is demonstrated on Figures
56
and
58
, the quivers
Q_{8}
and
Q_{10}
contain isomorphic mutable subquivers, same applies to
Q_{c;e;8}
and
Q_{c;e;10}
. Thus, we may set
\boldsymbol{\mu}_{11\,\circlearrowright\,10}=\boldsymbol{\mu}_{7\,\circlearrowleft\,8}=\boldsymbol{\mu}_{8\,\circlearrowright\,7}^{-1}
and
\boldsymbol{\mu}_{c;e;11\,\circlearrowright\,10}=\boldsymbol{\mu}_{c;e;7\,\circlearrowleft\,8}
.

The transformation
\boldsymbol{\mu}_{12\,\circlearrowright\,11}
is in fact very similarly to
\boldsymbol{\mu}_{8\,\circlearrowright\,7}
. Namely, we have already noticed that
Q^{n}_{11}
contains a subquiver, isomorphic to
Q^{n-1}_{\overline{w}_{0},c^{*}}
. Skipping the bottom row of the latter, that is considering only rows
n+2
to
2n-1
as shown on Figure
59
, we obtain the subquiver
Q^{n-2}_{\overline{w}_{0},c^{*}}
. Thus, we may set
Q^{n}_{11;0}=Q^{n}_{11}
and define
Q^{n}_{11;1}=\overline{\Phi}_{2}^{(n-2)}(Q^{n}_{11;0})
. The latter quiver in turn contains a subquiver
Q^{n-4}_{\overline{w}_{0},c}
and we proceed as in the definition of
\boldsymbol{\mu}_{8\,\circlearrowright\,7}
. We then define

Q^{n}_{12}=Q^{n}_{11;\left\lfloor\frac{n-2}{2}\right\rfloor}=\boldsymbol{\mu}_{12\,\circlearrowright\,11}(Q^{n}_{11}),

where
\boldsymbol{\mu}_{12\,\circlearrowright\,11}
is an alternating composition of transformations
\overline{\Phi}_{2}
and
\Phi_{2}
, starting with
\overline{\Phi}_{2}
on
n-1
letters and dropping the number of letters by 1 with each factor. The quiver
Q^{5}_{12}
is shown on Figure
60
.

Observe that the quiver
Q^{n}_{12}
contains subquivers
Q^{n-2k}_{\mathrm{Toda}}
for
1\leqslant k\leqslant\left\lfloor\frac{n}{2}\right\rfloor
. We define

\boldsymbol{\mu}_{13\,\circlearrowright\,12}=\boldsymbol{m}_{13\,\circlearrowright\,12}\circ\prod_{j=1}^{n+1}\boldsymbol{\mu}^{-1}_{n-2,0}(h_{j})\circ\prod_{k=1}^{\left\lfloor\frac{n-2}{2}\right\rfloor}\boldsymbol{\mu}_{n-2k,n-2k-2}^{-1}\circ\prod_{k=1}^{\left\lfloor\frac{n}{2}\right\rfloor}\tau_{n-2k}^{2k-n-1},

where the Baxter mutation sequence
\boldsymbol{\mu}^{-1}_{n-1,0}(h_{j})
is applied to the subquiver formed by
Q^{n-1}_{\mathrm{Toda}}
the node
h_{j}
, and the quasi-permutation
\boldsymbol{m}_{13\,\circlearrowright\,12}
is defined similarly to
\boldsymbol{m}_{10\,\circlearrowright\,9}
.
The transformation
\boldsymbol{\mu}_{c;e;13\,\circlearrowright\,12}
is defined by a simpler formula:

\boldsymbol{\mu}_{c;e;13\,\circlearrowright\,12}=\prod_{k=1}^{\left\lfloor\frac{n}{2}\right\rfloor}\left(\boldsymbol{\mu}_{n+2-2k,n-2k}^{-1}\circ\tau_{n-2k}^{2k-n-1}\right).

Finally, we observe that quivers
Q_{1}
and
Q_{11}
have isomorphic mutable subquivers, see Figures
49
and
59
, which allows us to complete the clock face by setting

\boldsymbol{\mu}_{13\,\circlearrowleft\,1}=\boldsymbol{\mu}_{c;e;12\,\circlearrowright\,11}.

This way we have

\boldsymbol{\mu}_{13\,\circlearrowleft\,1}(Q_{1})=Q_{13}.

Using similar considerations, we have

\boldsymbol{\mu}_{c;e;13\,\circlearrowleft\,1}(Q_{c;e;1})=Q_{c;e;13}\qquad\text{with}\qquad\boldsymbol{\mu}_{c;e;13\,\circlearrowleft\,1}=\boldsymbol{\mu}_{c;e;12\,\circlearrowright\,11}.

Proposition 7.14
.

We have equalities of cluster transformations

F_{c;e}=\boldsymbol{\mu}_{c;e;1\,\circlearrowright\,6}\qquad\text{and}\qquad F_{c_{+};e_{+}}=\boldsymbol{\mu}_{c_{+};e_{+};1\,\circlearrowright\,6}.

Proof..

We prove only the second equality, as the proof of the first one is similar. By the definition of the refined umbral move and that of transformations
\boldsymbol{\mu}_{k\,\circlearrowleft\,j}
, we have
F_{c_{+};e_{+}}=\boldsymbol{\mu}_{1\,\circlearrowleft\,6}
. Thus it suffices to show that

\boldsymbol{\mu}_{k\,\circlearrowleft\,j}=\boldsymbol{\mu}_{k\,\circlearrowright\,j}

for all, equivalently any,
1\leqslant j\neq k\leqslant 13
. Let us chose
j=9
and
k=13
. By Theorem
3.10
, it suffices to chase tropical variables through transformations
\boldsymbol{\mu}_{13\,\circlearrowleft\,9}
and
\boldsymbol{\mu}_{13\,\circlearrowright\,9}
, which is done in Lemmas
7.15
–
7.23
and Lemmas
7.24
–
7.27
respectively. Proofs of these Lemmas are combinatorial exercises, similar in spirit to that of Lemma
7.5
, and we omit them for brevity. Comparing the tropical variables of quivers
\boldsymbol{\mu}_{13\,\circlearrowright\,9}(Q_{9})
and
\boldsymbol{\mu}_{13\,\circlearrowleft\,9}(Q_{9})
completes the proof.
∎

Figure 61.
Quiver
Q_{13}^{5}
.

For the remainder of this section we write
\mathrm{y}_{f}
for the tropical variable, corresponding to the node labelled
f
in
Q_{9}
. Let us also introduce the following notations: for
j<k
we set

v_{k,j}^{(a,b)}=\mathrm{y}_{s_{k;j}}^{a}\mathrm{y}_{t_{k;j}}^{b}.\qquad\text{and}\qquad v_{k,j}^{a}=v_{k,j}^{(a,a)}

We also declare

v_{k+1,0}=v_{k,k}=\mathrm{y}_{c_{k}},\qquad\text{and}\qquad v_{0,0}=v_{1,0}=t_{1;1}=s_{1;1}=1.

Then for
k_{1}\leqslant k_{2}
,
1\leqslant j_{1}<k_{1}
,
1\leqslant j_{2}<k_{2}
we set

v_{[k_{1},j_{1}|k_{2},j_{2}]}=\prod_{r=j_{1}}^{k_{1}}v_{k_{1},r}\cdot\prod_{s=k_{1}+1}^{k_{2}-1}\prod_{r=1}^{s}v_{s,r}\cdot\prod_{r=1}^{j_{2}}v_{k_{2},r}.

Furthermore, we fix

h_{n+1}=c_{n}\qquad\text{and}\qquad v_{n+1,j}=h_{n+1-j}/h_{n+2-j},

so that

h_{n+1-j}=v_{[n,n|n+1,j]}.

Lemma 7.15
.

Images
\mathrm{y}_{\ell}^{(8)}=\boldsymbol{\mu}^{t}_{8\,\circlearrowleft\,9}(\mathrm{y}_{\ell})
of the tropical variables
\mathrm{y}_{\ell}
of
Q^{n}_{9}
under the tropical cluster transformation
\boldsymbol{\mu}^{t}_{8\,\circlearrowleft\,9}
may be written as follows. For all
k
we have

\mathrm{y}^{(8)}_{c_{k}}=\mathrm{y}_{c_{k}},\qquad\mathrm{y}^{(8)}_{h_{k}}=\mathrm{y}_{h_{k}},\qquad\mathrm{y}^{(8)}_{d_{k}}=\mathrm{y}_{d_{k}},\qquad\mathrm{y}^{(8)}_{t_{k,j}}=v_{k,j}^{(-k,1-k)},\\
\qquad\mathrm{y}^{(8)}_{s_{k,j}}=v_{k,j}^{(k+1,k)}.

Then

\mathrm{y}^{(8)}_{r_{k}}=\mathrm{y}_{r_{k}}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\mathrm{y}^{(8)}_{l_{k}}=\mathrm{y}_{l_{k}}\cdot\begin{cases}v_{k,k-1}^{(k,k-1)}&\text{if $n-k$ is even,}\\
\prod_{j=1}^{k-1}v_{k,j}^{(k+1-j,k-j)}&\text{if $n-k$ is odd.}\end{cases}

Lemma 7.16
.

Images
\mathrm{y}_{\ell}^{(7)}=\boldsymbol{\mu}^{t}_{7\,\circlearrowleft\,9}(\mathrm{y}_{\ell})
of the tropical variables
\mathrm{y}_{\ell}
of
Q^{n}_{9}
under the tropical cluster transformation
\boldsymbol{\mu}^{t}_{7\,\circlearrowleft\,9}
may be written as follows. For
\leqslant k\leqslant n+1
we have

\mathrm{y}^{(7)}_{h_{k}}=\mathrm{y}_{h_{k}},\qquad\mathrm{y}^{(7)}_{d_{k}}=\mathrm{y}_{d_{k}}.

For
1\leqslant k\leqslant n
we have

\mathrm{y}^{(7)}_{r_{k}}=\mathrm{y}_{r_{k}}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\mathrm{y}^{(7)}_{l_{k}}=\mathrm{y}_{l_{k}}\cdot\prod_{j=1}^{k-1}\left(v_{k-1,j}^{j-1}v_{k,j}^{(k+a_{n}(k)-j,k+a_{n}(k)-1-j)}\right).

For
1\leqslant k\leqslant n-1
we have

\mathrm{y}^{(7)}_{c_{k}}=v_{[k,k|k+1,k-1]},\qquad\mathrm{y}^{(7)}_{l_{k+1,k}}=v_{k+1,k}^{(-a_{n}(k+1)-1,-a_{n}(k+1))},\qquad\mathrm{y}^{(7)}_{r_{n,k}}=v_{n,k}^{(n+1,n)}.

For
2\leqslant k\leqslant n-1
,
1\leqslant j\leqslant k-1
we have

\displaystyle\mathrm{y}^{(7)}_{l_{k+1,j}}

\displaystyle=v_{[k,j+1|k+1,j-1]}^{-1}v_{k+1,j}^{(-a_{n}(k)-2,-a_{n}(k)-1)},

\displaystyle\mathrm{y}^{(7)}_{r_{k,j}}

\displaystyle=v_{k,j}^{(a_{n}(k)+2,a_{n}(k)+1)}v_{[k,j+1|k+1,j-1]}^{-1}.

Lemma 7.17
.

Images
\mathrm{y}_{\ell}^{(6)}=\boldsymbol{\mu}^{t}_{6\,\circlearrowleft\,9}(\mathrm{y}_{\ell})
of the tropical variables
\mathrm{y}_{\ell}
of
Q^{n}_{9}
under the tropical cluster transformation
\boldsymbol{\mu}^{t}_{6\,\circlearrowleft\,9}
may be written as follows. For
1\leqslant k\leqslant n
we have

\mathrm{y}^{(6)}_{h_{k}}=\mathrm{y}_{h_{k}},\qquad\mathrm{y}^{(6)}_{d_{k}}=\mathrm{y}_{d_{k}}.

For
1\leqslant k\leqslant n
we have

\mathrm{y}^{(6)}_{r_{k}}=\mathrm{y}_{r_{k}}\cdot v_{[k,1|k,k]}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\mathrm{y}^{(6)}_{l_{k}}=\mathrm{y}_{l_{k}}\cdot\prod_{j=1}^{k-1}\left(v_{k-1,j}^{j}v_{k,j}^{(k+a_{n}(k)-j,k+a_{n}(k)-1-j)}\right).

Now,

\mathrm{y}^{(6)}_{c_{k}}=\begin{cases}\mathrm{y}_{c_{n}}&\text{if $k=1$,}\\
v_{[n+2-k,0|n+2-k,n-k]}&\text{if $1<k\leqslant(n+1)/2$,}\\
v_{[k-1,n-k|k,n-k-1]}^{-1}&\text{if $k>(n+1)/2$.}\end{cases}

Then

\mathrm{y}^{(6)}_{l_{k,j}}=\begin{cases}v_{[k-1,j|k,j-1]}^{-1}v_{k,j}^{(-a_{n}(k)-1,-a_{n}(k))}&\text{if $1\leqslant j<k$,}\\
v_{[1,1|n,1]}\prod_{r=2}^{n}v_{r,1}^{(a_{n}(r)+2,a_{n}(r)+1)}&\text{if $j=k=1$,}\\
v_{[k,k|n+2-k,k-1]}\prod_{r=k+1}^{n+1-k}v_{r,k}^{(a_{n}(r)+2,a_{n}(r)+1)}&\text{if $j=k>1$,}\\
v_{n,k}^{(n+1,n)}&\text{if $j>k=1$,}\\
v_{n+1-k,j}^{(a_{n}(n+1-k)+2,a_{n}(n+1-k)+1)}v_{[n+1-k,j+1|n+2-k,j-1]}&\text{if $2\leqslant k<j$.}\end{cases}

Finally,

\mathrm{y}^{(6)}_{r_{k,j}}=\begin{cases}v_{k,j}^{(a_{n}(k)+1,a_{n}(k))}v_{[k,j+1|k+1,j]}&\text{if $n-k<j<k$,}\\
v_{k,j}^{(a_{n}(k)+1,a_{n}(k))}&\text{if $j=n-k<k$,}\\
v_{[k+1,k+1|n+1-k,k-1]}^{-1}\prod_{r=k+1}^{n+1-k}v_{r,k}^{(-a_{n}(k)-2,a_{n}(k)-1)}&\text{if $j=k$,}\\
v_{n+1-k,j}^{(-a_{n}(n+1-k)-1,-a_{n}(n+1-k))}&\text{if $j=n-k>k$,}\\
v_{[n-k,j+1|n+1-k,j-1]}^{-1}v_{n+1-k,j}^{(-a_{n}(n+1-k)-2,-a_{n}(n+1-k)-1)}&\text{if $k<j<n-k$.}\\
\end{cases}

Lemma 7.18
.

Images
\mathrm{y}_{\ell}^{(5)}=\boldsymbol{\mu}^{t}_{5\,\circlearrowleft\,9}(\mathrm{y}_{\ell})
of the tropical variables
\mathrm{y}_{\ell}
of
Q^{n}_{9}
under the tropical cluster transformation
\boldsymbol{\mu}^{t}_{5\,\circlearrowleft\,9}
may be written as follows.
We have

\mathrm{y}^{(5)}_{l_{k,k}}=v_{[k,k|n+2-k,k-1]}\prod_{r=k+1}^{n+1-k}v_{r,k}^{(a_{n}(r)+2,a_{n}(r)+1)},

\mathrm{y}^{(5)}_{c_{1}}=v_{[n+1,0|n+1,n-1]},

and

\mathrm{y}^{(5)}_{h_{k}}=\begin{cases}\mathrm{y}_{h_{n}}&\text{if $k=n$,}\\
v_{[n,n+2-k|n+1,n-k]}^{-1}&\text{if $k<n$.}\\
\end{cases}

For all other nodes
f
of
Q^{n}_{5}
the tropical variables are expressed by the same exact formulas as in the previous Lemma:
\mathrm{y}^{(5)}_{f}=\mathrm{y}^{(6)}_{f}
.

Lemma 7.19
.

Images
\mathrm{y}_{\ell}^{(4)}=\boldsymbol{\mu}^{t}_{4\,\circlearrowleft\,9}(\mathrm{y}_{\ell})
of the tropical variables
\mathrm{y}_{\ell}
of
Q^{n}_{9}
under the tropical cluster transformation
\boldsymbol{\mu}^{t}_{4\,\circlearrowleft\,9}
may be written as follows. For
1\leqslant k\leqslant n
we have

\mathrm{y}^{(4)}_{r_{k}}=\mathrm{y}_{r_{k}}\cdot v_{[k,1|k,k]},\qquad\mathrm{y}^{(4)}_{l_{k}}=\mathrm{y}_{l_{k}}\cdot\prod_{j=1}^{k-1}\left(v_{k-1,j}^{j}v_{k,j}^{(n+a_{n}(k)-j,n+a_{n}(k)-1-j)}\right).

Then

\mathrm{y}^{(4)}_{c_{k}}=\begin{cases}v_{[k,k|k+1,k]}&\text{if $k\leqslant n$,}\\
v_{[n,2n+1-k|n+1,2n-k]}^{-1}&\text{if $k>n$}\end{cases}

and

\mathrm{y}^{(4)}_{d_{k}}=\mathrm{y}_{d_{k}}.

Finally for all
1\leqslant j<k\leqslant n
we have

\displaystyle\mathrm{y}^{(4)}_{l_{k,j}}

\displaystyle=v_{[k-1,j|k,j-1]}^{-1}v_{k,j}^{(-a_{n}(k)-1,-a_{n}(k))},

\displaystyle\mathrm{y}^{(4)}_{r_{k,j}}

\displaystyle=v_{k,j}^{(a_{n}(k)+1,a_{n}(k))}v_{[k,j+1|k+1,j]}^{-1}.

Lemma 7.20
.

Images
\mathrm{y}_{\ell}^{(3)}=\boldsymbol{\mu}^{t}_{3\,\circlearrowleft\,9}(\mathrm{y}_{\ell})
of the tropical variables
\mathrm{y}_{\ell}
of
Q^{n}_{9}
under the tropical cluster transformation
\boldsymbol{\mu}^{t}_{3\,\circlearrowleft\,9}
may be written as follows. For
1\leqslant k\leqslant n
we have

\mathrm{y}^{(3)}_{d_{n+1-k}}=\mathrm{y}_{d_{n+1-k}}\cdot v_{[k,k|n+1,k]}\prod_{j=k+1}^{n}v_{j,k}^{(-a_{n}(j)-1,-a_{n}(j))}.

The remainder of the formulas coincide with those in the previous Lemma.

Lemma 7.21
.

Images
\mathrm{y}_{\ell}^{(2)}=\boldsymbol{\mu}^{t}_{2\,\circlearrowleft\,9}(\mathrm{y}_{\ell})
of the tropical variables
\mathrm{y}_{\ell}
of
Q^{n}_{9}
under the tropical cluster transformation
\boldsymbol{\mu}^{t}_{2\,\circlearrowleft\,9}
may be written as follows. For
1\leqslant k\leqslant n
we have

\mathrm{y}^{(2)}_{d_{k}}=\mathrm{y}_{d_{k}},\qquad\mathrm{y}^{(2)}_{l_{k}}=\mathrm{y}_{l_{k}}\cdot v_{[k,0|k,k-1]},\qquad\mathrm{y}^{(2)}_{r_{k}}=\mathrm{y}_{r_{k}}\cdot\prod_{j=1}^{k-1}v_{k,j}^{(a_{n}(k)+2,a_{n}(k)+1)}\prod_{j=0}^{k-1}v_{k+1,j}^{k+1-j}.

Then

\mathrm{y}^{(2)}_{c_{k}}=\begin{cases}v_{[k,1|k+1,1]}^{-1}&\text{if $k\leqslant(n+1)/2$,}\\
v_{[k,2k-n-1|k+1,2k-n-1]}&\text{if $k>(n+1)/2$.}\end{cases}

and

\mathrm{y}^{(2)}_{h_{k}}=\begin{cases}v_{[n,k|n+1,k-1]}^{-1}&\text{if $k<n$,}\\
v_{[n,n-1|n+1,n]}^{-1}&\text{if $k=n$.}\end{cases}

Finally, we have

\mathrm{y}^{(2)}_{l_{k,j}}=\begin{cases}v_{[k-1,k-j-1|k,k-j-1]}\cdot v_{k,k-j}^{(a_{n}(k),a_{n}(k)-1)}&\text{if}\;\;j<\min(k-1,n+1-k),\\
v_{k,1}^{(a_{n}(k),a_{n}(k)-1)}&\text{if}\;\;j=k-1\leqslant n+1-k,\\
\prod_{r=1}^{k-j-1}v_{[k-1,r|k,r]}\cdot v_{[k,1|k,k-j]}^{(a_{n}(k),a_{n}(k)-1)}&\text{if}\;\;j=n+1-k<k-1,\\
v_{k,j+k-n-1}^{(-a_{n}(k),1-a_{n}(k))}&\text{if}\;\;j=k-1>n+1-k,\\
v_{[k-1,j+k-n-1|k,j+k-n-2]}^{-1}\cdot v_{k,k+j-n-1}^{(-a_{n}(k)-1,-a_{n}(k))}&\text{if}\;\;n+1-k<j<k-1,\end{cases}

and

\mathrm{y}^{(2)}_{r_{k,j}}=\begin{cases}v_{k,k-j}^{(-a_{n}(k),1-a_{n}(k))}\cdot v_{[k,k-j+1|k+1,k-j+1]}^{-1}&\text{if}\;\;j<n+1-k,\\
v_{[k,1|k,k-j]}^{(-a_{n}(k),1-a_{n}(k))}\cdot\prod_{r=1}^{k-j+1}v_{[k,r|k+1,r]}^{-1}&\text{if}\;\;j=n+1-k,\\
v_{k,k+j-n-1}^{(a_{n}(k)+1,a_{n}(k))}\cdot v_{[k,k+j-n|k+1,k+j-n-1]}&\text{if}\;\;j>n+1-k.\end{cases}

Lemma 7.22
.

Images
\mathrm{y}_{\ell}^{(1)}=\boldsymbol{\mu}^{t}_{1\,\circlearrowleft\,9}(\mathrm{y}_{\ell})
of the tropical variables
\mathrm{y}_{\ell}
of
Q^{n}_{9}
under the tropical cluster transformation
\boldsymbol{\mu}^{t}_{1\,\circlearrowleft\,9}
may be written as follows. For
1\leqslant k\leqslant n
we have

\mathrm{y}^{(1)}_{h_{k}}=v_{n,n-1}\cdot\mathrm{y}_{h_{k}}.

We also have

\mathrm{y}^{(1)}_{c_{n}}=v_{[n,n-1|n,n]}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\mathrm{y}^{(1)}_{r_{n,1}}=v_{[n,1|n,n-1]}^{(-n,1-n)}v_{n,n}^{-1}\cdot\prod_{r=1}^{n}v_{[n,r|n+1,r]}^{-1}.

Finally, for
2\leqslant k\leqslant n-1
we have

\mathrm{y}^{(1)}_{r_{n,k}}=v_{n,k-1}^{(n,n-1)}.

The remainder of the formulas coincide with those in the previous Lemma.

Lemma 7.23
.

Images
\mathrm{y}_{\ell}^{(13)}=\boldsymbol{\mu}^{t}_{13\,\circlearrowleft\,9}(\mathrm{y}_{\ell})
of the tropical variables
\mathrm{y}_{\ell}
of
Q^{n}_{9}
under the tropical cluster transformation
\boldsymbol{\mu}^{t}_{13\,\circlearrowleft\,9}
may be written as follows. Let

u_{n,k}=v_{[k,2k-n|k,k]}^{n-2k-1}\cdot\prod_{r=1}^{2k-n}v_{k+1,r}^{r+n-2k-1}.

Then for
k>(n+2)/2
we have

\displaystyle\mathrm{y}^{(13)}_{l_{k,n+1-k}}

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}v_{[k-1,2k-n-2|k,2k-n-2]}\cdot v_{[k,2k-n-2|k,2k-n-1]}^{(k-1,k-2)},&\text{if $n-k$ is even,}\\
v_{[k-1,2k-n-2|k,0]}\cdot\prod_{r=1}^{k-2}v_{k,r}^{(k+a_{n}(k)-r-2,k+a_{n}(k)-r-3)},&\text{if $n-k$ is odd.}\end{cases}

\displaystyle\mathrm{y}^{(13)}_{r_{k,n+1-k}}

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}\prod_{r=1}^{2k-n-1}v_{k,r}^{(-r-k,-r-k+1)}\cdot u_{n,k},&\text{if $n-k$ is even,}\\
\prod_{r=2k-n-2}^{2k-n-1}v_{k,r}^{(-r-2,-r-1)}\cdot u_{n,k},&\text{if $n-k$ is odd,}\end{cases}

while for
k=(n+2)/2

\displaystyle\mathrm{y}^{(13)}_{r_{k,n+1-k}}

\displaystyle=v_{k,1}^{(-a_{n}(k)-2,-a_{n}(k)-1)}\cdot u_{n,k},

\displaystyle\mathrm{y}^{(13)}_{l_{k,n+1-k}}

\displaystyle=v_{k,1}^{(a_{n}(k),a_{n}(k)-1)}.

Then for
k>(n+1)/2
we have

\mathrm{y}^{(13)}_{c_{k}}=v_{[k,2k-n-1|k,k]}.

Finally, for
1\leqslant k<n/2
we have

\displaystyle\mathrm{y}^{(13)}_{s_{n+1-2k,j}}

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}v_{n+1-k,j}^{(n+1-k,n-k)}&\text{if $k$ is odd,}\\
v_{n+1-k,j}^{(n-k,n-k-1)}&\text{if $k$ is even,}\end{cases}

\displaystyle\mathrm{y}^{(13)}_{t_{n+1-2k,j}}

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}v_{n+1-k,j}^{(k-n,k-n+1)}&\text{if $k$ is odd,}\\
v_{n+1-k,j}^{(k-n+1,k-n+2)}&\text{if $k$ is even.}\end{cases}

The remainder of the formulas coincide with those in the previous Lemma.

Lemma 7.24
.

Images
\mathrm{y}_{\ell}^{(10)}=\boldsymbol{\mu}^{t}_{10\,\circlearrowright\,9}(\mathrm{y}_{\ell})
of the tropical variables
\mathrm{y}_{\ell}
of
Q^{n}_{9}
under the tropical cluster transformation
\boldsymbol{\mu}^{t}_{10\,\circlearrowleft\,9}
may be written as follows. For
1\leqslant k\leqslant n
we have

\mathrm{y}^{(10)}_{s_{k;j}}=\mathrm{y}_{s_{k;k-j}},\qquad\mathrm{y}^{(10)}_{t_{k;j}}=\mathrm{y}_{t_{k;k-j}},\qquad\mathrm{y}^{(10)}_{c_{k}}=v_{[k,1|k+1,k]}^{-1},\qquad\mathrm{y}^{(10)}_{h_{k}}=v_{[n,1|n+1,k-1]}^{-1},

and

\mathrm{y}^{(10)}_{d_{k}}=\mathrm{y}_{d_{k}}.

Then

\displaystyle\mathrm{y}^{(10)}_{l_{k}}

\displaystyle=\mathrm{y}_{l_{k}}\cdot\prod_{j=1}^{k-1}v_{[k-1,j|k,1]}\cdot\begin{cases}v_{[k,2|k,k-1]}^{k-1}&\text{if $n-k$ is even,}\\
\prod_{j=2}^{k-1}v_{k,j}^{(k+1-j,k-j)}&\text{if $n-k$ is odd,}\\
\end{cases}

\displaystyle\mathrm{y}^{(10)}_{r_{k}}

\displaystyle=\mathrm{y}_{r_{k}}\cdot\prod_{j=0}^{k}v_{k+1,j}^{k+1-j}\cdot\begin{cases}\mathrm{y}_{t_{k;1}}^{-1}\cdot v_{[k,1|k,k-1]}^{k+1}&\text{if $n-k$ is even,}\\
\prod_{j=1}^{k-1}v_{k,j}^{(j+2,j+1)}&\text{if $n-k$ is odd.}\\
\end{cases}

Lemma 7.25
.

Images
\mathrm{y}_{\ell}^{(11)}=\boldsymbol{\mu}^{t}_{11\,\circlearrowright\,9}(\mathrm{y}_{\ell})
of the tropical variables
\mathrm{y}_{\ell}
of
Q^{n}_{9}
under the tropical cluster transformation
\boldsymbol{\mu}^{t}_{11\,\circlearrowleft\,9}
may be written as follows. First, we have

\mathrm{y}^{(11)}_{l_{k}}=\mathrm{y}_{l_{k}}\cdot\mathrm{y}_{[k,0|k,k-1]},\qquad\mathrm{y}^{(11)}_{r_{k}}=\mathrm{y}_{r_{k}}\cdot\prod_{j=1}^{k-1}\left(\mathrm{y}_{s_{k;j}}^{a_{n}(k)+j+1}\mathrm{y}_{t_{k;j}}^{a_{n}(k)+j}\right)\cdot\prod_{j=0}^{k}v_{k+1,j}^{k+1-j}.

Then,

\mathrm{y}^{(11)}_{c_{k}}=v_{[k,1|k+1,1]}^{-1}\qquad\text{for}\qquad 1\leqslant k\leqslant n-1

and

\mathrm{y}^{(11)}_{h_{k}}=v_{[n,1|n+1,k-1]}^{-1}\qquad\text{for}\qquad 1\leqslant k\leqslant n+1.

For
2\leqslant k\leqslant n
and
1\leqslant j\leqslant k-1
let us set

\displaystyle a_{k,j}

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}v_{[k-1,k-j-1|k,k-j-1]}&\text{if}\quad j<k-1,\\
1&\text{if}\quad j=k-1.\end{cases}

\displaystyle b_{k,j}

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}\mathrm{y}_{s_{k,k-j}}^{a_{n}(k)}\mathrm{y}_{t_{k,k-j}}^{a_{n}(k)-1}&\text{if}\quad k<n,\\
\mathrm{y}_{s_{n,n-j}}^{n-j}\mathrm{y}_{t_{n,n-j}}^{n-j-1}&\text{if}\quad k=n.\end{cases}

Then

\mathrm{y}^{(11)}_{l_{k,j}}=a_{k,j}\cdot b_{k,j},\qquad\mathrm{y}^{(11)}_{r_{k,j}}=b^{-1}_{k,j}\cdot\begin{cases}v_{[k,k-j+1|k+1,k-j+1]}^{-1}&\text{if}\quad k<n,\\
v_{n,n-j},&\text{if}\quad k=n.\end{cases}

Finally. for
1\leqslant k\leqslant n
we have

\mathrm{y}^{(11)}_{d_{k}}=\mathrm{y}_{d_{k}}.

Lemma 7.26
.

Images
\mathrm{y}_{\ell}^{(12)}=\boldsymbol{\mu}^{t}_{12\,\circlearrowright\,9}(\mathrm{y}_{\ell})
of the tropical variables
\mathrm{y}_{\ell}
of
Q^{n}_{9}
under the tropical cluster transformation
\boldsymbol{\mu}^{t}_{12\,\circlearrowleft\,9}
may be written as follows. Setting
l_{k;0}=l_{k}
,
r_{k;0}=r_{k}
we have that variables
\mathrm{y}^{(12)}_{l_{k,j}}
,
\mathrm{y}^{(12)}_{r_{k,j}}
are the same as in the previous Lemma if
k+j\leqslant n
. Same applies to
\mathrm{y}^{(12)}_{c_{k}}
if
1\leqslant k\leqslant(n+1)/2
and to
\mathrm{y}^{(12)}_{h_{k}}
for all
k
. Then for
k
such that
k>(n+1)/2
we have

\mathrm{y}^{(12)}_{c_{k}}=v_{[k,1|k+1,2k-n]}^{-1}.

Furthermore, for
n/2+1<k\leqslant n
we have

\mathrm{y}^{(12)}_{l_{k,n+1-k}}=\begin{cases}\prod_{j=1}^{2k-2-n}v_{[k-1,j|k,2k-2-n]}\cdot v_{k,2k-1-n}^{(k-1,k-2)}&\text{if $n-k$ is even,}\\
\prod_{j=1}^{2k-2-n}v_{[k-1,j|k,j]}\cdot v_{[k,2|k,2k-1-n]}^{(1,0)}&\text{if $n-k$ is odd.}\\
\end{cases}

and for
k=n/2+1
we have

\mathrm{y}^{(12)}_{l_{k,n+1-k}}=v_{k,1}^{(a_{n}(k),a_{n}(k)-1)}.

Then

\mathrm{y}^{(12)}_{r_{k,n+1-k}}=\begin{cases}\prod_{j=1}^{2k-2-n}v_{k,j+1}^{(k-n-j,k+1-n-j)}&\text{if $n-k$ is even,}\\
v_{k,2k-n-1}^{(0,1)}&\text{if $n-k$ is odd.}\\
\end{cases}

For
1\leqslant k\leqslant(n-1)/2
and
1\leqslant j\leqslant n-2k
we have

\displaystyle\mathrm{y}^{(12)}_{s_{n+1-2k,j}}

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}v_{n+1-k,n+1-2k-j}^{(k,k-1)}&\text{if $k$ is odd,}\\
v_{n+1-k,n+1-2k-j}^{(1,0)}&\text{if $k$ is even.}\end{cases}

\displaystyle\mathrm{y}^{(12)}_{t_{n+1-2k,j}}

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}v_{n+1-k,n+1-2k-j}^{(1-k,2-k)}&\text{if $k$ is odd,}\\
v_{n+1-k,n+1-2k-j}^{(0,1)}&\text{if $k$ is even.}\end{cases}

Finally. for
1\leqslant k\leqslant n
we have

\mathrm{y}^{(12)}_{d_{k}}=\mathrm{y}_{d_{k}}.

Lemma 7.27
.

We have the equality
\boldsymbol{\mu}^{t}_{13\,\circlearrowright\,9}(\mathrm{y}_{\ell})=\boldsymbol{\mu}^{t}_{13\,\circlearrowleft\,9}(\mathrm{y}_{\ell})
for all tropical variables
\mathrm{y}_{\ell}
of
Q^{n}_{9}
.

8.
Gluing for Laurent rings

In this section we describe how the quantum universal Laurent rings associated to
\mathcal{P}^{\diamond}_{G,S}
behave under the gluing of surfaces along tacked circles. In order to do this, we need to define the algebra
\mathbb{L}_{S^{\prime};\phi}
associated to the gluing data
\phi\colon c_{+}\simeq c_{-}
. This we do in several steps as follows. We first associate to each cluster torus for
\mathcal{P}^{\diamond}_{G,S^{\prime}}
a symplectically reduced quantum torus and universal Laurent ring defined in Section
8.1
. Next, we localize each of these tori at the Ore denominator set described in (
8.15
), which commutes with all non-frozen cluster variables and is thus mutation-invariant. Finally, we impose conditions analogous to (
6.16
) on the poles and residues of elements of these localized quantum tori, and take invariants for an action of the Weyl group associated to the pair of tacked circles
c_{\pm}
. The resulting residue universal Laurent ring is described precisely in Definition
8.25
, and in Theorem
8.31
we prove that it is
\Gamma_{S;c}
-equivariantly isomorphic to
\mathbb{L}_{S}
.

8.1.
Symplectic reduction at a pair of tacked circles

Suppose that
S^{\prime}
is a marked surface with a pair of tacked circles
c_{\pm}
, and denote by
S
the surface obtained by gluing these two tacked circles together as is described in Section
2.3
.
Recall that for each cluster chart
Q
for
\mathcal{P}^{\diamond}_{SL_{n+1},S^{\prime}}
we have lattices
\Lambda_{S^{\prime},Q}\subset\Xi_{S^{\prime},Q}
with
2n
frozen
e
-basis elements
e_{c_{\pm},i}\in\Lambda_{S^{\prime},Q},~1\leq i\leq n
, as well as the
2n
frozen
\xi
-basis elements
\xi_{c_{\pm},i}\in\Xi_{S^{\prime},Q},~1\leq i\leq n
.

We now form a pair of lattices
\Lambda_{Q}^{c}\subset\Xi_{Q}^{c}

as “symplectic reductions” of
\Lambda_{S^{\prime},Q}
and
\Xi_{S^{\prime},Q}
. We first consider the sublattice
\Xi^{\omega}_{Q}\subset\Xi_{Q}
given by the orthogonal complement to the span of the elements
\xi_{c_{+},i}+\xi_{c_{-},i}
,

(8.1)

\displaystyle\Xi^{\omega}_{Q}=\left\{\xi\in\Xi_{S^{\prime},Q}~\big|~(\xi,\xi_{c_{+},i}+\xi_{c_{-},i})=0,\quad 1\leq i\leq n\right\},

and then take the quotient

(8.2)

\displaystyle\Xi_{Q}^{c}:=\frac{\Xi^{\omega}_{Q}}{\left\langle\xi_{c_{+},i}+\xi_{c_{-},i}\right\rangle_{i=1}^{n}}.

We write
\xi_{c,i}
for the image of
\xi_{c_{+},i}
in
\Xi_{Q}^{c}
. The reduced lattice
\Xi_{Q}^{c}
inherits a well-defined skew form from that of
\Xi_{Q}
, the kernel of which is canonically identified with that of
\Xi_{Q}
. We note that the elements (
8.1
) are skew-orthogonal to all mutable basis vectors
e_{k}
: indeed, the sublattice in
\Lambda_{Q}
spanned by all
e_{l}
except for the frozen basis vectors
e_{c_{\pm},i}
associated to the two tacked circles coincides with the based lattice
\Lambda_{S_{\circ}^{\prime}}
associated by Fock and Goncharov
[
FG09a
]
to the surface
S_{\circ}^{\prime}
where each
c_{\pm}
is replaced by a puncture, while the kernel of the skew form on
\Lambda_{S_{\circ}^{\prime}}
contains all
2n
elements
\xi_{c_{\pm},i}
. The lattices
\Lambda_{Q}^{\omega}
and
\Lambda_{Q}^{c}
are constructed similarly from
\Lambda_{Q}
, replacing the
\xi_{c_{\pm},i}
by the elements
\alpha_{c_{\pm},i}
from (
4.19
) corresponding to the simple roots of
G
.

Write
\mathcal{T}_{\Xi_{Q}}^{\omega}
for the quantum torus associated to
\Xi_{Q}^{\omega}
and
\mathcal{T}^{c}_{\Xi_{Q}}
for that associated to
\Xi_{Q}^{c}
, so that
\mathcal{T}_{Q}^{c}
is isomorphic to the quotient of
\mathcal{T}_{Q}^{\omega}
by the 2-sided ideal
\mathcal{I}_{Q}\subseteq\mathcal{T}_{\Xi_{Q}}^{\omega}
generated by the
n
elements
1-Y_{\xi_{c_{+},i}+\xi_{c_{-},i}}
, which lie in the center of
\mathcal{T}_{\Xi_{Q}}^{\omega}
. Note that we can also identify
\mathcal{T}_{\Xi_{Q}}^{\omega}
with the centralizer in
\mathcal{T}_{\Xi}
of the subalgebra generated by the
n
elements
Y_{\xi_{c_{+},i}+\xi_{c_{-},i}}
.

Let
\mathbb{L}_{Q}^{\omega}
denote the intersection of the universal Laurent ring
\mathbb{L}^{\mathcal{A}}_{Q}\subset\mathcal{T}_{\Xi_{Q}}
with
\mathcal{T}_{\Xi_{Q}}^{\omega}
. By Theorem
3.18
, we have

(8.3)

\displaystyle\mathbb{L}_{Q}^{\omega}=\bigcap_{k=1}^{d}\left(\mathcal{T}_{\Xi_{Q}}^{\omega}\cap\mathcal{T}_{\Xi_{\mu_{k}(Q)}}^{\omega}\right).

We define the reduced Laurent ring based in cluster
Q
by

(8.4)

\displaystyle\mathbb{L}_{\Xi_{Q}}^{c}=\mathbb{L}_{Q}^{\omega}/(\mathbb{L}_{Q}^{\omega}\cap\mathcal{I}_{Q})\subseteq\mathcal{T}_{\Xi_{Q}}^{\omega}/\mathcal{I}_{Q}\simeq\mathcal{T}_{\Xi_{Q}}^{c}.

We now show this definition is compatible with mutations, and formulate an easily verifiable criterion for membership in
\mathbb{L}^{c}_{Q}
. Since the linear combinations of frozen
\mathcal{A}
-variables
\xi_{c_{+},i}+\xi_{c_{-},i}
are skew-orthogonal to all mutable variables, no cluster
\mathcal{X}
-variable
e_{k}
can ever be contained in their span. Hence we still have well-defined partial completions
\mathcal{T}^{c}_{\Xi_{Q}}((Y_{-e_{k}}))
of the quantum torus associated to the quotient lattice, which have the property that the kernels of the natural maps
\mathcal{T}^{\omega}_{\Xi_{Q}}\rightarrow\mathcal{T}^{c}_{\Xi_{Q}}((Y_{-e_{k}}))\leftarrow\mathcal{T}^{\omega}_{\Xi_{Q}^{\prime}}
are precisely
\mathcal{I}_{Q},\mathcal{I}_{Q^{\prime}}
respectively.

Lemma 8.1
.

If
Q^{\prime}=\mu_{k}(Q)
, the quantum mutation map
\mu_{k}^{q}
induces an isomorphism of reduced Laurent rings
\mathbb{L}_{\Xi_{Q}}^{c}\simeq\mathbb{L}_{{\Xi_{Q}}}^{c}
.

Proof..

The key point is that, as mentioned above, the generators
1-Y_{\xi_{c_{+},i}+\xi_{c_{-},i}}
of
\mathcal{I}_{Q}
commute with all unfrozen cluster
\mathcal{X}
-variables
Y_{e_{k}}
in any cluster
Q
. But the tori
\mathcal{T}_{\Xi_{Q}}^{\omega},\mathcal{T}_{\Xi_{Q^{\prime}}}^{\omega}
are characterized as the centralizers of the elements
Y_{\xi_{c_{+},i}+\xi_{c_{-},i}}
. Since the quantum mutation map
\mu^{q}_{k}
from (
3.10
) is induced by conjugation by
\Psi^{q}(Y_{-e_{k}})
, it follows that
\mu_{k}^{q}
maps
\mathbb{L}^{\omega}_{Q}
into
\mathbb{L}^{\omega}_{Q^{\prime}}
. For the same reason, the ideal generators
1-Y_{\xi_{c_{+},i}+\xi_{c_{-},i}}
are invariant under
\mu^{q}_{k}
, and it follows easily from the characterization of
\mathcal{I}_{Q},\mathcal{I}_{Q^{\prime}}
as kernels given above
that
\mu^{q}_{k}(\mathbb{L}_{Q}^{\omega}\cap\mathcal{I}_{Q})=\mathbb{L}_{Q^{\prime}}^{\omega}\cap\mathcal{I}_{Q^{\prime}}
. This proves the Lemma.
∎

In view of Lemma
8.1
, we can unambiguously write
\mathbb{L}_{S^{\prime};\mathcal{A}}^{c}
for the
\mathcal{A}
-variable version of the reduced universal Laurent ring. By (
8.3
), we have

\mathbb{L}^{c}_{\Xi_{Q}}=\bigcap_{k=1}^{d}\frac{\mathcal{T}^{\omega}_{\Xi_{Q}}\cap\mathcal{T}^{\omega}_{\Xi_{\mu_{k}(Q)}}}{\mathcal{I}_{Q}\cap\mathcal{T}^{\omega}_{\Xi_{Q}}\cap\mathcal{T}^{\omega}_{\Xi_{\mu_{k}(Q)}}}\subseteq\frac{\mathcal{T}^{\omega}_{\Xi_{Q}}}{\mathcal{I}_{Q}}\simeq\mathcal{T}_{\Xi_{Q}}^{c}.

Since the elements in
\mathcal{T}_{\Xi_{Q}}^{c}\subset\mathcal{T}_{\Xi_{Q}}^{c}((Y_{-e_{k}}))
which remain Laurent polynomials under
\mathrm{Ad}(\Psi(Y_{-e_{k}}))
are exactly those contained in the intersection
\mathcal{T}_{\Xi_{Q}}^{c}\cap\mathcal{T}_{\Xi_{\mu_{k}(Q)}}^{c}
, we obtain

Corollary 8.2
.

An element
a\in\mathcal{T}_{\Xi_{Q}}^{c}
is contained in
\mathbb{L}_{\Xi_{Q}}^{c}
if and only if for each mutable direction
k
, its image in the partial completion
{\mathcal{T}}_{{\Xi_{Q}}}^{c}((Y_{-e_{k}}))
remains a Laurent polynomial under
\mathrm{Ad}(\Psi(Y_{-e_{k}}))
.

In the same way, we can construct the
\mathcal{X}
-variable version
\mathbb{L}_{\Lambda_{Q}}^{c}
of the symplectically reduced universal Laurent ring based in cluster
Q
, which is a subalgebra in
\mathbb{L}_{\Xi_{Q}}^{c}
. The conclusions of Lemma
8.1
and Corollary
8.2
clearly also hold for the
\mathbb{L}_{\Lambda_{Q}}^{c}
.

8.2.
Local gluing isomorphism for
G=PGL_{n+1}

The goal of this section is to construct
local gluing isomorphisms
associated to
c
-isolating triangulations of
S
and their images
\underline{\Delta}^{\prime}=\mathcal{C}_{c}
under the cutting functor.

Suppose that
Q_{\underline{\Delta}^{\prime};c_{\pm}}
is a
c_{\pm}
-isolating quiver subordinate to an ideal triangulation
\underline{\Delta}^{\prime}
of
S^{\prime}
. We write
{Q}^{\omega}_{\underline{\Delta}^{\prime};c_{\pm}}
for the quiver obtained from
Q_{\underline{\Delta}^{\prime};c_{\pm}}
by omitting the frozen
e
-basis vectors
\{e_{c_{\pm},i}\}
and replacing them with frozen
e
-basis vectors

\dot{e}_{c,i}:=e_{c_{+},i}+\overline{e}_{c_{-},i}+\dot{e}_{v^{+}_{i}},\quad 1\leq i\leq n,

where the
\overline{e}_{c_{-},i}
are the vectors defined in (
4.15
).
Recall the symplectically reduced lattices
\Lambda^{c}({{\underline{\Delta}^{\prime};c_{\pm}}}),~\Xi^{c}({{\underline{\Delta}^{\prime};c_{\pm}}})
associated to the quiver
Q_{\underline{\Delta}^{\prime};c_{\pm}}
. We write
\mathcal{T}(\underline{\Delta}^{\prime};c_{\pm}),~\mathcal{T}^{\mathcal{A}}(\underline{\Delta}^{\prime};c_{\pm})
for the corresponding quantum torus algebras. The lattice
\Lambda^{c}({{\underline{\Delta}^{\prime};c_{\pm}}})
admits the following basis (which we again caution is different from the one determining mutable directions in the cluster algebra):

(8.5)

\displaystyle\left\{\dot{e}_{\ell}~\big|~\ell\notin\{(c_{\pm},i)\}_{i=1}^{n}\sqcup\{v^{\pm 1}_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n}\right\}\sqcup\{\dot{e}_{v_{i}}=\dot{e}_{v^{+}_{i}}=-\dot{e}_{v^{-}_{i}}\}_{i=1}^{n}\sqcup\{\dot{e}_{c,i}\}_{i=1}^{n}.

We record the pairings

(\dot{e}_{v_{i}},\dot{e}_{(c,j)})=\mathfrak{A}_{i,j},\quad(\dot{e}_{v_{i}},\dot{e}_{v_{j}})=0=(\dot{e}_{(c,i)},\dot{e}_{(c,j)}),\qquad 1\leq i,j\leq n.

Notation 8.3
.

In analogy with the decomposition from Notation
5.6
for the glued surface
S
, we define a subset of the index set
{I}^{\geq 0}_{S^{\prime}}
for the quiver
{Q}^{\omega}_{\underline{\Delta}^{\prime};c_{\pm}}
by

{I}^{\geq 0}_{S^{\prime}}=\{(c,i)\}_{i=1}^{n}\sqcup\{v^{\pm}_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n+1},

and write
I^{<0}_{S^{\prime}}
for its complement in the indexing set
I_{S^{\prime}}
of the quiver
{Q}^{\omega}_{\underline{\Delta}^{\prime};c_{\pm}}
. Similarly, we write

(8.6)

\displaystyle\overline{I}^{>0}_{S^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=\{e_{v_{n+1}^{+}},e_{v_{n+1}^{-}}\}\sqcup\{(c,i),v_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n},

(8.7)

\displaystyle\overline{I}^{\geq 0}_{S^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=\{e_{v_{n+1}^{+}},e_{v_{n+1}^{-}}\}\sqcup\overline{I}^{>0}_{S^{\prime}},

so that
\{\dot{e}_{\ell}~|~\ell\in I^{<0}_{S^{\prime}}\sqcup\overline{I}^{\geq 0}_{S^{\prime}}\}
forms a basis for the symplectically reduced lattice
\Lambda^{c}({{\underline{\Delta}^{\prime};c_{\pm}}})
.
Again parallel to Notation
5.6
, we use the partitioning of this basis to make non-orthogonal splittings

\displaystyle\Lambda^{c}(\underline{\Delta}^{\prime};c_{\pm})

\displaystyle=\Lambda^{c}_{\leq 0}(\underline{\Delta}^{\prime};c_{\pm})\bigoplus\Lambda^{c}_{>0}(\underline{\Delta}^{\prime};c_{\pm}),

(8.8)

\displaystyle=\Lambda^{c}_{<0}(\underline{\Delta}^{\prime};c_{\pm})\bigoplus\Lambda^{c}_{\geq 0}(\underline{\Delta}^{\prime};c_{\pm}).

We write
\mathcal{T}_{{\leq 0}}(\underline{\Delta}^{\prime};c_{\pm}),~\mathcal{T}_{{>0}}(\underline{\Delta}^{\prime};c_{\pm})
for the corresponding quantum tori, and define similarly
\mathcal{T}_{{<0}}(\underline{\Delta}^{\prime};c_{\pm}),~\mathcal{T}_{{\geq 0}}(\underline{\Delta}^{\prime};c_{\pm})
.

Consider the anti-diagonal embedding of the braid group

B_{n+1}\rightarrow B_{n+1}(c_{+})\times B_{n+1}(c_{-}),\quad\sigma_{i}\longmapsto(\sigma_{i},\sigma_{i}^{-1}).

Inspecting the formulas (
5.25
), we see that the action of this subgroup by cluster transformations descends to the symplectically reduced universal Laurent ring
\mathbb{L}_{S^{\prime}}^{c}
, and moreover factors through the quotient to the Weyl group. From now on, we refer to this action as that of the Weyl group
W(c_{\pm})
associated to the glued pair of tacked circles.

If
\beta
is an element of the root lattice of
SL_{n+1}
, define a corresponding monomial element of the quantum torus
\mathcal{T}(\underline{\Delta}^{\prime};c_{\pm})
by

(8.9)

\displaystyle Y_{c,\beta^{\vee}}=Y_{\sum m_{k}\dot{e}_{c,k}},\qquad\beta=\sum_{k=1}^{n}m_{k}\alpha_{k}.

Dually, we define

(8.10)

\displaystyle Y_{c,\beta}:=Y_{\sum m_{k}\dot{e}_{v_{k}}},\quad\beta=\sum_{k=1}^{n}m_{k}\alpha_{k}.

With these notations we have

(8.11)

\displaystyle Y_{c,\alpha^{\vee}}Y_{c,\beta}=q^{2\langle\alpha,\beta\rangle}Y_{c,\beta}Y_{c,\alpha^{\vee}}.

The Weyl group
W(c_{\pm})
acts on the elements (
8.9
) and (
8.10
) via the reflection representation

(8.12)

\displaystyle w(Y_{c,\alpha})=Y_{c,w(\alpha)},\qquad w(Y_{c,\alpha^{\vee}})=Y_{c,w(\alpha)^{\vee}},\quad~w\in W(c_{\pm}),

and fixes all
e_{\ell}
with
\ell\in I^{>0}_{S^{\prime}}
.

Recall that each symplectically reduced cluster
\mathcal{X}
-torus
\mathcal{T}_{\Lambda^{c}_{S^{\prime}}}
carries a grading by the root lattice of
SL_{n+1}
, which coincides with the internal grading defined by conjugation with the elements
Y_{c,\alpha}
:

(8.13)

\displaystyle A\in\mathcal{T}_{\Lambda^{c}_{S^{\prime}}}\{\beta\}\iff AY_{{c,\alpha}}=q^{2\langle\alpha,\beta\rangle}Y_{{c,\alpha}}A\quad\text{for all }\alpha\in Q_{SL_{n+1}}.

We write

A=\sum_{\beta}A_{\beta}

for the decomposition of an elment
A
with respect to this internal grading.
Since the elements
Y_{{c,j}}
commute with all mutable variables the internal grading is mutation invariant and hence descends to the universal Laurent ring
\mathbb{L}^{c}_{S^{\prime}}
. In an isolating cluster
Q_{\underline{\Delta}^{\prime};c_{\pm}}
, the grading becomes especially simple to describe: we have

\deg(\dot{e}_{\ell})=\begin{cases}\alpha_{j}\quad&\ell=(c,j),~1\leq j\leq n\\
0\quad&\text{else.}\end{cases}

To make contact with the constructions of Section
6
involving
q
-difference operators on the torus of
GL_{n+1}
, it is convenient to consider an extended version
\Lambda_{\geq 0}^{c;ext}(\underline{\Delta}^{\prime})
of
\Lambda^{c}_{\geq 0}(\underline{\Delta}^{\prime})
in which we adjoin an additional frozen direction
e_{c,\varepsilon^{\vee}_{n+1}}
whose only nonzero pairings with the other basis elements are given by

(e_{c,\varepsilon^{\vee}_{n+1}},\dot{e}_{v_{n}})=1,\quad(e_{c,\varepsilon^{\vee}_{n+1}},\dot{e}_{v^{\pm}_{n+1}})=\mp 1.

In this way, we can associate vectors
e_{c,\lambda^{\vee}}
to arbitrary elements
\lambda\in\mathbb{Z}^{n+1}
of the
GL_{n+1}
weight lattice.
Similarly, to an arbitrary
\mu=\sum_{j=1}^{n+1}m_{j}\alpha_{j}\in\mathbb{Z}^{n+1}
(where we recall the notation
\alpha_{n+1}=\varepsilon_{n+1}
) we can associate the pair of vectors
e^{+}_{c,\mu},~e^{-}_{c,\mu}
given by

e^{\pm}_{c,\mu}=m_{n+1}\dot{e}_{v_{n+1}^{\pm}}\pm\sum_{j=1}^{n}m_{j}\dot{e}_{v_{j}}.

In particular, we have

e^{\pm}_{c,\varepsilon_{i}}=e_{v^{\pm}_{i}}.

With these definitions, we have

(e^{\pm}_{c,\mu},e_{c,\lambda^{\vee}})=\pm\langle\lambda,\mu\rangle.

The lattice
\Lambda_{\geq 0}^{c;ext}(\underline{\Delta}^{\prime})
has a basis

(8.14)

\displaystyle\{e^{+}_{c,\varepsilon_{i}},e_{c,\varepsilon_{i}^{\vee}}\}_{i=1}^{n+1}\sqcup\{\zeta^{\prime}={e}_{v^{+}_{n+1}}+{e}_{v^{-}_{n+1}}\}.

We extend the action of the Weyl group
W(c_{\pm})
in the natural way from
\Lambda_{\geq 0}^{c}(\underline{\Delta}^{\prime})
to
\Lambda_{\geq 0}^{c;ext}(\underline{\Delta}^{\prime})
, declaring the vector
\zeta^{\prime}
to be a fixed point
7
7

7

Recall that by the gluing quotient relations (
8.2
) we have
{e}_{v^{+}_{i}}+{e}_{v^{-}_{i}}={e}_{v^{+}_{j}}+{e}_{v^{-}_{j}}
for all
i,j
.
while letting the Weyl group act via the reflection representation permuting the basis vectors
\{e^{+}_{c,\varepsilon_{i}},e_{c,\varepsilon_{i}^{\vee}}\}_{i=1}^{n+1}
.
Note that the quantum torus
\mathcal{T}_{{\geq 0}}(\underline{\Delta}^{\prime};c_{\pm})
is recovered as the centralizer of the element
Y_{e^{+}_{c,\omega_{n+1}}}
in
\mathcal{T}(\Lambda^{ext}_{S^{\prime}_{\geq 0}})
. Then evidently we have

Lemma 8.4
.

The quantum torus
\mathcal{T}(\Lambda^{ext}_{S^{\prime}_{\geq 0}})
associated to the lattice
\Lambda^{ext}_{S^{\prime}_{\geq 0}}(\underline{\Delta}^{\prime})
is
W
-equivariantly isomorphic to
\mathcal{D}_{q}(T)[Z^{\pm 1}]
, where the central subalgebra in the latter generated by
Z
is identified the corresponding one in generated by
Y_{\zeta^{\prime}}
.

Consider the localization
\mathcal{T}_{\Lambda_{S^{\prime}}^{c};\mathrm{loc}}
of the quantum torus
\mathcal{T}_{\Lambda_{S^{\prime}}^{c}}
at the multiplicative set

(8.15)

\displaystyle Ø(c_{\pm})=\left\{\prod_{j}(1-q^{2k_{j}}Y_{{c,\beta_{j}}})\right\}_{\vec{k},\vec{\beta}}\subset\mathcal{T}_{\Lambda^{c}_{S^{\prime}}}

where
\vec{k}
ranges over all finite length sequences of integers, and
\vec{\beta}
over all sequences of positive roots (possibly with repetitions). Note that the denominator set
Ø(c_{\pm})
is mutation invariant since the
Y_{{c,\beta_{j}}}
commute with the subalgebra in
\mathcal{T}_{\Lambda_{S^{\prime}}^{c}}
corresponding to the unfrozen directions in
\Lambda_{S^{\prime}}^{c}
, and so the definition of
\mathcal{T}_{\Lambda_{S^{\prime}}^{c};\mathrm{loc}}
takes the same form in any cluster.

Let
\mathfrak{S}_{\alpha,k}\subset\mathcal{T}_{\Lambda_{S^{\prime}}^{c}}\{0\}
be the multiplicative set consisting of elements of internal degree zero which are not multiples of the element
1-q^{2k}Y_{c,\alpha}
(which we recall is central in
\mathcal{T}_{\Lambda_{S^{\prime}}^{c}}\{0\}
). Then both
\mathfrak{S}_{\alpha,k}
and the set of all nonzero elements in
\mathcal{T}_{\Lambda_{S^{\prime}}^{c}}\{0\}
form left Ore denominator sets in
\mathcal{T}_{\Lambda_{S^{\prime}}^{c}}
. If we write
\mathcal{T}_{\Lambda_{S^{\prime}}^{c};\mathrm{rat}}
for the Ore localization of
\mathcal{T}_{\Lambda_{S^{\prime}}^{c}}
at the latter denominator set, then just as in formula (
6.9
) of Section
6.2
, each divisor

(8.16)

\displaystyle d_{\alpha,k}(c)=\{q^{2k}Y_{c,\alpha}=1\}

defines a filtration on
\mathcal{T}_{\Lambda_{S^{\prime}}^{c};\mathrm{rat}}
with filtered pieces

\mathcal{T}_{\Lambda_{S^{\prime}}^{c};\mathrm{rat}}^{\alpha,k;m}=(1-q^{2k}w_{\alpha})^{m}\mathfrak{S}_{\alpha,k}^{-1}\mathcal{T}_{\Lambda_{S^{\prime}}^{c}}

and projectors
\pi_{\alpha,k;m}
.
We define evaluations and residues for elements of
\mathcal{T}_{\Lambda_{S^{\prime}}^{c};\mathrm{rat}}^{\alpha,k;0}
and
\mathcal{T}_{\Lambda_{S^{\prime}}^{c};\mathrm{rat}}^{\alpha,k;-1}
respectively in the same way as Definition
6.2
. Once again, since the denominators
Ø(c_{\pm})
commute with the
Y_{c,\alpha_{j}}
, the internal grading (
8.13
) extends to
\mathcal{T}_{\Lambda_{S^{\prime}}^{c};\mathrm{loc}}
and moreover preserves each of its filtered pieces relative to the divisors
d_{\alpha,k}
.

Remark 8.5
.

Since
1-q^{2k}Y_{c,\alpha}
is central in
\mathcal{T}_{\Lambda_{S^{\prime}}^{c}}\{0\}
, the quotient
\mathcal{T}_{\Lambda_{S^{\prime}}^{c};\mathrm{rat}}^{\alpha,k;0}\{0\}/\mathcal{T}_{\Lambda_{S^{\prime}}^{c};\mathrm{rat}}^{\alpha,k;1}\{0\}
is a ring and the restriction of the evaluation map to the internal degree zero piece
\mathcal{T}_{\Lambda_{S^{\prime}}^{c};\mathrm{rat}}^{\alpha,k;0}\{0\}
is a ring homomorphism.

Now we return to the context of a
c_{\pm}
-isolating cluster
Q_{\underline{\Delta}^{\prime};c_{\pm}}
. For each element
\beta
of the root lattice of
G
we use the element
Y_{c,\beta^{\vee}}=Y_{c,\beta^{\vee}}({\underline{\Delta}^{\prime};c_{\pm}})
from (
8.9
) to define an analog of the automorphism from (
6.11
):

(8.17)

\displaystyle\operatorname{Ad}_{Y_{c,\beta^{\vee}}}\colon A\longmapsto A^{[\beta]}=Y_{c,\beta^{\vee}}AY^{-1}_{c,\beta^{\vee}}.

Then for all
s_{\alpha}\in W(c_{\pm})
and
A\in\mathcal{T}_{\Lambda_{S^{\prime}}^{c};\mathrm{rat}}^{\alpha,k;0}
, we have (cf Lemma
6.3
)

(8.18)

\displaystyle(s_{\alpha}A)\big|_{d_{\alpha,k}}=A^{[k\alpha]}\big|_{d_{\alpha,k}}.

Finally, we observe that the constructions above admit analogs for the extended lattice
\Lambda^{c;ext}({\underline{\Delta}^{\prime};c_{\pm}})
in which we replace
\Lambda_{\geq 0}^{c}(\underline{\Delta}^{\prime})
by
\Lambda^{c;ext}_{\geq 0}(\underline{\Delta}^{\prime})
in the decomposition (
8.3
). We write
\mathcal{T}_{\mathrm{loc}}({\underline{\Delta}^{\prime};c_{\pm}})
for the localized quantum torus in the isolating cluster
Q_{\underline{\Delta}^{\prime};c_{\pm}}
, and
\mathcal{T}^{ext}_{\mathrm{loc}}({\underline{\Delta}^{\prime};c_{\pm}})
for its extended version. Similarly, we write
\mathcal{T}_{\geq 0;\mathrm{loc}}({\underline{\Delta}^{\prime};c_{\pm}})
for the localization at
Ø(c_{\pm})
of the quantum torus
\mathcal{T}_{\geq 0}({\underline{\Delta}^{\prime};c_{\pm}})
.

Now we can state the main definition of this section.

Definition 8.6
.

The (extended)
PGL_{n+1}

residue quantum torus

\mathcal{T}_{\operatorname{res}}({\underline{\Delta}^{\prime};c_{\pm}})
(respectively
\mathcal{T}^{ext}_{\operatorname{res}}({\underline{\Delta}^{\prime};c_{\pm}})
) for the
c_{\pm}
-isolating cluster
Q_{\underline{\Delta}^{\prime};c_{\pm}}
consists of all elements
A
of
\mathcal{T}_{\mathrm{loc}}({\underline{\Delta}^{\prime};c_{\pm}})
(respectively
\mathcal{T}^{ext}_{\mathrm{loc}}({\underline{\Delta}^{\prime};c_{\pm}})
) such that:

(1)

The element
A
is invariant under the action of the diagonally embedded Weyl group
W(c_{\pm})
;

(2)

The element
A
has at worst simple poles at the divisors
d_{\alpha,k}
, and for all elements
\nu
of the weight lattice of
PGL_{n+1}
and integers
k
its residues satisfy

(8.19)

\displaystyle\mathrm{res}_{d_{\alpha,k}}\left(A_{\nu}+A_{s_{\alpha}(\nu)+k\alpha}Y_{c,(\langle\nu,\alpha\rangle-k)\alpha^{\vee}}({\underline{\Delta};c_{\pm}})\right)=0.

The algebra
\mathcal{T}_{\operatorname{res}}({\underline{\Delta}^{\prime};c_{\pm}})
is generated by its subalgebras
\mathcal{T}_{<0}(\underline{\Delta}^{\prime};c_{\pm})
and
\mathcal{T}_{\geq 0;\operatorname{res}}(\underline{\Delta}^{\prime};c_{\pm})
, where the latter consists of all elements of the localized quantum torus
\mathcal{T}_{\geq 0;\mathrm{loc}}({\underline{\Delta}^{\prime};c_{\pm}})
satisfying the conditions in Definition
8.6
.
The following Lemma summarizes the relation of the algebra
\mathcal{T}_{\operatorname{res}}({\underline{\Delta}^{\prime};c_{\pm}})
to the residue algebra
\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{res}}
from Section
6
.

Lemma 8.7
.

The identification of quantum tori from Lemma
8.4
induces an isomorphism of algebras

\mathcal{T}^{ext}_{\geq 0;\operatorname{res}}(\underline{\Delta}^{\prime};c_{\pm})\simeq\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{res}}[Z^{\pm 1}].

The subalgebra
\mathcal{T}_{\geq 0;\operatorname{res}}(\underline{\Delta}^{\prime};c_{\pm})
in in
\mathcal{T}^{ext}_{\geq 0;\operatorname{res}}(\underline{\Delta}^{\prime};c_{\pm})
coincides with the centralizer of the element
Y_{e^{+}_{c,\omega_{n+1}}}
.

In particular, the algebra
\mathcal{T}^{ext}_{\geq 0;\operatorname{res}}(\underline{\Delta}^{\prime};c_{\pm})
is generated by
Y_{\zeta^{\prime}}^{\pm 1}
and the elements from point (2) of Lemma
6.14
.

Remark 8.8
.

The construction in Definition
8.6
can also be carried out at the level of
\Xi
-lattices to define the
SL_{n+1}
residue quantum torus
\mathcal{T}^{\mathcal{A}}_{\operatorname{res}}(\underline{\Delta}^{\prime};c_{\pm})
associated to a
c_{\pm}
-isolating cluster
Q_{\underline{\Delta}^{\prime};c_{\pm}}
. The algebra
\mathcal{T}^{\mathcal{A}}_{\operatorname{res}}(\underline{\Delta}^{\prime};c_{\pm})
consists of elements of the
Ø(c_{\pm})
-localized
\mathcal{A}
-quantum torus
\mathcal{T}^{\mathcal{A}}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\underline{\Delta}^{\prime};c_{\pm})
satisfying conditions (1) and (2), where
\nu
in (2) now ranges over the weight lattice of
SL_{n+1}
.

Now suppose that
Q_{\underline{\Delta};c}
is an isolating cluster for a simple closed curve
c
on a marked surface
S
,
\underline{\Delta}^{\prime}=\mathcal{C}_{c}(\underline{\Delta})
is the image of
\underline{\Delta}
under the cutting functor, and
Q_{\underline{\Delta}^{\prime};c_{\pm}}
the corresponding
c_{\pm}
-isolating quiver on the surface
S^{\prime}
.

Definition 8.9
.

We write
\mathbb{L}(\Lambda^{\mathrm{fr}}_{\underline{\Delta};c})
for the universal Laurent ring associated to the quiver
(\Lambda_{S}(Q_{\underline{\Delta};c}),\{e_{\ell}\}_{\ell\in I_{S}})
, where only the directions
\ell\in I_{S_{>0}}
are regarded as mutable, and all others are frozen.

As an algebra
\mathbb{L}(\Lambda^{\mathrm{fr}}_{\underline{\Delta};c})
is generated by the quantum torus
\mathcal{T}_{<0}(\underline{\Delta};c)
together with the universal Laurent ring
\mathbb{L}_{S_{\geq 0}}(\underline{\Delta};c)
associated
to the based lattice
(\Lambda_{S_{\geq 0}},\{e_{\ell}\}_{\ell\in I^{S_{\geq 0}}})
where the directions
e_{h_{\pm}}
are regarded as frozen and all others mutable.
Following Proposition
6.30
, we now construct a local gluing isomorphism

\eta\colon\mathcal{T}_{\operatorname{res}}(Q_{\underline{\Delta}^{\prime};c_{\pm}})\longrightarrow\mathbb{L}(\Lambda^{\mathrm{fr}}_{\underline{\Delta};c}).

Recall from Section
6.4
the space
\mathscr{F}
of compactly supported
\mathbb{Z}[q^{\pm 1}]
-valued functions on
\mathbb{Z}^{n+1}
, along with its subspace
\mathscr{V}
of functions vanishing outside the dominant cone as defined in (
6.22
). We define a faithful representation of the quantum torus
\mathcal{T}(Q_{\underline{\Delta};c})
on the
\mathbb{Z}[q^{\pm 1}]
-module

M[S_{\leq 0}]=\mathcal{T}_{\leq 0}(\underline{\Delta};c)\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}[q^{\pm 1}]}\mathscr{F}

of
\mathcal{T}_{\leq 0}(\underline{\Delta};c)
-valued functions on
\mathbb{Z}^{n+1}
as follows. For
\ell\in I^{S_{<0}}
, the generator
Y_{e_{\ell}}
multiplies the value of such a function from the left by
Y_{e_{\ell}}
. For
\ell\in I^{S_{>0}}=\{(s_{j},t_{j})\}_{j=1}^{n}
, the generator
Y_{e_{\ell}}
acts via the embedding of
\mathcal{T}_{S_{>0}}
into
\mathcal{T}(\Lambda_{GL_{n+1}})
described in Remark
6.1
, where we define the action of the generators
P_{i},X_{i}
of the latter quantum torus on
\mathscr{F}[S_{\leq 0}]
by

(8.20)

\displaystyle(P_{\epsilon_{i}}\circ\phi)(\lambda)=-q^{-1}Y_{{h_{+}}}\phi(\lambda-\varepsilon_{i}),\quad i=1,\ldots n+1

(X_{\alpha_{i}}\circ\phi)(\lambda)=-q^{2\langle\lambda,\alpha_{i}\rangle+1}\phi(\lambda),\quad i=1,\ldots n

and

(X_{\varepsilon_{1}}\circ\phi)(\lambda)=q^{2\lambda_{1}}\phi(\lambda).

We define the action of the remaining generators
Y_{h_{\pm}}
of
\mathcal{T}(Q_{\underline{\Delta};c})
by

\displaystyle(Y_{h_{+}}\circ\phi)(\lambda)

\displaystyle=-q^{-1}Y_{h_{+}}\phi(\lambda_{1},\ldots,\lambda_{n+1}-1),

(8.21)

\displaystyle(Y_{h_{-}}\circ\phi)(\lambda)

\displaystyle=-qY_{h_{-}}\phi(\lambda_{1}+1,\ldots,\lambda_{n+1}).

To relate more precisely with the constructions of Section
6
, consider the based lattice
\Lambda_{GL_{n+1}}
from Section
6.1
along with its extension
\Lambda_{GL_{n+1};\zeta}
by the rank 1 lattice
\mathbb{Z}\zeta
, which is equipped with the basis obtained by adjoining the vector
e_{h}=-\zeta-p_{\varepsilon_{1}}
. Then if we write
\Lambda_{GL_{n+1};\zeta_{*}}
for the quiver where the vector
e_{h}
is regarded as a frozen direction, we have an isometric embedding

(8.22)

\displaystyle\Lambda_{S_{\geq 0}}(\underline{\Delta};c)\longrightarrow\Lambda_{GL_{n+1};\zeta_{*}}

given by

e_{s_{i}}(\Lambda_{S_{\geq 0}})\mapsto e_{s_{i}}(\Lambda_{GL_{n+1};\zeta_{*}}),\quad e_{t_{i}}(\Lambda_{S_{\geq 0}})\mapsto e_{t_{i}}(\Lambda_{GL_{n+1};\zeta_{*}}),\qquad 1\leq i\leq n

e_{h_{+}}\mapsto e_{s_{n+1}}+e_{t_{n+1}},\quad e_{h_{-}}\mapsto e_{h}.

Then the action
\mathcal{T}(\Lambda_{GL_{n+1};\zeta})
on
\mathscr{F}[S_{\leq 0}]
where
P_{i},X_{i}
act via (
8.20
) and the central generator
Y_{\zeta}
acts by

(Y_{\zeta}\circ\phi)(\lambda)=Y_{h_{+}}Y_{h_{-}}^{-1}\phi(\lambda)

restricts to the action of
\mathcal{T}_{{\geq 0}}(\underline{\Delta};c)
defined using (
8.2
).

Lemma 8.10
.

The universal Laurent ring
\mathbb{L}(\Lambda_{GL_{n+1};\zeta_{*}})
of the extended quiver is given by

(8.23)

\displaystyle\mathbb{L}(\Lambda_{GL_{n+1};\zeta_{*}})\simeq\mathbb{L}(\Lambda_{GL_{n+1}})[Y_{\zeta}^{\pm 1}],

and the embedding (
8.22
) induces an isomorphism

\mathbb{L}_{S_{\geq 0}}(\underline{\Delta};c)\simeq\mathbb{L}(\Lambda_{GL_{n+1};\zeta_{*}})^{\langle P_{\omega_{n+1}}\rangle},

where the right hand side denotes the centralizer of
P_{\omega_{n+1}}
in
\mathbb{L}(\Lambda_{GL_{n+1};\zeta_{*}})
.

Proof..

Since each
p_{\varepsilon_{i}}
is already contained in the lattice
\Lambda_{GL_{n+1}}
, we can perform a change of basis in
\Lambda_{GL_{n+1};\zeta_{*}}
modifying only the frozen direction
e_{h}
via
e_{h}\mapsto e_{h}+p_{\varepsilon_{1}}=-\zeta
, and so the first claim of the Lemma follows. Since (
8.22
) bijectively maps the set of mutable directions in
\Lambda_{S_{\geq 0}}
to those in
\Lambda_{GL_{n+1};\zeta_{*}}
, the second claim follows from observing that the image of (
8.22
) is exactly the orthogonal complement to
p_{\omega_{n+1}}=\sum_{i=1}^{n+1}p_{\varepsilon_{i}}
in
\Lambda_{GL_{n+1};\zeta_{*}}
. Indeed, this becomes clear after passing to the basis of the latter given by all mutable directions together with
e_{h},e_{t_{n+1}}+e_{s_{n+1}}
and
e_{s_{n+1}}
since

(p_{\omega_{n+1}},e_{s_{i}})=(p_{\omega_{n+1}},e_{t_{i}})=0,\quad i<n+1,\quad(\boldsymbol{p},e_{h})=0=(p_{\omega_{n+1}},e_{t_{n+1}}+e_{s_{n+1}}),

while
(e_{s_{n+1}},p_{\omega_{n+1}})=1
. So (
8.22
) identifies
\mathcal{T}(\Lambda_{S_{\geq 0}})
with the centralizer of
P_{\omega_{n+1}}
in
\mathcal{T}(\Lambda_{GL_{n+1};\zeta_{*}})
, and since
P_{\omega_{n+1}}
commutes with all mutable variables the corresponding statement also holds for the universal Laurent rings.
∎

It follows from the isomorphism (
8.23
) in the Lemma that the action of
\mathbb{L}(\Lambda^{\mathrm{fr}}_{\underline{\Delta};c})
preserves the submodule

\mathscr{V}[S_{\leq 0}]=\mathcal{T}_{\leq 0}(\underline{\Delta};c)\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}[q^{\pm 1}]}\mathscr{V}\subset\mathscr{F}[S_{\leq 0}]

of all functions vanishing outside the dominant cone. Indeed, this is clear for the subalgebra of multiplication operators
\mathcal{T}_{<0}(\underline{\Delta};c)
, while by Lemma
6.15
the action of the algebra
\mathbb{L}(\Lambda_{GL_{n+1};\zeta_{*}})
(and hence that of
\mathbb{L}_{S_{\geq 0}}(\underline{\Delta};c)
by (
8.23
)) also preserves
\mathscr{V}[S_{\leq 0}]
.
Now consider the action of
\mathcal{T}^{ext}_{\mathrm{loc}}(Q_{\underline{\Delta}^{\prime};c_{\pm}})
on

\mathcal{T}_{\leq 0}(\underline{\Delta}^{\prime};c_{\pm})\otimes\mathbb{Q}[q^{\pm 1}](\boldsymbol{w})
induced by letting the subalgebra
\mathcal{T}_{<0}(Q_{\underline{\Delta}^{\prime};c_{\pm}})
act by left multiplication operators, and setting

(8.24)

\displaystyle(Y_{c,\varepsilon^{\vee}_{i}}\bullet f)(w)

\displaystyle=f(w_{i}\mapsto q^{2}w_{i}),

(8.25)

\displaystyle(Y_{c,\varepsilon^{+}_{i}}\bullet f)(w)

\displaystyle=-q^{-1}Y_{h_{+}}w_{i}f(w),

(8.26)

\displaystyle(Y_{\zeta^{\prime}}\bullet f)(w)

\displaystyle=Y_{h_{+}}Y_{h_{-}}^{-1}f(w).

It follows from Lemmas
6.8
and
8.7
that the action of the extended residue torus
\mathcal{T}^{ext}_{\operatorname{res}}(Q_{\underline{\Delta}^{\prime};c_{\pm}})
preserves the subspace

\mathscr{V}[S^{\prime}_{\leq 0}]=\mathcal{T}_{\leq 0}(\underline{\Delta}^{\prime};c_{\pm})\otimes\mathbb{Z}[q^{\pm 1}][\boldsymbol{w}]^{sym}.

Using the gluing diffeomorphism
S\simeq S^{\prime}/\phi
to identify the corresponding bicolored subgraphs on
S,S^{\prime}
we get an isomorphism of quantum tori

\iota_{\phi}:\mathcal{T}_{\leq 0}(\underline{\Delta};c)\simeq\mathcal{T}_{\leq 0}(\underline{\Delta}^{\prime};c_{\pm}).

Combining it with the algebraic Whittaker transform (
6.42
), we get an isomorphism of
\mathbb{Z}[q^{\pm 1}]
-modules

(8.27)

\displaystyle\iota_{\phi}\otimes\mathcal{W}\colon\mathscr{V}[S_{\leq 0}]\simeq\mathscr{V}[S^{\prime}_{\leq 0}].

Proposition 8.11
.

The identification of faithful representations (
8.27
) induces an algebra isomorphism

(8.28)

\displaystyle\eta_{c}=\eta_{c;\underline{\Delta}}\colon\mathbb{L}(\Lambda^{\mathrm{fr}}_{\underline{\Delta};c})\simeq\mathcal{T}_{\operatorname{res}}(Q_{\underline{\Delta}^{\prime};c_{\pm}})

intertwining the Dehn twist automorphisms:

(8.29)

\displaystyle\eta_{c}\circ\tau_{c}(S)=\tau_{c_{-}}(S^{\prime})\circ\eta_{c}

Proof..

Recall that the algebras
\mathbb{L}(\Lambda^{\mathrm{fr}}_{\underline{\Delta};c})
and
\mathcal{T}_{\operatorname{res}}(Q_{\underline{\Delta}^{\prime};c_{\pm}})
are generated by their factors
\mathcal{T}_{<0}(\underline{\Delta},c)
(resp.
\mathcal{T}_{<0}(\underline{\Delta}^{\prime};c_{\pm})
) and
\mathbb{L}_{S_{\geq 0}}(\underline{\Delta};c)
(resp.
\mathcal{T}_{\operatorname{res}}(Q_{\underline{\Delta}^{\prime};c_{\pm}})
). Clearly
\mathcal{T}_{<0}(\underline{\Delta},c)
and
\mathcal{T}_{<0}(\underline{\Delta}^{\prime};c_{\pm})
give the same subalgebra of the endomorphism ring of
\mathscr{V}[S_{\leq 0}]
under the identification (
8.27
). On the other hand, combining Lemmas
8.7
and
8.10
with the intertwining relations for the algebra generators in Proposition
6.30
, we deduce that the same is true of the pair of extended algebras
\mathbb{L}(\Lambda_{GL_{n+1};\zeta_{*}})
and
\mathcal{T}^{ext}_{\operatorname{res}}(Q_{\underline{\Delta}^{\prime};c_{\pm}})
. Since the action of
Y_{e^{+}_{c,\omega_{n+1}}}
is identified with that of
P_{\omega_{n+1}}
, the Proposition follows by the characterization of
\mathcal{T}_{\operatorname{res}}(Q_{\underline{\Delta}^{\prime};c_{\pm}})
and
\mathbb{L}_{\geq 0}(\underline{\Delta};c)
as the centralizers of these elements in their extended versions.
For the last claim, we note that formula (
5.27
) identifies the Dehn twist automorphism
\tau_{c}(S)
with the restriction to
\mathbb{L}(\Lambda^{\mathrm{fr}}_{\underline{\Delta};c})
of the automorphism
\tau
in (
6.4
), while (
5.28
) identifies
\tau_{c}(S^{\prime})
with the the restriction to
\mathcal{T}_{\operatorname{res}}(Q_{\underline{\Delta}^{\prime};c_{\pm}})
of the automorphism
\widetilde{\gamma}
. Hence we deduce (
8.29
) from the intertwining relation (
6.53
).
∎

Remark 8.12
.

As observed in the proof of Proposition
8.11
, it is immediate from the construction of the isomorphism
\eta_{c}
that it is indeed ‘local’ in the sense that

(8.30)

\displaystyle\ell\in I_{S}^{<0}\implies\eta_{c}(Y_{\ell})=Y_{\iota_{\phi}(\ell)},

and that
\eta_{c}
restricts to an isomorphism

(8.31)

\displaystyle\eta_{c}^{\geq 0}\colon\mathbb{L}_{S_{\geq 0}}(\underline{\Delta};c)\simeq\mathcal{T}_{\geq 0;\operatorname{res}}(\underline{\Delta}^{\prime};c_{\pm}).

Remark 8.13
.

The particular choice of faithful representation spaces
\mathscr{V}[S_{\leq 0}]
and
\mathscr{V}[S^{\prime}_{\leq 0}]
used to construct
\eta_{c}
is not essential: we get the same isomorphism (
8.28
) if we work instead with functions valued in the skew fraction fields or (partial) completions of the quantum tori associated to
\Lambda_{S_{\leq 0}}
and
\Lambda_{S^{\prime}_{\leq 0}}
respectively.

The quivers
Q_{\underline{\Delta};c}
and
Q^{\omega}_{\underline{\Delta}^{\prime};c_{\pm}}
are a prototypical example of a more general notion that we call a
locally glueable pair.

illustrated in Figure

, and the ones
{I}^{--}_{\mathrm{hat}},{I}^{+-}_{\mathrm{hat}},{I}^{++}_{\mathrm{hat}}
illustrated in Figure

.
