Definition 8.14
.

A
locally glueable pair
of handle signature
\varepsilon=(\varepsilon_{a},\varepsilon_{b})\in\{++,-+,--\}
consists of a pair of quivers
(Q,Q_{\mathrm{cut}})
together with the following additional data:

(1)

A decomposition of index sets
I=I_{<0}\sqcup I_{\geq 0}
of the quiver
Q
, and a bijection
I_{\geq 0}\simeq I^{\varepsilon}_{\mathrm{Toda}}
such that the sublattice in
\Lambda=\Lambda_{Q}
corresponding to directions
\{s_{j},t_{j}\}_{j=1}^{n}
is orthogonal to the sublattice
\Lambda_{<0}
corresponding to
I_{<0}
.

(2)

A decomposition of index sets
I_{\mathrm{cut}}=I_{\mathrm{cut}}^{<0}\sqcup I_{\mathrm{cut}}^{\geq 0}
and a bijection
I_{\mathrm{cut}}^{\geq 0}\simeq I^{\varepsilon}_{\mathrm{hat}}
such that the sublattice in
\Lambda_{Q^{\mathrm{cut}}}
corresponding to directions
\{e_{c,j}\}_{j=1}^{n}
is orthogonal to the sublattice
\Lambda^{\mathrm{cut}}_{{<0}}
corresponding to
I_{\mathrm{cut}}^{<0}
,
and the action of the Weyl group
W\simeq S_{n+1}
on the lattice
\Lambda_{\mathrm{cut}}
fixing all
e_{\ell}
for
\ell\in I^{<0}_{\mathrm{cut}}
and satisfying

\sigma(e_{v_{j,a}})=e_{v_{\sigma(j),a}},\quad\sigma(e_{v_{j,b}})=e_{v_{\sigma(j),b}},

\sigma_{i}(e_{c,j})=\begin{cases}e_{c,i}+e_{c,j}\quad&|i-j|=1\\
-e_{c,i}\quad&j=i\\
e_{c,j}\quad&\text{else}\end{cases}

is an action by isometries of
\Lambda_{\mathrm{cut}}
.

(3)

A bijection
I_{<0}\sqcup\{h_{a},h_{b}\}\simeq I^{<0}_{\mathrm{cut}}\sqcup\{v_{n+1,a},v_{n+1,b}\}
such that the induced map between sublattices of
\Lambda
and
\Lambda_{{\mathrm{cut}}}
is an isometry.

It is straightforward to extend the definition of the residue quantum torus from Definition
8.6
to the more general setting of a locally glueable pair
(Q,Q_{\mathrm{cut}})
.
Indeed, setting

\dot{e}_{v_{j,a}}={e}_{v_{j,a}}-{e}_{v_{j+1,a}},\qquad\dot{e}_{v_{j,b}}={e}_{v_{j,b}}-{e}_{v_{j+1,b}},\quad j=1,\ldots,n

the vectors
\{\dot{e}_{v_{j,a}}-\varepsilon_{a}\varepsilon_{b}\dot{e}_{v_{j,b}}\}
lie in the kernel of the skew form on
\Lambda_{\mathrm{cut}}
and so the form descends to the quotient lattice
\overline{\Lambda}_{\mathrm{cut}}
. We write
\mathcal{T}(\overline{\Lambda}_{\mathrm{cut}})
for the corresponding quantum torus, which again contains elements
Y_{c,\beta^{\vee}}
for each element of the root lattice as defined by formula (
8.9
). By our assumption on the isometric nature of the Weyl group action in part (2) of Definition
8.14
, the elements

\dot{e}_{v_{j}}\in\overline{\Lambda}_{\mathrm{cut}}
given by the images of the
\dot{e}_{v_{j,a}}
in the symplectically reduced lattice have zero pairing with all mutable directions. So we can use them to define mutation-invariant elements
Y_{c,\alpha}
of
\mathcal{T}(\overline{\Lambda}_{\mathrm{cut}})
as the unique ones of the form
Y_{\sum_{j}m_{j}\dot{e}_{v_{j}}}
satisfying the commutation relations (
8.11
). These elements define an internal grading as well as an Ore denominator set via (
8.15
), and by assumption we have an action of the Weyl group on the localized quantum torus
\mathcal{T}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\overline{\Lambda}_{\mathrm{cut}})
by cluster transformations. Hence we can define the residue quantum torus
\mathcal{T}_{\operatorname{res}}(Q_{\mathrm{cut}})
associated to
Q_{\mathrm{cut}}
as the set of all elements of
\mathcal{T}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\overline{\Lambda}_{\mathrm{cut}})
satisfying the conditions of Definition
8.6
. Writing
\mathbb{L}(Q^{\mathrm{fr}})
for the universal Laurent ring associated to the quiver
Q^{\mathrm{fr}}
obtained from
Q
by freezing all directions except
\{s_{j},t_{j}\}_{j=1}^{n}
, a straightforward adaptation of the construction used to prove Proposition
8.11
yields a local gluing isomorphism

(8.32)

\displaystyle\eta\colon\mathbb{L}(Q^{\mathrm{fr}})\simeq\mathcal{T}_{\operatorname{res}}(Q_{\mathrm{cut}}).

8.3.
Local gluing isomorphism for
G=SL_{n+1}
.

We now briefly discuss how to extend the local gluing map (
8.28
) to the larger quantum torus
\mathcal{T}^{\mathcal{A}}(Q_{\underline{\Delta};c})\supset\mathcal{T}(Q_{\underline{\Delta};c})
associated to the lattice
\Xi({\underline{\Delta};c})\supset\Lambda(\underline{\Delta};c)
.
The main technical
8
8

8

We would like to have a better general technique for handling locality properties of compatible pairs than the fairly ad hoc one we explain in this section.
issue here is that, unlike the situation for
e
-bases, the skew-pairing between elements of the
\xi
-basis associated to an ideal graph does not have an obviously local form. For this reason, our strategy will be to embed the lattice
\Xi(\underline{\Delta};c)
into a lattice obtained by a rational rescaling of
\Lambda(\underline{\Delta};c)
, then prove that the
PGL_{n+1}
local gluing map extends to the algebras associated to these rescaled lattices, and then finally check that the resulting isomorphism respects integrality with respect to the original
\Xi
-lattices.

Consider the compatible pair
(\Xi^{\mathrm{fr}}(\underline{\Delta};c),\Lambda^{\mathrm{fr}}(\underline{\Delta};c))
obtained from
(\Xi(\underline{\Delta};c),\Lambda(\underline{\Delta};c))
by freezing all directions except for those labelled by
I_{S}^{>0}
.
As in Definition
8.9
, we denote the corresponding
\mathcal{A}
-variable universal Laurent ring by

\mathbb{L}(\Xi^{\mathrm{fr}}_{\underline{\Delta};c})\subset\mathcal{T}^{\mathcal{A}}(\underline{\Delta};c).

Recall that the relation between the
e
- and
\xi
-bases is encoded by the ensemble matrix
p_{S}=p_{S}(\underline{\Delta};c)
. Consider the block decomposition of
p_{S}
with respect to the partition

I(Q_{\underline{\Delta};c})=I_{S}^{\leq 0}\sqcup I_{S}^{>0},

from Notation
5.6
, which we write as

\displaystyle p_{S}\;=\;\left[\begin{array}[]{c|c}A&B\\
\hline\cr C&D\end{array}\right].

Here
A
is the
I_{S}^{\leq 0}\times I_{S}^{\leq 0}
block,
B
is the
I_{S}^{\leq 0}\times I_{S}^{>0}
block, etc. Recall that the
(i,j)
-entry of this matrix records the coefficient of
\xi_{i}
in the expansion of
e_{j}
in the basis
\{\xi_{i}\}
for
\Xi
.

Inspecting the quiver
Q_{\underline{\Delta};c}
, it is clear that the only nonzero columns of
C
are the two associated to the handles
(h_{+},h_{-})
, each of which has exactly two nonzero entries:

C_{s_{1},h_{+}}=-1=C_{s_{n},h_{-}},\qquad C_{t_{1},h_{+}}=1=C_{t_{n},h_{-}}.

Similarly, the only nonzero rows of
B
are those indexed by
h_{+},h_{-}
:

B_{h_{+},s_{1}}=1=B_{h_{-},s_{n}},\qquad B_{h_{+},t_{1}}=-1=B_{h_{-},t_{n}}.

The matrix
D
is invertible, with the nonzero inverse entries of
D^{-1}
being given by

(8.35)

\displaystyle D^{-1}_{s_{i},t_{j}}=-\mathfrak{A}_{ij},\quad D^{-1}_{t_{i},s_{j}}=\mathfrak{A}_{ij}

where
\mathfrak{A}
is the Cartan matrix of type
A_{n}
.
It follows from these observations that
BD^{-1}C=0
, and so the inverse ensemble matrix is given by

\displaystyle(p_{S})^{-1}\;=\;\left[\begin{array}[]{c|c}A^{-1}&-A^{-1}BD^{-1}\\
\hline\cr-D^{-1}CA^{-1}&D^{-1}+D^{-1}CA^{-1}BD^{-1}\end{array}\right].

The
(i,j)
-entry of this matrix records the coefficient of
e_{i}
in the
\mathbb{Q}
-expansion of
\xi_{j}
in the basis
\{e_{i}\}
for
\Lambda^{\mathbb{Q}}_{Q_{\underline{\Delta};c}}
.

Lemma 8.15
.

Any element of
\Lambda_{S}^{\mathbb{Q}}
corresponding to a vector in the column-span of the rank-2 matrix
D^{-1}CA^{-1}
is a
\mathbb{Q}
-linear combination of

z^{>0}_{+}=\sum_{j=1}^{n}j(e_{s_{j}}+e_{t_{j}}),\quad z^{>0}_{-}=\sum_{j=1}^{n}j(e_{s_{n-j+1}}+e_{t_{n-j+1}}),

and the projection to
\Lambda_{\geq 0}
of any
\xi_{\ell}
with
\ell\in I_{\leq 0}
is a
\mathbb{Q}
-linear combination of the vectors

(8.38)

\displaystyle z_{+}=(n+1)e_{h_{+}}+\sum_{j=1}^{n}j(e_{s_{j}}+e_{t_{j}}),\quad z_{-}=(n+1)e_{h_{-}}+\sum_{j=1}^{n}j(e_{s_{n-j+1}}+e_{t_{n-j+1}}).

Proof..

This can be seen by directly computing
D^{-1}C
, which has exactly two nonzero columns in positions
h_{\pm}
. Alternatively, one can argue as follows: the columns of
D^{-1}CA^{-1}
are given by the projection of basis vectors
\xi_{\ell},\ell\in I_{S}^{\leq 0}
to
\Lambda^{\mathbb{Q}}_{I_{S}^{>0}}
with respect to the (non-orthogonal) direct sum decomposition

(8.39)

\displaystyle\Lambda^{\mathbb{Q}}_{I_{S}}=\Lambda^{\mathbb{Q}}_{I_{S}^{\leq 0}}\oplus\Lambda^{\mathbb{Q}}_{I_{S}^{>0}}

induced by partitioning the basis
\{e_{\ell}\}
according to
I_{S}=I_{S}^{\leq 0}\sqcup I_{S}^{>0}
. By definition of a compatible pair, any
\xi_{\ell}
with
\ell\in I_{S}^{\leq 0}
must be skew-orthogonal to all
e_{s_{j}},e_{t_{j}}
since the latter vectors are mutable. An easy calculation shows that the skew-orthogonal complement of
\{e_{s_{j}},e_{t_{j}}\}_{j=1}^{n}
is spanned by the vectors
e_{\ell}
with
\ell\in I_{S}^{<0}
together with the
z_{\pm}
.
Taking their projections to
\Lambda^{\mathbb{Q}}_{I_{S}^{>0}}
, we get the claims of the Lemma.
∎

Remark 8.16
.

Note that the coefficients of
z_{+},z_{-}
in in the expansion of any
\overline{\xi}_{i}
with
i\in I_{S}^{\leq 0}
are determined by the matrix
A
, since the coefficients of
e_{h_{\pm}}
in that expansion are entries of
A^{-1}
.

It is useful to consider the slightly larger version
{\mathcal{T}}_{\widetilde{\Xi}_{S}}
of
\mathcal{T}_{\Xi_{S}}
in which we replace the basis vectors
\xi_{h_{\pm}}
by their rescalings
\xi_{h_{\pm}}/(n+1)
. Here, and in the remainder of this section, we also assume we have extended scalars in the base ring to incorporate the needed fractional powers of
q
.

Now consider the following elements of the extended lattice
\widetilde{\Xi}_{S}
:

(8.40)

\displaystyle\tilde{\xi}_{s_{j}}=\xi_{s_{j}}-\frac{j\xi_{h_{+}}+(n+1-j)\xi_{h_{-}}}{n+1},\qquad\tilde{\xi}_{t_{j}}=\xi_{t_{j}}-\frac{j\xi_{h_{+}}+(n+1-j)\xi_{h_{-}}}{n+1}.

We write
\widetilde{\Xi}_{S_{>0}}
for the lattice spanned by the vectors (
8.40
).

Lemma 8.17
.

(1)

There exists an integer
N=M(n+1)
divisible by
(n+1)
such that

\widetilde{\Xi}_{S}\subset\widetilde{\Lambda}_{S}=\frac{1}{N}\Lambda_{<0}(\underline{\Delta})\oplus\mathbb{Z}\langle\frac{z_{\pm}}{N}\rangle\oplus\Lambda_{>0}(\underline{\Delta}).

(2)

Writing
\widetilde{\Lambda}_{GL_{n+1};\zeta}
for the lattice spanned by the vectors
\{e_{s_{j}}(\Lambda_{GL_{n+1}}),e_{t_{j}}(\Lambda_{GL_{n+1}})\}_{j=1}^{n}
in the basis (
6.2
) along with

\displaystyle\widetilde{\xi}_{s_{n+1}}

\displaystyle=(p_{\omega_{n+1}}+x_{\omega_{n+1}})/N,

\displaystyle\widetilde{\xi}_{t_{n+1}}

\displaystyle=x_{\omega_{n+1}}/N,

\displaystyle\widetilde{\zeta}

\displaystyle=\zeta/M,

the assignments

(8.41)

\displaystyle\tilde{\xi}_{s_{j}}\longmapsto\xi_{s_{j}}({\Lambda}_{GL_{n+1}})-jM\tilde{\xi}_{s_{n+1}}({\Lambda}_{GL_{n+1}}),

(8.42)

\displaystyle\tilde{\xi}_{t_{j}}\longmapsto\xi_{t_{j}}({\Lambda}_{GL_{n+1}})-jM\tilde{\xi}_{t_{n+1}}({\Lambda}_{GL_{n+1}})

for
1\leqslant j\leqslant n
and

\frac{z_{-}}{N}\longmapsto-\frac{\zeta}{M}-\frac{p_{\omega_{n+1}}}{N},\qquad\frac{z_{+}}{N}\longmapsto\frac{p_{\omega_{n+1}}}{N},

define an isometric embedding into
\widetilde{\Lambda}_{GL_{n+1};\zeta}
of the sublattice
\widetilde{\Lambda}_{S_{\geq 0}}
spanned by
\widetilde{\Xi}_{S_{>0}}
and the
z_{\pm}/N
as the orthogonal complement of
p_{\omega_{n+1}}
.

(3)

Under the embedding above, we have

(8.43)

\displaystyle e_{\ell}(\Lambda(\underline{\Delta};c))\mapsto e_{\ell}(\Lambda_{GL_{n+1}}),\quad\ell\in\{s_{j},t_{j}\}_{j=1}^{n}.

Proof..

By Lemma
8.15
, we know there exists
M
such that each
\xi_{\ell}
with
\ell\in I_{S}^{\leq 0}
lies in
\frac{1}{M}\Lambda_{<0}(\underline{\Delta})\oplus\mathbb{Z}\{\frac{z_{\pm}}{M}\}
. Now consider the block column operation on the matrix
p_{S}^{-1}
given by

\mathrm{col}_{2}\mapsto\mathrm{col}_{2}-\mathrm{col}_{1}BD^{-1}.

Then the resulting matrix has second column given by
(0,D^{-1})^{t}
.
Since
B
is zero except for its rows corresponding to
h_{\pm}
, recalling the formula (
8.35
) for
D^{-1}
we see that linear combinations of
e_{\ell}
corresponding to these columns are exactly the
\tilde{\xi}_{s_{j}},\tilde{\xi}_{t_{j}}
, and so taking
N=M(n+1)
implies the first claim of the Lemma.
By the identification of the Toda quiver as a subquiver in
Q_{\underline{\Delta};c}
, it is clear that the map
e_{\ell}(\Lambda(\underline{\Delta};c))\mapsto e_{\ell}(\Lambda_{GL_{n+1}}),\ell\in I_{S}^{>0}
is an isometry. Recall also that
(z_{\pm},e_{\ell})=0
for all
\ell\in I_{S}^{>0}
. Since the
\tilde{\xi}_{s_{j}},\tilde{\xi}_{t_{j}}
are expressed solely in terms of these
e_{\ell}
via the matrix
D^{-1}
, an easy calculation shows that the map (
8.41
) defines an isometric embedding of
\widetilde{\Xi}_{>0}
into
\widetilde{\Lambda}_{GL_{n+1}}
satisfying (
8.43
).

∎

Since each
e_{\ell}
with
\ell\in I_{S}^{>0}
is orthogonal to the
z_{\pm}
and
\Lambda_{<0}
, we have a well-defined universal Laurent ring
\mathbb{L}(\widetilde{\Lambda}^{\mathrm{fr}}_{\underline{\Delta};c})
with mutable directions
I_{S}^{>0}
. On the other hand, consider the extended universal Laurent ring
\widetilde{\Lambda}_{GL_{n+1},\zeta}\supset{\Lambda}_{GL_{n+1},\zeta}
with mutable directions
\{e_{s_{j}},e_{t_{j}}\}_{j=1}^{n}
.
Since by its definition in point (2) of the Lemma
\widetilde{\Lambda}_{GL_{n+1},\zeta}
has a
\mathbb{Z}
-basis consisting of the mutable directions and orthogonal frozen directions, its universal Laurent ring is is obtained from
\mathbb{L}_{{\Lambda}_{GL_{n+1},\zeta_{*}}}
by adjoining the corresponding roots of frozen generators and
q
:

\mathbb{L}_{\widetilde{\Lambda}_{GL_{n+1},\zeta_{*}}}=\mathbb{L}_{{\Lambda}_{GL_{n+1}}}\langle q^{\frac{1}{N}},Y_{\zeta/M},P_{\omega_{n+1}}^{\frac{1}{N}},X_{\omega_{n+1}}^{\frac{1}{N}}\rangle.

Then as in Lemma
8.23
, the algebra
\mathbb{L}(\widetilde{\Lambda}^{\mathrm{fr}}_{\underline{\Delta};c})
embeds into
\mathbb{L}_{\widetilde{\Lambda}_{GL_{n+1},\zeta_{*}}}
as the centralizer of
P_{\omega_{n+1}/N}
.

We extend the action of
\mathbb{L}_{{\Lambda}_{GL_{n+1},\zeta_{*}}}
on
\mathscr{F}[S_{\leq 0}]
to one of
\mathbb{L}_{\widetilde{\Lambda}_{GL_{n+1},\zeta_{*}}}
on the space of
\widetilde{\mathscr{F}}[S_{\leq 0}]
of
\mathcal{T}_{\Lambda_{\leq 0}}\langle q^{\frac{1}{N}},(-q)^{\frac{1}{2M}},Y_{h_{\pm}}^{\frac{1}{M}}\rangle
-valued functions on the lattice
\{\lambda\in\frac{1}{N}\mathbb{Z}^{n+1}~|~\lambda_{i}-\lambda_{i+1}\in\mathbb{Z}\}
in the natural way, letting

\displaystyle(P_{\omega_{n+1}}^{\frac{1}{N}}\circ\phi)(\lambda)

\displaystyle=(-q)^{\frac{1}{M}}Y^{\frac{1}{M}}_{h_{+}}\phi(\lambda-\omega_{n+1}/N)

\displaystyle(X_{\omega_{n+1}}^{\frac{1}{N}}\circ\phi)(\lambda)

\displaystyle=(-q)^{\frac{n}{2M}}q^{2|\lambda|/N}\phi(\lambda)

\displaystyle(Y_{\zeta/M}\circ\phi)(\lambda)

\displaystyle=Y^{\frac{1}{M}}_{h_{+}}Y^{-\frac{1}{M}}_{h_{-}}\phi(\lambda).

This way we get an action of
\mathcal{T}_{\widetilde{\Lambda}_{<0}}\otimes\mathbb{L}_{{\Lambda}_{GL_{n+1},\zeta_{*}}}
and hence of its subalgebra
\mathbb{L}_{\widetilde{\Lambda}_{S}}(I_{S}^{>0})
on
\widetilde{\mathscr{F}}[S_{\leq 0}]
, preserving the subspace
\widetilde{\mathscr{V}}[S_{\leq 0}]
of functions vanishing outside the cone of generalized partitions. The latter space has a Whittaker basis
W_{\lambda}
obtained by extending the top Pieri rule to the shift by
\omega_{n+1}/N
, so that
W_{\omega_{n+1}/N}=W_{\omega_{n+1}}^{1/N}=\mathbf{w}^{1/N}
.

Now let us discuss the symplectically-reduced lattice
\Xi^{c}({\underline{\Delta}^{\prime};c_{\pm}})
associated to a
c_{\pm}
-isolating cluster on
S^{\prime}
. In the corresponding lattice
\Xi({\underline{\Delta}^{\prime};c_{\pm}})
before symplectic reduction, we make the following integral change of basis in accordance with (
4.26
):

(8.44)

\displaystyle\dot{\xi}_{\ell}=\begin{cases}\sum_{r=1}^{i}\xi_{v^{\pm}_{r}},\qquad&\ell=v^{\pm}_{i},\quad 1\leq i\leq n+1\\
\xi_{\ell},\qquad&\text{otherwise.}\end{cases}

In this new basis we have

(\dot{e}_{v_{j}^{\pm}},\dot{\xi}_{v_{i}^{\pm}})=\delta_{+,-}\delta_{i,j}.

Since the
e_{v_{j}^{\pm}}
are mutable, for any
\ell\in I_{S^{\prime}}^{\leq 0}\setminus\{v_{n+1}^{\pm}\}
, we have
(e_{v_{j}^{\pm}},\xi_{\ell})=0
for all
j
by definition of a compatible pair. In particular, such
\xi_{\ell}
is orthogonal to all elements
\dot{e}_{v_{j}^{\pm}}
and therefore to the elements
\dot{e}_{v_{j}^{+}}+\dot{e}_{v_{j}^{-}}
defining the reduction constraint.
Hence we can choose the following basis in the reduced lattice
\Xi_{c}=\Xi_{Q_{\underline{\Delta}^{\prime};c_{\pm}}}^{c}
:

(8.45)

\displaystyle{\overline{\xi}}_{\ell}=\begin{cases}\dot{\xi}_{v^{\pm}_{n+1}},\qquad&\ell=v_{n+1}^{\pm}\\
\dot{\xi}_{v^{+}_{j}}+\dot{\xi}_{v^{-}_{n+1}}-\dot{\xi}_{v^{-}_{j}}+j\dot{\xi}_{(c,j)},\qquad&\ell=v_{i},\quad 1\leq j\leq n\\
\dot{\xi}_{\ell},\qquad&\text{otherwise.}\end{cases}

Then we have the pairings
(\overline{\xi}_{v_{i}},\overline{\xi}_{v_{j}})=0
, while

(\dot{e}_{v_{i}},\overline{\xi}_{v_{j}})=\delta_{ij},\quad 1\leq i,j\leq n.

Hence the degrees of elements of the
\overline{\xi}_{\ell}
basis with respect to the internal grading by the
SL_{n+1}
weight lattice are given by

\deg(\overline{\xi}_{\ell})=\begin{cases}\omega_{j}\quad&\ell=v_{j},~1\leq j\leq n\\
0\quad&\text{else.}\end{cases}

Remark 8.18
.

(1)

Note that despite the minus sign in (
8.45
), the elements
\{\overline{\xi}_{\ell}\}
are (images in
\Xi^{c}
of) non-negative integer linear combinations of the original basis
\{\xi_{\ell}\}
elements from the compatible pair
\Xi({\underline{\Delta}^{\prime};c_{\pm}})
. In particular, the
Y_{\overline{\xi}_{\ell}}
are elements of the universally Laurent ring
\mathbb{L}^{c}
.

(2)

Another natural choice of basis vectors in the
v_{j}
directions comes from reversing the roles of
+/-
by taking

\overline{\xi}^{o}_{v_{j}}=\dot{\xi}_{v^{-}_{j}}+\dot{\xi}_{v^{+}_{n+1}}-\dot{\xi}_{v^{+}_{j}}-j\dot{\xi}_{(c,j)}.

Like the
Y_{\overline{\xi}_{v_{j}}}
, the
Y_{\overline{\xi}^{o}_{v_{j}}}
are universally Laurent.

Notation 8.19
.

For each fundamental weight
\omega_{i}
of
SL_{n+1}
, we write
\omega_{c,i}
for the element of
\Xi^{c}
defined by

(8.46)

\displaystyle\omega_{c,i}=\xi_{c_{-},i}=-\xi_{c_{+},i}.

Similarly to the
PGL_{n+1}
case, we use the elements (
8.46
) to associate to each element of the weight lattice of
SL_{n+1}
a quantum torus element

Y_{c,\lambda}=Y_{\sum m_{k}{\omega}_{c,k}},\qquad\lambda=\sum_{k=1}^{n}m_{k}\omega_{k}.

We define
\dot{p}_{S^{\prime}}
to be the change-of-basis matrix

\dot{e}_{j}=\sum_{i}(\dot{p}_{S^{\prime}})_{i,j}\overline{\xi}_{i},

and again consider its block decomposition with respect to the partition
I_{S^{\prime}}=I_{S^{\prime}}^{\leq 0}\sqcup I_{S^{\prime}}^{>0}
:

\dot{p}_{S^{\prime}}\;=\;\left[\begin{array}[]{c|c}\dot{A}&\dot{B}\\
\hline\cr\dot{C}&\dot{D}\end{array}\right].

Now we have a canonical identification
I_{S}^{<0}\equiv I_{S^{\prime}}^{<0}
of index sets (coming from the identification of the corresponding sub graphs induced by the gluing diffeomorphism), which we extend to
I_{S}^{\leq 0}\equiv I_{S^{\prime}}^{\leq 0}
by identifying

I_{S}\ni h_{\pm}\leftrightarrow v^{\pm}_{n+1}\in I_{S^{\prime}}.

Since we have only changed the
e
-basis inside the
I_{S^{\prime}}^{>0}
block, and in the
\xi
-basis have replaced
\xi_{v_{n+1}}^{\pm}
by itself plus a linear combination of
\xi
-basis vectors indexed by
I_{S^{\prime}}^{>0}
, it follows that under the identification of black-white graphs above we have
\dot{A}=A
, where
A
is the block of
p_{S}
from (
8.3
).
The nonzero entries of
\dot{B}
and
\dot{C}
are given by

\dot{B}_{h_{+},(c,n)}=-1,\qquad\dot{B}_{h_{-},(c,1)}=1,\quad\dot{C}_{(c,n),h_{+}}=1,\qquad\dot{C}_{(c,n),h_{-}}=-1

The basis (
8.45
) is chosen so that the only nonzero-entries of the block
\dot{D}
are again encoded by the
A_{n}
-Cartan matrix:

(\dot{D})_{v_{i},(c,j)}=\mathfrak{A}_{ij}=-(\dot{D})_{(c,j),v_{i}}.

In particular, we again have
\dot{B}\dot{D}^{-1}\dot{C}=0
and it follows that

\displaystyle\dot{p}_{S^{\prime}}^{-1}\;=\;\left[\begin{array}[]{c|c}A^{-1}&-A^{-1}\dot{B}D^{-1}\\
\hline\cr-\dot{D}^{-1}\dot{C}A^{-1}&\dot{D}^{-1}+\dot{D}^{-1}\dot{C}A^{-1}\dot{B}\dot{D}^{-1}\end{array}\right].

From the computation of
\dot{D}^{-1}\dot{C}
, it follows as in Lemma
8.15
that the projection to
\Lambda^{c}_{>0}
of any
\overline{\xi}_{i}
with
i\in I_{S}^{\leq 0}
is a rational multiple of the vector
\sum_{j=1}^{n}j\dot{e}_{v_{j}}
. Since all such
\overline{\xi}_{i}
are fixed by the action of the Weyl group
W(c_{\pm})
, we get

Lemma 8.20
.

The projection to
\Lambda^{c}_{\geq 0}
of any
\overline{\xi}_{i}
with
i\in I_{S^{\prime}}^{\leq 0}
is a rational linear combination of the vectors

(8.49)

\displaystyle z^{\prime}_{+}=(n+1)e_{h_{+}}+\sum_{j=1}^{n}j\dot{e}_{v_{j}},\qquad z^{\prime}_{-}=(n+1)e_{h_{-}}-\sum_{j=1}^{n}j\dot{e}_{v_{j}}.

As in Remark
8.16
, the coefficients of
z^{\prime}_{+},z^{\prime}_{-}
in in the expansion of such a
\overline{\xi}_{i}
are determined by the matrix
A
, since the coefficients of
e_{h_{\pm}}
in that expansion are entries of
A^{-1}
.

We again introduce the larger version
\widetilde{\Xi}_{c}
of
\Xi_{c}
in which we replace the basis vectors
\overline{\xi}_{h_{\pm}}
by their rescalings
\overline{\xi}_{h_{\pm}}/(n+1)
.
By the form (
8.3
) of
\dot{p}_{S^{\prime}}^{-1}
, we see that
\widetilde{\Xi}_{c}
sits inside the lattice

\widetilde{\Lambda}^{c}=\frac{1}{N}\Lambda^{c}_{<0}(\underline{\Delta}^{\prime})\oplus\mathbb{Z}\langle\frac{z^{\prime}_{\pm}}{N}\rangle\oplus\Lambda_{>0}(\underline{\Delta}^{\prime}).

where
N
is the same as in Lemma
8.17
, and the lattice
\widetilde{\Lambda}^{c}_{\geq 0}=\langle\frac{z^{\prime}_{\pm}}{N}\rangle\oplus\Lambda_{>0}(\underline{\Delta}^{\prime})
has another basis given by the
{z^{\prime}_{\pm}}/{N}
together with the vectors
\{\omega_{c,j}\}_{j=1}^{n}
along with the vectors

(8.50)

\displaystyle\widetilde{\xi}_{v_{j}}

\displaystyle=\overline{\xi}_{v_{j}}-\frac{j\overline{\xi}_{h_{+}}+(n+1-j)\overline{\xi}_{h_{-}}}{n+1},\qquad j=1,\ldots,n

obtained by applying the applying the same column operations used in Lemma
8.17
. Consider the lattice
\widetilde{\Lambda}_{\geq 0}^{c;ext}(\underline{\Delta}^{\prime})
spanned by the vectors
\{e^{+}_{c,\omega_{j}},\widetilde{\xi}_{v_{j}}\}_{j=1}^{n}
together with

\displaystyle\widetilde{e}^{+}_{c,\omega_{n+1}}

\displaystyle=e^{+}_{c,\omega_{n+1}}/N,

\displaystyle\widetilde{e}_{c,\omega^{\vee}_{n+1}}

\displaystyle=e_{c,\omega^{\vee}_{n+1}}/N,

\displaystyle\widetilde{\zeta}^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\zeta^{\prime}/M.

The Weyl group action on
\Lambda_{\geq 0}^{c;ext}(\underline{\Delta}^{\prime})
naturally extends to
\widetilde{\Lambda}_{\geq 0}^{c;ext}(\underline{\Delta}^{\prime})
; note that the
{e}^{+}_{c,\omega_{n+1}},\widetilde{e}_{c,\omega^{\vee}_{n+1}}
and
\zeta^{\prime}
are all fixed vectors. Using the formulas (
5.24
) for the braid group action, we easily derive:

Lemma 8.21
.

The map

\displaystyle{\omega}_{c,j}

\displaystyle\mapsto\widetilde{e}^{+}_{c,\omega_{j}}(\widetilde{\Lambda}_{\geq 0}^{c;ext})-jM\widetilde{e}^{+}_{c,\omega_{n+1}},

\displaystyle\widetilde{\xi}_{v_{j}}

\displaystyle\mapsto\widetilde{e}_{c,\omega^{\vee}_{j}}(\widetilde{\Lambda}_{\geq 0}^{c;ext})-jM\widetilde{e}^{+}_{c,\omega^{\vee}_{n+1}}

\frac{z^{\prime}_{-}}{N}\mapsto-\frac{\zeta^{\prime}}{M}-\widetilde{e}_{c,\omega^{\vee}_{n+1}},\quad\frac{z_{+}}{N}\mapsto\widetilde{e}_{c,\omega^{\vee}_{n+1}}

defines a Weyl group-equivariant isometric embedding of
\widetilde{\Lambda}^{c}_{\geq 0}
as the orthgonal complement to
\widetilde{e}_{c,\omega^{\vee}_{n+1}}
in
\widetilde{\Lambda}_{\geq 0}^{c;ext}(\underline{\Delta}^{\prime})
.

Hence the residue quantum torus
\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}_{\geq 0;\operatorname{res}}(\underline{\Delta}^{\prime};c_{\pm})
associated to the lattice
\widetilde{\Lambda}^{c}_{\geq 0}
spanned by the
\{\widetilde{e}_{c,\omega_{j}},\widetilde{e}_{c,\omega^{\vee}_{j}}\}_{j=1}^{n}
together with the
z^{\prime}_{\pm}/N
embeds as the centralizer of
Y_{{e}^{+}_{c,\omega_{n+1}}}
in the residue quantum torus
\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}^{ext}_{\geq 0;\operatorname{res}}(\underline{\Delta}^{\prime};c_{\pm})
associated to
\Lambda_{\geq 0}^{c;ext}(\underline{\Delta}^{\prime})
. As an algebra, the latter is just isomorphic to
\mathcal{D}_{\mathrm{res}}\langle q^{\frac{1}{N}},Y_{\zeta^{\prime}/M},\mathbf{w}^{\frac{1}{n+1}},\mathbf{D}^{\frac{1}{n+1}}\rangle
.

We again extend the action of
\mathcal{T}^{ext}_{\operatorname{res}}(Q_{\underline{\Delta}^{\prime};c_{\pm}})
on
\mathcal{T}_{\leq 0}(\underline{\Delta}^{\prime};c_{\pm})[\boldsymbol{w}^{\pm}]^{\mathrm{sym}}
to an action of
\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}^{ext}_{\geq 0;\operatorname{res}}(\underline{\Delta}^{\prime};c_{\pm})
on

\widetilde{\mathscr{V}}[S^{\prime}_{\leq 0}]=\mathcal{T}_{\leq 0}(\underline{\Delta}^{\prime};c_{\pm})\otimes\mathbb{Q}[q^{\pm 1}][\boldsymbol{w}^{\pm}]^{\mathrm{sym}}[\mathbf{w}^{\frac{1}{N}},q^{\frac{1}{N}},(-q)^{\frac{1}{2M}},Y_{h_{\pm}}^{\frac{1}{M}}]

by setting

\displaystyle(Y_{e^{+}_{c,\omega_{n+1}}/N}\circ f)(w)

\displaystyle=(-q)^{1/M}Y^{\frac{1}{M}}_{h_{+}}w_{1}\ldots w_{n+1}f(w)

\displaystyle(Y_{e_{c,\omega^{\vee}_{n+1}}/N}\circ f)(\lambda)

\displaystyle=f(\{w_{i}\mapsto q^{\frac{1}{N}}w_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n})

\displaystyle(Y_{\zeta^{\prime}/M}\circ f)(w)

\displaystyle=Y^{\frac{1}{M}}_{h_{+}}Y^{-\frac{1}{M}}_{h_{-}}f(w).

In this way we get an faithful representation of the residue quantum torus
\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}_{\geq 0;\operatorname{res}}(\underline{\Delta}^{\prime};c_{\pm})
associated to the lattice
\widetilde{\Lambda}^{c}
on
\widetilde{\mathscr{V}}[S^{\prime}_{\leq 0}]
.
Now as in the
PGL_{n+1}
case, the algebraic Whittaker transform and the gluing identification
\iota_{\phi}
give rise to an isomorphism

\widetilde{\mathscr{V}}[S_{\leq 0}]\simeq\widetilde{\mathscr{V}}[S^{\prime}_{\leq 0}],

and following Proposition
8.11
this induces an isomorphism of algebras

(8.51)

\displaystyle\widetilde{\eta}_{c}^{\mathcal{A}}\colon\mathbb{L}(\widetilde{\Lambda}^{\mathrm{fr}}_{\underline{\Delta};c})\simeq\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}_{\geq 0;\operatorname{res}}(\underline{\Delta}^{\prime};c_{\pm})

extending (
8.28
).
Recall from our Convention
4.17
that the base ring over which both algebras
\mathbb{L}(\Xi^{\mathrm{fr}}_{\underline{\Delta};c})
and
\mathcal{T}^{\mathcal{A}}_{\operatorname{res}}(\underline{\Delta}^{\prime};c_{\pm})
are defined is always taken to contain
(-q)^{\frac{n}{2}}
.

Lemma 8.22
.

The map (
8.51
) restricts to an isomorphism

(8.52)

\displaystyle\eta^{\mathcal{A}}_{c}\colon\mathbb{L}(\Xi^{\mathrm{fr}}_{\underline{\Delta};c})\simeq\mathcal{T}^{\mathcal{A}}_{\operatorname{res}}(\underline{\Delta}^{\prime};c_{\pm})

under which

(8.53)

\displaystyle\eta^{\mathcal{A}}_{c}(H_{k}(c))=\chi_{\omega_{k}}(c),\quad\chi_{\omega_{k}}(c)=\sum_{\nu\in W(\omega_{k})}Y_{c,\nu}

and

(8.54)

\displaystyle\eta^{\mathcal{A}}_{c}(\xi_{t_{k}})=(-q)^{k(n+1-k)/2}\chi^{(0)}_{\omega^{\vee}_{k}}(\underline{\Delta}^{\prime};c_{\pm}),

(8.55)

\displaystyle\eta^{\mathcal{A}}_{c}(\xi_{s_{k}})=(-q)^{k(n+1-k)/2}\chi^{(1)}_{\omega^{\vee}_{k}}(\underline{\Delta}^{\prime};c_{\pm}),

where

\chi^{(j)}_{\omega_{k}}(\underline{\Delta}^{\prime};c_{\pm})=\sum_{\sigma\in W/\mathrm{Stab}(\omega_{k})}\sigma\left(\prod_{\langle\alpha,\omega_{k}\rangle<0}\frac{1}{1-Y_{c,\alpha}}Y_{\overline{\xi}_{v_{k}}+j\omega_{c,k}}(\underline{\Delta}^{\prime};c_{\pm})\right)

with the product being taken over all roots
\alpha
having negative pairing with
\omega_{k}
.

Proof..

What we need to check is that
\widetilde{\eta}_{c}^{\mathcal{A}}
respects integrality with respect to the original
\Xi
-lattices: in other words, that after adjoining the indicated fractional powers of
q
it maps an element of
\mathbb{L}(\widetilde{\Lambda}^{\mathrm{fr}}_{\underline{\Delta};c})
which is a Laurent polynomial in the generators
\{\xi_{\ell}\}_{\ell\in I_{S}}
to an element in
\mathcal{T}^{\mathcal{A}}_{\operatorname{res}}(\underline{\Delta}^{\prime};c_{\pm})
, and vice versa. It is clear from the constructions of the actions of the two algebras that we have

\widetilde{\eta}_{c}^{\mathcal{A}}(Y_{z_{\pm}/N})=Y_{z^{\prime}_{\pm}/N}.

Now recall that from Lemmas
8.15
and
8.20
and Remark
8.16
that any
\xi_{\ell}
with
\ell\in I_{\leq 0}
can be expressed as an element of
\Lambda_{S_{<0}}/N
(resp.
\Lambda_{S^{\prime}_{<0}}/N
) plus a multiple of
z_{\pm}/N
(resp.
z^{\prime}_{\pm}/N
), and that the coefficients of the latter vectors are determined by the entries of the
I_{\leq 0}
-blocks of the matrices
p_{S}^{-1},\dot{p}_{S^{\prime}}^{-1}
.
Since we have observed in (
8.3
) that these blocks are identical , it follows that

\widetilde{\eta}_{c}^{\mathcal{A}}(Y_{\xi_{\ell}})=Y_{\overline{\xi}_{\ell}}\qquad\text{for all }\ell\in I_{\leq 0}.

In particular, we have
\widetilde{\eta}_{c}^{\mathcal{A}}(Y_{\xi_{h_{\pm}}})=Y_{\overline{\xi}_{h_{\pm}}}
. So since the fractional coefficients of
\xi_{h_{\pm}}
in formula (
8.40
) are identical to those of
\overline{\xi}_{h_{\pm}}
in (
8.50
), we may cancel the corresponding quantum torus elements to derive the intertwining formulas (
8.54
) and (
8.55
), from which it follows that Laurent polynomials in the generators
\{\xi_{\ell}\}_{\ell\in I^{\geq 0}_{S}}
are mapped to elements of
\mathcal{T}^{\mathcal{A}}_{\operatorname{res}}(\underline{\Delta}^{\prime};c_{\pm})
and vice versa. Finally, the intertwining relation for the fundamental Hamiltonians follows from the corresponding relation for the Toda Hamiltonians
H_{k}
in Proposition
6.30
.
∎

Example 8.23
.

If
G=SL_{2}
, then under the homomorphism from Lemma
8.22
we have

\displaystyle(-q)^{-\frac{1}{2}}Y_{\widetilde{\xi}_{t_{1}}}

\displaystyle\longmapsto\frac{1}{1-Y_{c,\alpha_{1}}}Y_{\widetilde{\xi}_{v_{1}}}+\frac{1}{1-Y_{c,-\alpha_{1}}}Y_{-\widetilde{\xi}_{v_{1}}},

\displaystyle(-q)^{-\frac{1}{2}}Y_{\widetilde{\xi}_{s_{1}}}

\displaystyle\longmapsto\frac{1}{1-Y_{c,\alpha_{1}}}Y_{\widetilde{\xi}_{v_{1}}+\omega_{c,1}}+\frac{1}{1-Y_{c,-\alpha_{1}}}Y_{-\widetilde{\xi}_{v_{1}}-\omega_{c,1}}.

Since
\eta_{c}(\xi_{h_{\pm}})=\overline{\xi}_{h_{\pm}}
, clearing the common fractional powers on both sides as in the proof of Lemma
8.22
we see that

\displaystyle(-q)^{-\frac{1}{2}}Y_{\xi_{t_{1}}}

\displaystyle\longmapsto\frac{1}{1-Y_{c,\alpha_{1}}}Y_{\overline{\xi}_{v_{1}}}+\frac{1}{1-Y_{c,-\alpha_{1}}}Y_{\overline{\xi}_{v_{1}}^{o}}

\displaystyle(-q)^{-\frac{1}{2}}Y_{\xi_{s_{1}}}

\displaystyle\longmapsto\frac{1}{1-Y_{c,\alpha_{1}}}Y_{\overline{\xi}_{v_{1}}+\omega_{c,1}}+\frac{1}{1-Y_{c,-\alpha_{1}}}Y_{\overline{\xi}_{v_{1}}^{o}-\omega_{c,1}},

where the lattice element
\overline{\xi}_{v_{1}}^{o}
is defined as in Remark
8.18
.

Remark 8.24
.

In the original formulation of the residue conditions (
8.19
), the elements
Y_{c,\alpha^{\vee}}
do not lie in the universal Laurent ring
\mathbb{L}(\Lambda^{c}_{S^{\prime}})
. But in
\mathcal{A}
-variable residue quantum torus
\mathcal{T}^{\mathcal{A}}_{\operatorname{res}}(\underline{\Delta}^{\prime};c_{\pm})
one can clear out the denominators in the cross-ratio coordinates
Y_{c,\alpha^{\vee}}
to express the residue condition using only elements of the universal Laurent ring
\mathbb{L}(\Xi^{c}_{S^{\prime}})
.

8.4.
Global gluing isomorphism

Now we extend the local gluing isomorphisms from the previous sections to global ones, i.e. to the level of universal Laurent rings. The first step is to define the global analog of the residue quantum torus associated to a
c_{\pm}
-isolating cluster on
S^{\prime}
.

Since the elements of the denominator set (
8.15
) are polynomials in frozen
\mathcal{A}
-variables, it follows that
Ø(c_{\pm})
is invariant under mutation. Similarly, it is preserved under the action of the diagonally embedded Weyl group
W(c_{\pm})
by cluster transformations, since in any cluster Weyl group
W(c_{\pm})
acts by the reflection representation on commutative subalgebra generated by the elements
Y_{\omega_{c,j}}
.

Definition 8.25
.

Let
Q_{\underline{\Delta}^{\prime};c_{\pm}}
be an isolating cluster for
c_{\pm}
subordinate to an ideal triangulation
\underline{\Delta}^{\prime}
.
The
G=SL_{n+1}
(resp.
PGL_{n+1}
)
residue universal Laurent ring

\mathbb{L}_{G,S^{\prime};\phi}(\underline{\Delta}^{\prime})
based at
Q_{\underline{\Delta}^{\prime};c_{\pm}}
consists of all elements
A
of the residue quantum torus
\mathcal{T}_{\operatorname{res}}^{\mathcal{A}}(\underline{\Delta}^{\prime};c_{\pm})
(resp.
\mathcal{T}_{\operatorname{res}}(\underline{\Delta}^{\prime};c_{\pm})
) for which there exists an element
d=d(Y_{c,\alpha_{1}},\ldots Y_{c,\alpha_{n}})
of the denominator set
Ø(c_{\pm})
such that
dA
lies in the symplectically-reduced universal Laurent ring
\mathbb{L}^{c}_{\Xi(\underline{\Delta}^{\prime};c_{\pm})}
from (
8.4
).

Remark 8.26
.

As observed in in Remark
8.24
, the condition (
8.19
) can be equivalently expressed using elements of the universal Laurent ring in the isolating cluster
Q_{\underline{\Delta};c_{\pm}}
. When expressed in terms of the coordinates of a general cluster, these elements will be complicated elements of the corresponding quantum torus, and hence the residue conditions will no longer take the simple binomial form from Definition
8.6
.

Remark 8.27
.

More generally, given any locally glueable pair
(Q,Q_{\mathrm{cut}})
we can define a corresponding residue universal Laurent ring
\mathbb{L}_{\operatorname{res}}(Q_{\mathrm{cut}})
as the set of all elements
A
of the residue quantum torus
\mathcal{T}_{\operatorname{res}}(Q_{\mathrm{cut}})
such that for some
d
in the denominator set,
dA
lies in the symplectically reduced universal Laurent ring associated to
Q_{\mathrm{cut}}
.

The definition of
\mathbb{L}_{G,S^{\prime};\phi}
is phrased in terms of a particular special isolating cluster coordinate system
Q_{\underline{\Delta};c_{\pm}}
for the pair of tacked circles
c_{\pm}
.
We now show that the definition is actually independent of the choice of isolating cluster
Q_{\underline{\Delta};c_{\pm}}
.

Proposition 8.28
.

The algebra
\mathbb{L}_{G,S^{\prime};\phi}
does not depend on the choice of isolating cluster for the pair of tacked circles
c_{\pm}
: if
Q_{\underline{\Delta}^{\prime}_{1},c_{\pm}}
and
Q_{\underline{\Delta}_{2}^{\prime},c_{\pm}}
are two such clusters and

\mu_{1,2}\colon Q_{\underline{\Delta}^{\prime}_{2},c_{\pm}}\rightarrow Q_{\underline{\Delta}^{\prime}_{1},c_{\pm}}

the cluster transformation taking one to the other, we have

\mu_{1,2}\left(\mathbb{L}_{G,S^{\prime};\phi}(\underline{\Delta}^{\prime}_{2})\right)=\mathbb{L}_{G,S^{\prime};\phi}(\underline{\Delta}_{1}^{\prime}).

Proof..

We prove the proposition in the
G=SL_{n+1}
case, which subsumes the
PGL_{n+1}
one. Suppose that
A
is an element of
\mathbb{L}_{G,S^{\prime};\phi}(\underline{\Delta}^{\prime}_{2})
. Then since the denominator set
Ø(c_{\pm})
is mutation invariant and
dA
is universally Laurent for some
d\in Ø(c_{\pm})
, it follows that
\mu_{1,2}(A)
is an element of the
Ø(c_{\pm})
-localized quantum torus
\mathcal{T}_{\operatorname{res}}(\underline{\Delta}^{\prime};c_{\pm})
. Moreover since
A
is
W(c_{\pm})
-invariant and the Weyl group acts by cluster transformations, the same is true of
\mu_{1,2}(A)
. So what remains to be proved is that if
A
satisfies the residue conditions (
8.19
) defined using isolating cluster
Q_{\underline{\Delta}_{2}^{\prime},c_{\pm}}
, then it also satisfies the analogous conditions defined using isolating cluster
Q_{\underline{\Delta}_{1}^{\prime},c_{\pm}}
.

Given a root
\alpha
, we abbreviate

Y_{\alpha^{\vee}}=Y_{c,\alpha^{\vee}}({\underline{\Delta}^{\prime}_{1};c})\in\mathcal{T}({\underline{\Delta}^{\prime}_{1};c}),\quad Y^{\prime}_{\alpha^{\vee}}=Y_{c,\alpha^{\vee}}({\underline{\Delta}^{\prime}_{2};c_{\pm}})\in\mathcal{T}(\underline{\Delta}^{\prime}_{2};c)

for the elements defined by (
8.9
) in clusters
Q_{\underline{\Delta}^{\prime}_{1};c}
and
Q_{\underline{\Delta}^{\prime}_{2};c_{\pm}}
respectively.
For
l\in\mathbb{Z}
, set

f_{l\alpha}=\mu_{1,2}(Y^{\prime}_{l\alpha^{\vee}})Y_{l\alpha^{\vee}}^{-1}\in\operatorname{Frac}\left(\mathcal{T}_{{\underline{\Delta}_{1}^{\prime};c}}\right)_{0},

where the subscript indicates the
f_{l\alpha}
have degree zero with respect to the internal grading (
8.13
).
Since the
Y_{\omega_{c,j}}
are frozen
\mathscr{A}
-variables, no monomial
Y_{{c,\lambda}}
can ever appear as the argument of a quantum dilogarithm in the automorphism part of
\mu_{1,2}
, and so
\mu_{1,2}(Y^{\prime}_{l\alpha^{\vee}})
is regular and nonvanishing at each divisor
d_{\alpha,k}(c)
. In particular, the elements
f_{l\alpha}
are regular at all
d_{\alpha,k}(c)
. Note also that we have

(8.56)

\displaystyle 1=f_{0}=f_{l\alpha}f_{-l\alpha}^{[l\alpha]}.

Now the key point is that since the Weyl group
W(c_{\pm})
acts via the reflection representation (
8.12
) in any isolating cluster, for each root
\beta
and Weyl group element
w
we have

w\cdot Y_{\beta^{\vee}}=Y_{w(\beta)^{\vee}},\qquad w\cdot Y^{\prime}_{\beta^{\vee}}=Y^{\prime}_{w(\beta)^{\vee}}.

Since
s_{\alpha}(\alpha)=-\alpha
, this means that

(8.57)

\displaystyle s_{\alpha}\cdot f_{l\alpha}=f_{-l\alpha}.

Recall from Remark
8.5
that on the piece of internal degree zero, the restriction maps
|_{d_{\alpha,k}}
are ring homomorphisms. Combining with (
8.56
) and the property (
8.18
) we get that for any
r\in\mathbb{Z}
,

\displaystyle 1

\displaystyle=f_{l\alpha}\big|_{d_{\alpha,r}}\cdot\left((s_{\alpha}f_{l\alpha})^{[l\alpha]}\right)\big|_{d_{\alpha,r}}

\displaystyle=f_{l\alpha}\big|_{d_{\alpha,r}}\cdot f_{l\alpha}^{[(l-r)\alpha]}\big|_{d_{\alpha,r}}

and in particular
f_{l\alpha}^{2}\big|_{d_{\alpha,l}}=1.

Since
f_{l\alpha}
is obtained from a cluster monomial by conjugation by a product of quantum dilogarithms this forces the sign

(8.58)

\displaystyle f_{l\alpha}\big|_{d_{\alpha,l}}=1.

Now fix an element weight
\nu
of the
SL_{n+1}
-weight lattice, and a pair
(\alpha,k)
. We set

m=\langle\nu,\alpha\rangle-k,

so that
s_{\alpha}(\nu)+k\alpha=\nu-m\alpha
.
Then the condition (
8.19
) for
\mu_{1,2}(A)
defined relative to isolating cluster
Q(\underline{\Delta}^{\prime}_{1};c_{\pm})
says that

\displaystyle\mathrm{Res}_{\alpha,k}\left(\mu_{1,2}(A_{\nu})+\mu_{1,2}(A_{\nu-m\alpha})Y_{m\alpha^{\vee}}\right)=0.

If we take the difference of this condition and the image under
\mu_{1,2}
of the residue condition for
A
in the isolating cluster subordinate to
\underline{\Delta}^{\prime}_{2}
, and then multiply from the right by the
d_{\alpha,k}
-regular element
Y_{m\alpha^{\vee}}^{-1}
, we see that equivalence of the two residue conditions amounts to showing that

(8.59)

\displaystyle\mathrm{Res}_{\alpha,k}\left(\mu_{1,2}(A_{\nu-m\alpha})(f_{m\alpha}-1)\right)=0.

Working in the extended lattice
\tilde{\Xi}_{c}
if necessary, we can factor

\mu_{1,2}(A_{\nu-m\alpha})=BY_{(\nu-m\alpha)^{\vee}},

so that
A_{\nu-m\alpha}f_{m\alpha}=Bf_{m\alpha}^{[\nu-m\alpha]}Y_{(\nu-m\alpha)^{\vee}}
.
Since
B
has at most simple poles, formula (
8.59
) will follow provided we can show that

f_{m\alpha}^{[\nu-m\alpha]}\big|_{d_{\alpha,k}}=1.

But this follows from conjugating (
8.58
) by
Y_{(\nu-m\alpha)^{\vee}}
and using (
6.12
).
∎

Remark 8.29
.

(1)

The properties of the cluster transformation
\mu_{1,2}
used in the proof of Proposition
8.28
are that it intertwines the reflection representation of
W(c_{\pm})
by monomial transformations in the two cutting clusters; that it commutes with all
Y_{c,\lambda}
(and hence preserves the internal grading); and that the image of any
Y_{c,\beta^{\vee}}^{\prime}
is a subtraction-free noncommutative rational fraction regular at each divisor
d_{\alpha,k}(c)
.

(2)

Proposition
8.28
can also be proved by a direct calculation using the factorization of the mutation sequence realizing the re-isolation move given in Section
7
.

Since diffeomorphisms send isolating triangulations to isolating triangulations, it follows from Proposition
8.28
that the mapping class group
\Gamma_{S^{\prime}}
acts on
\mathbb{L}_{G,S^{\prime};\phi}
. This action factors through the quotient by
\left\langle\tau_{c_{+}}\tau_{c_{-}}\right\rangle
and thus gives rise to an action of the centralizer
\Gamma_{S;c}
of
\tau_{c}
in
\Gamma_{S}
on
\mathbb{L}_{G,S^{\prime};\phi}
.

Remark 8.30
.

The intersection of
\mathbb{L}_{G,S^{\prime};\phi}
with the degree zero internal-graded piece of the residue quantum torus defines a subalgebra which we denote by
\mathbb{L}_{G,S^{\circ}_{c}}
. If
A\in\mathbb{L}_{S^{\circ}_{c}}
, the residue conditions imply that
A=A_{0}
is regular at each divisor
d_{\alpha,k}
and is thus an element of the non-localized quantum torus.

Theorem 8.31

(Gluing universal Laurent rings in the case of an isolatable curve)
.

Suppose
c
is an oriented isolatable simple closed curve on
S
, and if
G
has rank
n=1
suppose that
S
is not a torus with a single puncture or tacked circle. Then in any
c
-isolating cluster
Q_{S,\underline{\Delta};c}
the local isomomorphism
\eta_{c,\underline{\Delta}}^{\mathcal{A}}
from (
8.52
) restricts to give isomorphisms of algebras

(8.60)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{L}_{SL_{n+1},S}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{L}_{SL_{n+1},S^{\prime};\phi}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{L}_{PGL_{n+1},S}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{L}_{PGL_{n+1},S^{\prime};\phi}}
\leftarrow
\rightarrow
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta_{c,\underline{\Delta}}}
\leftarrow
\rightarrow
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta_{c,\underline{\Delta}}}
\hookleftarrow
\rightarrow
\hookleftarrow
\rightarrow

Proof..

We prove the theorem using the intertwining relation for Baxter operators under Whittaker transform together with the 1-step criterion for universal Laurentness. Since the proof is the same for both groups
SL_{n+1}
and
PGL_{n+1}
, in the following we suppress it in the notations, and assume that we have made the necessary extension of scalars in the simply-connected case. First we show that the image of
\eta_{c}
is contained in the universal Laurent ring
\mathbb{L}_{S^{\prime};\phi}
. It is immediate from the local construction (
8.27
) that any element
\eta_{c,\underline{\Delta}}(A)
in the image of
\mathbb{L}_{S}
remains Laurent under mutations at all quiver nodes in the cluster
Q_{S^{\prime},\underline{\Delta}^{\prime};c_{\pm}}
except possibly for the ‘handles’
e_{v_{j}^{\pm}}
for
j=1,\ldots,n+1
. On the other hand, unless
G
is of type
A_{1}
and
S
is a torus with one puncture or tacked circle, we have in the cluster
Q_{S;\underline{\Delta},c}
two Baxter sequences of mutations associated to the Toda chain subquiver
9
9

9

The excluded case mentioned in the statement of the theorem is the degenerate one in which case the two vertices
h_{\pm}
are amalgamated together.
.
For example, the Baxter sequence associated to the ‘positive’ end of the Toda subquiver consists of the mutations

\mu_{\mathrm{Baxter},+}=\mu_{s_{1}}\circ\mu_{t_{1}}\circ\cdots\circ\mu_{s_{n}}\circ\mu_{t_{n}}\circ\mu_{h_{+}}.

With respect to the sign convention (
3.8
) for mutation of bases, the cluster transformation
\mu_{\mathrm{Baxter},h_{+}}
admits a factorization

\mu_{\mathrm{Baxter},h_{+}}=\mu_{\mathrm{Baxter},+}^{\prime}\circ\mu^{\sharp}_{\mathrm{Baxter},+}.

On the side of the surface
S^{\prime}
, consider the sequence of
n+1
commuting mutations at the vertices
v^{+}_{1},\ldots,v^{+}_{n+1}
of the quiver
Q_{\underline{\Delta}^{\prime};c_{\pm}}
. Using the same sign convention (
3.8
), the corresponding cluster transformation factors as

\mu_{\mathrm{hat},+}=\mu_{\mathrm{hat},+}^{\prime}\circ\mu^{\sharp}_{\mathrm{hat},+},

where

(8.61)

\displaystyle\mu^{\sharp}_{\mathrm{hat},+}=\mathrm{Ad}\left(\prod_{j=1}^{n+1}\Psi(Y_{v^{+}_{j}}^{-1})\right).

So recalling the action (
8.2
) of
Y_{h_{+}},Y_{s_{k}},Y_{t_{k}}
on
M[S_{\leq 0}]
along with that of the
Y_{v_{+}}
on
M[S^{\prime}_{\leq 0}]
defined by (
8.24
) and using the intertwining relation (
6.53
) for the inverse of the Baxter operator
Q^{o}(z)
with
z=Y_{h_{+}}^{-1}
, we deduce that

(8.62)

\displaystyle\mu^{\sharp}_{\mathrm{hat},+}\circ\eta_{c,\underline{\Delta}}=\eta_{c,\underline{\Delta}}\circ\mu^{\sharp}_{\mathrm{Baxter},+}.

The mutated quiver
\mu_{Baxter,+}(Q_{S;\underline{\Delta},c})
again contains a Toda subquiver formed by a subset of basis vectors
\{e_{\ell}:\ell\in I^{\prime}_{Toda}\}
, and recall from (
5.32
) that this set of vectors coincides with the image under
\mu^{\sharp}_{\mathrm{Baxter},+}
of the vectors
\{e_{\ell}:\ell\in I^{>0}_{S}\}
spanning the Toda subquiver of
Q_{S;\underline{\Delta},c}
:

\{e_{\ell}~\big|~\ell\in I^{\prime}_{Toda}\}=\{\mu_{\mathrm{Baxter},+}^{\prime}(e_{\ell})~\big|~\ell\in I^{>0}_{S}\}.

Hence for any
A\in\mathbb{L}_{S}(\underline{\Delta};c)
, the fact that
\mu_{Baxter,+}(A)
is universally Laurent implies that the element
\mu^{\sharp}_{\mathrm{Baxter},+}(A)
of the quantum torus
\mathcal{T}(\underline{\Delta};c)
remains Laurent under mutation in each direction
\ell\in I^{>0}_{S}
, and so
\mu^{\sharp}_{\mathrm{Baxter},+}(A)
lies in
\mathbb{L}(\Xi^{\mathrm{fr}}_{\underline{\Delta};c})
. Since
\eta_{c,\underline{\Delta}}
maps
\mathbb{L}(\Xi^{\mathrm{fr}}_{\underline{\Delta};c})
into the residue quantum torus, it follows from the intertwining relation (
8.62
) that
\mu^{\sharp}_{\mathrm{hat},+}(\eta_{c,\underline{\Delta}}(A))
and hence
\mu_{\mathrm{hat},+}(\eta_{c,\underline{\Delta}}(A))
is again a Laurent polynomial after clearing its denominator
d\in Ø(c_{\pm})
.

But since
\mu_{\mathrm{hat},+}
is a sequence of mutations in distinct commuting directions, it follows that
d\eta_{c}(A)
must remain Laurent under each individual mutation
\mu_{v_{i}^{+}}
in the sequence.
The same argument with the Baxter operator associated to the negative end shows that
d\eta_{c,\underline{\Delta}}(A)
remains Laurent under each mutation
\mu_{v_{i}^{-}}
, and is thus universally Laurent by the 1-step criterion.
This proves that image of the universal Laurent ring
\mathbb{L}_{S}
under
\eta_{c,\underline{\Delta}}
is indeed contained in
\mathbb{L}_{S^{\prime};\phi}
.

In the other direction, it is again immediate from the construction of the local gluing isomorphism that for all
B\in\mathbb{L}_{S^{\prime};\phi}
, the element
\eta_{c;\underline{\Delta}}^{-1}(B)\in\mathbb{L}(\Xi^{\mathrm{fr}}_{\underline{\Delta};c})
remains Laurent under all mutations except possibly for those in directions
h_{\pm}
. Moreover, for any such
B
we claim that the element
\mu^{\sharp}_{\mathrm{hat},+}(B)
is again contained in the residue quantum torus
\mathcal{T}^{\mathcal{A}}_{\operatorname{res}}(\underline{\Delta}^{\prime};c_{\pm})
. Indeed, by assumption
\mu^{\sharp}_{\mathrm{hat},+}(B)
is Laurent after clearing denominators from
Ø(c_{\pm})
, and its
W(c_{\pm})
symmetry is immediate from that of the product of quantum dilogarithms on the right-hand-side of (
8.61
). The fact that
\mu^{\sharp}_{\mathrm{hat},+}(B)
satisfies the residue condition is proved similarly to Proposition
8.28
: setting

g_{m\alpha}=\mu^{\sharp}_{\mathrm{hat},+}(Y_{m\alpha^{\vee}})Y^{-1}_{m\alpha^{\vee}},

one checks directly using the
q
-difference equation (
3.7
) that for any weight
\nu
we have

g_{m\alpha}^{[\nu-m\alpha]}\big|_{d_{\alpha,k}}=1,\quad m=\langle\nu,\alpha\rangle-k.

Now since
\mu^{\sharp}_{\mathrm{hat},+}(B)
lies in the residue quantum torus, it again follows from the intertwining relation (
8.62
) that
\eta_{c;\underline{\Delta}}^{-1}(B)
remains Laurent under the cluster transformation
\mu_{\mathrm{Baxter},+}
. Since the latter consists of mutations at distinct vertices, this implies that
\eta_{c;\underline{\Delta}}^{-1}(B)
must remain Laurent under the first mutation
h_{+}
of the sequence. By a similar argument with
\mu_{\mathrm{hat},-}
we deduce that
\eta_{c;\underline{\Delta}}^{-1}(B)
remains Laurent under mutation at
h_{-}
, and so by the 1-step criterion
\eta_{c;\underline{\Delta}}^{-1}(B)
lies in
\mathbb{L}_{S}
, completing the proof of the theorem.
∎

Remark 8.32
.

The argument used to prove Theorem
8.31
works without modification to show that the local gluing isomorphism associated to general locally glueable pair
(Q,Q_{\mathrm{cut}})
restricts to an isomorphism

\eta\colon\mathbb{L}({Q})\simeq\mathbb{L}_{\operatorname{res}}({Q_{\mathrm{cut}}}),

where
\mathbb{L}_{\operatorname{res}}({Q_{\mathrm{cut}}})
is defined as in Remark
8.27
.

Our next goal is to show that the isomorphism from Theorem
8.31
does not depend on the choice of isolating cluster
Q_{\underline{\Delta};c}
for
c
, and this way prove Theorem
5.17
. The key technical ingredient is the following proposition on the intertwining of double Baxter operators under local gluing isomorphisms.

Consider the quiver
\hat{Q}_{n,m}
obtained from the quiver
Q_{n,m}
in Section
7.1
by adding a single additional vertex as indicated in the left pane of Figure
64
. We decompose its index set as
I_{n,m}=I^{<0}_{n,m}\sqcup I^{\geq 0}_{n,m}
, where
I_{n,m}^{<0}=\{s_{j,m},t_{j,m}\}_{j=1}^{m}
, and fix the identification
I^{\geq 0}_{n,m}\simeq I_{\mathrm{Toda}}^{+,-}
by sending
s_{j,n}\mapsto s_{j},t_{j,n}\mapsto t_{j}
. Hence
\hat{Q}_{n,m}
forms part of a locally glueable pair
(\hat{Q}_{n,m},\hat{Q}^{\mathrm{cut}}_{n,m})
of handle signature
\varepsilon=(+,-)
, where the quiver
\hat{Q}^{\mathrm{cut}}_{n,m}
is illustrated in the left pane of Figure
65
for
(n,m)=(2,1)
. We write
\mathbb{L}_{n,m}
for the universal Laurent ring of
\hat{Q}_{n,m}
,
\mathbb{L}^{\operatorname{res}}_{n,m}
for the residue universal Laurent ring for
\hat{Q}^{\mathrm{cut}}_{n,m}
, and
\eta_{n,m}\colon\mathbb{L}_{n,m}\simeq\mathbb{L}^{\operatorname{res}}_{n,m}
for the corresponding local gluing isomorphism.

Observe that the quiver
\hat{Q}^{\mathrm{cut}}_{m,n}

\boldsymbol{\mu}^{\mathrm{cut}}_{n,m}=\varsigma_{n,m}\circ\prod_{j=1}^{m+1}\boldsymbol{\mu}_{m,0}(v_{j,a}),

e_{c,i}

) associated to the mutation sequence
