, the cluster transformations above give isomorphisms of algebras

\boldsymbol{\mu}_{m,n}\colon\mathbb{L}_{n,m}\rightarrow\mathbb{L}_{m,n},\qquad\boldsymbol{\mu}^{\mathrm{cut}}_{m,n}\colon\mathbb{L}^{\operatorname{res}}_{n,m}\rightarrow\mathbb{L}^{\operatorname{res}}_{m,n}.

Proposition 8.33
.

The local gluing isomorphisms
\eta_{n,m}
and
\eta_{m,n}
intertwine the double Baxter cluster transformation
\boldsymbol{\mu}_{n,m}
with the cluster transformation
\boldsymbol{\mu}^{\mathrm{cut}}_{n,m}
: we have a commutative square

(8.63)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{L}_{n,m}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{L}^{\operatorname{res}}_{n,m}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{L}_{m,n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{L}^{\operatorname{res}}_{m,n}}
\leftarrow
\rightarrow
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta_{n,m}}
\leftarrow
\rightarrow
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\boldsymbol{\mu}_{n,m}}
\leftarrow
\rightarrow
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\boldsymbol{\mu}^{\mathrm{cut}}_{n,m}}
\leftarrow
\rightarrow
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta_{m,n}}

Proof..

First, observe that the restriction of the local gluing isomorphism
\eta_{n,m}\colon\mathbb{L}_{n,m}\simeq\mathbb{L}_{n,m}^{\operatorname{res}}
can equivalently be constructed using a different pair of faithful representations
\varrho_{n,m},\varrho_{n,m}^{\mathrm{cut}}
of the two algebras. The representation space
\mathscr{V}=\mathscr{V}_{n,m}
for
\varrho_{n,m}
consists of all finitely supported
\mathbb{Q}(q)[t^{\pm 1}]((z))
-valued functions of
(\lambda,\nu)\in\mathbb{Z}^{n+1}\times\mathbb{Z}^{m+1}
which vanish unless both
\lambda
and
\nu
lie in their respective dominant cones of partitions, while the underlying space
\mathscr{V}_{\mathrm{cut}}
for
\varrho^{\mathrm{cut}}_{n,m}
consists of all finitely supported
\mathbb{Q}(q)[\boldsymbol{w}^{\pm 1},t^{\pm 1}]^{S_{n+1}}((z))
-valued functions on
\mathbb{Z}^{m+1}
which vanish unless
\nu
is a partition. We define an action
\varrho_{n,m}
of
\mathbb{L}_{n,m}
on
\mathscr{V}
by restricting the following action of the ambient quantum torus on the larger space of all
\mathbb{Q}(q)[t^{\pm 1}]((z))
-valued functions: if we prepare two sets of canonical pairs
\{P_{j,n},X_{j,n}\}
and
\{P_{j,m},X_{j,m}\}
acting via the formulas (
8.20
), the action of the generators
\{Y_{s_{j,n}},Y_{t_{j,n}}\}
and
\{Y_{s_{j,m}},Y_{t_{j,m}}\}
factors through the embeddings (
6.3
) to
\mathcal{T}_{GL_{n+1}}
and
\mathcal{T}_{GL_{m+1}}
,
and we define the action of the handle generators by

\varrho_{n,m}(Y_{h_{a}})=z^{-1}P_{n+1,n}P_{1,m}^{-1},\quad\varrho_{n,m}(Y_{h_{b}})=tP_{1,n}^{-1}.

That
\mathscr{V}
is indeed preserved by
\mathbb{L}_{n,m}
follows as in Lemma
6.15
.
To construct the representation
\varrho^{\mathrm{cut}}_{n,m}
, we again let
\{Y_{s_{j,m}},Y_{t_{j,m}}\}
act via (
8.20
), and define

\varrho_{n,m}(Y_{v_{j,a}})=-q^{-1}zw_{j},\quad\varrho_{n,m}(Y_{v_{j,b}})=-qtw^{-1}_{j},\quad Y_{c,i}\mapsto D_{i}D_{i+1}^{-1},

where
D_{i}
the the
q
-shift operator from (
6.6
). That
\mathscr{V}_{\mathrm{cut}}
is preserved by the action of
\mathbb{L}^{\operatorname{res}}_{n,m}
follows by combining Lemmas
6.8
and
6.15
. Then the
GL_{n+1}
Whittaker transform

\mathcal{W}_{GL_{n+1}}\colon\mathbb{V}\simeq\mathbb{V}_{\mathrm{cut}},\quad f(\lambda,\nu)\mapsto\hat{f}(\nu)=\sum_{\lambda}f(\lambda,\nu)\overline{W(\lambda)}

identifies
\mathbb{L}_{n,m}
and
\mathbb{L}^{\operatorname{res}}_{n,m}
as subrings of the endomorphism ring of
\mathscr{V}
, and it is easy to check this identification is the same as that induced by the original definition of the local gluing isomorphism.

In a similar way we define actions
\varrho_{m,n}
and
\varrho^{\mathrm{cut}}_{m,n}
of
\mathbb{L}_{m,n}
and
\mathbb{L}^{\operatorname{res}}_{m,n}
on the same spaces
\mathscr{V}
and
\mathscr{V}_{\mathrm{cut}}
.
For
\varrho_{m,n}
, the
\{Y_{s^{\prime}_{j,n}},Y_{t^{\prime}_{j,n}}\}
and
\{Y_{s^{\prime}_{j,m}},Y_{t^{\prime}_{j,m}}\}
and
Y_{h^{\prime}_{b}}
act by the same operators as their un-primed counterparts, while we define the action of the handle generator
Y_{h^{\prime}_{a}}
by

\varrho_{m,n}(Y_{h^{\prime}_{a}})=zP^{-1}_{1,n}P_{m+1,m}.

For
\varrho_{m,n}
, all primed generators act by the same operators as their unprimed counterparts, except for

\varrho_{m,n}(Y_{v_{j,a}})=-qz^{-1}w_{j}^{-1},\quad j=1,\ldots n+1.

Now consider the factorization of the Baxter cluster transformation
\boldsymbol{\mu}_{m,n}=\boldsymbol{\mu}^{\prime}_{m,n}\circ\boldsymbol{\mu}^{\sharp}_{m,n}
where we factor each mutation as in (
3.9
). As in Section
7.1
we write
\mathbf{Q}_{n,m}
for the operator on
\mathbb{V}_{n,m}
obtained by acting with the product of dilogarithms in
\boldsymbol{\mu}^{\sharp}_{m,n}
using the representation
\varrho_{n,m}
(that this operator indeed preserves
\mathscr{V}
follows from e.g. Corollary
7.12
. Similarly, write
\mathbf{Q}^{\mathrm{cut}}_{n,m}
for the product of quantum dilogarithms in
(\boldsymbol{\mu}^{\mathrm{cut}}_{m,n})^{\sharp}
.
Now we observe that the quantum torus representations
\varrho_{n,m}
and
\varrho_{m,n}
and their cut counterparts are related via

\varrho_{n,m}=\varrho_{m,n}\circ\mu_{n,m}^{\prime},\qquad\varrho^{\mathrm{cut}}_{n,m}=\varrho^{\mathrm{cut}}_{m,n}\circ\varsigma_{n,m}\circ\mu_{n,m}^{\prime},

so it follows that for all
A\in\mathbb{L}_{n,m}
and
B\in\mathbb{L}^{\operatorname{res}}_{n,m}
we have

\displaystyle\varrho_{m,n}(\boldsymbol{\mu}_{n,m}(A))

\displaystyle=\mathbf{Q}_{n,m}\circ\varrho_{n,m}(A)\circ\mathbf{Q}_{n,m}^{-1},

\displaystyle\varrho^{\mathrm{cut}}_{m,n}(\boldsymbol{\mu}_{n,m}(B))

\displaystyle=\mathbf{Q}^{\mathrm{cut}}_{n,m}\circ\varrho^{\mathrm{cut}}_{n,m}(B)\circ(\mathbf{Q}^{\mathrm{cut}}_{n,m})^{-1}.

Hence the Proposition will follow provided we can show that
\mathcal{W}_{GL_{n+1}}\circ\mathbf{Q}_{n,m}=\mathbf{Q}^{\mathrm{cut}}_{n,m}\circ\mathcal{W}_{GL_{n+1}}
. To prove this, we use the
GL_{m+1}
-Whittaker transform: writing

\mathscr{V}_{\mathrm{cut},\mathrm{cut}}=\mathbb{Q}(q)[\boldsymbol{w}^{\pm 1},\boldsymbol{u}^{\pm 1},t^{\pm 1}]^{S_{n+1}\times S_{m+1}}((z)),

we have an isomorphism

\mathcal{W}_{GL_{m+1}}\colon\mathscr{V}_{\mathrm{cut}}\simeq\mathscr{V}_{\mathrm{cut},\mathrm{cut}},\quad\hat{f}(\nu)\mapsto\hat{\hat{f}}=\sum_{\nu}\hat{f}(\nu)\overline{W(\nu)},

and it suffices to prove that
\mathcal{W}_{GL_{m+1}}\mathcal{W}_{GL_{n+1}}\mathbf{Q}_{n,m}=\mathcal{W}_{GL_{m+1}}\mathbf{Q}^{\mathrm{cut}}_{n,m}\mathcal{W}_{GL_{n+1}}
. But applying Proposition
7.10
for
(n,k)=(n,m)
it follows that

(8.64)

\displaystyle\mathcal{W}_{GL_{m+1}}\mathcal{W}_{GL_{n+1}}\mathbf{Q}_{n,m}=\prod_{j=1}^{n+1}\prod_{k=1}^{m+1}\Psi(zw_{j}/u_{k})^{-1}\circ\mathcal{W}_{GL_{m+1}}\mathcal{W}_{GL_{n+1}},

while applying the same Proposition
n+1
times with
(n,k)=(m,0)
to move
\mathbf{Q}^{\mathrm{cut}}_{n,m}
over
\mathcal{W}_{GL_{n+1}}
also identifies the composite
\mathcal{W}_{GL_{m+1}}\mathbf{Q}^{\mathrm{cut}}_{n,m}\mathcal{W}_{GL_{n+1}}
with the right hand side of (
8.64
). This completes the proof of the Proposition.
∎

Theorem 8.34
.

The isomorphism (
8.60
) does not depend on the choice of isolating cylinder for the curve
c
:
if
\mu_{S}\colon Q_{\underline{\Delta}_{1},c}\rightarrow Q_{\underline{\Delta}_{2},c}
is the cluster transformation connecting two isolating clusters for
c
on
S
and

\mu_{S^{\prime}}\colon Q_{\underline{\Delta}^{\prime}_{1},c_{\pm}}\rightarrow Q_{\underline{\Delta}^{\prime}_{2},c_{\pm}}
the one connecting the corresponding clusters on
S^{\prime}
, then we have a commutative square

(8.65)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{L}_{S,(\underline{\Delta}_{1},c)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{L}_{{S^{\prime};\phi};(\underline{\Delta}_{1},c)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{L}_{S,(\underline{\Delta}_{2},c)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{L}_{{S^{\prime};\phi};(\underline{\Delta}_{2},c)}}
\leftarrow
\rightarrow
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta_{c,\underline{\Delta}_{1}}}
\leftarrow
\rightarrow
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mu_{S}}
\leftarrow
\rightarrow
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mu_{S^{\prime}}}
\leftarrow
\rightarrow
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta_{c,\underline{\Delta}_{2}}}

In particular, the fundamental Hamiltonians
H_{k}(c)
do not depend on the choice of isolating cylinder for
c
.

Proof..

Recall that any two
c
-isolating triangulations can be connected by a sequence of umbral moves, Dehn twists along
c
, and flips at edges disjoint from the isolating cylinder. The equivariance of
\eta_{c}
under the Dehn twist has already been proved in Proposition
8.11
. In the case of a flip outside the isolating cylinder, we use the fact that the corresponding cluster transformations can be realized as the same (relative to the identification
\iota_{\phi}
) sequences of mutations in directions solely within
I_{S}^{<0}
and
I_{S^{\prime}}^{<0}
, and so the commutativity of (
8.65
) follows from the locality property (
8.30
) of the local gluing isomorphisms.
To handle the case of an umbral move, we use the factorizations of the cluster transformation
U_{c;e}
on
S
and its counterpart
U_{c_{+};e_{+}}
on
S^{\prime}
from Proposition
7.14
. The two factorizations have almost identical forms, with the only difference coming in steps
10\circlearrowright 9
and
13\circlearrowright 12
in which double Baxter sequences
\boldsymbol{\mu}_{n,n-1}
and
\boldsymbol{\mu}_{n,n-2}
in
\mu_{S}
are replaced by in
\mu_{S^{\prime}}
by
(n+1)
-fold products of Baxter sequences
\boldsymbol{\mu}_{n-1,0}
and
\boldsymbol{\mu}_{n-2,0}
respectively. The virtue of these factorizations is that the intermediate quivers
(Q_{c,k},Q_{c_{+},k})
form locally glueable pairs in the sense of Definition
8.14
. As in the case of a flip outside the isolating cylinder, it is clear from the locality property (
8.30
) that any piece in the factorization of
\mu_{S}
which is not one of the double Baxter operators mentioned above intertwines with its counterpart in
\mu_{S^{\prime}}
under the local gluing maps. On the other hand, the intertwining in the remaining cases follows from Proposition
8.33
, completing the proof of the commutativity of (
8.65
) in the case of an umbral move.

With this in hand, the independence of the
H_{k}(c)
on the choice of isolating cylinder follows immediately: indeed, we have
\eta_{c,\underline{\Delta}}(H_{k}(c;\underline{\Delta}))=\chi_{\omega_{k}}(c)
is a Laurent polynomial in frozen
\mathcal{A}
-variables and thus commutes with all mutations on
S^{\prime}
. So

\eta_{c,\underline{\Delta}_{2}}(\mu_{S}(H_{k}(c;\underline{\Delta}_{1})))=\mu_{S^{\prime}}(\eta_{c,\underline{\Delta}_{1}}(H_{k}(c;\underline{\Delta}_{1})))=\mu_{S^{\prime}}(\chi_{\omega_{k}}(c))=\chi_{\omega_{k}}(c)=\eta_{c,\underline{\Delta}_{2}}(H_{k}(c;\underline{\Delta}_{2})),

and applying the inverse of
\eta_{c,\underline{\Delta}_{2}}
the claim follows.
∎

Remark 8.35
.

Thanks to Theorem
8.34
, the cutting isomorphism
\eta_{c}
makes sense in arbitrary cluster coordinate systems
Q,Q^{\prime}
associated to
S,S^{\prime}
respectively. Indeed, pick an isolating cluster
Q_{S,\underline{\Delta},c}
subordinate to an arbitrary
c
-isolating ideal triangulation
\underline{\Delta}
, and let
Q_{S^{\prime},\underline{\Delta},c}
be the
c_{\pm}
-isolating cluster subordinate to the triangulation
\underline{\Delta}^{\prime}=\mathcal{C}_{c}(\underline{\Delta})
of
S^{\prime}
obtained by applying the cutting functor to
\underline{\Delta}
. Then if
\mu\colon Q\rightarrow Q_{S,\underline{\Delta},c}
and
\mu_{\mathrm{cut}}\colon Q_{S^{\prime},\underline{\Delta},c}\rightarrow Q^{\prime}
are the corresponding cluster transformations, the theorem guarantees that
\mu_{\mathrm{cut}}\circ\eta_{c}(\underline{\Delta})\circ\mu^{-1}
does not depend on the choice of
c
-isolating triangulation
\underline{\Delta}
.

Theorem 8.36
.

The isomorphism (
8.60
) is equivariant with respect to the actions by cluster transformations of the centralizer
\Gamma_{S;c}
of the Dehn twist
\tau_{c}
on the algebras
\mathbb{L}_{S},\mathbb{L}_{S^{\prime};\phi}
. It restricts to an isomorphism

(8.66)

\displaystyle\eta_{c}\colon\mathbb{L}_{S}^{M_{c}^{*}\mathcal{O}_{q}(T/W)}\simeq\mathbb{L}^{c}_{S^{\circ}},

where
\mathbb{L}_{S}^{M_{c}^{*}\mathcal{O}_{q}(T/W)}
is the subalgebra of
\mathbb{L}_{S}
consisting of elements which commute with all fundamental Hamiltonians
H_{k}(c)\in\mathbb{L}_{S}
associated to
c
.

Proof..

Fix a triangulation
\underline{\Delta}\in\widehat{\operatorname{Pt}}_{c}(S)
of
S
in which
c
has a shadow, and consider
\mathbb{L}_{S}
as being embedded in the corresponding quantum torus. Similarly, we consider the triangulation
\underline{\Delta}^{\prime}=\mathcal{C}_{c}(\underline{\Delta})
of
S^{\prime}
obtained by applying the cutting functor to
\underline{\Delta}
, and embed
\mathbb{L}_{S^{\prime};\phi}
in the corresponding residue quantum torus. Recall from formula (
4.34
) that the action of a mapping class
\gamma\in\Gamma_{S;c}
on
\mathbb{L}_{S}
is given by a composite
\varsigma^{S}_{\gamma}\circ F_{\gamma^{-1}(\underline{\Delta}),\underline{\Delta}}
where the cluster transformation
F_{\gamma^{-1}(\underline{\Delta}_{S}),\Delta}
is the composite of mutations associated to a sequence of flips taking
\underline{\Delta}
to
\gamma^{-1}(\underline{\Delta})
, and
\varsigma^{S}_{\gamma}
is the quasi-permutation morphism induced by the diffeomorphism
\gamma
. The action of
\gamma
on
\mathbb{L}_{S^{\prime};\phi}
is given by an analogous composite
\varsigma^{S^{\prime}}_{\gamma}\circ F_{\gamma^{-1}(\underline{\Delta}^{\prime}),\underline{\Delta}^{\prime}}
. Commutativity of the square

(8.67)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{L}_{S,(\gamma^{-1}(\underline{\Delta}),c)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{L}_{{S^{\prime};\phi};(\gamma^{-1}(\underline{\Delta}),c)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{L}_{S,(\underline{\Delta},c)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{L}_{{S^{\prime};\phi};(\underline{\Delta},c)}}
\leftarrow
\rightarrow
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta_{c,\gamma^{-1}(\underline{\Delta})}}
\leftarrow
\rightarrow
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varsigma^{S}_{\gamma}}
\leftarrow
\rightarrow
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varsigma^{S^{\prime}}_{\gamma}}
\leftarrow
\rightarrow
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta_{c,\underline{\Delta}}}

is immediate, since the morphisms
\varsigma^{S}_{\gamma},\varsigma^{S^{\prime}}_{\gamma}
are simply relabelings of quiver index sets (which correspond to faces of the bicolored graphs on
S,S^{\prime}
) induced by the same diffeomorphism
\gamma
. On the other hand, if we apply the previous theorem with

\underline{\Delta}_{1}=\underline{\Delta},\quad\underline{\Delta}_{2}=\gamma^{-1}(\underline{\Delta}),\quad\mu_{S}=F_{\gamma^{-1}(\underline{\Delta}),\underline{\Delta}},\quad\mu_{S^{\prime}}=F_{\gamma^{-1}(\underline{\Delta}^{\prime}),\underline{\Delta}^{\prime}},

we get the commutativity of the square

(8.68)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{L}_{S,(\underline{\Delta},c)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{L}_{{S^{\prime};\phi};(\underline{\Delta}^{\prime},c)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{L}_{S,(\gamma^{-1}(\underline{\Delta}),c)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{L}_{{S^{\prime};\phi};(\gamma^{-1}(\underline{\Delta}^{\prime}),c)}}
\leftarrow
\rightarrow
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta_{c,\underline{\Delta}}}
\leftarrow
\rightarrow
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F_{\gamma^{-1}(\underline{\Delta}),\underline{\Delta}}}
\leftarrow
\rightarrow
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F_{\gamma^{-1}(\underline{\Delta}^{\prime}),\underline{\Delta}^{\prime}}}
\leftarrow
\rightarrow
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta_{c,\gamma^{-1}(\underline{\Delta})}}

Stacking the second square on top of the first, it follows that the isomorphism
\eta_{c}
is equivariant for the action of
\Gamma_{S;c}
as claimed.

For the final statement about the commutant, recall that the
\mathbb{L}_{S^{\circ}}^{c}
the subalgebra in
\mathbb{L}_{S^{\prime};\phi}
consisting of elements which commute with all
Y_{c,\lambda}
. This condition is equivalent to commutativity with all elementary symmetric polynomials
\chi_{\omega_{k}}(c)
, and the latter are interwined under
\eta_{c}
with
H_{k}(c)\in\mathbb{L}_{S}
, which proves (
8.66
).
∎

8.5.
Non-isolatable curves and multiple cuts

We now discuss the extension of the previous theorems to non-isolatable simple closed curves
c
, so that
S^{\prime}=S_{+}\sqcup S_{-}
where
S_{-}
is a surface of genus
g>0
with exactly one tacked circle. In this case, we have no a priori definition of the algebra
\mathbb{L}_{res}^{c}
. Moreover, we do not have an action of the Weyl group by cluster transformations at the level of the cut surface
S^{\prime}
, since one of its components is the exceptional surface
S_{-}
. To handle these problems we use the trick of first cutting
S
along an auxiliary cycle. In the present article we do this for
g>1
, and leave the corner case
g=1
(which requires separate techniques) for the companion paper.

We start with a quick discussion of how to iterate the constructions from the previous section, allowing us to make multiple cuts on a surface
S
.

Definition 8.37
.

Suppose
S_{a,c}
is a surface with two pairs of tacked circles
a_{\pm},c_{\pm}
which admits both an
a_{\pm}
-isolating triangulation and a (possibly different)
c_{\pm}
-isolating tringulation.
Let
Q_{S_{a,c};c_{\pm}}
be a
c_{\pm}
-isolating cluster on
S_{a,c}
,
\mathcal{T}^{a,c}(S_{a,c})
the symplectic reduction of the corresponding quantum torus at both pairs of tacked circles
a_{\pm},c_{\pm}
, and
\mathcal{T}^{a,c}_{\mathrm{loc}}(S_{a,c};c_{\pm})
the localization of the latter at the denominator set
Ø(a_{\pm},c_{\pm})=Ø(a_{\pm})Ø(c_{\pm})
. We define the residue universal Laurent ring
\mathbb{L}_{S_{a,c};\phi_{a},\phi_{c}}
based in
c_{\pm}
-isolating cluster
Q_{S_{a,c};c_{\pm}}
to be the set of elements
A\in\mathcal{T}^{a,c}_{\mathrm{loc}}(S_{a,c};c_{\pm})
such that:

(1)

There exists some
d\in Ø(a_{\pm},c_{\pm})
such that
dA
lies in the symplectically reduced universal Laurent ring
\mathbb{L}^{a,c}_{S_{a,c}}
;

(2)

The element
A
is invariant under the action by cluster transformations of the product of Weyl groups
W(a_{\pm})\times W(c_{\pm})
;

(3)

The element
A
satisfies the residue conditions (
8.19
) for the divisors
d_{\alpha,k}(c)
, and for any
a_{\pm}
-isolating cluster
Q_{S_{a,c};a_{\pm}}
on
S_{a,c}
, its image
\mu(A)
in
\mathcal{T}^{a,c}_{\mathrm{loc}}(S_{a,c};a_{\pm})
under the cluster transformation
\mu:Q_{S_{a,c};c_{\pm}}\rightarrow Q_{S_{a,c};a_{\pm}}
satisfies the corresponding conditions for the divisors
d_{\alpha,k}(a)
.

Remark 8.38
.

(1)

It follows as in Proposition
8.28
that
\mathbb{L}_{S_{a,c};\phi_{a},\phi_{c}}
does not depend on the choice of
c_{\pm}
-isolating cluster
Q_{S_{a,c};c_{\pm}}
. Moreover, it is clear that we have
\mathbb{L}_{S_{a,c};\phi_{a},\phi_{c}}\simeq\mathbb{L}_{S_{c,a};\phi_{c},\phi_{a}}
, the isomorphism being induced by the
\mu
in part (3) of Definition
8.37
.

(2)

It is straightforward to extend Definition
8.37
to the case of a surface
S
with an arbitrary number of pairs
\{a_{\pm}^{(1)},\ldots,a^{(m)}_{\pm}\}
of tacked circles such that
S
admits an
a^{(i)}_{\pm}
-isolating triangulation for all
1\leq i\leq m
.

Now suppose that
a
and
c
are two disjoint essential simple closed curves on
S
, and let
S_{a}
be the surface obtained by cutting
S
along
a
. We denote by
S_{a,c}
the surface obtained by further cutting
S_{a}
along
c
.
If we write
\mathbb{L}_{S}
for the universal Laurent ring associated to the pair
(G,S)
, Theorem
8.31
provides canonical isomorphisms of algebras
\eta_{a}\colon\mathbb{L}_{S}\simeq\mathbb{L}_{S_{a};\phi_{a}}
and
\eta_{c}(S_{a})\colon\mathbb{L}_{S_{a}}\simeq\mathbb{L}_{S_{a,c};\phi_{c}}
. Moreover, since
\eta_{c}(S_{a})
sends each
Y_{\xi_{a_{\pm},i}}
to its counterpart on
S_{a,c}
, it follows that
\eta_{c}(S_{a})
descends to an isomorphism
\mathbb{L}^{a}_{S_{a}}\simeq\mathbb{L}_{S_{a,c};\phi_{c}}
between the corresponding symplectic reductions for the elements (
8.2
) associated to the pair
a_{\pm}
, and induces an isomorphism between as the localizations of the latter at the denominator set
Ø(a_{\pm})
. We abuse notations and denote the corresponding maps by the same symbol
\eta_{c}(S_{a})
. Our goal is to prove that
\eta_{c}(S_{a})
restricts to an isomorphism of residue universal Laurent rings, which amounts to showing that it intertwines the actions of the Weyl group
W(a_{\pm})
on
S_{a}
and
S_{a,c}
, as well as the residue conditions associated to the divisors
d_{\alpha,k}(a)
on the two surfaces. We first observe this is indeed the true in the special case that
S
admits an ideal triangulation
\underline{\Delta}
containing isolating cylinders for both curves
a
and
c
, where the needed intertwining properties follow immediately from the locality of both the Weyl group action and local gluing isomorphisms with respect to isolating cylinders:

Lemma 8.39
.

Suppose that
S
admits an ideal triangulation
\underline{\Delta}
containing isolating cylinders for both curves
a
and
c
. Then the isomorphism
\eta_{c}\colon\mathbb{L}^{a}_{S_{a}}\simeq\mathbb{L}_{S_{a,c};\phi_{c}}
is equivariant with respect to the actions of
W(a_{\pm})
by cluster transformations, and induces an isomorphism

(8.69)

\displaystyle\eta_{c}(S_{a})\colon\mathbb{L}_{S_{a};\phi_{a}}\simeq\mathbb{L}_{S_{a,c};\phi_{a},\phi_{c}},

The isomorphism (
8.69
) and its counterpart
\eta_{a}(S_{c})
fit into a commutative square

(8.70)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{L}_{S}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{L}_{S_{a};\phi_{a}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{L}_{S_{c};\phi_{c}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{L}_{S_{a,c};\phi_{a},\phi_{c}}}
\leftarrow
\rightarrow
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta_{a}(S)}
\leftarrow
\rightarrow
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta_{c}(S)}
\leftarrow
\rightarrow
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta_{c}(S_{a})}
\leftarrow
\rightarrow
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta_{a}(S_{c})}

Proof..

Since
S
admits an ideal triangulation
\underline{\Delta}
containing isolating cylinders for both curves
a
and
c
, we can apply the constructions of Definition
5.5
and
5.8
simultaneously in each isolating cylinder to produce quivers
Q(\underline{\Delta};a,c)
on
S
,
Q(\underline{\Delta}^{\prime};a_{\pm},c)
on
S_{a}
, and
Q(\underline{\Delta}^{\prime\prime};a_{\pm},c_{\pm})
on
S_{a,c}
. Then the fact that
\eta_{c}(S_{a})
intertwines the
W(a_{\pm})
action follows from the locality property (
8.30
) of
\eta_{c}(S_{a})
with respect to the isolating cylinder for
c
, and fact that the
W(a_{\pm})
action is realized locally within the isolating cylinder for
a
. For the same reason, it follows that
\eta_{c}(S_{a})
intertwines the
a_{\pm}
-residue conditions on
S_{a}
and
S_{a,c}
, and thus induces an isomorphism
\mathbb{L}_{S_{a};\phi_{a}}\simeq\mathbb{L}_{S_{a,c};\phi_{a},\phi_{c}}
. The commutativity of the square (
8.70
) is again immediate from the locality of
\eta_{a},\eta_{c}
with respect to the disjoint isolating cylinders for
a,c
.
∎

Remark 8.40
.

Observe that
S
admits an ideal triangulation containing isolating cylinders for curves
a
and
c
with
a\cap c=\emptyset
if and only if each connected component of the surface
S_{a,c}
has at least one special point which is not one of the tacks on
a_{\pm},c_{\pm}
.

We can bootstrap this Lemma to obtain the following Corollary extending part of its conclusion to the case in which the curves
a
and
c
cannot be simultaneously isolated.

Corollary 8.41
.

Suppose that the surface
S_{c}
obtained by cutting
S
along
c
has a connected component
S_{-}
given by a surface of genus
g>1
with a single tacked circle
c_{-}
, and as above let
a
be a simple closed curve on
S
with
a\cap c=\emptyset
. Then the isomorphism
\eta_{c}\colon\mathbb{L}^{a}_{S_{a}}\simeq\mathbb{L}_{S_{a,c};\phi_{c}}
is equivariant with respect to the actions of
W(a_{\pm};S_{a})
and
W(a_{\pm};S_{a,c})
by cluster transformations, and induces an isomorphism

(8.71)

\displaystyle\eta_{c}(S_{a})\colon\mathbb{L}_{S_{a};\phi_{a}}\simeq\mathbb{L}_{S_{a,c};\phi_{a},\phi_{c}}.

Proof..

Since
g(S_{-})>1
we can choose a non-separating curve
d
on the connected component of
S_{a,c}
containing
c_{-}
which is disjoint from
c_{-}
and
a_{\pm}
. We abuse notation and denote by the same symbol the corresponding curve on
S_{a}
, and write
S_{a,d}
for the surface obtained by cutting
S_{a}
along
d
. Then it follows from Lemma
8.39
that we have a commutative square of isomorphisms

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{L}^{a}_{S_{a}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{L}^{a}_{S_{a,c};\phi_{c}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{L}^{a}_{S_{a,d};\phi_{d}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{L}^{a}_{S_{a,c,d};\phi_{c},\phi_{d}}}
\leftarrow
\rightarrow
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta_{c}(S_{a})}
\leftarrow
\rightarrow
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta_{d}(S_{a})}
\leftarrow
\rightarrow
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta_{d}(S_{a,c})}
\leftarrow
\rightarrow
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta_{c}(S_{a,d})}

Moreover, the same Lemma implies that
\eta_{d}(S_{a}),\eta_{c}(S_{a,d}),\eta_{d}(S_{a,c})
are all
W(a_{\pm})
equivariant and intertwine residue conditions for the divisors
d_{\alpha,k}(a)
, and so we deduce the same is true of
\eta_{c}(S_{a})
as claimed.
∎

Now we can define the residue universal Laurent ring
\mathbb{L}_{S^{\prime};\phi_{c}}
in the exceptional case that
S^{\prime}=S_{+}\sqcup S_{-}
where
S_{-}
is a surface of genus
g>1
with exactly one tacked circle. Write
\mathbb{L}^{c}_{S^{\prime}}
for the symplectically reduced universal Laurent ring associated to the surface
S^{\prime}
relative to the pair of tacked circles
c_{\pm}
, and
\mathbb{L}^{c;\mathrm{loc}}_{S^{\prime}}
for its localization at the denominator set
Ø(c_{\pm})
. Then given a non-separating simple closed curve
a
on
S
disjoint from
c
which becomes a curve on
S_{-}
after cutting along
c
, we have an isomorphism of
Ø(c_{\pm})
localizations

\eta_{a}(S_{c})\colon\mathbb{L}^{c;\mathrm{loc}}_{S^{\prime}}\simeq\mathbb{L}^{c;\mathrm{loc}}_{S_{a,c};\phi_{c}}.

Definition 8.42
.

Let
(S,a,c)
be as in the paragraph above. We define the residue universal Laurent ring
\mathbb{L}_{S^{\prime};\phi}
relative to
a
by

\mathbb{L}_{S^{\prime};\phi}(a)=\eta_{a}(S_{c})^{-1}\left(\mathbb{L}_{S_{a,c};\phi_{a},\phi_{c}}\right).

With this definition, it follows from Corollary
8.41
that we have an isomorphism of algebras

(8.72)

\displaystyle\eta_{c;a}\colon\mathbb{L}_{S}\simeq\mathbb{L}_{S^{\prime};\phi}(a),\quad\eta_{c;a}=\eta_{a}^{-1}(S_{c})\circ\eta_{c}(S_{a})\circ\eta_{a}(S).

Theorem 8.43
.

The algebra
\mathbb{L}_{S^{\prime};\phi}(a)
in Definition
8.42
does not depend on the choice of auxiliary curve
a
: for all non-separating simple closed curves
a_{1},a_{2}
on
S
disjoint from
c
which become curves on
S_{-}
after cutting along
c
, we have
\mathbb{L}_{S^{\prime};\phi}(a_{1})=\mathbb{L}_{S^{\prime};\phi}(a_{2})
and
\eta_{c;a_{1}}=\eta_{c;a_{2}}
.

Proof..

Consider first the case that
a_{1}\cap a_{2}=\varnothing
, so that we have a surface
S_{c,a_{1},a_{2}}
obtained by cutting
S
along all three curves. Then we can repeatedly apply Lemma (
8.39
) to get the following chain of equalities

\displaystyle\eta_{c;a_{1}}=\eta_{a_{1}}^{-1}\eta_{c}\eta_{a_{1}}

\displaystyle=\eta_{a_{1}}^{-1}\eta_{a_{2}}^{-1}\eta_{a_{2}}\eta_{c}\eta_{a_{1}}=\eta_{a_{1}}^{-1}\eta_{a_{2}}^{-1}\eta_{c}\eta_{a_{2}}\eta_{a_{1}}

(8.73)

\displaystyle=\eta_{a_{2}}^{-1}\eta_{a_{1}}^{-1}\eta_{c}\eta_{a_{1}}\eta_{a_{2}}=\eta_{a_{2}}^{-1}\eta_{a_{1}}^{-1}\eta_{a_{1}}\eta_{c}\eta_{a_{2}}=\eta_{a_{2}}^{-1}\eta_{c}\eta_{a_{2}}=\eta_{c;a_{2}}.

On the other hand, if
a_{1}\cap a_{2}\neq\varnothing
, the assumption that
S_{-}
has genus greater than 1 guarantees that there exists a non-separating closed simple curve
b
such that
a_{1}\cap b=a_{2}\cap b=\varnothing
. So we have
\eta_{c;a_{1}}=\eta_{c;b}=\eta_{c;a_{2}}
, which completes the proof.

∎

Hence we have a single canonical isomorphism

(8.74)

\displaystyle\eta_{c}\colon\mathbb{L}_{S}\simeq\mathbb{L}_{S^{\prime};\phi}.

Theorem 8.44
.

The isomorphism (
8.74
) is equivariant with respect to the action of the mapping class group
\Gamma_{S;c}
.

Proof..

The idea of the proof is to use the freedom to express
\eta_{c}=\eta_{c,a}
for any choice of auxiliary curve
c
. Indeed, it follows from Theorem
8.36
that
\eta_{c,a}
intertwines any morphism in
\widehat{\operatorname{Pt}}\vphantom{\operatorname{Pt}}^{o}_{a_{j},c}(S)
with the corresponding morphism on the
S^{\prime}
side. So the Proposition will follow if we can show that any morphism
\mu
in
\widehat{\operatorname{Pt}}_{c}(S)
can be factored into a composition
\mu=\mu_{k}\dots\mu_{1}
, where for each factor
1\leqslant j\leqslant k
there exists an auxiliary curve
a_{j}
such that
\mu_{j}
is a morphism in
\widehat{\operatorname{Pt}}\vphantom{\operatorname{Pt}}^{o}_{a_{j},c}(S)
. To see this, recall that a general morphism
\mu\colon\underline{\Delta}\to\underline{\Delta}^{\prime}
in
\widehat{\operatorname{Pt}}_{c}(S)
takes the form
\mu=A_{\gamma}F_{\gamma^{-1}(\underline{\Delta}^{\prime}),\underline{\Delta}}
. If
e_{c},e^{\prime}_{c}
are the shadows of
c
in
\underline{\Delta},\underline{\Delta}^{\prime}
, then since
\gamma
preserves
e^{\prime}_{c}
the latter is a shadow of
c
in
\gamma^{-1}(\underline{\Delta}^{\prime})
as well. Consider a non-separating simple closed curve
a\subset S_{-}(e_{c})
such that
\underline{\Delta}
is an object in
\operatorname{Pt}^{o}_{a,c}(S)
, meaning that there exists an arc
e_{a}\in\underline{\Delta}
which is a shadow of
a
. Then take any triangulation
\underline{\Delta}^{\prime\prime}
of
S
which agrees with
\gamma^{-1}(\underline{\Delta}^{\prime})
on the subsurface
S_{+}(e_{c}^{\prime})
, and in the subsurface
S_{-}(e_{c}^{\prime})
contains a shadow of
a
among its arcs: since
a
and
c
are disjoint, such a triangulation certainly exists.
This way we get a morphism
F_{\underline{\Delta}^{\prime\prime},\underline{\Delta}}
in
\operatorname{Pt}_{a,c}(S)
. On the other hand, the morphism
F_{\gamma^{-1}(\underline{\Delta}^{\prime}),\underline{\Delta}^{\prime\prime}}
can be factored into a product of flips
F_{d_{r}}\dots F_{d_{1}}
on
S_{-}(e^{\prime}_{c})
. Given an arc
d_{j}
, let
e_{j}\neq e^{\prime}_{c}
be an edge of the quadrilateral, containing
d_{j}
is a diagonal, and
a_{j}
be a simple closed curve on
S_{-}(e^{\prime}_{c})
of which
e_{j}
is a shadow. Then
F_{d_{j}}
is a morphism in
\operatorname{Pt}^{o}_{a_{j},c}(S)
. We now turn to the morphism
A_{\gamma}
. Factoring
\gamma=\gamma_{-}\gamma_{+}
, where
\gamma_{\pm}\in\Gamma_{S_{\pm}(e^{\prime}_{c})}\subset\Gamma_{S}
, we see that
A_{\gamma_{+}}
is a morphism in
\widehat{\operatorname{Pt}}\vphantom{\operatorname{Pt}}^{o}_{a_{r},c}
, while
A_{\gamma_{-}}
can be further factored into a composition of Dehn twists
\tau_{a_{r+s}}\dots\tau_{a_{r+1}}
on
S_{-}(e^{\prime}_{c})
. Evidently,
\tau_{a_{j}}
is a morphism in
\widehat{\operatorname{Pt}}\vphantom{\operatorname{Pt}}^{o}_{a_{j},c}
, and we arrive at the desired factorization

\mu=\tau_{a_{r+s}}\dots\tau_{a_{r+1}}A_{\gamma_{+}}F_{d_{r}}\dots F_{d_{1}}F_{+}.

∎
